### [1] [Evaluating LLM Usage for Efficient and Explainable Numerical and Classified Implicit Sentiment Analysis of Product Desirability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23701)
*Sherri Weitl-Harms,John Hastings*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces an LLM-based framework for quantifying product desirability from qualitative feedback. Using PDT datasets with human annotations, zero-shot LLM sentiment scoring achieved Pearson correlations up to 0.97 and classification accuracy up to 94%, far surpassing lexicon and transformer baselines. GPT-4o-mini delivered near state-of-the-art performance at 94% lower cost, with added confidence ratings and rationale explanations for interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Qualitative product feedback captures nuanced user experiences, but extracting and quantifying implicit sentiment from unstructured text is challenging. Traditional lexicon-based and transformer approaches underperform, and manual annotation is costly and unscalable. The authors seek a scalable, accurate, interpretable, and cost-efficient automated method for sentiment measurement in product desirability assessment.

Method: The authors propose a framework employing LLMs (GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, and others) in a zero-shot setting to generate both continuous numerical sentiment scores and categorical sentiment classifications directly from qualitative PDT responses. Two PDT datasets (ZORQ and CARMA, 106 respondent term groupings) with gold-standard human annotations are used. LLM outputs are evaluated via Pearson correlation (numerical) and accuracy (classification). The framework also produces model confidence ratings and human-readable rationale explanations (xAI). Baselines include lexicon-based (VADER) and transformer-based (DeBERTa) methods.

Result: LLMs achieved Pearson correlations up to 0.97 and classification accuracy up to 94% across both datasets, closely matching expert labels. Results were statistically significant, robust across multiple data formats, and accompanied by high model confidence. Lexicon-based and DeBERTa baselines failed to produce statistically significant results. GPT-4o-mini matched larger models' performance at 94% lower cost.

Conclusion: Combining the PDT survey method with a cost-efficient LLM (GPT-4o-mini) yields rich, accurate sentiment scores and high-level user impressions suitable for product evaluation. The framework's confidence ratings and rationale explanations enhance interpretability and trust. This approach provides actionable insights for product development, improvement, and marketing strategy.

Abstract: Qualitative product feedback can reveal nuanced user experiences, but its implicit sentiment is difficult to measure. This paper presents a scalable and interpretable framework that uses large language models (LLMs) to quantify product desirability from such data. Using two Product Desirability Toolkit (PDT) datasets from ZORQ and CARMA comprising 106 respondent term groupings with gold-standard human annotation, zero-shot continuous numerical sentiment scoring and categorical sentiment classification are evaluated without relying on explicit review scores. Across the datasets, LLMs generated numerical sentiment scores directly from qualitative responses and closely matched expert labels, achieving Pearson correlations up to 0.97 and classification accuracy up to 94%. LLMs maintained robustness even when handling data presented in multiple forms and consistently expressed high confidence. In contrast, lexicon-based and transformer baselines did not produce statistically significant results. Among the models tested, GPT-4o-mini achieved performance comparable to larger models at 94% lower cost, supporting scalable deployment.
  The framework also incorporates model confidence ratings and human-readable rationale explanations (xAI), improving interpretability, transparency, and trust while supporting practical use in product satisfaction assessment. In general, using the PDT tool as a survey method along with a cost efficient LLM for sentiment analysis has the potential to provide for product evaluation with results that are rich in terms of sentiment scores (both numerical and classified sentiment) and in terms of the high-level user impressions of the product that can be used to identify ideas for product development and improvement, as well as marketing ideas for target audiences.

</details>


### [2] [Ground Then Rank: Revisiting Knowledge-Based VQA with Training-Free Entity Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23881)
*Qian Ma,Qiong Wu,Zhengyi Zhou,Yao Ma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies that KB-VQA bottlenecks lie in entity identification and evidence grounding. It proposes IBA (Identify-Before-Answer), a training-free framework that decouples entity selection (via MLLM from a small candidate set) from evidence re-ranking (via an off-the-shelf textual re-ranker), outperforming fine-tuned MM-RAG baselines on Encyclopedic-VQA and InfoSeek while reducing complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MM-RAG methods tightly couple entity discrimination and evidence ranking into a single re-ranking stage, which is computationally expensive and lacks generalization. The authors observe that while MLLMs struggle with open-ended entity naming, they excel at selecting the correct entity from a constrained candidate set. This insight motivates decoupling entity identification from evidence grounding.

Method: The IBA (Identify-Before-Answer) framework operates in two decoupled stages: (1) Entity Identification — an MLLM is prompted to select high-confidence entities using only candidate names (without full passages), leveraging its comparative reasoning over a small set; (2) Evidence Selection — an off-the-shelf textual re-ranker selects the most informative evidence passages given the identified entity. This training-free pipeline replaces the conventional single-stage multi-modal re-ranking approach.

Result: IBA consistently outperforms fine-tuned multi-modal re-ranking baselines on Encyclopedic-VQA and InfoSeek datasets. Ablation analyses show gains stem from both improved entity identification accuracy and the selection of more informative evidence once the correct entity is fixed. The method also reduces training and inference costs compared to coupled MM-RAG approaches.

Conclusion: Decoupling entity identification from evidence re-ranking is both more effective and more efficient than tightly coupled MM-RAG pipelines. The IBA framework demonstrates that a simple, training-free approach leveraging MLLMs' comparative selection ability over candidate entities can surpass fine-tuned baselines, with reproducibility ensured via public implementation.

Abstract: Knowledge-Based Visual Question Answering (KB-VQA) requires grounding visual queries to external knowledge beyond directly observable content in images. While recent multi modal large language models (MLLMs) show strong perceptual abilities, they struggle on KB-VQA tasks requiring groundings from both fine-grained entity and evidence levels. Most existing multi-modal retrieval augmented generation (MM-RAG) methods tightly couple entity discrimination and section-level evidence ranking into a single re-ranking stage, leading to high cost and limited generalization. In this work, we revisit existing MM-RAG solutions from a workflow perspective and argue both entity-level and fact-level groundings are key bottlenecks. We observe that although MLLMs often fail under open-ended entity naming, they can better identify the correct entity when selecting from a small set of candidate names. Based on this insight, we propose a simple and training-free identify-before-answer IBA framework that decouples entity identification from section-level re-ranking. Our approach prompts an MLLM to select high-confidence entities using only candidate names, followed by an off-the-shelf textual re-ranker for evidence selection. Experiments on Encyclopedic-VQA and InfoSeek show that our method consistently outperforms fine-tuned multi-modal re-ranking baselines while reducing training and inference complexity. Additional analyses reveal that the improvements arise not only from better entity identification, but also from selecting more informative evidence once correct entity is fixed. Our implementation is made public to ease reproducibility.

</details>


### [3] [One Year Later...The Harms Persist, But So Do We!](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23884)
*Annika Marie Schoene,Cansu Canca,Gautham Vijay Kumar,Anson Antony*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Six proprietary LLMs were stress-tested across 16 DSM-5 conditions using adversarial attacks, revealing that safety safeguards only hold reliably for suicide/self-harm, while conditions like eating disorders and depression show up to 100% failure rates. The paper introduces an eight-dimension harm taxonomy and calls for condition-specific safeguards before deployment in vulnerable settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used in mental health contexts, but current safety mechanisms are applied inconsistently across different clinical conditions. The authors seek to systematically quantify these gaps and establish a standardized framework for evaluating and addressing harms.

Method: Evaluation of six proprietary LLMs across 16 DSM-5 mental health conditions using four adversarial attack variants (designed to bypass safety filters). The authors developed an eight-dimension harm taxonomy (covering categories such as exacerbation of symptoms, normalization of harmful behaviors, inappropriate clinical advice, etc.) and a multi-dimensional evaluation framework for systematic assessment.

Result: Safety safeguards performed reliably only for suicide and self-harm content. By contrast, conditions including eating disorders, substance use disorder, and major depressive disorder exhibited failure rates approaching or reaching 100% under adversarial prompting, indicating severe and systematic gaps in current safety alignment.

Conclusion: Current LLM safety mechanisms are condition-blind rather than condition-aware. The authors argue that ethical deployment requires clearly defined, condition-specific harm taxonomies and corresponding safeguards. Until these are implemented, integrating LLMs into mental health or educational settings poses significant risk to vulnerable populations.

Abstract: General-purpose large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for mental health-related conversations, yet safety safeguards remain inadequate and inconsistent across clinical conditions. This study evaluates six proprietary LLMs across 16 DSM-5 conditions using four adversarial attack variants, introducing an eight-dimension harm taxonomy and a multi-dimensional evaluation framework. Results show that safeguards hold reliably only for suicide and self-harm, while conditions such as eating disorders, substance use disorder, and major depressive disorder exhibit failure rates of up to 100%. We argue that ethical design and deployment of these LLMs demand clearly defined harm categories across clinical conditions and implementation of safeguards accordingly. Until such safeguards are in place, these models pose significant risks to vulnerable populations, making their growing integration into educational settings a particularly concerning.

</details>


### [4] [Do LLM Attribution Metrics Transfer? Auditing Retrieval-Augmented Generation Evaluation Across Datasets and Constructs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23915)
*Tianyu Ding,Aditya Nannapaneni,Juan Pablo De la Cruz Weinstein*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Automatic metrics for LLM retrieval-augmented generation attribution are not interchangeable across evaluation constructs. An audit of eight scorers reveals per-dataset ranking inversions, with the best metric on one dataset collapsing to chance on another. A naive "best-on-average" rule fails leave-one-dataset-out validation, demonstrating that metric choice must be validated on the target dataset. LLM judges avoid chance-level collapses but are costly, non-deterministic, and merely relocate the validation burden.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior practice treats automatic attribution metrics as interchangeable, but no systematic audit has examined whether any scorer transfers reliably across different evaluation constructs (provenance/topicality, generated-answer attribution, fact-check entailment) and their constituent datasets. This work asks whether any automatic scorer stays within the 95% CI of the best audited scorer on every dataset of a multi-dataset construct.

Method: The authors audit eight automatic scorers — lexical, embedding, and BERTScore baselines plus entailment/grounding-trained models (clean NLI, FEVER NLI, MiniCheck) — across three evaluation constructs using multiple datasets. Transferability is assessed by checking whether any scorer stays within the 95% confidence interval of the best audited scorer on every dataset. Analyses include Kendall tau rank correlation across datasets, leave-one-dataset-out regret analysis, and investigation of length/truncation artifacts. A prompt-based LLM judge is also benchmarked for comparison.

Result: No scorer transfers universally. In generated-answer attribution (AttributionBench, n=1,610; HAGRID, n=2,150), per-dataset rankings invert (Kendall τ = -0.64, p = 0.031, AttributedQA vs. LFQA). An NLI scorer achieving AUROC 0.90 on short-claim AttributedQA collapses to 0.53 (chance) on long-form LFQA, where BERTScore wins at 0.91; this is not a length or truncation artifact. The leave-one-dataset-out mean held-out regret is 0.172 AUROC, worse than fixing one scorer. The LLM judge avoids chance-level collapse but is ~100× costlier and non-deterministic.

Conclusion: Metric choice for attribution evaluation must be validated on the target dataset rather than learned from others. No single automatic scorer transfers reliably across all datasets within a construct. LLM-based judges mitigate certain failure modes but introduce new costs and merely relocate — rather than remove — the validation burden.

Abstract: Practice often treats automatic metrics for attribution in LLM retrieval-augmented generation as interchangeable. We audit eight automatic scorers -- lexical, embedding, and BERTScore baselines alongside entailment/grounding-trained models (clean and FEVER NLI, the checker MiniCheck) -- across three evaluation constructs (provenance/topicality, generated-answer attribution, and fact-check entailment), asking whether any scorer transfers: stays within the 95% confidence interval of the best audited scorer on every dataset of a multi-dataset construct. In the construct with the most multi-dataset human-labeled coverage -- generated-answer attribution (AttributionBench's four source datasets, n = 1,610, with independent HAGRID, n = 2,150) -- none does: the per-dataset metric rankings invert (Kendall tau = -0.64, p = 0.031 on AttributedQA vs. LFQA), and an off-the-shelf NLI scorer that is best on short-claim AttributedQA (AUROC 0.90) collapses to AUROC 0.53 (chance) on long-form LFQA, where BERTScore wins (0.91); the flip is not a length or truncation artifact. This instability has a concrete decision cost: a naive "best-on-average" rule for choosing an evaluator fails leave-one-dataset-out (mean held-out regret 0.172 AUROC, worse than fixing one scorer), so metric choice must be validated on the target dataset rather than learned from others. A prompt-based LLM judge avoids the chance-level collapses the automatic scorers suffer (no LFQA collapse) but is not uniformly best, ~100x costlier, and non-deterministic -- relocating, not removing, the validation burden.

</details>


### [5] [When Retrieval Metrics Mislead: Measuring Policy Signal in Long-Horizon Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23937)
*Tianyu Ding,Juan Pablo De la Cruz Weinstein*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper challenges the common practice of using exact-match retrieval recall as a proxy for downstream policy utility. Testing on tau-bench with Qwen classifiers, the authors find that retrieved policy clauses yield classification performance close to gold clauses despite low exact-match recall (7% rank-1), suggesting that recall underestimates true utility and advocating for evaluation directly with retrieved policies in the loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exact-match retrieval recall is widely used as a heuristic for whether a retriever provides useful policy context to downstream models, but this proxy has not been rigorously tested. The paper aims to determine whether exact-match recall genuinely reflects downstream policy classification utility in a realistic benchmark setting.

Method: The authors use tau-bench for pre-action policy classification with Qwen2.5-3B and 7B classifiers. They first establish a gold-policy baseline using compact structured state representations, then replace the benchmark-designated policy clause with the top-ranked clause from a retriever operating on decision-time context. They compare macro-F1 across three conditions: gold clauses, retrieved clauses, and control conditions (mismatched-policy, no-policy). Two different retrievers are tested, and results are reported with task-cluster 95% confidence intervals.

Result: Under gold-policy conditioning, compact structured state improves macro-F1 by 0.13–0.17 over raw trajectories. Exact-match rank-1 recall is only 7% for airline states. Yet the 3B classifier achieves macro-F1 0.58 with retrieved clauses vs. 0.60 with gold clauses (Δ = -0.02, 95% CI [-0.23, +0.21]), while mismatched-policy and no-policy controls score 0.32 and 0.21. No significant macro-F1 difference is detected between retrieved and gold clauses, though the CI is too wide to establish non-inferiority. The pattern holds with a second retriever and at 7B scale, with variation across fine-tuning configurations.

Conclusion: Exact-match clause recall can substantially underestimate downstream policy utility in this benchmark. The authors recommend evaluating retrieval quality by feeding retrieved policies directly into the classification loop rather than relying on recall alone.

Abstract: Exact-match retrieval recall is often used as a proxy for whether a retriever supplies useful policy context to a downstream decision model. We test this proxy for pre-action policy classification in tau-bench using Qwen2.5-3B/7B classifiers. Under gold-policy conditioning, a compact structured state improves macro-F1 over raw trajectories by 0.13-0.17 after tuning. We then replace the benchmark-designated policy clause with the top-ranked clause retrieved from decision-time context. Although the exact governing clause is retrieved at rank 1 for only 7% of airline states, the primary 3B classifier obtains macro-F1 0.58 with retrieved clauses versus 0.60 with gold clauses (Delta=-0.02, task-cluster 95% CI [-0.23,+0.21]); mismatched-policy and no-policy controls score 0.32 and 0.21. We do not detect a macro-F1 difference between retrieved and gold clauses in this configuration, although the interval remains too wide to establish non-inferiority. The same qualitative pattern appears with a second retriever and at 7B, while varying across fine-tuning configurations. These results indicate that exact-match clause recall can underestimate downstream policy utility in this benchmark setting, motivating evaluation with retrieved policies in the classification loop rather than recall alone.

</details>


### [6] [QuechuaTok: Morphological Boundary Accuracy as a Necessary Metric for Tokenizer Evaluation in Agglutinative Low-Resource Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23943)
*Maria Contreras*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: QuechuaTok presents a benchmark comparing four tokenization strategies (BPE, Unigram LM, WordPiece, and morphology-aware PRPE) for Southern Quechua, demonstrating that standard metrics like fertility rate are insufficient for agglutinative languages. PRPE achieves 83.33% morphological boundary accuracy versus BPE's 6.67%, despite BPE having the lowest fertility rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard tokenization evaluation metrics such as fertility rate fail to capture morphological correctness for agglutinative languages like Southern Quechua, where words encode rich morphological information through extensive suffixation. There is a need for a systematic evaluation framework that incorporates morphology-aware metrics.

Method: The authors benchmark four tokenizers — BPE, Unigram LM, WordPiece, and a morphology-aware PRPE tokenizer — on a 200k-sentence Southern Quechua corpus. They evaluate using three metrics: fertility rate, out-of-vocabulary (OOV) rate, and a novel morphological boundary accuracy (MorphAcc) metric, using the SQUOIA finite-state morphological analyzer as a silver-standard reference.

Result: BPE achieves the lowest fertility rate (1.636 at 16k vocabulary) but only 6.67% MorphAcc, indicating it memorizes surface forms without capturing morphological structure. PRPE achieves the highest MorphAcc at 83.33%, revealing that fertility rate alone is misleading for evaluating tokenizers on agglutinative languages.

Conclusion: Morphological boundary accuracy is a necessary complement to fertility rate when evaluating tokenizers for agglutinative languages. Morphology-aware tokenization (PRPE) significantly outperforms statistical subword methods on morphological segmentation quality. All code and models are publicly released.

Abstract: Tokenization is a foundational step in NLP pipelines, yet standard evaluation metrics such as fertility rate fail to capture morphological correctness for agglutinative languages. We present QuechuaTok, a systematic benchmark comparing four tokenization strategies - BPE, Unigram LM, WordPiece, and a morphology-aware PRPE tokenizer - for Southern Quechua (quz), a low-resource agglutinative language spoken by 8-10 million people in South America. Using a 200k-sentence corpus and the SQUOIA finite-state morphological analyzer (Rios, 2016) as silver standard, we evaluate three metrics: fertility rate, OOV rate, and morphological boundary accuracy (MorphAcc). Our results show that BPE achieves the lowest fertility rate (1.636 at 16k vocab) by memorizing surface word forms, while achieving only 6.67% MorphAcc. PRPE achieves 83.33% MorphAcc - the highest of all systems - demonstrating that fertility rate alone is insufficient to evaluate tokenizers for agglutinative languages. All code and models are publicly available at kaggle.com/code/macmaky/quechuatok

</details>


### [7] [Layer-wise Probing of wav2vec 2.0 and Whisper for Consonant Cluster Reduction in African American English](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23948)
*Hamid Mojarad,Kevin Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper probes wav2vec2 and Whisper speech models to reveal that African American English consonant cluster reduction is encoded as structured gradient phonological variation, not simple deletion, with both models distinguishing reduced from canonical forms and retaining underlying stop cues in reduced segments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Consonant cluster reduction (CCR) in African American English is a widespread phonological process and a known source of ASR performance disparity, yet how modern speech models internally represent this phenomenon remains underexplored. The authors aim to uncover whether self-supervised and supervised speech models encode CCR as simple segmental deletion or as structured, gradient phonological variation.

Method: Speaker-independent layer-wise probing of two models — wav2vec2-base (self-supervised) and Whisper-small (supervised) — using two diagnostic tasks: (1) segmental reduction detection (classifying whether a segment is reduced or canonical) and (2) segmental restoration of underlying cluster identity (recovering the underlying stop consonant from reduced forms).

Result: Both models distinguish reduced and canonical forms with high accuracy across layers. Critically, reduced segments retain cues to their underlying stops, demonstrating that CCR is not encoded as simple deletion but as structured gradient phonological variation where information about the deleted consonant persists in the representation.

Conclusion: Modern speech models encode AAE consonant cluster reduction in a linguistically structured manner, capturing gradient phonological patterns rather than treating reduction as binary segmental deletion. This has implications for understanding both the linguistic knowledge acquired by speech models and potential pathways to mitigate ASR disparities.

Abstract: Self-supervised and supervised speech models are increasingly used to investigate which linguistic information their internal representations encode, and at what level of abstraction they encode it. One underexplored phenomenon is consonant cluster reduction (CCR) in African American English (AAE), a widespread phonological process and a source of automatic speech recognition (ASR) disparity. To examine how CCR is represented, we conduct speaker-independent layer-wise probing of wav2vec2-base and Whisper-small using two tasks: segmental reduction detection and segmental restoration of underlying cluster identity. Both models distinguish reduced and canonical forms with high accuracy. Crucially, reduced segments retain cues to their underlying stops, indicating that CCR is encoded as structured gradient phonological variation rather than simple segmental deletion. These results demonstrate structured phonological encoding of AAE CCR patterns in modern speech models.

</details>


### [8] [EXPO-SQL: Execution-based Clause-level Policy Optimization for Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23693)
*Jaehoon Lee,CheolWon Na,Suyoung Bae,Jin-Seop Lee,Jihyung Lee,YunSeok Choi,Jee-Hyong Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EXPO-SQL introduces clause-level reward signals for RL-based Text-to-SQL training, replacing coarse query-level rewards with fine-grained feedback derived from execution error analysis and incremental clause execution, achieving state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for Text-to-SQL assign uniform query-level rewards that treat all clauses equally, failing to distinguish correct from incorrect clauses. This coarse-grained design provides insufficient learning signals, hindering the model's ability to identify and correct specific erroneous parts of a generated SQL query.

Method: EXPO-SQL proposes clause-level policy optimization. It identifies erroneous clauses by analyzing execution results through two mechanisms: (1) parsing execution error messages to locate faulty clauses, and (2) performing clause-wise incremental execution to isolate which specific clause causes a mismatch. These fine-grained per-clause rewards then guide RL training, providing targeted supervision signals for each SQL component.

Result: Experiments on widely-used Text-to-SQL benchmarks show EXPO-SQL significantly outperforms existing supervised fine-tuning, prompting, and RL-based methods, validating the effectiveness of clause-level learning.

Conclusion: Fine-grained clause-level rewards, enabled by execution-based error analysis, provide substantially better training signals for RL in Text-to-SQL compared to coarse query-level rewards, leading to significant performance gains.

Abstract: Text-to-SQL enables users to query databases using natural language by generating executable SQL queries. Recent methods have increasingly adopted Large Language Models based reinforcement learning (RL) to leverage execution feedback for training. However, existing RL methods assign uniform query-level rewards to all clauses in a SQL query, treating correct and incorrect clauses equally. This coarse-grained reward design leads to insufficient learning signals for correct SQL generation. To address this issue, we propose EXPO-SQL (EXecution-based clause-level Policy Optimization for Text-to-SQL) which provides fine-grained supervision through clause-level rewards. To assign clause-level rewards, our method identifies erroneous clauses by analyzing execution results, including error messages and clause-wise incremental execution. Experiments on widely-used Text-to-SQL benchmarks demonstrate that EXPO-SQL significantly outperforms existing supervised fine-tuning, prompting, and RL-based methods through fine-grained clause-level learning. Our code is available at https://github. com/jhn25/EXPO-SQL.

</details>


### [9] [Does My Embedding Reflect That $A = B$? Evaluating Mathematical Equivalence in Embedding Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23959)
*Jiaying Ye,Samarth Rao,Leo Carlin,Kedar Chintalapati,Saharsh Bhargava,Rachit Jaiswal,Michael Zhou,Jared Darlington,Jarod Alper,Vasily Ilin,Henry Kvinge*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Current embedding models fail to capture mathematical equivalence across differently-worded statements; the authors introduce the MELD dataset to benchmark this and propose a contrastive learning method aligning informal and formal mathematical text, improving retrieval on both cross-formal and natural language tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mathematical knowledge is often siloed by subfield-specific terminology, causing semantically equivalent statements to appear lexically distinct. Breakthroughs frequently occur when researchers bridge these gaps. With growing formalization resources (e.g., Lean), there is a pressing need for tools that can reliably navigate between different mathematical "languages." Existing embedding models, however, are suspected of relying on surface-level lexical cues rather than capturing deep mathematical equivalence.

Method: The authors first construct the MELD (Mathematically Equivalent but Lexically Different Pairs) dataset, consisting of pairs of natural language mathematical statements that are mathematically equivalent but expressed using different terminology. They benchmark state-of-the-art embedding models on MELD. Motivated by poor performance, they propose a contrastive learning framework that trains embeddings to pull together informal (natural language) statements with their corresponding formalizations (e.g., Lean code), thereby encouraging representations grounded in mathematical semantics rather than lexical similarity.

Result: State-of-the-art embedding models predominantly group mathematical statements by shared terminology rather than by underlying mathematical content. The proposed contrastive approach, which aligns informal statements with formal counterparts, yields improved performance on both informal-formal retrieval tasks and on the purely natural-language MELD benchmark.

Conclusion: Current embeddings are inadequate for capturing mathematical equivalence. Training with a contrastive objective that bridges informal and formal mathematical representations is a promising direction for building models that understand mathematics at a semantic, cross-lingual level within the discipline.

Abstract: Because mathematics is highly abstract, a single statement can take very different forms depending on what subfield it is framed in. There are many examples where breakthroughs occurred after researchers discovered that a question had already been answered in a different field. At the same time, the growth of new resources related to formalization has increased the need for tools that enable efficient and reliable navigation between mathematical 'languages' (e.g., from Lean to natural language). In this paper, we investigate whether current embedding models capture mathematical equivalence. To do this, we introduce the Mathematically Equivalent but Lexically Different Pairs (MELD) Dataset, a collection of mathematically equivalent statements that are expressed in very different language. We show that current state-of-the-art embedding models tend to group statements by the terminology used to make them instead of the underlying math. Motivated by this, we propose a contrastive approach to learning embeddings of mathematical text that focuses on aligning informal statements with different formalizations. Our experiments demonstrate that this leads to improvements not only on informal-formal retrieval tasks but also on MELD, which only contains natural language statements.

</details>


### [10] [Faithful by Construction: Claim-Anchored Attribution for Multi-Document Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23989)
*Shuo Guan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CAMS introduces a modular Extract-Select-Rewrite pipeline for multi-document summarization where atomic claims with token-level provenance serve as the attribution unit. By anchoring every summary sentence to a support-checked claim linked to source spans, it achieves substantially better faithfulness and citation precision than end-to-end LLMs while maintaining comparable summary quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end LLMs produce fluent multi-document summaries but suffer from hallucination and offer only coarse-grained (document/passage-level), post-hoc attributions, making individual statements difficult to verify. The paper aims to build a summarization system that is attribution-oriented and faithfulness-oriented by construction, preserving fine-grained, multi-source traceability.

Method: The CAMS framework revives the Extract-Select-Rewrite paradigm with four stages: (1) extract atomic claims with token-level provenance from every source document; (2) cluster equivalent claims across documents while flagging inter-source conflicts; (3) select a support-aware and salient subset of claims; (4) rewrite the selection into a summary where every sentence is anchored to a support-checked claim linking back to source spans. The pipeline also incorporates constrained rewriting and verification to encourage factual faithfulness.

Result: On MultiNews, CAMS matches strong end-to-end and span-attribution baselines on summary quality while substantially improving faithfulness and citation precision, lifting multi-source attribution accuracy by roughly two-thirds. It also exposes a controllable faithfulness-coverage trade-off that end-to-end models leave implicit. Zero-shot transfer is tested on WCEP, and conflict handling is analyzed on DiverseSumm.

Conclusion: Modular, claim-anchored pipelines with token-level provenance offer a viable alternative to end-to-end LLMs for multi-document summarization, delivering structurally guaranteed traceability, significantly better attribution accuracy, and an explicit faithfulness-coverage trade-off without sacrificing summary quality.

Abstract: End-to-end large language models (LLMs) produce fluent multi-document summaries but remain prone to hallucination, and the attributions they offer are typically coarse (whole documents or passages) and generated post hoc, leaving each summary statement hard to verify. We revisit the modular Extract--Select--Rewrite paradigm and recast its intermediate representation as the unit of attribution. We present CAMS, a Claim-Anchored Multi-document Summarization framework that (i) extracts atomic claims with token-level provenance from every source document, (ii) clusters equivalent claims across documents while flagging inter-source conflicts, (iii) selects a support-aware and salient subset, and (iv) rewrites the selection into a summary in which every sentence is anchored to a support-checked claim that links back to one or more source spans. Because content is localized before it is realized, the pipeline is attribution-oriented by construction and faithfulness-oriented by construction: it structurally preserves fine-grained, multi-source traceability while using support-aware selection, constrained rewriting, and verification to encourage, rather than guarantee, factual faithfulness. We evaluate quality, faithfulness, and localization on MultiNews, analyze conflict handling on DiverseSumm, and test zero-shot transfer on WCEP, using a two-regime protocol that separates reference-free citation quality from gold-aligned localization accuracy, and we add an evaluator-decoupled audit that tests citation precision with a support model never used for selection or verification. CAMS matches strong end-to-end and span-attribution baselines on summary quality while substantially improving faithfulness and citation precision, lifting multi-source attribution accuracy by roughly two-thirds, and exposing a controllable faithfulness--coverage trade-off that end-to-end models leave implicit.

</details>


### [11] [RASC+: Retrieval-Constrained LLM Adjudication for Clinical Value Set Authoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23992)
*Sumit Mukherjee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper presents a two-stage approach for clinical value set completion: Stage 1 optimizes candidate-pool recall via vocabulary-aware retrieval, and Stage 2 employs a constrained LLM adjudicator (GPT-5) for code selection. The method increases pool recall from 0.553 to 0.730 and boosts macro F1 to 0.549 (full test) and 0.533 (held-out publisher), significantly outperforming prior baselines while maintaining auditability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical value set completion is critical for quality measurement, phenotyping, and clinical decision support. Prior work (RASC benchmark) showed that direct zero-shot LLM generation performs poorly because clinical code systems are large, version-controlled, and not reliably memorized. The authors aim to improve performance while preserving the safety constraint that all returned codes must come from an auditable candidate pool.

Method: A two-stage pipeline: (1) Candidate-pool construction optimized for recall using Qwen3-based retrieval with vocabulary-aware expansion and code-display rescue retrieval, boosting recall to 0.730. (2) Constrained LLM adjudication where a blinded GPT-5 selector chooses codes from the candidate pool, replacing the original SAPBert cross-encoder. This ensures all returned codes are traceable to an auditable pool.

Result: On the full 3,744-value-set RASC test split: candidate-pool recall improved from 0.553 (baseline) to 0.730; held-out-publisher recall reached 0.655. With SAPBert on the expanded pool, macro F1 was only 0.287 (full) and 0.233 (held-out). GPT-5 adjudication on the same pool achieved macro F1 of 0.549 (full) and 0.533 (held-out), a substantial improvement.

Conclusion: Retrieval-constrained LLM adjudication substantially improves value set completion performance while maintaining the safety constraint that all returned codes originate from an auditable candidate pool. The stage-wise approach decouples recall optimization from precision-oriented selection, with LLMs excelling at the latter.

Abstract: Clinical value sets define the standardized terminology codes used in quality measurement, phenotyping, cohort construction, and clinical decision support. The recently introduced Retrieval-Augmented Set Completion (RASC) benchmark showed that direct zero-shot large language model (LLM) generation is poorly suited to this task: clinical code systems are large, version-controlled, and not reliably memorized by language models. We study a stage-wise alternative in which candidate-pool construction is optimized for recall and a constrained LLM adjudicator is optimized for candidate selection. On the full 3,744-value-set RASC test split, Qwen3-based retrieval with vocabulary-aware expansion and code-display rescue retrieval increases candidate-pool recall from the original RASC retrieval baseline of 0.553 to 0.730; on the held-out-publisher stratum, pool recall is 0.655. The higher-recall pool alone is not sufficient: applying the original SAPBert cross-encoder to this expanded pool gives full-test macro F1 of 0.287 and held-out-publisher macro F1 of 0.233. Replacing the stage-2 selector with blinded GPT-5 adjudication over the same pool increases full-test macro F1 to 0.549 and held-out-publisher macro F1 to 0.533. These results show that retrieval-constrained LLM adjudication can substantially improve value set completion while preserving the safety constraint that all returned codes must come from an auditable candidate pool.

</details>


### [12] [Towards Spec Learning: Inference-Time Alignment from Preference Pairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24004)
*Dhriti Krishnan,Tejas Goyal,Jaromir Savelka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Spec learning compiles brief user instructions and a small set of preference judgments into natural-language specifications (prompts) that steer LLM behavior at inference time, outperforming DPO on dense-preference domain datasets without any model parameter updates, while producing human-readable, interpretable artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Steering LLMs toward desired behaviors currently involves either brittle, manual prompt engineering through iterative trial-and-error, or prohibitively expensive preference-based fine-tuning (e.g., DPO/RLHF). There is a need for a method that is both efficient (requiring minimal data and no training) and interpretable.

Method: Spec learning takes a brief user instruction plus a small set of preference judgments (comparisons between response pairs). These are algorithmically compiled into natural-language specifications — essentially structured prompts that encode the preference signal. These specifications are then applied at inference time to condition the LLM's behavior, requiring no gradient updates or parameter changes to the underlying model.

Result: Responses generated using compiled specifications often outperform those from Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) on datasets from specialized domains where the preference signal is dense. The resulting specifications are human-readable and serve as transparent, interpretable embodiments of the learned preference signal.

Conclusion: Spec learning offers a lightweight, interpretable, and effective alternative to both manual prompt engineering and preference-based fine-tuning. It demonstrates that explicit, human-readable specifications derived from minimal preference data can rival or surpass weight-based alignment methods like DPO in domains with dense preference signals.

Abstract: Steering a large language model (LLM) toward a desired behavior typically relies on an iterative process of hand-crafting a prompt based on a careful inspection of the model's responses. This is an involved, brittle, and error-prone process. Preference-based fine-tuning is a more rigorous but often prohibitively expensive solution. We propose spec learning, a framework that relies on a brief user instruction and a small set of preference judgments. These are compiled into specifications in the form of natural-language prompts for an LLM. Specifications condition LLMs at inference time, and no parameter updates to the underlying models are required. We show that the responses generated based on the compiled specifications often outperform direct preference optimization (DPO) on datasets from specialized domains whose preference signal is dense. Unlike opaque weight updates, the resulting specifications are human-readable and double as interpretable and transparent written embodiments of the preference signal that produced them.

</details>


### [13] [CAVEWOMAN: How Large Language Models Behave Under Linguistic Input and Output Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24083)
*Morayo Danielle Adeyemi,Ryan A. Rossi,Franck Dernoncourt*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Cavewoman, a two-channel evaluation protocol showing that compressing model outputs reduces inference cost (1.4-3x), while compressing inputs backfires by increasing net cost (~1.15-2.7x) due to longer responses and accuracy collapse. Additionally, even correct compressed generations often semantically diverge from unconstrained baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The "caveman style" of dropping grammar and shortening text is widely promoted to reduce LLM inference costs, but no prior work has systematically disentangled whether compression applied to the user prompt vs. the model response yields different cost and quality outcomes.

Method: Cavewoman is a two-channel evaluation protocol that independently compresses the input prompt and the output response across five reduction levels. It scores each generation along three axes: task accuracy, realized per-item cost (accounting for both input and output token counts), and reference-text agreement against the model's own unconstrained generation. Eight models are evaluated on five datasets, with both channels measured on identical items. Additional analyses include length-controlled re-scoring, multiple-comparisons correction, and complementary semantic similarity measures.

Result: Output compression yields consistent cost savings: 1.4-2.4x on most API models and benefits on all four open-weight models under public-tier pricing. Input compression is a strict lose-lose: it raises net cost ~1.15x on average (up to 1.8x on the worst dataset and 2.7x under stronger compression) because models produce longer responses even as accuracy drops. Furthermore, roughly half of all correct generations from non-reasoning models exhibit surface text that no longer entails the model's own unconstrained baseline, a semantic divergence that persists through multiple robustness checks.

Conclusion: Compression recommendations must be channel-specific: output compression reliably reduces cost, while input compression is counterproductive. The observed semantic divergence under input compression suggests that even "correct" short-prompt generations may be substantively different from what the model would otherwise produce, raising concerns beyond simple accuracy metrics.

Abstract: "Talk short. Drop grammar. Save token." This caveman style is widely promoted as a way to cut inference cost, but whether it actually saves anything depends on which channel (the user's prompt or the model's response) is being compressed. We present Cavewoman, a two-channel evaluation protocol that scores every generation on task accuracy, realized per-item cost, and reference-text agreement against the model's unconstrained reference. We evaluate eight models on five datasets at five reduction levels, with both channels measured on the same items. Output compression cuts realized cost on most API models (1.4-2.4x per model, up to 3x in the best case) and on all four open-weight models under public-tier pricing. Input compression has the opposite effect, a strict lose-lose: it raises net cost rather than lowering it (~1.15x on the five-benchmark mean, up to 1.8x on the worst dataset and 2.7x under stronger compression), because models compensate with longer responses even as accuracy collapses. Under the same setting, surface text diverges from the unconstrained reference: on the non-reasoning models, roughly half of all generations are correct yet their surface text no longer entails the model's own unconstrained baseline generation. The divergence survives length-controlled re-scoring, multiple-comparisons correction, and replication under complementary semantic measures. Code and data are available at https://github.com/danielle34/cavewoman.

</details>


### [14] [Predicting Poets' Origins from Verse: A Computational Analysis of Regional Linguistic Fingerprints in the Complete Tang Poems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24093)
*Chi-Sheng Chen,Hung-Yun Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Using character n-gram TF-IDF and neural models on a corpus of 357 Tang poets linked to geographic origins, the study predicts regional origin above chance, finds linguistic distance-decay with geographic distance, temporal variation in regional signal strength, and historically meaningful misclassifications, positioning interpretable ML as a hypothesis generator for literary history.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the geographic origin of Tang-dynasty poets leaves a detectable and interpretable linguistic trace in their poetic works, and to demonstrate how machine learning can serve as a hypothesis-generating tool for literary-historical inquiry.

Method: Aggregated all poems from the Complete Tang Poems (Quan Tang Shi) for 357 poets, linked poets to their administrative circuit of origin via CBDB, framed origin prediction as multi-class classification. Used character n-gram TF-IDF with interpretable domain features (imagery, season, allusion), plus classical and neural models including a hierarchical frozen-encoder representation with GuwenBERT. Applied Mantel test for distance-decay, and analyzed temporal variation across Tang periods (Early, High, Late).

Result: South vs. North origin predicted at 0.69 accuracy (baseline 0.53); finer circuit-level prediction above chance. Mantel test showed linguistic distance correlates with geographic distance (r=0.40, p≈0.09). South/North separability near chance in High Tang, strongest in Late Tang. In Early Tang, all confident misclassifications were southern poets mislabeled as northern. GuwenBERT only matched TF-IDF performance; combining them added no benefit.

Conclusion: Regional origin leaves a measurable linguistic signal in Tang poetry, modulated by historical period and consistent with known historical dynamics (court homogenization, regional divergence, northern prestige). Character n-grams suffice to capture the signal, and interpretable machine learning can productively generate hypotheses for literary history.

Abstract: We ask whether the geographic origin of Tang-dynasty poets leaves a detectable linguistic trace in their work. Aggregating every poem attributed to each author in the Complete Tang Poems (Quan Tang Shi) and linking poets to their administrative circuit of origin via the China Biographical Database (CBDB), we build a poet-level corpus of 357 poets across the ten Tang circuits and frame origin prediction as multi-class classification. Using character $n$-gram TF-IDF together with interpretable domain features (imagery, season, and allusion), classical and neural models predict a poet's broad region (South vs.\ North) at $0.69$ accuracy, well above the $0.53$ majority baseline, and finer circuit-level origin above chance. Beyond classification, three findings emerge. (i) Linguistic distance between circuits grows with geographic distance (Mantel $r=0.40$, $p\approx0.09$ over nine circuits), evidence of a distance-decay effect in poetic language. (ii) The signal interacts with time: South/North separability is at chance in the High Tang and strongest in the Late Tang, consistent with court-driven homogenization at the empire's height followed by regional divergence. (iii) The model's confident errors are historically meaningful -- in the Early Tang, every misclassification is a southern poet read as northern, reflecting the prestige of the northern court idiom. We further show that, when given the whole corpus through a hierarchical frozen-encoder representation, a classical-Chinese transformer (GuwenBERT) only matches -- not beats -- simple TF-IDF, and that combining them adds nothing, indicating that character $n$-grams already capture the regional signal. Our results position interpretable machine learning as a hypothesis generator for literary history.

</details>


### [15] [PORTER: Language-Grounded Event Representations for Portable Structured EHR Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24102)
*Lin Lawrence Guo,Adam Paul Yan,Emily Vettese,Lillian Sung*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PORTER decouples EHR event representation from fixed vocabularies by using frozen text encoders for event descriptions and a dedicated numeric pathway, enabling vocabulary-independent transfer across institutions without retraining while maintaining in-domain performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EHR foundation models rely on fixed discrete vocabularies for clinical events, making them unable to handle unseen concepts, new concept-attribute combinations, or numeric values. This limits cross-institutional transfer and even transfer across deployment pipelines within the same institution, necessitating costly vocabulary harmonization.

Method: PORTER uses three key components: (1) a frozen text encoder to represent clinical events via their natural language descriptions, decoupling from fixed vocabularies; (2) a dedicated numeric pathway for integrating continuous values (e.g., lab results); and (3) an autoregressively pretrained temporal backbone that learns clinical dynamics over patient timelines. Transfer is evaluated by rendering the same patient timelines with unseen event descriptions and testing on MIMIC.

Result: PORTER matched the mean AUROC of a fixed-vocabulary model across 74 pediatric clinical prediction tasks. Under cross-vocabulary transfer (unseen event descriptions), it recovered 97.1% of the target-vocabulary-trained model's AUROC. On MIMIC transfer, the fixed-vocabulary model dropped 69% of events; PORTER outperformed it. The numeric pathway improved magnitude sensitivity without disrupting concept identity. PORTER also beat a task-specific text serialization comparator at 329× lower amortized compute. Transfer quality tracked preservation of patient-level representation geometry, not text encoder scale.

Conclusion: PORTER demonstrates that language-grounded, vocabulary-independent EHR foundation models are feasible, reducing vocabulary harmonization needs while preserving in-domain performance and enabling efficient cross-task, cross-institution reuse.

Abstract: Most electronic health record (EHR) foundation models encode clinical events as discrete event tokens from a fixed vocabulary and therefore cannot directly represent events containing unseen concepts or new combinations of concepts and attributes such as numeric values. This limits transfer across institutions and even across deployment pipelines within the same institution. We introduce PORTER, a language-grounded structured EHR foundation model that decouples event representation from this fixed vocabulary. PORTER represents events through their descriptions using a frozen text encoder, integrates numeric values through a dedicated pathway, and learns clinical dynamics over patient timelines with an autoregressively pretrained temporal backbone. Across 74 clinical prediction tasks at a pediatric hospital, PORTER matched the mean AUROC of a fixed-vocabulary model with the same temporal backbone and pretraining objective. When the same patient timelines were rendered using event descriptions not seen during pretraining, PORTER transferred without retraining or vocabulary mapping, recovering 97.1% of the mean AUROC of a model trained directly on the target vocabulary. When transferred to MIMIC, PORTER outperformed the fixed-vocabulary model, which dropped 69% of events because their tokens were unseen. Mechanistic analyses showed cross-vocabulary transfer tracked preservation of patient-level representation geometry rather than the scale of the text encoder, and the numeric pathway improved sensitivity to magnitude without disrupting clinical concept identity. PORTER also achieved higher AUROC than a task-specific text serialization comparator, at 329-fold lower amortized compute. PORTER is a step toward vocabulary-independent EHR foundation models that reduce the need for vocabulary harmonization while preserving in-domain performance and enabling efficient cross-task reuse.

</details>


### [16] [Decoherence as Defence and the Magnitude of Noise Regularisation: A Rigorous N -Qubit Theory of Stochastic Quantum Neural Networks for Adversarially Robust Network Intrusion Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24219)
*Gautier-Edouard Edouard Filardo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper resolves two open problems concerning stochastic quantum neural networks (SQNNs) for intrusion detection: proving a decoherence-contraction theorem that quantifies robustness of depolarising noise against adversarial attacks, and deriving an adaptive-penalty formula showing per-gate quantum dropout acts as a curvature-weighted L2 regularizer. Both mechanisms are validated experimentally on NSL-KDD data under FGSM/PGD attacks, and a neutral-atom hardware realization is outlined.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A prior conference study left open whether "true quantum dropout" (per-gate stochastic deactivation) could regularize where a depolarising channel could not, and whether the loose adversarial-resilience bound could be replaced by a predictive, quantitative theory. This paper aims to resolve both questions and extend the framework to real data and neutral-atom hardware.

Method: The authors formulate an N-qubit SQNN via the stochastic master equation and vectorised Liouvillian. They prove a decoherence-contraction theorem showing that depolarising noise of strength γ over L entangling layers contracts Pauli read-out weights by (1−4γ/3)^{wL}. They derive an adaptive-penalty formula: per-gate dropout implements a curvature-weighted L₂ penalty (p(1−p)/2)∑θ²∂²_θL, maximized at p=1/2. Experiments use the NSL-KDD dataset under white-box FGSM and PGD attacks, with multiple seeds (7–30) for statistical validity. A neutral-atom feasibility analysis is also provided.

Result: Under strong ℓ∞/ℓ₂ attacks, the depolarising SQNN is significantly more robust than the noiseless circuit (PGD-20, p=0.04, large effect) and never suffers catastrophic robustness collapse (noiseless models fall from 95% to 47%). Robustness stems from a noise-reshaped training boundary rather than attack-time gradient contraction. The dropout penalty formula is confirmed: both mechanisms reduce the train-test gap by ~0.01 (p<10⁻⁴ and p=0.004), are statistically indistinguishable, and increasing dropout past p=1/2 does not help. The prior single-seed dichotomy does not survive replication.

Conclusion: Depolarising noise provides certified, quantitative adversarial robustness via the decoherence-contraction theorem, while per-gate quantum dropout and depolarising noise are both effective but modest regularizers governed by distinct penalty structures (weight-space vs. output-space). The framework is viable for neutral-atom hardware, and future work should rely on multi-seed statistical validation rather than single-seed claims.

Abstract: Stochastic quantum neural networks (SQNNs) encode neuronal activations as qubits, synaptic topology as entanglement, and neural noise through a Lindblad master equation. A recent conference study applied a ring-entangled SQNN to collaborative intrusion detection and reached three conclusions: ring entanglement is \emph{essential} for non-local anomaly detection; an adversarial-resilience bound holds but is \emph{conservative}; and the depolarising channel \emph{fails} to act as a dropout-style regulariser, behaving instead as output noise. It left open whether a per-gate stochastic deactivation (``true quantum dropout'') could regularise where the depolarising channel could not, and whether the loose robustness bound could be replaced by a predictive theory. This paper resolves both and extends the framework to real data and to neutral-atom hardware. We give an $N$-qubit formulation through the stochastic master equation and its vectorised Liouvillian, and prove a \emph{decoherence-contraction theorem}: a depolarising channel of strength $γ$ over $L$ entangling layers contracts every weight-$w$ Pauli read-out by a factor $(1-4γ/3)^{wL}$ (for the weight-$1$ read-out used here, $(1-4γ/3)^{L}$); building on the general noise-as-defence result of Du et al., we make this quantitative and operational for intrusion detection. On the real NSL-KDD dataset under white-box FGSM and PGD attacks, a depolarising SQNN trained with the channel is, over seven seeds under strong $\ell_\infty$/$\ell_2$ attacks, significantly more robust than the noiseless circuit ($\ell_\infty$ PGD-$20$, $p=0.04$, large effect) and, critically, never suffers the catastrophic robustness collapse that the noiseless model and gradient-trained classical detectors (which fall from $95\%$ to $47\%$) do, cutting robustness variance roughly twofold; we show this robustness arises from a noise-reshaped training boundary rather than from attack-time gradient contraction. For generalisation, we derive an adaptive-penalty formula showing that per-gate dropout implements a curvature-weighted $L_2$ penalty $\tfrac{p(1-p)}{2}\sumθ^2\partial^2_θL$ in weight space, maximised at $p=1/2$, whereas depolarising noise implements an output-space penalty. A $30$-seed study confirms the formula's quantitative prediction: both mechanisms reduce the train-test gap by a small but statistically significant margin ($\approx\!0.01$; $p<10^{-4}$ and $p=0.004$), are statistically indistinguishable from each other, and the effect is concentrated where overfitting is largest; increasing the dropout rate past $1/2$ does not help, as the formula predicts. The single-seed dichotomy of prior work does not survive replication. We close with a neutral-atom realisation and a feasibility-by-$N$ analysis.

</details>


### [17] [Metis: Bridging Text and Code Memory for Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24151)
*Zijie Dai,Siuhin He,Hui Li,Qihui Zhou,Jiajun Li,Mingcong Song,Guoping Long,Hongjie Si,Xin Yao,Lin Zhang,James Cheng,Xiao Yan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares text-based versus code-based memory representations for self-evolving agents, finds they have complementary trade-offs, and proposes Metis — a hierarchical dual-representation memory system that organizes textual experience into plans/facts/pitfalls and selectively crystallizes recurring plans into callable tools, achieving up to 20.6% accuracy improvement and 22.8% cost reduction on the AppWorld benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-evolving agent systems store past experience either as natural-language text or as executable code tools, but the choice is made arbitrarily at design time rather than based on the nature of the experience. The trade-offs between these two representations are poorly understood, and neither representation alone may be optimal.

Method: The authors first conduct a controlled study isolating text memory vs. code memory over an identical set of experiences to analyze complementary trade-offs in construction cost, execution efficiency, and transferability. Based on these findings, they design Metis: a hierarchical dual-representation memory that (1) organizes textual experience into three categories — execution plans, environment facts, and common pitfalls; and (2) selectively crystallizes recurring execution plans into validated, callable code tools only when reuse frequency justifies the generation cost.

Result: On the AppWorld benchmark for interactive agents, Metis improves task accuracy by up to 20.6% over the ReAct baseline while reducing execution cost by up to 22.8%. Compared to representative self-evolving agent systems, Metis consistently achieves a better balance among accuracy, execution efficiency, and memory-construction cost.

Conclusion: Text memory and code memory exhibit complementary strengths: text is broadly applicable with low construction cost, while code offers superior execution efficiency and transferability. A hierarchical dual-representation approach that uses text by default and selectively crystallizes high-frequency patterns into code yields the best overall trade-off for self-evolving agents.

Abstract: Self-evolving agents improve over time by distilling experience from past executions and reusing it in future tasks. Existing systems represent such experience either as natural-language text injected into the agent context or as code exposed as callable tools. However, the choice between these representations is typically made at design time rather than derived from the characteristics of the experience itself, leaving the trade-offs between them poorly understood. We present the first controlled study that isolates text memory and code memory over an identical set of experiences. Our results show that the two forms exhibit complementary trade-offs in construction cost, execution efficiency, and transferability, such that neither representation alone is sufficient. Guided by these findings, we propose Metis, a self-evolving agent system built on a hierarchical dual-representation memory. Metis organizes textual experience into execution plans, environment facts, and common pitfalls, and selectively crystallizes recurring plans into validated callable tools. This design combines the broad applicability of text memory with the execution efficiency of code memory while incurring tool-generation cost only when justified by repeated reuse. We evaluate Metis on AppWorld, a challenging benchmark for interactive agents. The results show that Metis improves task accuracy by up to 20.6% over ReAct while reducing execution cost by up to 22.8%. Compared with representative self-evolving agent systems, Metis consistently achieves a better balance between accuracy, execution efficiency, and memory-construction cost.

</details>


### [18] [Measuring User's Mental Models of Speech Translation in Human-AI Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24644)
*HyoJung Han,Nishant Balepur,Jordan Boyd-Graber,Marine Carpuat*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates how users develop mental models of machine translation (MT) systems through a cross-lingual question answering framework, finding that users improve their ability to predict MT errors with practice, especially when they have source language knowledge or access to speech transcriptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Millions use MT daily, but little is known about how users perceive system capabilities and limitations — specifically, how well they can predict when MT output is likely to be wrong, which is critical for effective human-AI collaboration.

Method: The authors propose a cross-lingual question answering framework where participants answer questions based on foreign-language content, choosing either to accept MT output or request professional re-translation. User behavior and accuracy are analyzed across varying translation quality conditions, with and without speech transcription access, to assess mental model formation and evolution over time.

Result: Users develop stronger mental models with practice, particularly when they possess some source language knowledge, primarily by detecting surface-level error cues. Providing speech transcriptions further improves mental model development.

Conclusion: Cross-lingual question answering is a promising downstream task for studying MT mental models and advancing understanding of human-AI collaboration, with practical implications for designing MT interfaces that help users better calibrate trust in system outputs.

Abstract: Millions of people use machine translation (MT) tools daily, yet little is known about their perception of what systems can and cannot do. This paper studies users' mental models of speech translation systems through a new framework based on cross-lingual question answering, where users either accept MT output or request professional re-translation to answer questions based on the information presented in a foreign language. By analyzing user behavior and accuracy trends across varying translation qualities, we examine to what extent they can predict where the system is likely to be wrong, and how this mental model evolves. Users develop stronger mental models with practice, especially when they have some knowledge of the source language, primarily by relying on surface-level error cues. Moreover, providing speech transcriptions can help users develop better mental models. Our results show the promise of cross-lingual question answering as a downstream task for studying MT mental models and advancing our understanding of human-AI collaboration.

</details>


### [19] [MedBench v5: A Dynamic, Process-Oriented, and Hallucination-Aware Benchmark for Clinical Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24155)
*Ding Jinru,Jiang Chuchu,Lu Lu,Pang Wenrao,Bian Mouxiao,Gao Zhuangzhi,Chen Jiangyuan,Peng xinwei,Chen Ruiyao,Ren Sijie,Lu Renjie,Han Bin,Liu Meiling,and Xu Jie*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MedBench v5 is a process-oriented clinical AI benchmark featuring dual-dimensional evaluation across 63 tasks, switchable information-flow stressors for degradation analysis, dynamic process auditing with failure fingerprints, and hallucination propagation monitoring. Experiments reveal that strong end-task performance can mask process instability, particularly in contradiction detection and self-correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical AI benchmarks suffer from three critical gaps: lack of process visibility (only evaluating final outputs, not reasoning steps), absence of atomic medical skill evaluation, and no integrated hallucination detection mechanisms. These shortcomings prevent understanding how and why clinical models fail.

Method: MedBench v5 introduces four innovations: (1) a dual-dimensional framework with 14 Clinical Cognitive Responsiveness sub-dimensions and 4 Medical Atomic Skills agent environments spanning 63 tasks; (2) three switchable information-flow stressors (omission, contradiction, evidence delay) enabling factorized degradation analysis; (3) a dynamic process audit protocol with five reasoning nodes that generates model-specific failure fingerprints; (4) hallucination propagation monitoring tracking four interaction phases — initiation, propagation, anchoring, and contradiction.

Result: Experiments on frontier models demonstrate a dissociation between final task accuracy and process stability. Stressors predominantly disrupt contradiction detection, diagnosis updating, hallucination propagation, and contradiction-based self-correction, while final evidence grounding superficially remains stable, revealing hidden vulnerabilities.

Conclusion: MedBench v5 establishes a unified infrastructure for clinical AI evaluation that goes beyond accuracy metrics, enabling capability profiling, controllable stress testing, process auditing, and hallucination trajectory analysis — ultimately revealing that process-level robustness cannot be inferred from endpoint performance alone.

Abstract: Existing medical AI benchmarks lack process visibility, atomic skill evaluation, and integrated hallucination detection. We introduce MedBench v5, a redesigned benchmark for clinical multimodal models (language, vision-language, and agent systems) that moves from static QA to dynamic, process-oriented evaluation. MedBench v5 features: (1) a dual-dimensional framework combining Clinical Cognitive Responsiveness (14 sub-dimensions) and Medical Atomic Skills (4 agent environments), covering 63 tasks; (2) three switchable information-flow stressors (omission, contradiction, evidence delay) for factorized degradation analysis; (3) a dynamic process audit protocol with five reasoning nodes that produces model-specific failure fingerprints; (4) hallucination propagation monitoring across initiation, propagation, anchoring, and contradiction interaction-capturing silent hallucination. Experiments on frontier models show that strong overall task performance does not guarantee process stability: stressors mainly disrupt contradiction detection, diagnosis updating, hallucination propagation, and contradiction-based self-correction, while final evidence grounding can remain superficially stable. MedBench v5 provides a unified infrastructure for capability profiling, controllable stress testing, process auditing, and hallucination trajectory analysis in clinical AI evaluation.

</details>


### [20] [Task Decomposition for Efficient Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24734)
*Nupoor Gandhi,Emma Strubell*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes decomposing complex structured annotation tasks into sub-tasks to reduce aggregate inferential load, introduces a formal model based on degrees of freedom in annotation spaces inspired by centering theory, and presents a procedure for optimally allocating sub-tasks across heterogeneous annotators (humans and models) under budget constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured annotation is expensive and complex, with each aspect imposing distinct inferential challenges. Existing workflows assign entire examples to single annotators, ignoring that modern projects can leverage heterogeneous groups (models + humans with varying expertise). The key gap is how to redesign tasks to discriminately allocate effort across annotators based on distinct annotation challenges.

Method: The authors propose decomposing annotation tasks into sub-tasks, drawing on centering theory's notion of "centers" (salient anchor entities). They introduce a formal model of inferential load measured by degrees of freedom in the valid annotation space. They demonstrate that identifying centers via sub-tasks constrains output space complexity, and decompositions isolating center identification reduce aggregate inferential load. They provide decomposition guidelines and a budget-constrained allocation procedure for assigning sub-tasks across heterogeneous annotators to maximize quality.

Result: The theoretical model shows that center-identifying sub-task decompositions reduce the degrees of freedom in the annotation output space, thereby lowering aggregate inferential load. Examples from prior work are cited as demonstrating improved cost-efficiency from this approach.

Conclusion: Decomposing structured annotation tasks into sub-tasks that isolate center identification is an effective strategy for reducing inferential load. Combined with a principled allocation procedure, this enables more cost-efficient use of heterogeneous annotator pools under fixed budgets.

Abstract: High-quality annotations of structured representations are expensive to collect over large corpora. Manual annotation of structure is laborious, and model-based annotation, although cheaper to generate, requires expensive validation and potentially significant supervision to ensure that the annotation quality is strong enough to be useful downstream. In traditional annotation workflows, annotation of each complete example is performed end-to-end by a single annotator. However, structured annotation is complex, and each aspect of the task represents a unique challenge with an associated inferential load for a given annotator. Modern annotation projects can incorporate heterogeneous groups of annotators, including both models and human annotators with varying domain and linguistic expertise. It remains unclear, however, how to redesign annotation tasks in this setting, where efforts are discriminately allocated across heterogeneous annotators with respect to distinct annotation challenges.
  We propose to decompose annotation tasks into sub-tasks in order to reduce the aggregate inferential load of annotation projects. Inspired by the notion of centers from centering theory, we introduce a formal model of inferential load based on the degrees of freedom in the space of valid annotations. Using this model, we show that identifying these centers (i.e. salient anchor entities realized by annotation sub-tasks) constrains the output space complexity, and decompositions which isolate and advance center identification reduce the aggregate inferential load. We provide guidelines for decomposing complex structured annotation tasks, supported by examples demonstrating improved cost-efficiency from our prior work. Finally, we present a procedure for allocating sub-tasks across annotators to maximize quality under a fixed budget.

</details>


### [21] [BehaviorBench: Benchmarking Foundation Models for Behavioral Science Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24162)
*Jin Huang,Yutong Xie,Wanli Song,Xingjian Zhang,Walter Yuan,Matthew O. Jackson,Qiaozhu Mei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BehaviorBench is a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating foundation models on four core behavioral science capabilities (prediction/simulation, strategic decision-making, trait inference, and knowledge application) at both individual and distributional levels. The authors also introduce Be.FM-1.5, a fine-tuned behavioral foundation model that excels at distributional alignment while remaining competitive on individual metrics, highlighting the importance of population-level evaluation for behavioral validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite growing use of foundation models in behavioral science, there is no systematic understanding of how well these models perform across diverse behavioral tasks, contexts, and populations. Existing evaluations typically focus on individual-level accuracy, neglecting distributional (population-level) alignment, which is critical for behavioral validity.

Method: The authors construct BehaviorBench, a benchmark spanning four core behavioral capabilities: (1) behavior prediction and simulation, (2) strategic decision-making, (3) subject-trait inference, and (4) behavioral knowledge application. They evaluate both proprietary general-purpose models and the Be.FM family of behavioral foundation models. They further develop Be.FM-1.5 by fine-tuning on behavioral data. Evaluation is conducted at two levels: individual-level (per-subject accuracy) and distributional-level (population alignment metrics).

Result: Proprietary general-purpose models excel at individual-level prediction and knowledge-intensive tasks, while behavioral foundation models achieve substantially stronger distributional alignment. Be.FM-1.5 leads on distributional metrics and remains competitive on individual-level metrics, demonstrating that proper behavioral fine-tuning can close the gap between general-purpose and domain-specific models.

Conclusion: Distributional evaluation is essential for behavioral validity in AI systems. BehaviorBench provides a standardized foundation for developing and assessing behaviorally aligned models, and Be.FM-1.5 demonstrates strong potential for a broad range of behavioral science applications.

Abstract: Foundation models have been increasingly applied to behavioral science domains such as psychology, sociology, and economics. While these models show promise in individual tasks such as survey response prediction and human-subject experiment simulation, there remains no systematic understanding of how well they perform across diverse behavioral science tasks, contexts, and populations. We introduce BehaviorBench, a comprehensive benchmark that evaluates foundation models along four core capabilities: (1) behavior prediction and simulation, (2) strategic decision-making, (3) subject-trait inference, and (4) behavioral knowledge application. Crucially, BehaviorBench evaluates model outputs at both the individual and distributional levels, capturing not only per-subject accuracy but also population-level alignment, an essential requirement for behavioral validity. Leveraging the tasks in BehaviorBench, we further develop Be.FM-1.5, extending the Be.FM family of behavioral foundation models fine-tuned on behavioral data.
