Our results reveal a considerable gap: proprietary general-purpose models excel at individual-level prediction and knowledge-intensive tasks, whereas behavioral foundation models, fine-tuned on behavioral data, achieve substantially stronger distributional alignment. Notably, Be.FM-1.5 leads on distributional metrics and remains competitive on individual-level metrics, suggesting that proper behavioral adaptation can close the gap. Our results highlight the importance of distributional evaluation, establish BehaviorBench as a foundation for developing and assessing behaviorally aligned AI systems, and demonstrate Be.FM-1.5's potential for a broad range of behavioral science studies. Our BehaviorBench and Be.FM-1.5 models can be accessed via https://umich-foreseer.github.io/behaviorbench/.

</details>


### [22] [A Pāninian Foundation for Indic Language Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24172)
*Ritwik Banerjee,Lav R. Varshney*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Indic NLP is fragmented because tools are built per-language, ignoring a shared morphosyntactic architecture derived from Pāṇini's Sanskrit grammar. The authors argue this Pāṇinian framework provides a unifying computational backbone, and propose a four-part benchmark suite to make it explicit, measurable, and actionable, while also raising the interpretability question of whether neural models implicitly learn Pāṇinian categories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Over a billion speakers use Indic languages, but NLP infrastructure for them is fragmented and underdeveloped. The structural cause is that the field organizes tools and benchmarks around individual languages or small genealogical families, building separate resources for each language. This overlooks a deep convergence: through millennia of contact with Sanskrit, Indic languages share a morphosyntactic architecture formalized in Pāṇini's Aṣṭādhyāyī, which cuts across genealogical lines. The authors argue that leveraging this common framework can unify sparse, disparate Indic resources into a single high-resource metalanguage, improving accuracy, data efficiency, and cross-lingual transfer.

Method: The paper proposes a four-part benchmark suite designed to render the shared Pāṇinian morphosyntactic architecture explicit and measurable. The suite is intended to ground Indic NLP evaluation in linguistically motivated categories derived from the Aṣṭādhyāyī, enabling systematic probing of whether computational models capture these structures. The benchmarks serve both as practical evaluation tools and as instruments for interpretability research, investigating whether neural models trained on Indic languages spontaneously learn Pāṇinian categories.

Result: The paper is a position/proposal paper rather than an empirical study, so it does not report experimental results. Its primary contribution is the conceptual argument that the Pāṇinian framework provides a unifying computational architecture and the concrete proposal of a four-part benchmark suite to operationalize this shared structure for more accurate, data-efficient, and transferable Indic NLP systems.

Conclusion: Grounding Indic NLP benchmarks in the Pāṇinian framework can transform the field by merging many sparse, language-specific resources into a unified high-resource metalanguage. This promises gains in accuracy, data efficiency, and transferability. The proposed benchmark suite also opens a line of interpretability research: determining whether neural models implicitly recover Pāṇinian linguistic categories, with implications for both computational linguistics and cognitive science.

Abstract: More than a billion people communicate in Indic languages, yet the natural language processing infrastructure serving them remains fragmented and underdeveloped. The cause is structural: the field organizes its tools and benchmarks around individual languages or small subsets of genealogical language families, building separate analyzers, parsers, and datasets for each language and starting over for the next. This overlooks a deep regularity. Through more than two millennia of convergence around Sanskrit, Indic languages came to share a morphosyntactic architecture formalized in Pānini's grammar, the Astādhyāyī. This cuts across genealogical lines, uniting languages through a common framework. We argue that this Pāninian framework supplies a unifying computational architecture the field has lacked, and that benchmarks grounded explicitly in it would make Indic language systems more accurate, more data-efficient, and more transferable, effectively merging many apparently disparate and sparse Indic language resources into a single high-resource metalanguage bedrock. We propose a four-part benchmark suite to render this shared architecture explicit, measurable, and ready to be leveraged for practical applications. Moreover, we underscore the question it raises for interpretability research: whether neural models trained on these languages come to represent Pānini's categories on their own.

</details>


### [23] [A Synthetic Reliability-Aware PINN Benchmark for Offshore Wind Turbine Support-Structure Monitoring with Bayesian Inverse Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24176)
*Puneet Kant,Monika Tanwar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DigiTurbine introduces a PINN benchmark for offshore wind turbine monopile monitoring that combines Euler-Bernoulli beam physics with Bayesian inverse identification and FORM reliability screening, validated on NREL 5MW-inspired synthetic cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online structural health monitoring of offshore wind turbine support structures demands fast state estimation from sparse sensor data. High-fidelity FE/aeroelastic models are too computationally expensive for real-time loops, while pure data-driven surrogates need large training datasets. A physics-informed approach that balances speed, data efficiency, and reliability quantification is needed.

Method: The DigiTurbine workflow embeds a simplified Euler-Bernoulli beam equation with Winkler soil foundation into the PINN training objective. This is coupled with Bayesian-prior-informed inverse identification for parameter estimation and uncertainty quantification, plus First Order Reliability Method (FORM) screening for reliability assessment. Validation uses synthetic configurations with analytical or finite-difference ground truth referenced to the NREL 5MW turbine.

Result: Not explicitly stated in the abstract — the paper presents the benchmark framework itself rather than specific performance metrics. Results would concern the accuracy of state estimation, inverse identification, and reliability screening against synthetic ground truth.

Conclusion: Not explicitly stated in the abstract. The contribution is the benchmark methodology combining physics-informed learning with reliability analysis for OWT monitoring, providing a reproducible synthetic validation framework.

Abstract: Reliable structural health monitoring (SHM) of offshore wind turbine (OWT) support structures requires fast state estimation from sparse measurements. Repeated high fidelity finite element or aeroelastic analyses are difficult to use directly in online monitoring loops, while purely data-driven surrogates can require large training sets. This paper presents Digi Turbine, a synthetic reliability-aware Physics Informed Neural Network (PINN) benchmark for OWT monopile support structure monitoring. The workflow embeds a simplified Euler Bernoulli beam equation with Winkler soil foundation in the training objective, couples it with Bayesian-prior-informed inverse identification, and adds First Order Reliability Method (FORM) screening. All validation uses synthetic configurations with analytical or finite-difference ground truth motivated by the NREL 5MW reference turbine context.

</details>


### [24] [Aspect-Based Sentiment Evolution and its Correlation with Review Rounds in Multi-Round Peer Reviews: A Deep Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24188)
*Ruxue Hana,Haomin Zhoua,Jiangtao Zhong,Chengzhi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper analyzes multi-round peer review comments from Nature Communications, training an aspect-level sentiment classifier (LCF-BERT-CDM achieving 82.65% Macro-F1) on ~5,000 manually annotated sentences. Findings reveal that as review rounds progress, positive sentiment increases, negative sentiment declines, and sentiment scores negatively correlate with total review rounds, particularly for aspects like "experiments," "research significance," and "result analysis."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on peer review sentiment analysis is limited to coarse-grained analysis and fails to distinguish between review rounds. The dynamic evolution of reviewers' focus and sentiment across multiple review stages remains underexplored, motivating this fine-grained, round-aware investigation.

Method: The authors collected 11,063 accepted papers' multi-round review comments from Nature Communications, segmented them into fine-grained aspect clusters, and built a manually annotated corpus of ~5,000 review sentences. Several deep learning models for aspect sentiment classification were trained, with LCF-BERT-CDM yielding the best results (Macro-F1: 82.65%). Statistical and correlation analyses then examined sentiment evolution across rounds and associations between aspect sentiment scores and total review rounds.

Result: LCF-BERT-CDM achieved a Macro-F1 of 82.65%. Statistically, as review rounds increase, positive sentiment proportions rise and negative sentiment proportions fall. Aspect sentiment scores are negatively correlated with total review rounds, with "experiments," "research significance," and "result analysis" showing the strongest correlations.

Conclusion: There is a clear, measurable relationship between aspect-level sentiment dynamics and the number of review rounds: sentiment becomes progressively more positive across rounds, and lower sentiment scores are associated with more review rounds, especially for core evaluative aspects.

Abstract: Mining sentiment information from the textual content of peer review comments offers valuable insights into the scientific evaluation process. However, previous studies are often constrained by coarse-grained analysis and the lack of differentiation across review rounds. Notably, the dynamic shifts in reviewers' focus and sentiment tendencies throughout multiple review stages remain underexplored. To address this gap, the present study investigates the distribution and evolution of aspect-level sentiments and examines their correlation with the number of review rounds. We begin by segmenting the multi-round review comments of 11,063 accepted papers from Nature Communications and identifying fine-grained review aspect clusters. A manually annotated corpus of approximately 5,000 review sentences is then constructed. Using this dataset, we train a series of deep learning-based aspect sentiment classification models. Among them, the LCF-BERT-CDM model achieves the best performance, with a Macro-F1 score of 82.65%. Subsequent statistical analysis reveals a consistent trend: as the number of review rounds increases, the proportion of positive sentiments rises, while negative sentiments decline. Correlation analysis further indicates that aspect sentiment scores are negatively associated with the total number of review rounds. Key aspects exhibiting stronger correlations include "experiments", "research significance" and "result analysis".

</details>


### [25] [MMed-Bench-IR: A Heterogeneous Benchmark for Multilingual Medical Information Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24200)
*Junhyeok Lee,Han Jang,Hyeonjin Goh,Kyu Sung Choi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MMed-Bench-IR is a multilingual medical retrieval benchmark spanning 6 languages and 3 tasks (QA retrieval, concept discrimination, evidence retrieval) with zero concept/query overlap. Evaluation reveals catastrophic cross-lingual failure: strong English biomedical encoders (0.818 nDCG@10) plummet to near-zero performance in Japanese (0.056), exposing a gap invisible to English-only benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate cross-lingual alignment, concept discrimination, and evidence retrieval in isolation, failing to measure how biomedical expertise and multilingual coverage interact. Clinical RAG systems increasingly need multilingual retrieval against English evidence corpora, but no benchmark disentangles these axes to reveal genuine capability breadth.

Method: The authors construct MMed-Bench-IR with three structurally heterogeneous, non-overlapping tasks: (1) cross-lingual medical QA retrieval using 6,127 UMLS-grounded queries across 6 languages, (2) concept discrimination with 4,975 confusion sets at three difficulty tiers, and (3) multilingual evidence retrieval with 2,040 quality-assured queries for RAG. Ten systems from six paradigm families are evaluated, with zero concept and query overlap by design to ensure aggregate scores reflect true capability breadth.

Result: Severe cross-lingual performance collapse: biomedical encoders achieving 0.818 nDCG@10 in English drop to 0.056 in Japanese. This degradation is invisible under English-only evaluation. The three-task, zero-overlap design successfully exposes the interaction failure between biomedical specialization and multilingual coverage across all six evaluated paradigm families.

Conclusion: Multilingual medical retrieval remains critically underdeveloped, with current biomedical encoders exhibiting catastrophic cross-lingual failure. MMed-Bench-IR provides the first benchmark that disentangles the interaction between biomedical expertise and multilingual coverage, revealing that English-only benchmarks systematically mask severe real-world deficiencies in clinical RAG systems.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) in clinical settings increasingly requires multilingual retrieval against predominantly English evidence corpora. Multilingual medical retrieval demands three capabilities: cross-lingual alignment, concept discrimination, and evidence retrieval. However, existing benchmarks evaluate these only in isolation, leaving the interaction between biomedical expertise and multilingual coverage unmeasured. We introduce MMed-Bench-IR, a benchmark designed to disentangle these axes across 6 languages and three structurally heterogeneous tasks: (1) cross-lingual medical QA retrieval with 6,127 queries grounded in the Unified Medical Language System (UMLS), (2) concept discrimination over 4,975 confusion sets at three difficulty tiers, and (3) multilingual evidence retrieval for RAG with 2,040 quality-assured queries. The three tasks share zero concept and query overlap by design, ensuring that aggregate scores reflect genuine capability breadth. Evaluation of ten systems across six paradigm families reveals severe cross-lingual failure: biomedical encoders that score 0.818 nDCG@10 in English drop to 0.056 in Japanese, a gap that English-only benchmarks cannot detect.

</details>


### [26] [SURGELLM: Rethinking Multi-Task Evaluation through Task-Aware Feature Gating with Class-Balanced Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24259)
*Noor Islam S. Mohammad,Ulug Bayazit*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Surgellm introduces a unified transformer framework with three lightweight modules — surgical feature gates, task-conditioned prefix tokens, and Instance-Weighted Normalization (IWN) — to address mismatched inductive biases, class-imbalance corruption, and lack of external lexical knowledge conditioning in fine-tuned encoders. IWN achieves macro-F1 of 0.940 across four diverse NLP tasks, with a random-vocabulary control confirming gains are lexical rather than parametric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuned transformer encoders deployed across heterogeneous NLP tasks suffer from three compounding problems: (1) mismatched inductive biases between pretraining and downstream tasks, (2) class-imbalance corruption of feature statistics, and (3) no mechanism to condition attention on external lexical knowledge. No existing approach addresses all three simultaneously.

Method: The Surgellm framework integrates three dedicated lightweight modules: (a) a surgical feature gate — a learned per-dimension sigmoid function over curated lexical indicators and [CLS] representations, provably degenerating to identity when features are uninformative; (b) task-conditioned prefix tokens — quantized feature values and task identity prepended to every input; (c) Instance-Weighted Normalization (IWN) — removes class-prior bias from gate statistics. An excess-risk bound is proved, linking gate benefit to surgical feature alignment. Evaluation spans SST-2, multi-hop retrieval, LLM-prompt attribution, and authorship detection (17,830 examples, 11 model variants, 3 seeds). A random-vocabulary ablation serves as control.

Result: The IWN variant achieves a macro-F1 of 0.940, outperforming the strongest non-IWN baseline by +0.036 overall and by +0.130 on authorship detection specifically. The random-vocabulary control yields an average F1 drop of -0.028, confirming that gains stem from lexical knowledge rather than increased parametric capacity.

Conclusion: Surgellm provides a unified, theoretically grounded solution to three key problems in fine-tuned multi-task encoders. The lexical nature of improvements is validated by the random-vocabulary ablation. Code, vocabularies, and a 99.5%-recovery auto-extraction recipe are publicly released to facilitate reproduction.

Abstract: Fine-tuned encoders deployed across heterogeneous NLP tasks face three compounding problems: mismatched inductive biases, class-imbalance corruption of feature statistics, and no mechanism to condition attention on external lexical knowledge. We introduce \textbf{\surgellm}, a unified transformer framework that addresses each with a dedicated lightweight module: a \emph{surgical feature gate} (learned per-dimension sigmoid over curated lexical indicators and \texttt{[CLS]}; provably degenerates to identity when features are uninformative), \emph{task-conditioned prefix tokens} (quantized feature values and task identity prepended to every input), and \emph{Instance-Weighted Normalization} (IWN; removes class-prior bias from gate statistics). We prove an excess-risk bound linking gate benefit to \emph{surgical feature alignment}. Across four tasks, SST-2, multi-hop retrieval, LLM-prompt attribution, and authorship detection, covering 17,830 examples and eleven model variants over three seeds, the IWN variant achieves macro-F1 \textbf{0.940} ($+0.036$ over the strongest non-IWN baseline; $+0.130$ on authorship detection). A random-vocabulary control ($-0.028$ avg.\ F1) confirms gains are lexical, not parametric. Code, vocabularies, and a $99.5\%$-recovery auto-extraction recipe are released.

</details>


### [27] [Pigeonholing: Bad prompts hurt models to collapse and make mistakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24267)
*Hyunji Nam,Keertana Chidambaram,Dorottya Demszky,Natasha Jaques*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper identifies "pigeonholing" — a phenomenon where bad in-context examples in LLM conversations cause performance degradation, mode collapse, and stance flipping. The authors demonstrate this across 10 tasks and 10 models, showing monotonic worsening with more conversation turns. They propose RLVR with synthetic errors as a mitigation, achieving 43–60% improvement over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In-context learning is known to be effective, but the authors observe that even unintentionally bad contexts — such as users asking models to justify incorrect math or failing to correct buggy code — can severely degrade LLM performance. This problem, termed "pigeonholing," has not been systematically studied, motivating a rigorous empirical investigation and the development of mitigation strategies.

Method: The authors design experiments covering two scenarios: (1) when the user suggests a solution, and (2) when the assistant's own previous incorrect responses appear in context. They evaluate 10 models across 10 verifiable and open-ended tasks, measuring performance drop, answer diversity (mode collapse), and stance changes on controversial topics. They also propose RLVR (Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards) augmented with synthetic errors as a mitigation technique, training models to resist pigeonholing.

Result: Pigeonholing manifests as: (1) repeating incorrect context answers (38–40% performance drop), (2) converging to narrow answer sets in coding/text generation, and (3) flipping stances to align with user or assistant claims. Performance worsens almost monotonically with conversation turns (14%+ additional drop when repeated mistakes increase from 1 to 5). Mode collapse occurs even with correct examples. RLVR with synthetic errors improves model robustness under bad contexts by 43–60% over vanilla RLVR.

Conclusion: Pigeonholing is a significant and pervasive vulnerability in LLM in-context learning that escalates with conversation length and occurs even without malicious intent. The proposed RLVR with synthetic errors offers a promising mitigation, substantially improving model resilience against bad contexts.

Abstract: While in-context learning is generally shown to be effective in Large Language Models (LLMs), bad contexts can cause performance degradation and mode collapse, a phenomenon we call "pigeonholing." **Unintentionally bad** contexts can happen without malicious jailbreaking intents: For example, a user asks the model to justify an incorrect math theorem or fails to correct the model's buggy code. Specifically, we investigate ``pigeonholing" in two scenarios: (1) when the user suggests a solution, and (2) when the conversation context includes the assistant's previous (incorrect) responses. Our experiments across 10 verifiable and open-ended tasks with 10 different models show that pigeonholing manifests in several ways: (1) repeating the incorrect answers from context (leading to 38-40% performance drop), (2) converging on a narrow set of answers in coding and text generation without exploring alternatives, and (3) flipping stance on controversial topics to align with the user or the assistant's previous claims. We find that pigeonholing worsens almost monotonically with the number of conversation turns (performance drops by additional 14+% as repeated mistakes increase from 1 to 5), and pigeonholing-induced mode collapse can happen even when the provided example is correct. As a step toward mitigation, we propose RLVR with synthetic errors which improves models by 43-60% under bad contexts compared to vanilla RLVR baselines.

</details>


### [28] [CALIBER: Calibrating Confidence Before and After Reasoning in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24281)
*Conor Finlay,Joshua Kurien,Saurabh Dash,Marzieh Fadaee,Beyza Ermis*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CALIBER introduces a state-dependent confidence elicitation framework for reasoning language models, distinguishing between pre-thinking (prompt-level success) and post-answering (answer-level correctness) confidence estimates, each supervised with matched targets. It reduces ECE by 52.5% over baselines and generalizes well under distribution shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods elicit confidence from reasoning models only once (either before or after thinking), ignoring that confidence is state-dependent: pre-thinking confidence should estimate the probability of eventually solving the prompt correctly, while post-answering confidence should estimate whether the specific answer produced is correct. Mismatching supervision targets to information states leads to poor calibration.

Method: CALIBER elicits two distinct confidence estimates: one after seeing the prompt (pre-thinking) and one after producing the answer (post-thinking). Each estimate is supervised with a matched target: prompt-level binary success for the pre-thinking estimate, and answer-level binary correctness for the post-thinking estimate. This unified protocol ensures position-target alignment across the reasoning pipeline. Experiments span 7B and 30B models on BigMathDigits, GPQA, TriviaQA, and SimpleQA.

Result: On BigMathDigits with a 7B model, CALIBER reduces ECE by 52.5% over the strongest single-confidence baseline, achieves best Brier score and AUROC, and stays within 2.1 points of best accuracy. On a 30B model, it achieves best ECE on BigMathDigits. Out-of-distribution, CALIBER achieves best ECE and Brier score on GPQA and TriviaQA, remaining competitive on SimpleQA. Ablations confirm position-target alignment is most beneficial under distribution shift.

Conclusion: Confidence in reasoning models is fundamentally state-dependent, and aligning supervision targets to the model's information state at each position yields substantially better calibration, particularly under distribution shift. CALIBER provides a simple yet effective unified protocol that outperforms single-confidence baselines across diverse benchmarks.

Abstract: Reasoning language models are increasingly asked not only to answer difficult questions, but also to estimate their likelihood of success. Existing methods typically elicit confidence only once: either before thinking or after answering. We argue that confidence in reasoning models is state-dependent: before thinking, confidence should estimate the chance of the model correctly solving the prompt, while after thinking it should predict whether the realized answer is likely to be correct. This distinction determines the appropriate supervision target: prompt-level success should supervise confidence estimates made after seeing the prompt, while individual answer-level correctness should supervise confidence estimates made after answering. We introduce CALIBER (Calibration Before and After Reasoning), which elicits both estimates and supervises each with the target matched to its information state. Under this unified protocol, CALIBER reduces Expected Calibration Error (ECE) by 52.5% over the strongest single-confidence baseline on BigMathDigits for the 7B model, while achieving the best Brier score and AUROC, and remains within 2.1 points of the best accuracy. Further, on a larger 30B model, CALIBER achieves the best ECE on BigMathDigits while remaining competitive in Brier score and AUROC. Out of distribution, it achieves the best ECE and Brier score on GPQA and TriviaQA, and remains competitive on SimpleQA. Ablations further show that this position-target alignment is most beneficial under distribution shift where it consistently reduces calibration error across all out-of-distribution benchmarks.

</details>


### [29] [AVOC: Enhancing Hour-Level Audio-Video Understanding in Omni-Modal LLMs via Retrieval-Inspired Token Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24286)
*Yijing Chen,Wenhui Tan,Xiaoyi Yu,Yuyue Wang,Xin Cheng,Kaisi Guan,Hao Jiang,Xiangyang Li,Guojie Zhu,Ruihua Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AVOC is a token compression framework for long-form audio-video understanding in Omni-modal LLMs. It reframes compression as a top-K retrieval problem using three IR-inspired criteria — relevance, importance, and diversity — to select the most informative tokens within a fixed context budget, achieving SOTA on long-form benchmarks with strong needle-in-a-haystack performance up to one hour.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-form audio-video understanding in Multimodal LLMs is hindered by limited context windows and severe information redundancy. Existing approaches struggle to efficiently compress multimodal tokens while preserving the information necessary for accurate query answering over extended durations.

Method: AVOC introduces a learnable token compression module between modality encoders and the LLM backbone. Token compression is reformulated as a top-K retrieval problem: given a fixed context budget, select the most informative token subset. Three classical IR criteria are operationalized: (1) Relevance — tokens most pertinent to the user query; (2) Importance — tokens carrying high intrinsic semantic value; (3) Diversity — tokens that are non-redundant and collectively cover the content. These are instantiated as tailored mechanisms and integrated into a unified retrieval-style compression pipeline.

Result: AVOC achieves SOTA on long-form audio-video benchmarks, outperforming the second-best model by +4.9 points on OmniVideoBench and +5.5 points on LVOmniBench in average accuracy. It also maintains robust performance on the Audio-Video Needle-in-a-Haystack task at durations up to one hour.

Conclusion: Reformulating multimodal token compression as an IR-style top-K retrieval problem — guided by relevance, importance, and diversity — is highly effective for long-form audio-video understanding. AVOC significantly advances the state of the art while preserving performance over extended durations.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models have achieved remarkable progress in short-form audio-video understanding, yet long-form audio-video comprehension remains challenged by limited context windows and severe information redundancy. To address these bottlenecks, we propose AVOC, a framework for long-form audio-video understanding in Omni-modal Large Language Models. AVOC introduces a learnable token compression module between the modality encoders and the LLM backbone. We reframe multimodal token compression as a top-$K$ retrieval problem: given a fixed context budget, the module must retrieve a compact subset of tokens that best supports answering the user query. We draw inspiration from three classical Information Retrieval criteria for selecting informative units from a large candidate pool: relevance, importance, and diversity. AVOC instantiates each criterion as a tailored mechanism for audio-video understanding, and integrates them into a unified retrieval-style compression pipeline. Experiments show that AVOC achieves state-of-the-art performance on long-form audio-video benchmarks, surpassing the second-best model by 4.9 and 5.5 points in average accuracy on OmniVideoBench and LVOmniBench, respectively. Moreover, AVOC maintains robust performance on Audio-Video Needle-in-a-Haystack task at durations up to one hour.

</details>


### [30] [Prague Dependency Treebank -- Consolidated 2.0: Enriching a Complex Annotation Scheme](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24324)
*Marie Mikulová,Jiří Mírovský,Milan Straka,Pavlína Synková,Jan Štěpánek,Barbora Štěpánková,Jan Hajič*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces PDT-C 2.0, the second consolidated version of the Prague Dependency Treebank, a nearly 4-million-token, genre-diversified Czech corpus with multi-layered annotation including meaning representation, coreference, and discourse relations, culminating almost 30 years of development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To present the final consolidated version of a long-term project that systematically integrates multiple linguistic layers — from morphology to meaning representation with inter-sentential phenomena — into a uniformly annotated, large-scale resource for Czech, enabling both linguistic research and NLP tool development.

Method: The paper describes the consolidation of the Prague Dependency Treebank framework, which involves multi-layered annotation (surface syntax, deep syntax/meaning representation, coreference, discourse relations), harmonization across genres, and the release of compatible lexicons and trained parsers under the CC BY-NC-SA license.

Result: The outcome is PDT-C 2.0: a uniformly and coherently annotated corpus spanning diverse genres, totaling nearly 4 million tokens, accompanied by fully compatible lexicons and trained parsers, made publicly available.

Conclusion: PDT-C 2.0 represents the culmination of a nearly 30-year effort, providing a richly annotated, multi-layered language resource that serves both continuous linguistic research and international benchmarking of NLP tools, as well as enabling conversions into other formalisms.

Abstract: The Prague Dependency Treebank framework is unique in its attempt to systematically include and link different layers of language, including a meaning representation with several types of inter-sentential phenomena, especially coreference and discourse relations. We present its second consolidated version (PDT-C 2.0), which concludes almost 30-years long project of sustained development of the resource to a uniformly and coherently annotated, genre-diversified, almost 4 million token language resource of Czech language, with accompanying fully compatible lexicons. In addition to continuous linguistic research, the richly linguistically annotated corpus is also widely used in international comparisons of the development of traditional and novel NLP tools as well as in conversions into other formalisms. The corpus and the trained parsers are available under the CC BY-NC-SA licence.

</details>


### [31] [Transformer-Based Language Models Across Domain Verticals: Architectures, Applications and Critical Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24331)
*Guruprakash J,Krithika L. B*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A comprehensive survey that taxonomizes transformer architectures, examines post-2023 developments (instruction tuning, RLHF, DPO, MoE, RAG), surveys real-world deployments across seven domains, and provides a critical assessment of architectural trade-offs, energy costs, alignment methods, and benchmark saturation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid pace of new transformer-based language model releases has made it difficult for practitioners to distinguish meaningful, durable innovations from incremental announcements. A systematic, critical survey is needed to bridge this gap between mechanism and practical deployment.

Method: The authors conduct a two-level survey: (1) a mechanistic taxonomy organizing transformer families into encoder-only, decoder-only, encoder-decoder, long-context, permutation-based, and generator-discriminator variants, extended with post-2023 developments; (2) a use-level survey of deployments across healthcare, finance, legal, education, customer service, creative writing, and scientific work. This is followed by a critical assessment comparing architectures along four deployment-relevant axes and quantifying the parameter-count vs. energy-cost trade-off.

Result: The paper delivers a structured taxonomy of transformer families, maps specific transformer capabilities to domain-appropriate applications, quantifies the trade-off between parameter count and energy cost, and critically evaluates how alignment methods, data provenance, and benchmark saturation complicate the notion of "state of the art." It also identifies underexplored research questions.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that separating durable architectural ideas from hype requires joint consideration of mechanism, deployment context, alignment, data provenance, and benchmark integrity. It calls for greater attention to under-researched questions in these areas.

Abstract: Transformer-based language models have become the default substrate for natural language processing and the pace of new releases has made it hard for practitioners to separate durable ideas from the noise of incremental announcements. This review works at two levels. At the level of mechanism, we organise the main transformer families into a working taxonomy, covering encoder-only, decoder-only, encoder-decoder, long-context, permutation-based, and generator-discriminator variants. We then extend the discussion to post-2023 developments that changed the picture in practice: instruction tuning, reinforcement learning from human feedback, direct preference optimisation, mixture-of-experts scaling, retrieval augmentation and the current flagship model families from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta, Mistral and DeepSeek. At the level of use, we survey deployments across healthcare, finance, legal, education, customer service, creative writing and scientific work. Based on this we link each to the specific capabilities that make a transformer the appropriate tool. The contribution of this paper is a critical assessment that is based on the survey. We compare architectures on four axes that matter to deployment decisions, we quantify the trade-off between parameter count and energy cost. We also discuss how alignment methods, data provenance and benchmark saturation change what it means to call a model "state of the art". The final section lists the research questions that we think deserve more attention.

</details>


### [32] [Meet UD_Czech-PDTC: A Large and Genre-Rich Treebank in Universal Dependencies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24337)
*Marie Mikulová,Barbora Štěpánková,Daniel Zeman,Jan Štěpánek,Milan Straka,Jan Hajič*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper describes the conversion of the newly consolidated Prague Dependency Treebank (PDT-C) — over twice the size and genre diversity of the original — into Universal Dependencies (UD), highlighting topological, POS, and relation granularity differences between the two schemes and arguing that PDT's rich multi-layer annotation subsumes all information needed for UD trees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To integrate the substantially expanded and consolidated Prague Dependency Treebank into Universal Dependencies while addressing systematic annotation divergences between the PDT and UD schemes, thereby enriching UD with a larger, more genre-diverse Czech resource.

Method: The authors analyze topological differences in dependency structures, granularity mismatches in POS tags and relation inventories, and perform a rule-based conversion mapping from PDT's multi-layer annotation scheme to UD, using illustrative examples to demonstrate diverging annotation motivations and conversion strategies.

Result: PDT-C is successfully converted to UD, more than doubling the size of Czech UD data while adding genre diversity. The conversion reveals numerous small but systematic differences in structure topology and label granularity, all resolvable because PDT's richer multi-layer annotation provides sufficient information.

Conclusion: Despite PDT being less "universal" and more language-specific than UD, its comprehensive multi-layer annotation fully supports conversion to UD trees and offers additional linguistic value beyond what UD represents.

Abstract: Czech has been part of Universal Dependencies since its first release in 2015. It has also been one of the best represented languages, with the Prague Dependency Treebank being order of magnitude larger than most other UD treebanks. More recently, three other datasets from the Prague family were added and the annotations thoroughly revisited, forming the "Prague Dependency Treebank-Consolidated" (PDT-C). In comparison to the original PDT, PDT-C is more than twice as large, but it is also much more diverse in terms of genres and domains. In this paper, we describe the conversion of the new resource to Universal Dependencies. While the two annotation schemes are relatively similar at the first sight, there are numerous small differences in topology of the dependency structures and in granularity of the POS and relation type inventories. We demonstrate a selection of such differences on examples, discuss the diverging motivations, as well as ways to overcome the differences during conversion. We argue that while PDT is less "universal" and more tightly bound to one language, its multi-layer annotation is rich and provides all information needed for basic UD trees, and much more.

</details>


### [33] [Automatic Part-of-Speech Tagging of Arabic-English Dictionary Senses through WordNet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24359)
*Diaa M. Fayed,Aly A. Fahmy,Mohsen A. Rashwan,Wafaa K. Fayed*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A resource-light algorithm transfers POS tags from English WordNet to Arabic dictionary senses via translation equivalents, achieving high accuracy for linking bilingual dictionaries to WordNet-LMF.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building NLP tools for resource-poor languages typically requires costly linguistic expertise, large annotated corpora, or rich lexicons. This motivates resource-light approaches that leverage existing resources (like WordNet) for languages lacking such infrastructure.

Method: The algorithm takes senses from the Al-Mawrid Arabic-English bilingual dictionary, identifies English translation equivalents, looks up their POS tags in Princeton WordNet, disambiguates them, and transfers the resolved POS tags back to the Arabic dictionary senses.

Result: High POS tagging accuracy was achieved on the Al-Mawrid dictionary senses with minimal cost, demonstrating the viability of the resource-light transfer approach.

Conclusion: POS tagging of bilingual dictionary senses via cross-lingual transfer from WordNet is an effective resource-light strategy, enabling dictionary standardization into WordNet-LMF format where synsets serve as the fundamental organizational unit.

Abstract: This paper proposed an algorithm for part-of-speech (POS) tagging senses of a bilingual dictionary. The algorithm is applied on the Al-Mawrid Arabic-English dictionary. The tagging task is accomplished by transferring the POS tags of the English translation equivalences (TEs) to the dictionary senses after dis-ambiguities process. The English POS tags of senses are acquired from the Princeton WordNet. POS tagging of bilingual dictionary senses is prerequisite to link a bilingual dictionary to WordNet and/or standardizing that dictionary into WordNet-LMF format where the synset (set of synonyms), not word, is the basic brick. The registered accuracy is high though the cost is little. Building NLP/HLT tools needs linguistic experts, large investments, and long time. For statistical approach, we need large annotated corpora and for rule-based approach, we need large lexicon that contains rich linguistic and world knowledge. That motivates the appearance of what are called resource-light approaches to develop natural language processing (NLP) tools for poor-resource languages.

</details>


### [34] [MorfFlex: Handling Rich Morphology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24366)
*Jaroslava Hlaváčová,Marie Mikulová,Barbora Štěpánková,Milan Straka,Jan Hajič*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MorfFlex is a compact morphological dictionary architecture leveraging inflectional and derivational patterns to efficiently encode over 100 million Czech wordforms and 1 million lemmas, supporting corpus annotation consistency and NLP tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Morphologically rich languages like Czech present a challenge for dictionary development due to the combinatorial explosion of wordforms. A naive listing approach is infeasible; thus, a pattern-based representation is needed to manage complexity while supporting consistent annotation and NLP tool development.

Method: The authors propose MorfFlex, a morphological dictionary architecture. Internally, it uses manually maintained source files and conversion scripts encoding a sophisticated system of inflectional and derivational patterns. These patterns compactly generate the output dictionary, distributed as a flat list of ⟨wordform, lemma, tag⟩ triplets. The paper demonstrates the architecture through MorfFlex CZ, the Czech instantiation.

Result: MorfFlex CZ encodes over 100 million wordforms and more than 1 million lemmas using a pattern-based internal representation that dramatically reduces storage size. It underpins annotation consistency in the Prague Dependency Treebanks and serves as the lexical backbone for the state-of-the-art morphological tagger/disambiguator MorphoDiTa.

Conclusion: MorfFlex provides an effective, pattern-driven solution for managing large-scale morphological dictionaries for highly inflectional and derivational languages, demonstrating clear practical value in both corpus annotation workflows and downstream NLP applications.

Abstract: We present MorfFlex, a morphological dictionary architecture suitable for languages with extensive regularity in both inflection and derivation. As the primary example of MorfFlex in use we introduce MorfFlex CZ, a morphological dictionary of Czech. It is distributed as a simple, unstructured list of <wordform, lemma, tag> triplets, however, its manually maintained, unpublished source files and conversion scripts encode a sophisticated system of inflectional and derivational patterns. These patterns dramatically reduce the otherwise enormous size of the dictionary, which currently contains over 100 million wordforms and more than 1 million lemmas. The MorfFlex CZ dictionary serves as an essential resource for ensuring the consistency of manual morphological annotation in the Prague Dependency Treebanks and underpins state-of-the-art automatic tools such as MorphoDiTa. In this paper, we focus on: (i) presenting an effective method for managing the rich morphological system within the dictionary, and (ii) demonstrating the utility of such a language resource for maintaining annotation consistency in corpora and supporting the development of advanced NLP applications.

</details>


### [35] [On the Stability of Prompt Ranking in Large Language Model Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24381)
*Shaoshuai Du,Penghao Liang,Yixian Shen,Chuanqi Shi,Hang Zhang,Lun Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Prompt rankings are unstable under minor evaluation variations (random seeds, data subsets). Top-performing prompts frequently change identity, undermining reliable selection. The authors propose a stability-aware selection strategy using a lower confidence bound that accounts for both performance and variance, improving robustness in unstable settings while remaining competitive otherwise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard prompt selection workflow assumes that prompt rankings are stable under minor evaluation variations. This assumption is unverified and potentially flawed, meaning practitioners may be selecting suboptimal prompts due to evaluation noise from random seeds and limited data subsets.

Method: The authors systematically quantify prompt ranking instability across three open-weight LLMs and two benchmark tasks under two variability sources: random seeds and limited evaluation subsets. They measure rank correlation (Spearman, Kendall tau) and track changes in top-1 prompt identity. They then propose a stability-aware selection strategy using a lower confidence bound (LCB) that incorporates both mean performance and variance, selecting the prompt with the best worst-case performance bound.

Result: Overall rank correlations (Spearman, Kendall tau) are moderate to high, but the identity of the top-ranked prompt changes frequently under both seed and subset variation. The proposed LCB strategy improves selection robustness in unstable settings and remains competitive with standard top-1 selection in stable regimes.

Conclusion: Evaluation uncertainty in prompt selection is a significant and underappreciated problem. Incorporating variance through a lower confidence bound strategy yields more reliable prompt selection. The findings carry implications for both practitioners selecting prompts and researchers benchmarking LLMs.

Abstract: Prompt-based interaction has become a dominant paradigm for using large language models (LLMs), where multiple candidate prompts are evaluated and the top-ranked one is selected for downstream use. This workflow implicitly assumes that prompt rankings are stable under minor variations in evaluation conditions. In this paper, we systematically study prompt ranking stability under common sources of variability, including random seeds and limited evaluation subsets. Across three open-weight LLMs and two benchmark tasks, we find that while overall rank correlations are often moderate to high, the identity of the top-performing prompt frequently changes, leading to unreliable selection decisions. To address this issue, we propose a simple stability-aware selection strategy based on a lower confidence bound, which accounts for both performance and variance. Our results show that this approach improves robustness in unstable settings while remaining competitive in more stable regimes. These findings highlight the importance of accounting for evaluation uncertainty in prompt selection and LLM benchmarking.

</details>


### [36] [AutoSpecNER: A Fine-Grained Named Entity Recognition Dataset for Vehicle Specification Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24387)
*Jordan Lee,Filippos Ventirozos,Abdirahman Abdullahm,Ioanna Nteka,Peter Appleby,Matthew Shardlow*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AutoSpecNER is a new expert-annotated dataset for fine-grained vehicle NER containing 659 ads with 15 entity categories. DeBERTa achieves 90% micro-F1, substantially outperforming rule-based (43%) and LLM (77.8%) baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automotive NER resources are scarce despite vehicle advertisements containing rich specification information. No existing dataset provides fine-grained entity annotation across the diverse specification types found in vehicle listings.

Method: The authors constructed AutoSpecNER, an expert-annotated dataset of 659 vehicle advertisements with 10,000+ entities spanning 15 fine-grained categories (e.g., MODEL, ENGINE_SPEC, BATTERY_CAPACITY). Annotation quality was ensured via inter-annotator agreement (91.5%). They benchmarked three approaches: rule-based extraction, fine-tuned transformer encoders, and large language models.

Result: DeBERTa achieved the highest micro-F1 score of 90%, significantly surpassing the rule-based baseline (43%) and the best-performing LLM (77.8%).

Conclusion: Fine-tuned transformer encoders, particularly DeBERTa, remain superior to both rule-based systems and LLMs for domain-specific fine-grained NER tasks. AutoSpecNER fills a critical resource gap in automotive NER and provides a strong benchmark for future work.

Abstract: Vehicle advertisements contain rich specification information, but automotive NER resources remain limited. We introduce AutoSpecNER, an expert-annotated dataset for fine-grained entity recognition in vehicle listings. The dataset includes 659 advertisements from a popular car-selling website, with over 10,000 entities annotated across 15 categories, including MODEL, ENGINE_SPEC, and BATTERY_CAPACITY. Annotation quality was validated through inter-annotator agreement, achieving an average score of 91.5%. We benchmark rule-based extraction, fine-tuned transformer encoders, and large language models. DeBERTa achieves the best performance with a 90% micro-F1 score, outperforming the rule-based baseline (43%) and the strongest large language model (77.8%).

</details>


### [37] [Beyond Logprobs: A Multi-Signal Confidence Engine for LLM-Based Document Field Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24420)
*Nitesh Kumar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ExtractConf introduces a cross-document confidence estimation framework that uses disagreement between two structurally different LLM extraction calls (Hunter and Mapper) to distinguish trustworthy from untrustworthy field extractions, achieving 0.928 ROC AUC on invoices and enabling reliable human-in-the-loop workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In high-stakes document processing, silently wrong LLM extractions are more dangerous than missing ones. Existing confidence methods (token log-probabilities, verbalized confidence, self-consistency) all exhibit all-positive collapse at practical thresholds, failing to reliably separate trustworthy from untrustworthy extractions. A field-level confidence estimator is needed that works across domains without retraining.

Method: ExtractConf uses two structurally asymmetric extraction calls on the same document: (1) Hunter — a field-guided call that extracts each field under schema-slot completion pressure, prone to hallucinating values for absent fields; (2) Mapper — a document-guided call that scans holistically, prone to missing visually non-salient values. Their disagreement is independently informative. The system fuses cross-call disagreement signals with LLM-internal uncertainty, OCR, image quality, and spatial layout features into a classifier requiring no domain-specific rules or retraining.

Result: On DocILE (55-field invoices, 26% failure rate): 0.928 ROC AUC, 70% reduction in selective prediction risk over logprob-mean, 99.1% accuracy at 80% coverage. Zero-shot transfer to CORD receipts: 0.858 AUC. Lightweight Lasso recalibration reduces ECE by 89% and Brier score by 43%.

Conclusion: Cross-call disagreement between structurally different extraction strategies provides a robust, generalizable signal for field-level confidence estimation. The signals transfer across document domains (invoices to receipts) with minimal recalibration, enabling practical human-in-the-loop deployment without per-domain retraining.

Abstract: In high-stakes document processing pipelines, including financial reconciliation, compliance verification, and procurement automation, an LLM extraction that is silently wrong is more dangerous than one that is visibly absent. The central challenge is not extraction accuracy alone but reliable confidence estimation: knowing, field by field, whether an extraction can be trusted for automation or deferred to human review. Token-level log-probabilities, verbalized confidence, and multi-sample self-consistency all collapse toward all-positive behaviour at practical thresholds, offering no reliable separation between trustworthy and untrustworthy extractions.
  We present ExtractConf, a cross-domain, field-agnostic confidence engine that grounds confidence estimation in two structurally different readings of the same document. A field-guided Hunter call extracts each field under schema-slot completion pressure; a document-guided Mapper call scans holistically and surfaces values grounded in document content. This asymmetry yields different failure modes: Hunter hallucinates values for absent fields, while Mapper misses visually non-salient ones. Their disagreement is independently informative. ExtractConf fuses cross-call disagreement, LLM-internal uncertainty, OCR, image quality, and spatial layout into a classifier requiring no domain-specific rules or retraining. On DocILE (55-field invoices, 26% failure rate), it achieves 0.928 ROC AUC and reduces selective prediction risk by 70% over logprob-mean. At 80% coverage, accuracy reaches 99.1%, enabling a practical human-in-the-loop workflow. Zero-shot transfer to CORD receipts achieves 0.858 AUC; lightweight Lasso recalibration reduces ECE by 89% and Brier by 43%, confirming the signals generalise across document domains.

</details>


### [38] [Escaping the Self-Confirmation Trap: An Execute-Distill-Verify Paradigm for Agentic Experience Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24428)
*Shiding Zhu,Yudi Qi,Yajie Wang,Jiaze Li,Chao Song,Yaorui Shi,Yibo Miao,Hanqi Gao,Kai Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EDV proposes a three-stage framework (Execute-Distill-Verify) that uses multiple heterogeneous agents to collaboratively construct reliable experiences, avoiding the Self-Confirmation Trap where single-agent loops misidentify wrong-but-consistent trajectories as successful. Evaluated on tau2-bench, Mind2Web, and MMTB, EDV consistently outperforms strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent experience learning relies on single-agent loops where the same agent executes tasks, summarizes outcomes, and decides what to remember. This creates a Self-Confirmation Trap: the agent misidentifies internally consistent but objectively wrong trajectories as successful experiences, leading to cumulative errors during memory retrieval and reuse. The authors aim to break this flawed self-reinforcing cycle.

Method: EDV employs three decoupled stages: (1) Execute — multiple heterogeneous agents explore the same task space in parallel, generating diverse candidate trajectories; (2) Distill — a dedicated third-party agent comparatively analyzes these trajectories to produce candidate experiences, reducing executor-centric summarization bias; (3) Verify — the execution group validates candidates via a consensus mechanism, with only approved experiences written into shared or private memory.

Result: EDV consistently outperforms strong baselines across three challenging long-horizon benchmarks: tau2-bench, Mind2Web, and MMTB, demonstrating that reliable experience construction is essential for robust agent self-evolution.

Conclusion: Decoupling execution, distillation, and verification into a collaborative multi-agent framework effectively mitigates the Self-Confirmation Trap. Reliable experience construction through heterogeneous agent consensus is critical for robust and cumulative LLM agent self-improvement in open-world settings.

Abstract: Experience-driven self-evolution is critical for large language model (LLM) agents to improve through open-world interaction. However, existing experience learning methods mostly rely on single-agent loops, where the same agent executes tasks, summarizes outcomes, and determines memory content. This setup makes agents vulnerable to the Self-Confirmation Trap: wrong-but-self-consistent trajectories are misidentified as successful experience, leading to cumulative errors during retrieval and reuse. To address this issue, we propose EDV, an Execute-Distill-Verify framework for reliable experience learning. In the Execute stage, multiple heterogeneous agents explore the same task space in parallel to generate diverse candidate trajectories. In the Distill stage, a dedicated third-party agent comparatively analyzes these trajectories to produce candidate experiences, reducing executor-centric summarization bias. In the Verify stage, the execution group validates candidates via a consensus mechanism, and only approved experiences are written into shared or private memory. By decoupling the three stages, EDV transforms experience learning from isolated self-reflection into collaborative construction, filtering erroneous and noisy content before memory insertion. We evaluate EDV on three challenging long-horizon benchmarks: tau2-bench, Mind2Web and MMTB. Results show EDV consistently outperforms strong baselines, validating that reliable experience construction is essential for robust agent self-evolution. Our code is available at https://github.com/shidingz/EDV.

</details>


### [39] [The African Language Tax: Quantifying the Cost, Latency, and Context Penalty of Tokenizing African Languages in Frontier LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24460)
*Olaoye Anthony Somide*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: African languages face a structural "token fertility" penalty in commercial LLMs, where the same meaning requires more tokens than English. Measured across 20 African languages, the median tokenization premium is 1.88x (GPT-5), reaching up to 8.92x for N'Ko. This translates to up to 8.9x inference cost, equivalent latency multipliers, and as little as 11% effective context window. No current tokenizer eliminates the penalty, though Gemma 4 reduces it. The authors release measurement tools and a leaderboard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While the tokenization penalty for non-English languages is documented generally in multilingual settings, it has never been systematically measured for African languages at the level of enterprise deployment economics (inference cost, latency, effective context capacity). Given that commercial LLMs bill per token, this structural inequity directly encodes a digital divide into subword vocabulary, disproportionately harming speakers who can least afford it.

Method: The authors measure token fertility across 20 African languages from five language families using three scripts (Latin, Ge'ez/Ethiopic, N'Ko). They use parallel corpora (primarily FLORES-200+, with Nigerian Pidgin via MAFAND-MT) to isolate language effects from content. They evaluate 11 frontier and open tokenizers, compute tokenization premium ratios relative to English, measure corpus invariance (FLORES vs SIB-200, Pearson r = 0.9998), and translate token ratios into deployment metrics: inference cost multipliers, latency multipliers, and effective context window percentages.

Result: Every African language carries a tokenization premium above English: median 1.88x on GPT-5/o200k_base, max 8.92x for N'Ko. Ethiopic and N'Ko scripts suffer worst (7-9x). The penalty is near-invariant across corpora. In deployment terms: up to 8.9x inference cost and generation-latency multiplier (N'Ko vs English, GPT-5; 7.4x for Amharic); effective context window drops to 11% of English's. Gemma 4 is the best tokenizer, reducing mean premium from 3.31x (cl100k_base) to 2.38x, but no tokenizer eliminates the penalty.

Conclusion: The tokenization penalty constitutes a structural digital divide encoded at the subword vocabulary level. The burden falls hardest on economically disadvantaged language communities. The authors release afri-fertility (open measurement tool), a public leaderboard, results dataset, and mitigation guidance to support African NLP builders in addressing this inequity.

Abstract: Commercial large language models bill, scale latency, and budget context per token. Yet tokenizers assign more subword tokens to the same meaning in some languages than in others, so speakers of languages with high token-fertility pay a structural penalty before a model is ever invoked. This penalty is documented for multilingual settings in general, but it has not been measured systematically for African languages at the level of enterprise deployment economics and cognitive context capacity. We measure it across 20 African languages spanning five language families and three scripts (Latin, Ge'ez/Ethiopic, N'Ko; 19 appear in the primary FLORES-200+ corpus, with Nigerian Pidgin measured via MAFAND-MT only), using parallel corpora so that the language effect is isolated from content. Across 11 frontier and open tokenizers on FLORES-200+, every African language carries a tokenization premium above English (median 1.88x on GPT-5 / o200k_base, up to 8.92x for N'Ko); the penalty is largest for Ethiopic and N'Ko scripts (reaching 7-9x) and is near-invariant across corpora (FLORES vs SIB-200 Pearson r = 0.9998). Translated into deployment terms, this results in up to 8.9x inference cost and an equivalent generation-latency multiplier (N'Ko vs English on GPT-5; 7.4x for Amharic), and as little as 11% of English's effective context window. The best currently available tokenizer for African languages, Gemma 4, reduces the mean premium from 3.31x (cl100k_base) to 2.38x, but no tokenizer eliminates the penalty. We release an open measurement tool (afri-fertility), a public leaderboard, a results dataset, and mitigation guidance for African builders. The penalty falls hardest on the languages whose speakers can least afford it, a digital divide encoded directly into the subword vocabulary.

</details>


### [40] [UOL@IDEM at BEA 2026 Shared Task 1: Neural Fusion and Feature-Rich Modeling for L1-Aware Vocabulary Difficulty Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24501)
*Nouran Khallaf,Serge Sharoff*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: UOL@IDEM’s closed-track system for the BEA 2026 shared task predicts L1-aware vocabulary difficulty via regression across Spanish, German, and Chinese. It combines multilingual contextual embeddings (BGE-M3, multilingual E5, LaBSE) with engineered features (frequency, form similarity, retrieval, semantic alignment, cognates, MLM predictability), achieving RMSEs of 1.132, 1.037, and 0.891 respectively, with strong ranking but miscalibration on easy items.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting vocabulary difficulty in a second language (L2) must account for the learner’s first language (L1), as cross-linguistic factors like cognate similarity and semantic alignment influence perceived difficulty. The shared task’s closed-track setting constrains model choice, motivating a hybrid approach that enriches strong multilingual representations with linguistically informed engineered features to capture L1-sensitive signals.

Method: The task is cast as regression. Three separate systems are trained for Spanish, German, and Mandarin Chinese. The method fuses multilingual contextual sentence embeddings (BGE-M3, multilingual E5, LaBSE) with handcrafted features spanning: frequency, surface-form properties, retrieval-based evidence, semantic alignment between L1 and L2, cognate similarity, and masked language model (MLM) predictability scores. Feature ablation and error analysis are conducted to assess contribution and calibration quality.

Result: Development results show consistent improvement over the official closed-track baselines. Official RMSE scores: Spanish 1.132, German 1.037, Chinese 0.891. Sentence-embedding encoders (BGE-M3, multilingual E5, LaBSE) prove most effective. Feature analysis reveals frequency as the most stable predictor; contextual predictability, form similarity, retrieval, and semantic features contribute complementary L1-sensitive signals. Error analysis indicates strong ranking performance but systematic overprediction (poor calibration) on the easiest vocabulary items.

Conclusion: Combining multilingual sentence embeddings with linguistically motivated engineered features yields robust L1-aware vocabulary difficulty prediction across typologically diverse languages. While ranking performance is strong, future work should address calibration for low-difficulty items, possibly through post-hoc recalibration or loss functions sensitive to easy-instance overprediction.

Abstract: This paper describes UOL@IDEM's closed-track submission to the BEA 2026 shared task on L1-aware vocabulary difficulty prediction. We model the task as regression and train separate systems for Spanish, German, and Mandarin Chinese\footnote{Below we use \emph{Chinese} for brevity.}. Our system combines multilingual contextual representations with engineered features capturing frequency, surface form, retrieval evidence, semantic alignment, cognate similarity, and masked-language-model predictability. Development results show consistent gains over the official closed-track baselines, with sentence-embedding encoders such as BGE-M3, multilingual E5, and LaBSE performing best. Official submissions achieve RMSE scores of 1.132, 1.037, and 0.891 for Spanish, German, and Chinese, respectively. Feature analysis identifies frequency as the most stable predictor, while contextual predictability, form similarity, retrieval, and semantic features provide complementary L1-sensitive signals. Error analysis shows strong ranking performance but weaker calibration for the easiest items, which are often overpredicted. See https://github.com/Nouran-Khallaf/UoL-IDEM-BEA2026-Vocabulary-Difficulty-Prediction

</details>


### [41] [Poster: Exploring the Limits of Audio-Based Detection of Turkish Phone Call Scams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24523)
*Arda Eren,Micheal Cheung,Youqian Zhang,Grace Ngai,Eugene Yujun Fu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: First public Turkish scam call dataset (100 audio-transcript pairs) used to evaluate 7 LLMs across 3 model families under 3 input conditions, finding transcript-based inputs outperform direct audio and human correction offers little gain, underscoring the need for inclusive, low-resource language AI safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scam phone call detection research has been biased toward high-resource languages like English, leaving low-resource languages such as Turkish vulnerable due to scarce annotated data and limited technological defenses.

Method: The authors introduce a multi-modal dataset of 100 aligned audio-transcript pairs (scam and benign Turkish phone calls). Seven LLMs (Gemini 2.5 Flash/Flash-Lite/Pro, GPT-4o, Qwen Max/Plus/Turbo) are evaluated under three conditions: raw audio input, automatic ASR transcripts, and native-speaker-refined transcripts.

Result: Transcript-based inputs consistently outperform direct audio processing. Human-corrected and uncorrected (ASR) transcripts perform comparably, indicating that manual refinement yields marginal gains over automatic transcription for scam detection.

Conclusion: Culturally and linguistically inclusive AI safety research is urgently needed. Current multi-modal LLMs are not yet robust for low-resource language fraud prevention, and transcript-based pipelines remain the more reliable approach.

Abstract: Scam phone calls exploit vulnerable communities worldwide, yet research on detection has focused almost exclusively on English and other high-resource languages. In low-resource settings such as Turkish, detection is especially difficult, as annotated data is scarce and technological defenses remain limited. This research investigates how large language models (LLMs) can support scam detection in Turkish by introducing the first public multi-modal dataset of 100 aligned audio-transcript pairs of scam and benign conversations. We evaluate seven LLMs spanning three model families: Gemini 2.5 (Flash, Flash-Lite, Pro), GPT-4o, and Qwen (Max, Plus, Turbo), under three input conditions: raw audio, automatic speech-to-text transcripts, and transcripts refined by a native speaker. Our results suggest that transcript-based inputs consistently outperform direct audio processing, while human-corrected and uncorrected transcripts perform comparably. By centering a low-resource language and real world threat, this work highlights the urgent need for culturally and linguistically inclusive AI safety research and more robust multi-modal systems for fraud prevention.

</details>


### [42] [AGORA: An Archive-Grounded Benchmark for Agentic Workplace Document Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24526)
*Honglin Guo,Qi Zhang,Yu Zhang,Weijie Li,Rui Zheng,Zhikai Lei,Qiyuan Peng,Zhiheng Xi,Tao Gui,Qi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Agora is a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on archive-grounded reasoning — finding sparse evidence across large, messy document collections. With 362 questions over 9,664 documents (372M tokens), it forces agents to explore deliberately. The best model achieves only 59.4% accuracy, revealing the task remains largely unsolved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as agents that reason over documents rather than parametric knowledge. Existing benchmarks fail to jointly stress three critical dimensions: archive-groundedness (reasoning over real document collections), agentic exploration (deliberate search rather than exhaustive scanning), and cross-domain coverage. Agora fills this gap with a benchmark that exceeds any model's context window, compelling strategic exploration.

Method: The benchmark is constructed via an agentic pipeline with three stages: (1) cross-document task synthesis to generate questions requiring evidence from multiple documents across a collection; (2) leakage-preventing obfuscation to ensure models cannot exploit memorized parametric knowledge; and (3) difficulty filtering to retain only challenging instances. Agora comprises 362 questions paired with 8 domain collections containing 9,664 authentic workplace documents totaling 372M tokens.

Result: Evaluating eight models on Agora, the strongest model achieves only 59.4% accuracy, with notable performance variation across domains. This indicates the task is far from solved and highlights the challenge of deliberate, agentic exploration over large document archives.

Conclusion: Archive-grounded reasoning remains a significant open challenge for LLM agents. Agora provides a rigorous testbed that exposes weaknesses in current models' ability to strategically navigate and synthesize information from large, heterogeneous document collections, pointing to the need for better exploration and multi-document reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed as agents that reason over documents rather than answer from parametric knowledge. We study archive-grounded reasoning: locating sparse evidence across a large, messy collection of workplace files, reconciling inconsistent terminology, units, and time conventions, and computing an answer. Existing benchmarks address only parts of this setting and none jointly stresses archive-groundedness, agentic exploration, and cross-domain coverage. We introduce Agora, a benchmark pairing 362 questions with eight domain collections of 9,664 authentic documents and 372M tokens, far exceeding any model's context window, so agents must explore deliberately rather than scan exhaustively. Agora is built by an agentic pipeline combining cross-document task synthesis, leakage-preventing obfuscation, and difficulty filtering. Evaluating eight models, we find the task far from solved: even the strongest reaches only 59.4% accuracy, with notable variation across domains.

</details>


### [43] [NatureBench: Can Coding Agents Match the Published SOTA of Nature-Family Papers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24530)
*Yuru Wang,Lejun Cheng,Yuxin Zuo,Sihang Zeng,Bingxiang He,Che Jiang,Junlin Yang,Yuchong Wang,Kaikai Zhao,Weifeng Huang,Kai Tian,Zhenzhao Yuan,Jincheng Zhong,Weizhi Wang,Ning Ding,Bowen Zhou,Kaiyan Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: NatureBench is a cross-discipline benchmark of 90 tasks from Nature-family papers that evaluates whether AI coding agents can perform genuine scientific discovery. The strongest frontier agent surpasses prior SOTA on only 17.8% of tasks, and successes come from methodological translation rather than true invention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior benchmarks for evaluating AI agents on scientific research suffer from environment fragmentation, undermining reproducibility and credibility. There is a need to assess whether agents can go beyond reproducing known results to achieving genuine discovery on real, peer-reviewed scientific problems.

Method: NatureBench comprises 90 tasks distilled from peer-reviewed Nature-family publications across disciplines. It is built using NatureGym, an automated pipeline that creates standardized, per-task containerized environments from source papers, solving environment fragmentation. Ten frontier agent configurations were evaluated under a strict protocol with web search disabled, using the g>0.1 criterion. Method pathway analysis was conducted to understand how agents succeed or fail.

Result: The strongest model surpasses SOTA on only 17.8% of tasks (g>0.1). Agent successes are driven primarily by methodological translation — converting scientific tasks into familiar supervised prediction problems — rather than genuine scientific invention. Failures are dominated by wrong method choice and insufficient compute budget, not task misunderstanding.

Conclusion: Current frontier AI coding agents are far from capable of genuine scientific discovery. The benchmark, pipeline, and leaderboard are released publicly to facilitate further research, with the NatureGym pipeline offering a standardized framework to improve reproducibility in agent-on-research evaluation.

Abstract: We introduce NatureBench, a cross-discipline benchmark of 90 tasks distilled from peer-reviewed Nature-family publications, designed to evaluate whether AI coding agents can move beyond reproduction toward discovery on real scientific problems. NatureBench is built on NatureGym, an automated pipeline that constructs a standardized, per-task containerized environment from a source paper, addressing the environment-fragmentation problem that has limited the credibility of prior agent-on-research benchmarks. Evaluating ten frontier agent configurations under a strict web-search-disabled protocol, we find that the strongest model surpasses SOTA on only 17.8% of tasks under the g>0.1 criterion. Analysis of method pathways reveals that agents succeed primarily through methodological translation, converting scientific tasks into familiar supervised prediction problems, rather than through genuine scientific invention. Failures are dominated by wrong method choice and insufficient compute budget, not by task misunderstanding. We release the benchmark, the NatureGym pipeline, and a public leaderboard with maintainer-side reproduction. Code: https://github.com/FrontisAI/NatureBench

</details>


### [44] [Cross-Lingual Exploration for Parametric Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24579)
*Elisha Diskind,Itamar Trainin,Uri Shaham,Leshem Choshen,Idan Szpektor,Omri Abend*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Cross-lingual prompting strategies significantly improve retrieval of parametric factual knowledge from LLMs across 17 languages, outperforming native-language scaling and enhancing cross-lingual consistency beyond accuracy gains alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parametric knowledge in LLMs is unevenly accessible across languages; standard inference fails to surface localized facts, causing poor cross-lingual knowledge transfer and inconsistency.

Method: The authors identify four dimensions of cross-lingual exploration governing parametric knowledge retrieval and systematically evaluate them using multilingual factual benchmarks spanning 17 typologically diverse languages.

Result: Cross-lingual exploration significantly improves knowledge transfer and factual recall, establishes a more efficient compute Pareto frontier than native-language scaling, and yields cross-lingual consistency gains exceeding what accuracy improvements alone would predict.

Conclusion: Multilingual prompt exploration is a highly effective inference-time strategy for unlocking latent parametric knowledge in LLMs.

Abstract: Parametric knowledge in Large Language Models is not equally accessible across languages. As a result, standard inference techniques often struggle to surface localized facts, leading to failures in cross-lingual knowledge transfer and consistency. In this work, we investigate techniques for accessing hidden factual knowledge by exploring cross-lingual prompting strategies. We identify four inherent dimensions of cross-lingual exploration that directly govern parametric knowledge retrieval and evaluate them on multilingual factual benchmarks covering 17 typologically diverse languages. Our results demonstrate that cross-lingual exploration significantly improves knowledge transfer and factual recall, representing a more efficient compute Pareto frontier than native-language scaling. Furthermore, we observe corresponding improvements in cross-lingual consistency, exceeding what can be explained by accuracy gains alone. Overall, our work establishes multilingual prompt exploration as a highly effective inference-time strategy for unlocking latent parametric knowledge.

</details>


### [45] [MEMPROBE: Probing Long-Term Agent Memory via Hidden User-State Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24595)
*Enze Ma,Yufan Zhou,Wei-Chieh Huang,Jie Yang,Huanhuan Ma,Zixuan Wang,Chengze Li,Chunyu Miao,Philip S. Yu,Zhen Wang*

Main category: cs.CL
