TL;DR: MEMPROBE is a benchmark that evaluates LLM agent long-term memory by directly auditing how well structured user state can be reconstructed from stored memory, rather than indirectly through downstream task performance. Testing 5 memory systems across 50 simulated users with 1,550 hidden user-state dimensions, it reveals that task success and memory recoverability are distinct capabilities: task completion saturates even without memory, while user-state recovery remains moderate (~0.6) and degrades under top-k retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of long-term memory in LLM agents relies on indirect proxies like downstream task success or personalization quality, leaving the memory artifact itself unaudited. The authors argue that memory should be evaluated directly as a structured, auditable post-interaction artifact, enabling measurement of what user state the agent actually retains.

Method: MEMPROBE instantiates memory-equipped agents assisting simulated users, each with a hidden, taxonomy-anchored user-state bank containing 31 dimensions. Agents interact across leak-controlled task trajectories, after which the user-state bank is reconstructed from the resulting memory under both full-store and top-k access conditions. The benchmark spans 50 simulated users (1,550 recovery targets) and evaluates 5 representative memory systems using synthetic ground truth for scalable measurement.

Result: Task completion nearly saturates across all systems, including a memoryless baseline, while category-balanced user-state recovery remains moderate at approximately 0.6 and drops further under top-k retrieval constraints. This demonstrates that successful assistance and recoverable memory are distinct, decoupled capabilities.

Conclusion: MEMPROBE is the first benchmark to directly study memory recovery by reconstructing retained user state against ground truth. The authors propose recovery as a concrete optimization objective for future memory agents, positioning MEMPROBE as a step toward agents that grow more faithful the longer they know their users.

Abstract: Long-term memory promises LLM agents that grow more capable across sessions, maintaining an accurate, evolving understanding of the user that interaction forms. In practice, however, this memory is evaluated mostly through downstream behavior, such as later answers, personalization quality, or task success, which tests that understanding only indirectly and leaves the memory artifact itself largely unaudited. We argue that long-term memory should instead be evaluated as an auditable post-interaction artifact: after ordinary assistance, what structured user state can be reconstructed from the memory the agent leaves behind? We instantiate this view in MEMPROBE, a benchmark in which a memory-equipped agent assists simulated users, each carrying a hidden, taxonomy-anchored user-state bank, across a trajectory of leak-controlled tasks, after which that bank is reconstructed from the agent's resulting memory under both full-store and top-k access. Built on synthetic ground truth for efficient, scalable measurement, MEMPROBE spans 50 simulated users with 31 hidden dimensions each (1,550 recovery targets) and tests 5 representative memory systems. Testing state-of-the-art memory agents, we find that successful assistance and recoverable memory behave as distinct capabilities. Task completion nearly saturates, even for a memoryless baseline, while category-balanced recovery stays moderate (about 0.6) and drops further under top-k retrieval. MEMPROBE is the first benchmark to study memory recovery directly, reconstructing the user state a system retains and scoring it against ground truth. We see recovery as a concrete objective for future memory agents to optimize, and MEMPROBE as a step toward an environment where agents are trained to remember their users, growing more faithful the longer they know them.

</details>


### [46] [To Compare, or Not to Compare: On Methodological Practices in Evaluating Social Bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24596)
*Federico Marcuzzi,Xuefei Ning,Roy Schwartz,Iryna Gurevych*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Current social bias evaluations of LLMs produce contradictory results due to methodological fragmentation. The paper introduces a unified framework that contrasts isolated vs. forced-choice comparative settings, revealing a systematic paradigm gap: comparative settings aggressively catalyze latent discrimination, Chain-of-Thought reasoning amplifies bias rather than mitigating it, and this comparative prejudice scales with model size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The literature on LLM social bias evaluation suffers from methodological fragmentation, leading to contradictory conclusions. The authors identify that benchmark-level structural framing—particularly the distinction between isolated demographic assessments and forced-choice comparative settings—has been largely ignored, confounding results.

Method: A unified, controllable evaluation framework that standardizes heterogeneous benchmarks to systematically contrast two structural paradigms: (1) isolated demographic assessments and (2) forced-choice comparative settings. The framework disentangles the effects of Chain-of-Thought reasoning, neutral fallback options, and other structural artifacts across multiple model families.

Result: A massive paradigm gap was discovered: isolated assessments limit prejudice activation, while comparative settings catalyze latent discrimination. CoT reasoning exacerbates bias under comparative settings. This systemic bias persists even with neutral fallback options or when models claim random answering. The comparative prejudice phenomenon generalizes and scales positively with model size.

Conclusion: Researchers must leverage comparative settings to robustly audit hidden biases, but practitioners cannot safely rely on comparative deployments in ambiguous real-world tasks. This dual guideline reconciles the tension between thorough bias auditing and safe deployment.

Abstract: As Large Language Models are increasingly deployed in critical applications, robustly evaluating their social biases is paramount. However, the current literature suffers from widespread methodological fragmentation, which yields contradictory conclusions. This stems largely from ignoring the structural framing of benchmark-level evaluations. To resolve this, we introduce a unified and controllable framework that standardizes heterogeneous benchmarks to systematically contrast isolated demographic assessments with forced-choice comparative settings. Crucially, this allows us to disentangle the confounding effects of Chain-of-Thought reasoning, neutral fallback options, and other structural artifacts in social bias evaluations. Our evaluation across multiple model families reveals a massive, systematic paradigm gap: while isolated assessments limit prejudice activation, comparative settings act as aggressive catalysts for latent discrimination, a shift primarily driven by underspecified contexts. Alarmingly, CoT reasoning exacerbates social biases under comparative settings, and this systemic bias persists as a deterministic prejudice even when models are provided neutral fallback options or claim to answer randomly. Finally, we demonstrate that this comparative prejudice is a generalized phenomenon that scales positively with model size. Ultimately, we offer a crucial methodological guideline: while researchers must leverage comparative settings to robustly audit hidden biases, practitioners cannot safely rely on comparative deployments in ambiguous real-world tasks.

</details>


### [47] [Qwen-AgentWorld: Language World Models for General Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24597)
*Yuxin Zuo,Zikai Xiao,Li Sheng,Fei Huang,Jianhong Tu,Yuxuan Liu,Tianyi Tang,Xiaomeng Hu,Yang Su,Qingfeng Lan,Yantao Liu,Qin Zhu,Yinger Zhang,Bowen Yu,Haiquan Zhao,Haiyang Xu,Jianxin Yang,Jiayang Cheng,Junyang Wang,Lianghao Deng,Mingfeng Xue,Tianyi Bai,Yang Fan,Yubo Ma,Yucheng Li,Zeyu Cui,Zhihai Wang,Zhihui Xie,Zhuorui Ye,An Yang,Dayiheng Liu,Jingren Zhou,Ning Ding*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Qwen-AgentWorld, a family of language-model-based world models for agentic environment simulation, trained on 10M+ trajectories across 7 domains via a three-stage pipeline (CPT, SFT, RL). It presents AgentWorldBench for evaluation and demonstrates two paradigms: decoupled environment simulation for scalable RL training, and world-model training as effective warm-up for downstream agentic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To push the boundaries of general agents by investigating how world modeling based on language models can serve as a core cognitive mechanism for reasoning and planning, enabling scalable and controllable environment simulation.

Method: A three-stage training pipeline: (1) CPT — injects general-purpose world modeling via state transition dynamics and professional corpora; (2) SFT — activates next-state-prediction reasoning via long chain-of-thought; (3) RL — sharpens simulation fidelity using a hybrid rubric-and-rule reward framework. Models come in 35B-A3B and 397B-A17B sizes. Evaluation uses AgentWorldBench, built from real-world interactions of 5 frontier models on 9 benchmarks.

Result: Qwen-AgentWorld significantly outperforms existing frontier models on AgentWorldBench. As a decoupled simulator, it enables scalable agentic RL that surpasses real-environment-only training. As a unified foundation model, world-model pretraining serves as effective warm-up, improving performance across 7 agentic benchmarks.

Conclusion: Language-model-based world models represent a promising direction for building more capable general agents, both as standalone environment simulators for scalable RL and as foundational pretraining for downstream agentic tasks.

Abstract: A world model predicts environment dynamics based on current observations and actions, serving as a core cognitive mechanism for reasoning and planning. In this work, we investigate how world modeling based on language models can further push the boundaries of general agents. (i) We first focus on building foundation models for agentic environment simulation. We introduce Qwen-AgentWorld-35B-A3B and Qwen-AgentWorld-397B-A17B, the first language world models capable of simulating agentic environments covering 7 domains via long chain-of-thought reasoning. Leveraging more than 10M environment interaction trajectories of 7 domains in real-world environments, we develop Qwen-AgentWorld through a three-stage training pipeline: CPT injects general-purpose world modeling capabilities from the state transition dynamics and augmented professional corpora, SFT activates next-state-prediction reasoning, and RL sharpens simulation fidelity through a tailored framework with hybrid rubric-and-rule rewards. To evaluate language world models, we present AgentWorldBench, a comprehensive benchmark constructed from real-world interactions of 5 frontier models on 9 established benchmarks. Empirical results demonstrate that Qwen-AgentWorld significantly outperforms existing frontier models. (ii) Beyond foundation models, we further investigate two complementary paradigms through which world modeling enhances general agents. First, as a decoupled environment simulator, Qwen-AgentWorld supports scalable and controllable simulation of thousands of real-world environments for agentic RL, yielding gains that surpass real-environment training alone. Second, as a unified agent foundation model, world-model training acts as a highly effective warm-up that improves downstream performance across 7 agentic benchmarks. Code: https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen-AgentWorld

</details>


### [48] [Same Lesson, Different Story: Cross-Lingual Reconstruction of Cultural Narratives in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24610)
*Jory Alshaalan,Haya Albaker,Abeer Aldayel,Aljawharah Alabdullatif,Rehab Alahmadi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study evaluates whether multilingual LLMs preserve culturally grounded meaning when generating narratives from semantically equivalent proverbs across 15 languages. Using 414 proverbs and 13k generated narratives, results show that while semantic meaning is largely preserved, agency, social positioning, and narrative structure are systematically redistributed. Strong inter-model convergence suggests shared semantic abstractions, but the authors caution that relying solely on semantic similarity may overestimate cultural preservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The challenge of evaluating cultural grounding arises when multiple cultures convey the same moral lesson through distinct cultural forms. It remains uncertain whether LLMs preserve culturally grounded meaning when equivalent moral lessons are expressed across languages, motivating a systematic multilingual evaluation.

Method: A multilingual narrative evaluation framework is introduced, integrating a cross-linguistic collection of 414 proverbs spanning 15 languages. Four LLMs generate 13k narratives using semantically equivalent proverbs as culturally grounded prompts. The analysis examines meaning preservation across languages, cross-lingual conditioning effects on narrative realization, and inter-model convergence.

Result: Cross-lingual prompting largely preserves proverb-level semantic meaning but systematically redistributes agency, social positioning, and narrative structure. Strong inter-model convergence occurs in both monolingual and cross-lingual settings, indicating reliance on shared semantic abstractions despite architectural and linguistic differences.

Conclusion: More comprehensive evaluations of cultural grounding are needed. Relying exclusively on semantic similarity in multilingual narrative assessments may overestimate cultural preservation by neglecting culturally meaningful variations in narrative expression.

Abstract: The evaluation of cultural grounding context becomes complex when multiple cultures convey the same moral lesson. This challenge is particularly relevant to large language models (LLMs), which produce narratives across a wide range of languages and cultural contexts. However, it remains uncertain whether these models preserve culturally grounded meaning when equivalent moral lessons are conveyed through distinct cultural forms. This study introduces a multilingual evaluation narrative framework that integrates a cross-linguistic collection of 414 proverbs spanning 15 languages and uses four LLMs to generate 13k narratives. By employing semantically equivalent proverbs as culturally grounded prompts, the analysis assesses whether models preserve meaning across languages, how cross-lingual conditioning influences narrative realization, and whether different model families converge on similar interpretations. Results indicate that cross-lingual prompting largely preserves proverb-level semantic meaning while systematically redistributing agency, social positioning, and narrative structure. Additionally, strong inter-model convergence is observed in both monolingual and cross-lingual settings, suggesting that multilingual LLMs rely on shared semantic abstractions despite architectural and linguistic differences. These findings shed light on the need for more comprehensive evaluations of cultural grounding. Relying exclusively on semantic similarity in multilingual narrative assessments may overestimate cultural preservation by neglecting culturally meaningful variations in narrative expression.

</details>


### [49] [Privacy-Preserving RAG via Multi-Agent Semantic Rewriting: Achieving Confidentiality Without Compromising Contextual Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24623)
*Yuanhe Zhao,Tianyu Zhang,Huafei Xing,Derek F. Wong,Jianbin Li,Tao Fang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A multi-agent framework for privacy-preserving Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) that uses three specialized agents to sanitize retrieved content via semantic rewriting, removing sensitive identifiers while preserving meaning. It reduces privacy leakage dramatically (e.g., from 144 to 1 instance in LLaMA-3-8B) with minimal semantic loss, and operates as an offline preprocessing step with no inference latency overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems enhance LLMs with external knowledge but introduce privacy risks in sensitive deployments, where malicious prompts can extract personally identifiable information (PII) from retrieved documents. Existing mitigation methods either degrade semantic fidelity or add inference latency, motivating a solution that balances privacy protection with contextual preservation.

Method: A multi-agent architecture with three specialized agents: (1) a Privacy Extraction Agent that identifies sensitive identifiers in retrieved content, (2) a Semantic Analysis Agent that captures the core meaning and contextual relationships, and (3) a Reconstruction Agent that rewrites the content by replacing sensitive information while preserving semantics. The framework functions as an asynchronous offline preprocessing module, so rewritten content is cached and served without additional online latency.

Result: Evaluated on ChatDoctor and Wiki-PII datasets across six LLMs, the framework reduced targeted information exposure in LLaMA-3-8B from 144 baseline instances to just 1. It achieved a BLEU-1 score of 0.122, surpassing the SAGE method's 0.117, indicating stronger contextual fidelity. The offline preprocessing design introduced zero additional latency to online inference.

Conclusion: The proposed multi-agent semantic rewriting framework effectively mitigates privacy leakage in RAG systems under targeted attacks while maintaining high semantic fidelity and introducing no inference overhead. The public release of source code supports reproducibility and further research.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation enhances large language models by incorporating external knowledge, but deploying it in sensitive scenarios risks privacy leakage via malicious prompts. To address this, we propose a multi-agent framework that sanitizes retrieved content through semantic rewriting. By employing three specialized agents for privacy extraction, semantic analysis, and reconstruction, our approach collaboratively removes sensitive identifiers while preserving the semantic core. We evaluate the framework on the ChatDoctor and Wiki-PII datasets across six large language models. Experimental results demonstrate a significant reduction in privacy leakage under targeted attacks. For instance, we reduced targeted information exposure in LLaMA-3-8B from 144 instances in the baseline to just 1. Furthermore, we maintain strong contextual fidelity with a BLEU-1 score of 0.122, outperforming the existing SAGE method's 0.117. Finally, the framework operates as an asynchronous preprocessing module, introducing no additional latency to online inference, as all rewriting is executed as a one-time offline preprocessing step. To promote reproducibility, the source code of this work is publicly available at https://github.com/foursoils/Privacy-Preserving-RAG.

</details>


### [50] [The Warrant Gap: Claim-Conditioned Re-scoring for Fact-Checking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24627)
*Arka Ujjal Dey,John Collomosse*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SIFT is a claim-conditioned evidence re-scoring method that, when paired with the WSP metric (an automatic NLI-based check of whether cited warrants entail a claim), improves fact-checking accuracy where naive decomposition fails. WSP strongly correlates with human gold evidence judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM fact-checking systems achieve high label accuracy but often produce "Supports" verdicts where the cited evidence does not actually entail the claim. Structured decomposition can inspect these warrants but standard extraction protocols lose the full-claim context necessary for proper facet evaluation.

Method: SIFT (claim-conditioned re-scoring of extracted evidence spans against the full claim) is paired with WSP (Warranted Supports Proportion), an automatic NLI metric that verifies whether a cited warrant entails the claim. Evaluation spans four open-source backbones across FEVER, SciFact, 5PILS, and DP datasets.

Result: SIFT recovers accuracy on cells where naive decomposition costs up to 27.6 points. It raises WSP above direct prompting baselines. WSP calibrates against human gold evidence with AUC 0.92 and precision 0.98.

Conclusion: SIFT and WSP together address the warrant-quality gap in LLM fact-checking by ensuring cited evidence genuinely supports claims, with WSP serving as a reliable automatic proxy for human evidence-quality judgments.

Abstract: Fact-checking systems built on LLMs achieve high verdict accuracy on standard benchmarks, yet routinely output Supports labels whose cited evidence does not license the claim. Structured decomposition is the natural way to inspect those warrants, but rigid extraction protocols strip the full-claim context that facets need. We introduce SIFT -- claim-conditioned re-scoring of extracted evidence spans against the full claim -- paired with WSP (Warranted Supports Proportion), an automatic NLI check that the cited warrant entails the claim. We evaluate on FEVER, SciFact, 5PILS, and DP across four open-source backbones. SIFT recovers accuracy on cells where naive decomposition costs up to 27.6 points, while raising WSP above direct prompting; WSP itself calibrates against human gold evidence at AUC 0.92 and precision 0.98.

</details>


### [51] [Harmonic: Hierarchical State Space Models for Efficient Long-Context Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24650)
*Petr Nyoma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Harmonic is a hierarchical SSM that stacks three recurrent layers at different timescales, where each level receives the prediction error (not hidden state) from the level below. It outperforms Transformers and Mamba on long-sequence language modeling, scales to 1B params by replacing attention in TinyLlama, and eliminates RoPE positional encoding limits while maintaining O(L) complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers scale quadratically O(L²) with sequence length, limiting long-context modeling. Existing SSMs like Mamba are efficient but underperform on very long sequences. The authors aim to build a recurrent architecture that matches or exceeds Transformer quality while maintaining linear complexity, and that can natively handle arbitrary-length sequences without positional encoding constraints like RoPE.

Method: Harmonic uses three hierarchically stacked recurrent SSM levels operating at progressively slower timescales. Critically, each level receives the prediction error of the level below rather than raw hidden states — a predictive coding / error-feedback mechanism. At 1B scale, the authors replace all attention layers in TinyLlama 1.1B with HarmonicBlock, producing "Hallamonic," which removes RoPE positional encoding entirely.

Result: On enwiki8 (28M params): Harmonic beats Transformer by +1.4% at 1K, +6.7% at 8K, +11.4% at 32K tokens (bpt). It beats Mamba by 0.7–1.8% at every length. At 64K tokens, Transformer and Mamba OOM on 80GB H100; Harmonic trains successfully (6.169 bpt). Results replicate on WikiText-103. At 1B scale, Hallamonic maintains stable loss across 1K–8K on Lambada and fineweb-edu, while TinyLlama degrades catastrophically past its 2K RoPE limit (+9.4 bpt gap at 8K on Lambada).

Conclusion: Hierarchical SSMs with prediction-error gating (Harmonic) offer a compelling alternative to attention: linear complexity, superior long-sequence performance, and freedom from positional encoding limits. The architecture scales from 28M to 1B parameters and can serve as a drop-in replacement for attention layers in existing LMs.

Abstract: We present Harmonic, a hierarchical state space model (SSM) for language modeling. The architecture stacks three recurrent levels at progressively slower timescales; each level receives the prediction error of the level below as input, rather than its raw hidden state. On enwiki8 with equal token budgets, Harmonic outperforms a comparable Transformer (28M params) by +1.4% at 1K tokens, +6.7% at 8K tokens, and +11.4% at 32K tokens (bpt, lower is better). It also outperforms Mamba at every tested length by 0.7--1.8%. At 64K tokens, both Mamba and Transformer run out of memory on an 80GB H100; Harmonic trains successfully, reaching 6.169 bpt. Results replicate on WikiText-103 (H-TF gap +1.7% to +7.2% across 1K--32K). At 1B parameter scale, replacing all attention layers in TinyLlama 1.1B with HarmonicBlock eliminates the RoPE positional encoding limit: the resulting Hallamonic model maintains stable loss across sequence lengths 1K--8K on two independent clean benchmarks (Lambada and fineweb-edu held-out), while TinyLlama degrades catastrophically past its 2K-token RoPE limit (gap: +9.4 bpt at seq=8K on Lambada). Compute is O(L) per forward pass vs. O(L^2) for attention.
  Logs: https://github.com/Omibranch/harmonic-logs.

</details>


### [52] [AI-PAVE-Br: Leveraging Large Language Models for Enhanced Product Attribute Value Extraction through a Golden Set Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24655)
*Murilo Gazzola,Hugo Gobato Souto,Samuel Silva,Júlia Schubert Peixoto,Felipe Siqueira,André Luis Pedroso de Morais,Caio Gomes*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces AI-PAVEBr, an LLM-based system for Product Attribute Value Extraction tailored to Brazilian Portuguese e-commerce, along with a manually annotated benchmark dataset called the Golden Set. The system significantly outperforms traditional NER baselines via targeted prompt engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Brazilian e-commerce generates massive, complex product data, but existing PAVE methods fail to handle Portuguese linguistic nuances and diverse product descriptions. There is a lack of specialized tools and reproducible benchmarks for this non-English market.

Method: The authors develop AI-PAVEBr, a system using Large Language Models with targeted prompt engineering for PAVE. They also construct the Golden Set — a manually annotated dataset organized by Entity, Category, and Subcategories — to serve as a benchmark for Portuguese PAVE tasks.

Result: AI-PAVEBr, through carefully designed prompting strategies, dramatically outperforms conventional Named Entity Recognition baselines on the Golden Set benchmark.

Conclusion: The work delivers a superior, scalable PAVE solution for the Brazilian e-commerce market and contributes a publicly available, high-quality annotated dataset to the NLP community, advancing reproducible research in non-English information extraction.

Abstract: The explosive growth and complexity of product data within the dynamic Brazilian e-commerce landscape demand robust and specialized methods for structured information extraction. Traditional approaches to Product Attribute Value Extraction (PAVE) often struggle with the linguistic nuances and sheer diversity of product descriptions in Portuguese. To address this critical gap, this paper introduces two major contributions. First, we present AI-PAVEBr, a specialized system engineered with Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform high-accuracy PAVE specifically for Brazilian e-commerce catalogs. Second, to facilitate reproducible research and provide a definitive benchmark, we introduce and share the Golden Set, a new, meticulously curated, and manually annotated dataset for PAVE in Portuguese. We detail the creation process and structure (Entity, Category, Subcategories) of this high-quality reference set. Our experiments conclusively show that AI-PAVE-Br, leveraging targeted prompt engineering, dramatically outperforms conventional Named Entity Recognition (NER) baselines. This work not only delivers a superior, scalable solution for a major non-English market but also enriches the NLP community with a valuable, publicly available resource for future PAVE research.

</details>


### [53] [DREAM: Dense Retrieval Embeddings via Autoregressive Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24667)
*Yixuan Tang,Yi Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DREAM trains dense retrieval models by leveraging an LLM's next-token prediction loss as a supervisory signal: retriever-generated similarity scores modulate attention in a frozen LLM, and the resulting prediction loss backpropagates gradients to the retriever, outperforming baselines on BEIR and RTEB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional contrastive training of dense retrievers requires expensive labeled positive/negative document pairs. The authors explore whether the autoregressive next-token prediction objective of LLMs can serve as an alternative supervision source, since relevant documents should make target output prediction easier.

Method: DREAM injects query-document similarity scores (from the retriever) into selected attention heads of a frozen LLM. These scores weight how much attention each candidate document receives. The LLM's next-token prediction loss on the target output is then backpropagated through the attention mechanism to provide gradients for training the retriever, without updating the LLM itself.

Result: DREAM consistently outperforms existing baselines on BEIR and RTEB retrieval benchmarks across embedding backbones ranging from 0.5B to 3B parameters.

Conclusion: Autoregressive modeling via LLM next-token prediction loss is a viable and promising alternative to contrastive objectives for training dense retrievers, removing the need for explicit positive/negative pair labeling.

Abstract: Dense retrieval embedding models are a fundamental component of modern retrieval-based AI systems. Most dense retrievers are trained with contrastive objectives, which require labeled positive and negative document pairs that are often costly and difficult to obtain. In this work, we investigate whether the autoregressive next-token prediction objective of a large language model (LLM) can provide supervision for dense retrieval. The intuition is simple: if a document contains information relevant to a query, conditioning on that document should make the target output easier for the LLM to predict. A key challenge is that the next-token prediction loss is computed inside the LLM, while the retriever is a separate embedding model. To address this challenge, we propose DREAM (Dense Retrieval Embeddings via Autoregressive Modeling), which injects retriever-generated query-document similarity scores into selected attention heads of a frozen LLM. During training, these scores determine how much attention each candidate document receives while the LLM predicts the target output. The resulting prediction loss provides gradients for retriever training through the attention mechanism. We evaluate DREAM on retrieval benchmarks BEIR and RTEB using embedding backbones ranging from 0.5B to 3B parameters. DREAM consistently outperforms existing baselines across different model scales. These results demonstrate that DREAM provides a promising approach for training dense retrievers through autoregressive modeling.

</details>


### [54] [CN-NewsTTS Bench: a target-level automatic benchmark for raw-input Chinese news TTS pronunciation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24714)
*Shijun Luo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CN-NewsTTS Bench is a benchmark for evaluating Chinese news TTS systems on correct pronunciation of dense written forms (scores, abbreviations, mixed-script names, etc.) directly from raw text. The best of seven tested systems achieves 0.879 strict accuracy; several fall below 0.60.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chinese news text frequently contains written forms (scores, model names with hyphens, ranges, unit symbols, percentages, abbreviations, mixed Chinese-Latin-digit names) where preserving the written string in speech can alter meaning. Existing TTS evaluation lacks a targeted benchmark for this problem in raw-text scenarios, without relying on SSML hints, LLM rewriting, or manual edits.

Method: The authors introduce CN-NewsTTS Bench v0.1, comprising a 200-record development set, an 800-record public test set, and 992 public auto-evaluable targets. They employ a three-ASR ensemble to produce fixed transcripts, an automatic target scorer, and evaluate seven product TTS systems. Additional diagnostics include ASR-route analysis, ASR-subset ablations, category-level results, confidence intervals, and provider configuration metadata.

Result: The best-performing TTS system achieved 0.879 strict accuracy on target pronunciation. Several systems scored below 0.60, indicating substantial room for improvement. The paper also provides detailed breakdowns by category, ASR diagnostics, and system configuration comparisons.

Conclusion: Chinese news TTS systems exhibit widely varying capability in correctly rendering dense written forms from raw text. The benchmark establishes a reproducible, open framework for measuring and improving this aspect of TTS quality, highlighting that even the best system still makes errors on over 12% of targets.

Abstract: Chinese news text contains dense written forms such as scores, hyphenated model names, ranges, unit symbols, percentages, English abbreviations, and mixed Chinese-Latin-digit names. These forms are frequent in real listening workflows, and a text-to-speech (TTS) system can preserve the written string while changing the spoken meaning. We introduce CN-NewsTTS Bench v0.1, an open target-level benchmark for evaluating whether Chinese news TTS products pronounce such targets correctly from raw text, without user-side rules, LLM rewriting, SSML hints, or manual edits. The release contains a 200-record development set, an 800-record public test set, 992 public auto-evaluable targets, fixed transcripts from a three-ASR ensemble, an automatic target scorer, and initial results for seven product TTS systems. We additionally report ASR-route diagnostics, ASR-subset ablations, category-level results, confidence intervals, and provider configuration metadata. The best system reaches 0.879 strict accuracy, while several systems remain below 0.60.

</details>


### [55] [Self-Recognition Finetuning can Prevent and Reverse Emergent Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23700)
*Arush Tagade,Shaoheng Zhou,Jiaxin Wen,Shi Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Emergent misalignment (EM) arises from destabilizing a model's aligned character rather than learning harmful content. Self-generated text recognition (SGTR) finetuning, a character-targeted defense, effectively prevents EM by fortifying aligned character, while both SGTR and benign finetuning can reverse EM when restoring degraded capabilities. Additional evidence—identity self-report diversity, self-recognition corruption, and system prompt removal—supports EM as aligned-character destabilization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work suggests EM operates via disruption of aligned character (activating misaligned persona vectors) rather than direct harmful content learning. This motivates investigating whether character-targeted interventions—specifically SGTR finetuning—can defend against EM, and whether EM is better understood as character destabilization rather than adoption of a coherent misaligned persona.

Method: Two-stage finetuning experiments across three models (GPT-4.1, Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, Seed-OSS-36B-Instruct) on multiple EM datasets. SGTR finetuning is compared against benign finetuning baselines (domain-specific data, general knowledge, word counting) in both reversal and prevention settings. Additional experiments: measuring identity self-report diversity after EM finetuning, corrupting self-recognition to test exacerbation of misalignment, and removing identity-bearing system prompts to observe EM effect reduction.

Result: All interventions produce comparable EM reversal, but only when restoring EM-degraded capabilities. For prevention, only SGTR finetuning consistently reduces misalignment without worsening any individual metric, indicating character fortification drives prevention. EM finetuning induces diversity in identity self-reports; artificially corrupting self-recognition exacerbates EM misalignment; removing the identity-bearing system prompt substantially reduces EM finetuning's effect.

Conclusion: EM should be reframed not as adoption of a coherent misaligned persona but as destabilization of the model's aligned character. Character-targeted defenses like SGTR finetuning are effective specifically for prevention by fortifying aligned character.

Abstract: Emergent misalignment (EM) has been linked to the activation of misaligned persona vectors and evil character traits, suggesting that EM operates through disruption of the model's aligned character rather than direct learning of harmful content. Motivated by this connection, we study self-generated text recognition (SGTR) finetuning as a character-targeted intervention that is distinct from existing in-training defenses. We conduct two-stage finetuning experiments across three models (GPT-4.1, Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, Seed-OSS-36B-Instruct) and multiple EM datasets to compare SGTR finetuning against benign finetuning baselines (correct domain-specific data, general knowledge, and word counting) to find it an effective defense in both reversal and prevention settings. We find that all interventions produce comparable EM reversal, but only when restoring capabilities that EM had degraded. For prevention, only SGTR finetuning consistently reduces misalignment without exacerbating any individual metric, suggesting that character fortification specifically drives prevention. We provide further evidence for EM's relation to the LLM's default character by showing that EM finetuning induces diversity into the LLM's identity self-reports, artificially corrupting self-recognition exacerbates misalignment caused by EM finetuning, and that removing the model's identity-bearing system prompt substantially reduces the effect of EM finetuning. Together, these findings reframe EM not as the adoption of a coherent misaligned persona but as the destabilization of aligned character.

</details>


### [56] [CANDLE: Character-level Arabic Noise Deduplication using Lightweight Encoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24758)
*Faris Alasmary,Taif Nono,Orjuwan Zaafarani,Kholood Al Tabash,Ahmad Ghannam,Anas Salamah,Shouq Sadah,Lahouari Ghouti*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CANDLE is a lightweight CTC-based system for Arabic character-level deduplication that frames normalization as sequence alignment, achieving strong SER (5.37%) without handcrafted rules, with a distilled 2-layer variant that cuts depth by 3× while reducing LLM tokenizer fertility by up to 12.8%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Repeated characters in Arabic text pose a disambiguation challenge: they can indicate either correct orthography (e.g., gemination) or informal elongation common in social media. Existing solutions rely on handcrafted rules, dictionaries, or morphological analyzers, which are brittle and language-specific. The authors aim for a lightweight, learning-based alternative that requires no such resources.

Method: The core contribution is a novel application of Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) to character deduplication, reformulating normalization as a sequence alignment problem over a character-level encoder. A 6-layer CTC model is trained and evaluated. Knowledge distillation compresses it into a 2-layer student (3× depth reduction). The downstream impact is measured via tokenizer fertility reduction across multiple Arabic LLM tokenizers.

Result: The CTC model achieves a Sentence Error Rate as low as 5.37% and consistently outperforms a classification baseline by large margins across three benchmarks (clean newspaper, manually curated ambiguous cases, real-world social media). The distilled 2-layer model matches performance with minimal degradation. Downstream, normalization yields up to 12.8% relative reduction in tokenizer fertility across diverse Arabic LLM tokenizers.

Conclusion: CTC is a highly effective formulation for character-level Arabic deduplication, outperforming classification baselines while remaining lightweight and distillation-friendly. The practical benefit of reduced tokenizer fertility directly lowers inference costs and improves context window utilization for Arabic LLMs. Code and models are publicly released.

Abstract: Handling repeated characters in text can be tricky, since they can represent either the correct spelling of a word or informal character elongation often seen in social media posts. We present CANDLE, a lightweight system for character-level Arabic noise deduplication that addresses this challenge without relying on handcrafted rules, dictionaries, or morphological analyzers. At the heart of CANDLE is a novel application of Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) to this task, a formulation not previously explored for character deduplication, which frames normalization as a sequence alignment problem over a character-based encoder. Evaluated on three benchmarks spanning clean newspaper, manually curated ambiguous cases, and real-world social media text, the CTC model achieves a Sentence Error Rate (SER) as low as $5.37\%$ and consistently outperforms a classification-based baseline by a large margin. To reduce inference overhead, we distill the 6-layer CTC model into a 2-layer student, achieving a $3\times$ depth reduction with minimal performance degradation. Beyond deduplication accuracy, normalization yields a practical downstream benefit: a relative reduction in tokenizer fertility of up to $12.8\%$ across a diverse set of Arabic LLM tokenizers, directly lowering inference costs and improving context window utilization. We release all code and models publicly to support reproducibility and advance future research\footnote{https://github.com/abjadai/candle}.

</details>


### [57] [Posterior Refinement: Fast Language Generation via Any-Order Flow Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24773)
*Manan Agarwal,Sheel Shah,Chanhyuk Lee,Jaehoon Yoo,Jerry Huang,Seunghoon Hong,Aditi Raghunathan,Jinwoo Kim,Nicholas M. Boffi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FMLM+ extends Flow Map Language Models with masking-style noise schedules, enabling Posterior Refinement: a single-step generation with a posteriori token-level consistency scoring that allows adaptive self-correction, matching discrete baselines with 32x fewer NFEs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing non-autoregressive models face a tradeoff: Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs) offer inference-time flexibility but suffer from factorization error and sample collapse; Flow Map Language Models (FMLMs) achieve excellent few-step generation via joint sequence transport but sacrifice flexible refinement. The gap is a framework that combines both strengths.

Method: FMLM+ equips FMLM with masking-style noise schedules. During single-step full-sequence generation, it simultaneously produces a posteriori global consistency scores for each token. This enables Posterior Refinement, an inference-time strategy where the model adaptively self-corrects its outputs based on these scores, iteratively erasing and regenerating low-confidence tokens.

Result: FMLM+ with Posterior Refinement matches the performance of discrete baselines using 32x fewer NFEs (number of function evaluations). It improves the speed-quality tradeoff over both MDM and FMLM families across diverse benchmarks.

Conclusion: FMLM+ bridges the gap between MDMs and FMLMs, combining joint sequence transport with flexible inference-time refinement. It provides a scalable foundation for high-fidelity non-autoregressive language modeling.

Abstract: Non-autoregressive generation offers a powerful paradigm for iterative refinement, allowing models to recursively critique, erase and regenerate arbitrary subsets of tokens. However, existing non-autoregressive models fail to realize this potential. Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs) suffer from factorization error, causing sample quality to collapse when generating multiple tokens simultaneously. Flow Map Language Models (FMLMs) circumvent this bottleneck via joint sequence transport for excellent few-step generation, but sacrifice the inference-time flexibility of MDMs. We introduce FMLM+, a framework that bridges this gap by equipping FMLM with masking-style noise schedules. While generating the full sequence in a single step, FMLM+ simultaneously scores the global consistency of each token a posteriori. We leverage this to introduce Posterior Refinement, a novel inference-time refinement strategy that enables the model to adaptively self-correct its outputs, matching the performance of discrete baselines with 32x fewer NFEs. Across diverse benchmarks, we demonstrate that FMLM+ with Posterior Refinement improves the speed--quality tradeoff over both MDM and FMLM families, providing a scalable foundation for high-fidelity language modeling.

</details>


### [58] [Are We Ready For An Agent-Native Memory System?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24775)
*Wei Zhou,Xuanhe Zhou,Shaokun Han,Hongming Xu,Guoliang Li,Zhiyu Li,Feiyu Xiong,Fan Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A systematic study decomposing LLM agent memory into four modules (representation/storage, extraction, retrieval/routing, maintenance), evaluating 12 systems across 11 datasets. Findings: no single architecture dominates; effectiveness depends on alignment with workload bottlenecks; localized maintenance is more cost-efficient than global reorganization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent memory evaluations treat memory systems as black boxes using end-to-end metrics (F1, BLEU), neglecting critical system-level concerns: operational costs, architectural trade-offs across memory modules, and robustness under dynamic knowledge updates.

Method: Proposes an analytical framework decomposing agent memory into four modules: memory representation and storage, extraction, retrieval and routing, and maintenance. Evaluates 12 representative memory systems and two baselines across five benchmark workloads (11 datasets) using both end-to-end and fine-grained ablation studies.

Result: No single architecture dominates all scenarios — effectiveness depends on memory structure alignment with workload bottlenecks. Ablation studies quantify individual module effects on representation fidelity, retrieval precision, update correctness, and long-horizon stability. Localized maintenance proves more cost-efficient than global reorganization under realistic workloads.

Conclusion: The paper identifies promising directions for building agent-native memory systems and emphasizes the need for data-management-oriented evaluation beyond black-box metrics. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Memory for large language model (LLM) agents has rapidly evolved from simple retrieval-augmented mechanisms into a data management system that supports persistent information storage, retrieval, update, consolidation, and dynamic lifecycle governance throughout agent execution. Despite this evolution, existing evaluations still benchmark agent memory mainly through end-to-end task success metrics (e.g., F1, BLEU), while treating the underlying system as a monolithic black box. As a result, critical system-level concerns, including operational costs, architectural trade-offs across memory modules, and robustness under dynamic knowledge updates, remain insufficiently explored. In this paper, we present a systematic experimental study of agent memory from a data management perspective. We propose an analytical framework that decomposes agent memory into four core modules: memory representation and storage, extraction, retrieval and routing, and maintenance. Under this framework, we evaluate 12 representative memory systems and two reference baselines across five benchmark workloads spanning 11 datasets. Our extensive end-to-end evaluation shows that no single architecture dominates across all scenarios; instead, effectiveness depends heavily on how well the memory structure aligns with the workload bottleneck. Furthermore, through fine-grained ablation studies, we quantify their individual effects on representation fidelity, retrieval precision, update correctness, and long-horizon stability. Finally, we reveal cost-performance trade-offs under realistic workloads, showing localized maintenance is more cost-efficient than global reorganization. Based on these findings, we identify promising directions towards building truly agent-native memory systems. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/OpenDataBox/MemoryData.

</details>


### [59] [Paying to Know: Micro-Transaction Markets for Verified Product Information in Agentic E-Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24783)
*Filippos Ventirozos,Matthew Shardlow*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that agent-native micropayment infrastructure shifts the core NLP challenge in e-commerce from recommendation/conversion to cost-optimal acquisition of verified, trustworthy product information, and outlines a market architecture along with key NLP research problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The arrival of autonomous buyer agents and micropayment rails (x402, AP2) fundamentally changes scarcity in e-commerce: exhaustive search is cheap, but trustworthy, decision-relevant product information is not. Current commercial NLP treats shopping chatbots as recommenders or conversion funnels, which addresses the wrong bottleneck under this new paradigm.

Method: The authors sketch an architecture for an agentic e-commerce market where buyer agents make micro-transactions (fractions of a cent) to progressively unlock verified seller- and reviewer-supplied data — service histories, test reports, bills of materials, audited metrics — under a freemium model with reputational scoring of reviewer trustworthiness. They then translate this vision into concrete NLP research problems: cost-optimal information acquisition, data pricing and negotiation, real-time entity resolution, grounded value exchange, and privacy-preserving persona modelling.

Result: The paper is primarily a position/vision paper and does not present empirical results. It offers a framework identifying five concrete NLP problem areas that would become critical under agentic e-commerce with micropayments, and argues these deserve the field's attention over chat fluency.

Conclusion: Agentic e-commerce with micropayment infrastructure rewards genuine product quality and enables truer competition than ranking-based storefronts. The NLP community should pivot from optimizing conversational fluency toward the harder problems of information verification, pricing, negotiation, entity resolution, value grounding, and privacy-preserving user modeling.

Abstract: Commercial NLP treats the shopping chatbot as a recommender or a conversion tool: its job is to match a user to a catalogue entry and close a sale. We argue that the arrival of agent-native micro-payment rails (e.g., x402, AP2) changes what is scarce. When the buyer is an autonomous agent that can investigate exhaustively, the bottleneck is no longer matching products but acquiring trustworthy, decision-relevant information about them. We envision agentic e-commerce as a micro-transaction market for verified information: buyer agents spend fractions of a cent to progressively unlock seller- and reviewer-supplied data -- service histories, third-party test reports, bills of materials, audited sales and support metrics -- paid for a la carte under a freemium model, with reviewer trust scored reputationally. We sketch the architecture of such a market and argue that it rewards genuine product quality and yields truer competition than ranking-based storefronts. We then translate the vision into concrete NLP problems -- cost-optimal information acquisition, data pricing and negotiation, real-time entity resolution, grounded value exchange, and privacy-preserving persona modelling -- and argue that these, not chat fluency, deserve the field's attention.

</details>


### [60] [SHERLOC: Structured Diagnostic Localization for Code Repair Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24820)
*Hovhannes Tamoyan,Sean Narenthiran,Erik Arakelyan,Mira Mezini,Boris Ginsburg*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SHERLOC is a training-free framework that uses a reasoning LLM with compact repository tools and self-recovery for fault localization. It achieves SOTA accuracy@1 (84.33% on SWE-Bench Lite) and recall@1 (81.27% on Verified), and when its diagnostic output is fed to repair agents, resolve rate improves by +5.95 pp while reducing localization tokens by 36.7%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agents waste ~50% of their budget on fault localization before editing, and existing localization frameworks are evaluated only as file retrieval rather than producing actionable diagnostic context that repair agents need.

Method: SHERLOC (Structured Hypothesis-driven Exploration and Reasoning for Localization) is a training-free framework that pairs a reasoning LLM with compact repository tools and a self-recovery mechanism. It requires no fine-tuning or multi-agent orchestration.

Result: SHERLOC achieves 84.33% accuracy@1 on SWE-Bench Lite and 81.27% recall@1 on SWE-Bench Verified. At ~30B parameters it matches or surpasses other agentic methods. Injecting its locations and diagnostic findings into repair agents yields +5.95 pp resolve rate on average while cutting localization tokens by 36.7% and total tokens by 23.1%.

Conclusion: SHERLOC demonstrates that structured, hypothesis-driven reasoning with lightweight tooling can outperform larger agentic systems for fault localization, and that providing diagnostic context—not just file locations—significantly boosts downstream repair performance.

Abstract: LLM agents solve repository-level coding tasks through multi-turn tool use, but utilize half their budget on locating faults before editing. Dedicated localization frameworks have emerged, yet are still evaluated as file retrieval rather than actionable diagnosis, producing locations without the diagnostic context a repair agent needs. We introduce SHERLOC (Structured Hypothesis-driven Exploration and Reasoning for Localization), a training-free framework pairing a reasoning LLM with compact repository tools and self-recovery, without fine-tuning or multi-agent orchestration. SHERLOC reaches state-of-the-art localization across model scales: 84.33% accuracy@1 on SWE-Bench Lite and 81.27% recall@1 on SWE-Bench Verified; at ~30B parameters, it matches or outperforms other agentic methods. Injecting our locations and diagnostic findings into repair agents yields, on average, +5.95 pp resolve rate on SWE-Bench Verified while cutting localization and total tokens by 36.7% and 23.1%.

</details>


### [61] [L3Cube-MahaPOS: A Marathi Part-of-Speech Tagging Dataset and BERT Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24825)
*Hariom Ingle,Ronit Ghode,Ishwari Gondkar,Jidnyasa Harad,Raviraj Joshi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces L3Cube-MahaPOS, a gold-standard Marathi POS tagging dataset (32,354 manually annotated sentences, 16 UD-aligned tags), along with benchmarks across six model families, achieving 88.67% accuracy with MahaBERT-v2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Marathi (83M+ speakers) is severely under-resourced in annotated corpora for POS tagging, with unique challenges: rich morphology, free word order, no capitalization cues, and pervasive Hindi-English code-mixing. No standardised gold-standard dataset existed prior.

Method: Manual annotation of 32,354 news-domain sentences by Marathi-proficient annotators using a 16-tag Universal Dependencies-aligned scheme. Preprocessing includes Unicode normalization, Devanagari-aware tokenization, and noise filtering. Benchmarking across six model families: HMM, CRF, BiLSTM, BiLSTM+CharCNN, MuRIL, and MahaBERT-v2.

Result: Best system (MahaBERT-v2, a Marathi-specific transformer) achieves 88.67% token-level accuracy and 81.67% macro-F1 across 15 evaluated tag classes. All models are compared, with neural and transformer-based approaches substantially outperforming statistical baselines.

Conclusion: The dataset and benchmarks establish a strong foundation for Marathi POS tagging research. All resources — dataset, annotation guidelines, and model checkpoints — are publicly released to advance low-resource Indic NLP.

Abstract: Part-of-Speech (POS) tagging is a foundational NLP task underpinning machine translation, information extraction, and syntactic parsing. Despite Marathi being spoken by over 83 million people and ranking among the top twenty most spoken languages worldwide, it remains severely under-resourced in annotated corpora and standardised evaluation benchmarks. Marathi presents unique challenges for computational modelling owing to its rich morphology, relatively free word order, lack of capitalisation conventions, and pervasive code-mixing with Hindi and English. We introduce L3Cube-MahaPOS, a gold-standard POS tagging dataset for Marathi comprising 32,354 manually annotated sentences drawn from news text. Annotation was performed entirely manually by a team of Marathi-proficient annotators following a 16-tag Universal Dependencies-aligned scheme. A structured preprocessing pipeline covering Unicode normalisation, Devanagari-aware tokenisation, and noise filtering ensures label consistency across all splits. We benchmark the dataset across six model families spanning HMM, CRF, BiLSTM, BiLSTM+CharCNN, MuRIL, and the Marathi-specific transformer MahaBERT-v2. The best system achieves 88.67\% token-level accuracy and a macro-F1 of 81.67% over 15 evaluated tag classes. We release the dataset, annotation guidelines, and trained model checkpoints to foster further research in Marathi NLP.

</details>


### [62] [Less is More: Quality-Aware Training Data Selection for Scientific Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24828)
*Maria Nefeli Paraskevopoulou,Tatiana Passali,Grigorios Tsoumakas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a large-scale biomedical summarization dataset (1.88M PMC articles) and demonstrates that filtering training data by reference quality (author-written abstract alignment with the source) improves summarization model performance and training efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing scientific summarization datasets suffer from two key problems: (1) author-written abstracts used as gold references vary widely in quality and alignment with the full article, and (2) publicly available datasets are too small and unstructured for modern long-context models.

Method: The authors construct a dataset of 1.88M PubMed Central articles with author-written abstracts. They then analyze reference quality using source-grounded metrics (e.g., factuality/alignment measures) and model-based metrics. These quality signals are used to select high-quality training subsets. Models are trained on quality-filtered subsets and compared against random sampling at matched training sizes.

Result: Training on quality-selected subsets outperforms random sampling at matched sizes and can match or exceed larger random subsets on factuality-oriented metrics. Author-written abstracts exhibit significant variance in their alignment with full articles.

Conclusion: Reference quality is a critical factor in scientific long-document summarization. Quality-aware data selection improves training efficiency, enabling smaller but higher-quality training sets to rival or surpass larger randomly sampled datasets.

Abstract: Scientific long-document summarization datasets commonly treat author-written abstracts as gold reference summaries, although their quality and alignment with the source article vary. At the same time, publicly available scientific summarization datasets remain limited in scale and structure for modern long-context models. In this work, we address both challenges by a) constructing and releasing one of the largest biomedical and life science datasets for long-document summarization, containing 1.88 million PMC articles, and b) analyzing the reference quality of author-written abstracts with source-grounded and model-based metrics. We show that author-written abstracts vary in their alignment with the full article and that these quality signals can guide training-data selection. Training on selected high-quality subsets outperforms random sampling at matched training sizes and can match or exceed larger random subsets on factuality-oriented metrics. Our findings suggest that reference quality is an important factor in scientific summarization and that quality-aware data selection can improve training efficiency.

</details>


### [63] [Quantifying Prior Dominance in RAG Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23695)
*Barak Or*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Normalized Context Utilization (NCU) metric to measure how well RAG models extract information from context versus relying on parametric memory. It finds that smaller language models often match or outperform larger ones in factual extraction tasks, and that larger/commercial models exhibit "Prior Dominance" — overriding external evidence with parametric priors, sometimes suffering from confidence collapse (Negative Transfer) when contradicted.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG evaluations rely on discrete heuristics that suffer from "epistemic blindness" — they cannot distinguish whether a model's correct answer stems from genuine contextual information extraction or from parametric memory recall. This makes it impossible to rigorously assess a model's true contextual adherence.

Method: The authors propose the Normalized Context Utilization (NCU) metric, which uses continuous token log-probabilities under three conditions: zero-shot (no context), oracle (gold-standard context), and adversarial (contradictory context). NCU strictly quantifies contextual information gain by isolating the effect of provided context from the model's parametric priors. They evaluate open-source models (1.5B–72B parameters) and a proprietary commercial API on strict factual extraction tasks without Chain-of-Thought reasoning.

Result: For strict factual extraction, traditional scaling laws show extreme diminishing returns: efficient SLMs match or outperform high-capacity architectures. "Prior Dominance" correlates with model scale and proprietary alignment — the commercial API overrode explicit external evidence in nearly half of adversarial conflicts and frequently exhibited Negative Transfer (systemic confidence collapse) when parametric priors were contradicted.

Conclusion: Small Language Models demonstrate a structural epistemic advantage and superior contextual adherence in strict extraction workflows, challenging the assumption that larger models are universally better for RAG. The NCU metric provides a principled framework for evaluating genuine context utilization beyond discrete accuracy heuristics.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds Large Language Models in external knowledge, yet current evaluations rely on discrete heuristics that suffer from ''epistemic blindness'' - failing to distinguish genuine contextual information extraction from parametric memory recall. To address this, we introduce the Normalized Context Utilization (NCU) metric, leveraging continuous token log-probabilities across zero-shot, oracle, and adversarial conditions to strictly quantify contextual information gain. Evaluating architectures ranging from 1.5B to 72B parameters alongside a proprietary commercial API reveals that for strict factual extraction (without Chain-of-Thought reasoning), traditional scaling laws exhibit extreme diminishing returns: highly efficient Small Language Models (SLMs) match or outperform high-capacity architectures. Furthermore, we demonstrate that ``Prior Dominance'' correlates with model scale and proprietary alignments. The evaluated commercial API not only overrode explicit external evidence in nearly half of adversarial conflicts, but also frequently suffered from systemic confidence collapse (Negative Transfer) when its parametric priors were contradicted. Our findings highlight the structural epistemic advantage and superior contextual adherence of SLMs in strict extraction workflows.

</details>


### [64] [Towards Version-aware Operations and Transaction Memories for Multi-layer MeMo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24040)
*Peiran Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MeMo introduces language models with explicit multi-layer correlation matrix memories (CMMs) that support knowledge updates through direct memory editing. A version-aware operation layer compiles high-level operations (replace, obsolete, keep-history, rollback, trace) into ordered transactions of primitive memory edits, using auxiliary Version CMM and Transaction CMM structures, reducing the need for full retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce or eliminate the need for retraining language models when knowledge changes, by enabling direct editing of explicit memories rather than updating model weights through gradient-based optimization.

Method: A version-aware operation layer that compiles high-level knowledge update operations (replace, obsolete, keep-history, rollback, trace) into ordered transactions of MeMo-native primitive calls. Two auxiliary CMMs are introduced: a Version CMM (V-CMM) mapping version transitions to transaction handles, and a Transaction CMM (T-CMM) storing reusable change contents and inverse programs. Supports both direct sequence-level edits and structured diff-level inputs.

Result: The paper outlines an evaluation framework rather than reporting empirical results, proposing assessment along dimensions of update success, rollback capability, traceability, locality, and transaction reuse.

Conclusion: Explicit memory architectures with version-aware, transactional editing operations offer a viable alternative to retraining for knowledge updates in language models, providing auditability, reversibility, and reuse of change operations.

Abstract: MeMo proposes language models with explicit multi-layer correlation matrix memories (CMMs), where memorization, retrieval, and forgetting are architectural operations. This paper asks how such memories can reduce the need for retraining when knowledge changes. For changes expressible as MeMo memory associations, the model's accessible knowledge can be updated by editing explicit memories rather than retraining the whole model. We propose a version-aware operation layer in which high-level operations such as replace, obsolete, keep-history, rollback, and trace are compiled into MeMo-native primitive calls over sequences and tokens. The key observation is that a version-aware operation is rarely a single MeMo association. It is an ordered transaction of primitive edits, for example forgetting one sequence-token chain, memorizing another, preserving a historical chain, and recording an inverse program. The framework introduces two auxiliary CMMs: a Version CMM (V-CMM) for mapping version transitions to transaction handles, and a Transaction CMM (T-CMM) for storing reusable change contents and inverse programs. It supports both direct sequence-level edits and structured diff-level inputs, and outlines an evaluation route for update success, rollback, traceability, locality, and transaction reuse.

</details>


### [65] [Selective Capability Unlearning in End-to-End Spoken Language Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24063)
*Akanksha Singh,Vinod Kumar Kurmi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies "capability persistence" in autoregressive SLU models, where suppressing an intent does not eliminate its conditional slot-generation behavior, and proposes Binding Subspace (BSU), a representation-level method to isolate and attenuate intent-conditioned directions, reducing forced-prefix recoverability while preserving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern SLU systems deployed in real-world settings may need specific functionalities (intents and their slot-generation behaviors) removed due to policy or safety constraints. However, in autoregressive models, suppressing a target intent fails to eliminate the conditional slot-generation mapping, allowing reconstruction of the original intent-slot structure when the intent prefix is externally supplied. This structural failure is termed "capability persistence."

Method: The authors propose Binding Subspace (BSU), a representation-level framework that identifies and isolates intent-conditioned directions in the model's representation space that underlie the conditional mapping from intent to slots. BSU then attenuates these directions to disrupt the persistent capability without affecting other functionalities.

Result: Across SLU benchmarks, BSU substantially reduces forced-prefix recoverability — meaning the model can no longer reconstruct the original intent-slot structure when the intent prefix is externally supplied — while preserving retained performance on non-target functionalities.

Conclusion: Capability persistence is a critical structural failure in autoregressive SLU models that traditional intent suppression fails to address. BSU provides an effective representation-level solution that selectively disrupts the intent-conditioned slot mapping, offering a practical approach for safe and compliant deployment of SLU systems.

Abstract: Modern spoken language understanding (SLU) systems are increasingly deployed in real-world settings, where specific functionalities may need to be removed due to policy or safety constraints. In SLU, a functionality corresponds to an intent and its associated slot-generation behavior. However, in autoregressive models, suppressing a target intent does not eliminate the conditional mapping that generates slots conditioned on that intent. When the intent prefix is externally supplied, the model can reconstruct the original intent-slot structure. We identify this structural failure as \textbf{\emph{capability persistence}}. We propose \textit{\underline{B}inding \underline{S}ubspace (BSU)}, a representation-level framework that isolates and attenuates intent-conditioned directions underlying this mapping. Across SLU benchmarks, BSU substantially reduces forced-prefix recoverability while preserving retained performance.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [66] [Emergent Relational Order in LLM Agent Societies: From Collective Affect to Authority Stratification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23764)
*Zhiyuan Ji,Xinyu Chen,Ziqi Dai,Shiyun Tang,Chunyu Wei,Yueguo Chen*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper uses an LLM-based multi-agent simulation (CAREB-MAS) grounded in Affect Control Theory, Social Identity Theory, and Durkheimian collective affect to show that Fei Xiaotong's Differential Order Pattern—kinship-based relational gradation and cooperation decay—emerges spontaneously from general social mechanisms under long-horizon interactions, rather than being purely culturally specific.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fei Xiaotong's Differential Order Pattern is often treated as a culturally specific description of Chinese rural society, but its mechanistic basis is under-operationalized. Prior LLM-based multi-agent simulations have focused on short-term coordination and have not addressed how long-horizon social structures like Differential Order might emerge from general social mechanisms.

Method: The authors propose CAREB-MAS, a multi-agent framework in which agents reason through an emotion-ethics-belief chain and maintain dynamically evolving egocentric identities. The macro environment provides only individual production, preference-based allocation, and minimal interaction protocols. Long-horizon simulations are run to observe emergent social structure, with production structure varied to test sensitivity.

Result: Agents spontaneously reproduce five core Differential Order phenomena: (1) stable labor specialization, (2) guanxi-based economic ethics, (3) relational decay of cooperation, (4) emergent relational authority, and (5) clan-based center-periphery stratification. Patterns shift from kin-centered integration toward functional interdependence when production structure changes.

Conclusion: Differential Order can be interpreted as a structure-sensitive emergent outcome of general social mechanisms rather than a purely culturally specific phenomenon. LLM-based multi-agent simulation offers a viable interdisciplinary framework for studying social structure and change.

Abstract: Fei Xiaotong's Differential Order Pattern characterizes rural society as egocentric and relationally graded, with cooperation attenuating over social distance. Although often treated as culturally specific, its mechanistic basis remains under-operationalized, and prior LLM-based simulations have mainly addressed short-term coordination rather than long-horizon social structure. We propose CAREB-MAS, a multi-agent framework grounded in Affect Control Theory, Social Identity Theory, and Durkheimian collective affect. Agents reason through an emotion-ethics-belief chain and maintain dynamically evolving egocentric identities, while the macro environment specifies only individual production, preference-based allocation, and minimal interaction protocols. Across long-horizon simulations, agents spontaneously reproduce five core Differential Order phenomena: stable labor specialization, guanxi-based economic ethics, relational decay of cooperation, emergent relational authority, and clan-based center-periphery stratification. These patterns shift with production structure from kin-centered integration toward greater functional interdependence. Extensive experiment results support interpreting Differential Order as a structure-sensitive emergent outcome of general social mechanisms, with LLM-based multi-agent simulation providing an interdisciplinary framework for studying social structure and change.

</details>


### [67] [Decentralized Coordination of Autonomous Traffic Through Advanced Air Mobility Corridors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23832)
*Jasmine Jerry Aloor,Hamsa Balakrishnan*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Contrary to the belief that decentralized corridor-based AAM operations are inefficient, this paper demonstrates that autonomous aircraft can learn to self-organize into corridor flows using only local information, achieving high corridor conformance and requiring tactical interventions primarily only at high traffic densities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the prevailing assumption that dedicated AAM corridor operations, while easy to implement, are inherently inefficient in the absence of centralized traffic management, and to explore whether autonomous aircraft can self-organize effectively in decentralized settings.

Method: The authors employ a decentralized, multi-agent learning approach where fixed-wing autonomous aircraft use only local information to self-organize into corridor flows. The approach is tested across three scenario types: a single corridor with post-exit metering, two consecutive corridors, and a corridor that splits into two.

Result: Aircraft conformed to corridor boundaries more than 94% of the time and reached their goals efficiently. Tactical separation interventions were infrequent in low- and medium-density settings, becoming frequent only under high traffic density.

Conclusion: Decentralized, self-organized corridor operations are viable for AAM traffic, offering an efficient alternative to centralized traffic management except under high-density conditions where tactical interventions increase.

Abstract: The use of dedicated corridors for Advanced Air Mobility (AAM) traffic is one of the most commonly proposed pathways to integrating them into existing airspace operations. Most prior research has focused on the design of networks of AAM corridors and conflict resolution for aircraft within corridors. It is also generally believed that while attractive from an implementation perspective, corridor-based operations may be inefficient, especially in the absence of centralized traffic management.
  In this paper, we show that contrary to this belief, it is possible for autonomous aircraft to learn to self-organize into corridor flows in decentralized settings. We illustrate our approach using scenarios in which fixed-wing aircraft need to safely and efficiently traverse (1) a single corridor with metering after the exit, (2) a sequence of two consecutive corridors, and (3) a corridor that splits into two. We find that in decentralized settings with only local information, the aircraft are able to conform to the corridor boundaries more than 94% of the time and reach their goal in a relatively efficient manner. Furthermore, tactical interventions to handle violations of the separation minimum are needed only infrequently in low- and medium-density settings. However, such tactical interventions become more frequently necessary only when traffic density is high.

</details>


### [68] [Generating Realistic Individual Activity Schedules via Activity Location Allocation Based on Simulated Travel Times](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24566)
*Tatsuya Mitomi,Yahya Gamal,Esra Suel,Gary Polhill,Daniel Konioukhov,Alison Heppenstall*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Proposes an iterative dynamic programming framework for generating individual daily activity schedules that better align simulated travel times with survey-reported travel times, achieving a 52.2% reduction in discrepancy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realistic individual-level activity schedules are crucial for urban planning and epidemic modeling, but generating them is difficult due to privacy constraints on observed data and the challenge of preserving travel time consistency between synthetic schedules and travel survey data.

Method: An iterative refinement framework that uses dynamic programming to allocate activity locations based on simulated travel times, repeatedly adjusting schedules until travel time discrepancies with survey data are minimized.

Result: The proposed method reduces the discrepancy between simulated travel times and survey-reported travel times by 52.2% relative to the first iteration, as demonstrated through numerical experiments with dummy data.

Conclusion: Iterative dynamic programming effectively generates more realistic activity schedules by aligning simulated travel times with empirical survey data, offering a practical solution when only population and travel survey data are available.

Abstract: Individual level daily activity schedules are essential for a wide range of applications, including infectious disease control, urban transportation planning, and policy design. In practice, such schedules are typically generated by combining population data with travel survey data.
