These data sources are used because they are often publicly available, whereas observed individual activity schedules are difficult to obtain due to privacy concerns. However, because of the complexity of mobility modelling, it is difficult to generate realistic activity schedules that also preserve travel times consistent with those reported in travel surveys. To address this issue, we propose a framework for generating activity schedules that iteratively applies a dynamic programming method to allocate activity locations based on simulated travel times. Numerical experiments with dummy data show that the proposed method reduces the discrepancy between simulated travel times and those reported in travel surveys by 52.2% relative to the first iteration through iterative refinement.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [69] [Cryptographic certificates of validity for trustworthy AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23768)
*Murdoch J. Gabbay*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Proposes cryptographic certificates of validity for AI agents, where a logical policy predicate is compiled into polynomial constraints and proven via succinct cryptographic proofs (optionally zero-knowledge). This enables independently verifiable action certificates without trusting the agent or re-executing computation, positioned between formal verification and cryptographic authentication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap between heavy-weight formal verification of source code and simple cryptographic authentication for AI agents. The authors seek a middle-ground solution where an agent's actions can be independently certified as compliant with a formal policy, without requiring trust in the agent or redundant re-execution by the verifier.

Method: The approach involves three steps: (1) formally specifying a correctness or policy condition as a logical predicate; (2) compiling this predicate into a witness-checking problem expressed over polynomial constraints; (3) applying a succinct cryptographic proof system (e.g., SNARKs/STARKs), with optional zero-knowledge, to produce an independently checkable certificate that the condition holds for a given action.

Result: The paper outlines the approach at a high level, presents the core mathematical translation, and situates the proposal within related paradigms including proof-carrying code, zkVMs, formal methods, and agent governance. It identifies open questions in specification, auditing, and deployment.

Conclusion: Cryptographic certificates of validity offer a promising framework for accountable agentic AI by combining formal specification with succinct, trustless verification. Full realization requires addressing specification, auditing, and deployment challenges.

Abstract: We propose cryptographic certificates of validity for agentic AI systems. The core idea is to formally specify a correctness or policy condition as a logical predicate, compile this predicate to a witness-checking problem over polynomial constraints, and use a succinct cryptographic proof system (and optionally zero-knowledge) to certify that the condition holds.
  This offers a middle ground between formal verification of source code, and cryptographic authentication. An agent's action can be accompanied by an independently checkable proof that it satisfies an agreed formal policy, without requiring the verifier to trust the agent or to re-execute computation. We outline the approach at a high level, give the core mathematical translation, relate the proposal to proof-carrying code, zkVMs, formal methods, and agent governance, and note the specification, auditing, and deployment questions that a full implementation must answer.

</details>


### [70] [AutoPRAC: Automating Attack Discovery for PRAC-Based Rowhammer Defenses using Model Checkers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23905)
*Joyce Qu,Gururaj Saileshwar*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: AutoPRAC is an automated framework using model checkers to formally verify the security of DDR5 PRAC-based Rowhammer defenses, which uncovered a flaw in the MOAT defense allowing up to 34 undetected activations above threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of PRAC (Per-Row Activation Counting) security in DDR5 relies on manually crafted attack patterns and lacks formal verification or automated techniques to detect implementation flaws.

Method: AutoPRAC models PRAC implementations as bounded state machines and uses model checking to verify safety-critical security properties against a worst-case oracle attacker. Violations produce concrete counterexample traces.

Result: AutoPRAC discovered a previously unreported flaw in MOAT's counter-reset policy, allowing up to 34 activations to go undetected above the Rowhammer threshold.

Conclusion: AutoPRAC demonstrates that automated formal methods can effectively discover subtle security flaws in Rowhammer mitigations and serves as an early-stage design aid for PRAC implementations.

Abstract: Per-Row Activation Counting (PRAC) in DDR5 is a specification to mitigate Rowhammer attacks by tracking activations per row and triggering mitigative refreshes when needed. However, the security of PRAC designs is currently evaluated using human-crafted attack patterns and we lack formal verification of their security properties, or automated techniques to detect implementation flaws. In this work, we present AutoPRAC, the first automated technique to test the security of PRAC-based defenses using model checkers. AutoPRAC models PRAC implementations as bounded state machines and checks security-critical safety properties against a worst-case oracle attacker. If a property is violated, the framework produces a concrete counterexample trace corresponding to a successful attack. Using AutoPRAC, we uncover a previously unreported flaw in MOAT, a state-of-the-art PRAC defense, in its counter-reset policy that allows up to 34 activations to go undetected above the Rowhammer threshold. Our results demonstrate that AutoPRAC can automatically discover subtle security flaws in Rowhammer mitigations and serves as an early-stage design aid for attack discovery on PRAC designs.

</details>


### [71] [BipBipCache: Pipeline-Aware Integration of Low-Latency Tweakable Encryption in an Embedded Cache Controller](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23941)
*Corbin Hibler,Firas Hassan,Eric McKanna*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: BipBipCache is a direct-mapped cache controller that integrates the BipBip tweakable block cipher to encrypt both cache data and tags in real time, protecting against cold-boot, bus, and SRAM readout attacks. The design uses a C³-style 24+40 bit decomposition per 64-bit word, overlaps encryption latency with tag operations to reduce effective write penalty from 6 to 3 cycles, and is verified on Xilinx Artix-7 FPGA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Consumer and embedded processors retain sensitive data in on-chip SRAM caches that remain readable after power loss or physical probing. Maintaining ciphertext directly in the memory array is necessary to achieve confidentiality against cold-boot, bus snooping, and SRAM readout attacks.

Method: The authors design BipBipCache, a direct-mapped cache controller embedding the BipBip tweakable block cipher (TBC) using a C³-style decomposition (24-bit + 40-bit) per 64-bit word. They reconstruct the first pipelined hardware BipBip encryptor from an existing decryptor-centric specification and coordinate it with a 3-cycle decryptor inside the cache datapath. Encryption latency is partially hidden by overlapping the first three encryptor stages with tag decryption and hit detection. Correctness is verified against the official BipBip C++ reference implementation (five test vectors each), and the design is synthesized on a Xilinx Artix-7 FPGA.

Result: Architecturally, the 6-cycle encryption latency yields an effective write penalty of only 3 cycles because the initial encryptor stages overlap with tag decryption and hit detection, with write commitment occurring after hit verification. FPGA implementation on Xilinx Artix-7 consumes 3,356 LUTs (16.1% of device), with cryptographic logic accounting for approximately 79% of LUTs. End-to-end hardware operation is confirmed.

Conclusion: BipBipCache demonstrates a practical, FPGA-verified encrypted cache architecture that provides confidentiality for on-chip SRAM-resident data against physical attacks, with the key insight that encryption latency can be effectively mitigated through pipelined overlap with tag operations in the cache datapath.

Abstract: Consumer and embedded processors store sensitive data in on-chip SRAM caches that remain readable after power loss or physical probing unless ciphertext is maintained in the memory array itself. This paper presents BipBipCache, a direct-mapped cache controller that integrates the BipBip tweakable block cipher (TBC) to encrypt cache data and tags in real time using a C$^3$-style 24+40 bit decomposition of each 64-bit word. We reconstruct the first pipelined hardware BipBip encryptor from a decryptor-centric specification and coordinate it with a 3-cycle decryptor inside the cache datapath.
  Our threat model targets confidentiality of cache-resident contents against cold-boot, bus, and SRAM readout attacks. A key architectural result is that 6-cycle encryption latency does not fully translate into 6-cycle write penalty: the first three encryptor stages overlap with tag decryption and hit detection, leaving an effective 3-cycle write commitment after hit verification. We verify encryptor and decryptor correctness against the official BipBip C++ reference (five vectors each), report FPGA resource utilization on Xilinx Artix-7 (3,356 LUTs, 16.1% of device; crypto logic ~79% of LUTs), and confirm end-to-end operation on hardware.

</details>


### [72] [Maestro Order: A Model-Agnostic Orchestration Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23983)
*Hidayet Aksu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Maestro Order is a model-agnostic orchestration framework that composes unreliable models (prone to hallucinations) into reliable systems using four primitives — decompose, ensemble, verify, recurse — under a budget-aware controller. Simulation shows verification can amplify reliability from 0.55 to 0.999 with four gates, while the cost-aware controller achieves target reliability far cheaper than voting alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern language models are fast and fluent but produce confident errors (hallucinations) — useful enough to deploy, dangerous enough to mistrust. The authors aim to systematically transform any black-box unreliable solver into a reliable problem-solving system without modifying the underlying model, addressing the fundamental tension between model capability and trustworthiness.

Method: The work introduces Maestro Order, a harness with (1) four structural primitives: decompose (break problems into sub-problems), ensemble (aggregate multiple solver outputs), verify (gate outputs through verifiers), and recurse (iterate on failures); (2) a budget-aware controller that allocates compute to stages with highest marginal reliability per unit cost; (3) online measurement of verifier discrimination to inform allocation. The architecture is deterministic, observable, and fault-tolerant. Evaluation uses Monte Carlo simulation over a parameterized solver/verifier model rather than real models, enabling clean measurement of scaling laws.

Result: Key quantitative findings from simulation: verification amplifies reliability geometrically (0.55 → 0.98 with two verifier gates, → 0.999 with four); voting improves reliability only above random-chance baseline and is bounded by shared errors; the budget-aware controller reaches target reliability at a fraction of the cost of voting alone by selecting the cheapest effective mechanism per reliability regime. The paper also identifies failure modes: verifier gaming, correlated errors across ensemble members, and error compounding in decomposition chains.

Conclusion: The core prescriptive takeaway is threefold: build robust verifiers/checkers, diversify solver ensembles to decorrelate errors, and let a cost-aware controller dynamically route compute to where information gain is highest. The framework demonstrates that orchestration alone can transform unreliable components into reliable systems without model modification.

Abstract: A single forward pass of a capable model is a fast, fluent, and unreliable problem-solver: it is right often enough to be useful and wrong often enough to be dangerous; in language models, such confident errors are known as hallucinations. We present Maestro Order, a model-agnostic orchestration harness that turns unreliable solvers into reliable problem-solving systems by composing them according to four structural primitives (decompose, ensemble, verify, and recurse) and a budget-aware controller that decides where to spend compute. The harness treats any model as a black-box base solver behind a uniform interface, layers a verifier ensemble whose discrimination is measured online, and allocates verification and voting to the stages with the highest marginal reliability per unit cost. We give the architecture, the message and state schema, the controller algorithm, and the engineering that makes it deterministic, observable, and fault-tolerant. We then specify an evaluation methodology (reliability at fixed cost, coverage, calibration, and ablations) and report results from a faithful Monte Carlo simulation of the harness over a parameterized solver/verifier model. The simulation reproduces the predicted laws quantitatively: verification amplifies reliability geometrically (e.g. $0.55\to0.98$ with two gates, $\to0.999$ with four), voting helps only above chance and is limited by shared errors, and a budget-aware controller reaches a target reliability at a small fraction of the cost of voting alone by selecting the cheapest mechanism for each regime. We close with failure modes (verifier gaming, correlated errors, and decomposition error compounding) and concrete guidance: build robust checkers, diversify solvers, and let the controller put compute where the information is.

</details>


### [73] [PixJail: Self-Evolving Paper-to-Pipeline Reproduction for Text-to-Image Jailbreak Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24081)
*Leyi Sheng,Han Sun,Zhen Sun,Yuntao Yue,Jinlin Wu,Xinlei He,Jiaheng Wei*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: PixJail is a self-evolving paper-to-pipeline agent framework that automates the reproduction of T2I jailbreak evaluations by constructing paper-specific attack modules and runnable pipelines, achieving 2.1% average reproduction error across eleven methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: T2I jailbreak evaluation suffers from poor reproducibility and unfair comparisons because it is a pipeline-level problem spanning prompt transformation, image generation, safety filtering, and multimodal judging. Existing benchmarks and reproduction workflows cannot keep pace with rapidly evolving attack techniques, and results across papers are difficult to reliably replicate.

Method: PixJail is a self-evolving paper-to-pipeline agent framework. Given a T2I jailbreak paper and optional reference code, it constructs a paper-specific attack module and a runnable evaluation pipeline under a unified contract. It maintains a memory bank storing paper digests, attack evolution patterns, reusable templates, failure cases, and versioned artifacts to enable experience reuse across future reproduction efforts.

Result: The framework reproduced eleven representative T2I jailbreak methods (including both code-available and code-unavailable papers). Under original settings, it accurately recovered prior results with an average error of 2.1% and a median error of 0%.

Conclusion: PixJail can serve as a unified foundation for future T2I jailbreak reproduction and evaluation, significantly reducing manual effort and enabling fair, reproducible comparisons.

Abstract: As Text-to-Image (T2I) jailbreak techniques evolve rapidly, existing benchmarks and reproduction workflows often struggle to keep pace. More importantly, T2I jailbreak evaluation is not a single prompt-level test, but a pipeline-level problem shaped by multiple stages, including prompt transformation, image generation, safety filtering, and multimodal judging. This makes results across papers difficult to reliably reproduce and fairly compare. To bridge this gap, we propose PixJail, a self-evolving paper-to-pipeline agent framework for reproducible T2I jailbreak evaluation. Given a T2I jailbreak paper and optional reference code, PixJail rapidly constructs a paper-specific attack module and a runnable evaluation pipeline under a unified contract, while faithfully reproducing the original experimental results. PixJail further maintains a memory bank that stores paper digests, attack evolution patterns, reusable templates, failure cases, and versioned artifacts, enabling future reproduction efforts to reuse prior experience. We reproduce eleven representative T2I jailbreak methods, including both code-available and code-unavailable papers. Under their original settings, our framework accurately recovers prior results with minimal error (2.1\% average, 0\% median). We hope that PixJail can serve as a unified foundation for future T2I jailbreak reproduction and evaluation, significantly reducing manual effort.

</details>


### [74] [DoHFuse: A Dual-Branch Architecture with DMAGLSTM for Website Fingerprinting over DNS over HTTPS/3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24105)
*ZunDong Zhang,Yanan Cheng,Zhaoxin Zhang,Xueyang Huo,Changjiang Wu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that DNS over HTTP/3 (DoH/3), even with padding obfuscation deployed in IoT environments, remains highly vulnerable to Website Fingerprinting (WF) attacks. The authors release the first real-world DoH/3 benchmark dataset and propose DoHFuse, a dual-branch LSTM model achieving ~88% accuracy in closed-world (449 classes) and 0.951 F1 in open-world detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With secure DNS protocols (DoH, DoT) widely adopted in IoT for privacy, and DoH/3 increasingly deployed with padding strategies, it is critical to assess whether these mechanisms actually mitigate WF attacks in realistic IoT/edge-network scenarios. No prior work has studied WF on DoH/3 using real-world traffic.

Method: The authors (1) collect and publicly release the first real-world DoH/3 traffic benchmark dataset from practical network environments; (2) propose DoHFuse, a dual-branch learning framework combining inter-arrival time sequences with refined statistical features via an improved DMAG-LSTM designed to capture burst-aligned temporal patterns; (3) evaluate in both closed-world (449-class classification) and open-world (anomaly detection) settings.

Result: In closed-world (449 classes): accuracy 88.05%, precision 88.56, recall 87.96, F1 87.83. In open-world detection: AUPRC 0.975, F1 0.951 (precision 0.906, recall 1.0). These results show DoH/3 padding alone is insufficient against WF attacks.

Conclusion: DoH/3 traffic remains highly susceptible to Website Fingerprinting despite commonly deployed padding mechanisms, indicating that padding alone cannot ensure privacy protection in IoT-scale encrypted DNS communications. Stronger or complementary defenses are needed.

Abstract: As personal data privacy becomes increasingly critical in Internet of Things (IoT) environments, secure DNS protocols such as DNS over HTTPS (DoH) and DNS over TLS (DoT) have been widely adopted to protect device communications. However, without effective obfuscation, these protocols remain vulnerable to Website Fingerprinting (WF) attacks that can reveal user activity. With the ongoing deployment of DNS over HTTP/3 (DoH/3) in modern networked systems, padding strategies have been increasingly applied in practice. It is therefore essential to investigate whether DoH/3 can effectively mitigate WF attacks in realistic IoT and edge-network scenarios. To address this, we first collect and publicly release the first real-world benchmark dataset of DoH/3 traffic, generated from domain resolution processes in practical network environments. We further propose DoHFuse, a dual-branch learning framework that integrates inter-arrival time sequences and refined statistical features through an improved DMAG-LSTM, specifically designed to capture burst-aligned temporal patterns. Experimental results show that DoHFuse achieves an accuracy of 88.05% (precision 88.56, recall 87.96, F1 87.83) in a closed-world setting of 449 classes, and an AUPRC of 0.975 with an F1 score of 0.951 (precision 0.906, recall 1.0) in open-world detection. These findings demonstrate that DoH/3 traffic remains susceptible to WF attacks, suggesting that commonly deployed padding mechanisms alone are insufficient to ensure privacy protection in IoT-scale encrypted DNS communications.

</details>


### [75] [CORE-BREW: LLR-Based Soft Decoding for Robust Multi-Bit LLM Watermarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24163)
*Joeun Kim,HoEun Kim,Young-Sik Kim*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: CORE-BREW is a multi-bit LLM watermarking method that uses soft-decision decoding with calibrated per-token log-likelihood ratios, offering improved robustness under edits and strict false-positive control through two detection modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ECC-based LLM watermarks rely on hard-decision decoding, which discards token-level reliability information. The authors aim to exploit this information via soft-decision decoding to improve robustness and detection accuracy while maintaining strict FPR guarantees.

Method: CORE-BREW calibrates the watermark channel by targeting a fixed hit rate (p-star), yielding closed-form per-token log-likelihood ratios (LLRs). It supports two detection modes: Strict-Safe (preserving a bounded-distance designated-codeword acceptance region) and FPR-Calibrated (using likelihood-based scoring with lightweight list decoding to characterize the FPR-TPR trade-off).

Result: Experiments on open-source LLMs under token-level edits and paraphrasing show improved low-FPR discrimination and robustness over prior multi-bit watermarking baselines, while maintaining comparable semantic quality.

Conclusion: CORE-BREW effectively leverages soft information from the watermark channel, enabling principled soft-decision decoding that outperforms hard-decision baselines in both robustness and false-positive control for multi-bit LLM watermarking.

Abstract: Reliable provenance for LLM outputs requires multi-bit watermarks that remain robust under editing while maintaining strict false-positive control. Existing ECC-based LLM watermarks rely largely on hard-decision decoding, discarding token-level reliability information. We propose CORE-BREW, a Constant-hit-Rate Embedding extension of block-wise BREW for robust multi-bit watermarking. CORE-BREW calibrates the watermark channel by targeting a fixed hit rate p-star, yielding closed-form per-token log-likelihood ratios (LLRs) for principled soft-decision decoding. It supports two detection modes: Strict-Safe, which preserves the bounded-distance designated-codeword acceptance region, and FPR-Calibrated, which uses likelihood-based scoring and lightweight list decoding to characterize the FPR-TPR trade-off. Experiments on open-source LLMs under token-level edits and paraphrasing demonstrate improved low-FPR discrimination and robustness over prior multi-bit watermarking baselines while maintaining comparable semantic quality.

</details>


### [76] [ComputeFHE: A Privacy-Preserving General-Purpose Computation Library](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24379)
*Faris Serdar Tasel,Efe Ciftci*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: ComputeFHE is an open-source C++ library built on OpenFHE that simplifies FHE application development by offering encrypted integer/fixed-point types with familiar imperative operations. It supports conventional TFHE gates and an optimized ALU architecture, achieving up to 3.9× performance gains by reducing bootstrapping operations, and includes a simulation mode for debugging and cost analysis without actual cryptographic computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) preserves data confidentiality during computation, but practical adoption is hindered by high computational costs and significant development complexity. There is a need for accessible tools that lower the barrier to implementing privacy-preserving applications.

Method: The authors developed ComputeFHE, a C++ library on top of OpenFHE. It provides encrypted integer and fixed-point data types with arithmetic, logical, comparison, conditional, and oblivious array-access operations using an imperative programming style. The library implements two computation strategies: (1) conventional TFHE arithmetic via standard two-input logic gates, and (2) an optimized Arithmetic Logic Unit (ALU) architecture using FHE-friendly logic primitives. A simulation mode is included for testing, debugging, and complexity analysis without performing actual cryptographic operations, yielding circuit complexity and bootstrapping cost estimates.

Result: The optimized ALU architecture significantly reduces the number of bootstrapping operations required, achieving performance improvements of up to 3.9× for selected operations compared to conventional TFHE gate-based arithmetic.

Conclusion: ComputeFHE provides a practical, accessible framework that simplifies both the development and evaluation of privacy-preserving algorithms based on TFHE, making FHE more approachable for application developers.

Abstract: Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) enables computations to be performed directly on encrypted data while preserving data confidentiality. However, its practical applications remain limited by high computational costs and development complexity. This paper presents ComputeFHE, an open-source C++ library that facilitates the development of privacy-preserving applications based on the TFHE cryptosystem. The library provides encrypted integer and fixed-point data types together with arithmetic, logical, comparison, conditional, and oblivious array-access operations which allow developers to implement algorithms using a familiar imperative programming paradigm. ComputeFHE supports both conventional TFHE arithmetic based on standard two-input logic gates and an optimized Arithmetic Logic Unit (ALU) architecture utilizing FHE-friendly logic primitives. Experimental results demonstrate significant reductions in the number of required bootstrapping operations, achieving performance improvements of up to 3.9x for selected operations. In addition, the library includes a simulation mode that enables testing, debugging, and complexity analysis without performing actual cryptographic computations while providing circuit complexity and bootstrapping costs. Built on top of OpenFHE, ComputeFHE offers a practical and accessible framework for developing and evaluating privacy-preserving algorithms and applications.

</details>


### [77] [Red-Teaming the Agentic Red-Team](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24496)
*Dario Pasquini,Michal Bazyli,Taras Fedynyshyn,Artem Sorokin*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: First in-depth security analysis of agentic offensive security systems reveals pervasive design flaws allowing API key theft, persistence, and full host compromise—even within sandboxes. Authors construct a cyber kill chain for these systems and propose a hardened architecture with actionable design principles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While the community has rapidly commoditized agentic systems for offensive security operations, the security posture of these agents themselves has been largely ignored. The work aims to fill this gap by systematically analyzing vulnerabilities in widely-used offensive security agents.

Method: The authors perform a comprehensive security analysis of the most popular agentic offensive-security tools, identifying common design flaws. They introduce a full cyber kill chain framework tailored to such agentic systems, mapping adversary progression from initial LLM manipulation through lateral movement, persistence, guardrail bypass, and sandbox escape. Based on this analysis, they derive a robust reference architecture and design principles.

Result: Most analyzed tools share common design flaws enabling active adversaries to exfiltrate API keys, establish persistent footholds, and fully compromise the operator's machine—even when the agent runs inside a sandboxed container. The proposed kill chain and architecture-level mitigations address these attack paths.

Conclusion: The commoditization of offensive security agents has outpaced attention to their own security. Architectural-level design principles, rather than patchwork fixes, are necessary to harden these systems against the identified attack chains and prevent compromise of operators.

Abstract: The use of agentic systems to perform offensive security operations has moved from a theoretical possibility to a commoditized capability. However, while the community has focused on creating more and more capable agents, less attention has been allocated to assessing the security of those systems.
  In this work, we present the first in-depth security analysis of the most widely used agentic systems for offensive security operations. We show that most of these tools share common design flaws that enable an active adversary to exfiltrate API keys, establish persistent footholds, and fully compromise the operator's machine, even when the agent operates inside a sandboxed container. To support our analysis, we introduce a full cyber kill chain for such agentic systems, capturing the progression from initial LLM manipulation to lateral movement, persistence, guardrail bypass, and sandbox escape.
  Building on our security analysis, we derive a robust architecture for agentic offensive-security tools and propose actionable, broadly applicable design principles that mitigate the disclosed attack paths at the architectural level.

</details>


<div id='cs.HC'></div>

# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [78] [Embodied Explainability and Ontological Obstacles: Why We Struggle to Explain the Answers of Large Language Models (LLMs)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23840)
*Marvin Pafla,Jesse Hoey,Kate Larson,Mark Hancock*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper argues that explainability should be reconceived not as extracting internal model properties, but as an embodied, enactive process where understanding emerges through situated practice and affordances for probing, coordinating, and repairing behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current XAI practices treat explainability as a property residing inside models, extracting "explanations" from internals. This leads to ontological errors: surrogate abstractions are mistaken for the model's own, and the explainer's participatory role in sense-making is overlooked. The authors contend this misnaming skews purpose and increases overreliance.

Method: The paper employs argumentation through demonstrations and conceptual analysis grounded in Dourish's embodied interaction and enactivist cognition theory. It identifies two key ontological obstacles — surrogate import and participatory neglect — and critically examines their manifestation in LLM XAI practice.

Result: The analysis reveals that many so-called "explanations" are misnamed because they fail to account for how understanding actually arises — through shared practice and affordance-based action. Embodied explanations, by contrast, reorganize sense-making by making what matters publicly available for coordinated action.

Conclusion: Explainability claims should be reserved for designs that provide genuine affordances for users to probe, coordinate, and repair model behavior in situated practice. The field should shift from "looking inside" models toward designing for embodied sense-making.

Abstract: Explainability is often framed as a property of an AI model, with explanations extracted from its internals and shown to users. In this argument paper, we instead provide an embodied account of explainability based on Dourish and enactivist cognition: understanding is created in use as people act on affordances in shared practice. Using demonstrations and conceptual analysis, we reveal ontological obstacles when "looking inside" large language models: surrogates import external abstractions that can be mistaken for the model's, and focusing on internal reasoning misses that explainers participate in their own understanding. We discuss these obstacles in XAI practice, arguing that many explanations are misnamed, which skews their purpose and can increase overreliance. Finally, we highlight how embodied explanations reorganize sense-making by making what matters publicly available for action, and argue that explainability claims should be reserved for designs that provide affordances to probe, coordinate, and repair behaviour in situated practice.

</details>


### [79] [Do Language Models Pass the Bechdel Test? Auditing Gender Biases in LLM-Generated Screenplays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24022)
*Megha N. Govindu,Stephanie T. Wang,Sorelle A. Friedler,Danaé Metaxa*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This study audits gender bias in LLM-generated screenplays by applying the Bechdel test and social network analysis to compare scripts from GPT-5, Gemini 3 Pro, and Claude Sonnet 4.5 against 768 human-written counterparts. Human scripts pass the Bechdel test more often, but some network metrics (centrality, homophily, triadic relationships) show LLM scripts can exhibit less bias in certain dimensions—though all script types display representational bias under most measures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly adopted in media production (journalism, filmmaking), understanding their impact on narrative representation is critical. Prior work has established that LLMs perpetuate social biases such as gender bias, but assessments have largely focused on output content rather than structural/relational patterns. The authors aim to fill this gap by examining the network structure of character interactions in LLM-generated screenplays.

Method: The authors automated the Bechdel test—a measure requiring (1) at least two named women, (2) who talk to each other, (3) about something other than a man—and applied social network analysis (centrality, homophily, triadic relationships) to compare 768 human-written screenplays against scripts generated by three SOTA LLMs: GPT-5, Gemini 3 Pro, and Claude Sonnet 4.5.

Result: Human-written screenplays are significantly more likely to pass the Bechdel test than LLM-generated scripts. However, network analyses reveal a nuanced picture: on metrics like centrality, homophily, and triadic closure, LLM-generated scripts sometimes demonstrate less gender bias than human scripts. Despite these mixed findings, all script types—both human and AI-generated—exhibit some form of representational bias under most measures examined.

Conclusion: The findings underscore the continued need for rigorous quantitative assessment of representational bias in both traditional and AI-generated media. The authors advocate for incorporating structural/network-based bias metrics alongside content-based evaluations to more comprehensively audit fairness in AI-generated narratives.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in media production from journalistm to filmmaking, what impact do they have on the stories being told? Prior work has shown LLMs to perpetuate social biases, including those related to gender. We complement existing literature on gender bias in LLM outputs by auditing the network structure of LLM-generated movie screenplays through automating the Bechdel test, a popular measure of women's representation in literary and film works. We also introduce the use of social network analysis measures to further analyze representational bias in LLM-generated scripts. We evaluate screenplays generated by three state-of-the-art LLMs (GPT-5, Gemini 3 Pro, and Claude Sonnet 4.5) against 768 corresponding human-written screenplays, finding that human-written scripts are more likely to pass the Bechdel test. However, other network analyses, like centrality, homophily, and triadic relationships demonstrate that in some cases LLM-scripts have less bias, although all script types demonstrate some representational bias under most measures. We conclude by discussing the continued need for further quantitative assessments of media representations and AI-generated content.

</details>


### [80] [The impact of generative artificial intelligence on academic development of Chinese students in humanities and social sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24104)
*Lei Fan,Fangxue Liu*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A large-scale survey of Chinese HSS students reveals that while over half report GenAI enhances motivation, creativity, and academic performance, concerns about accuracy, overreliance, and privacy persist; students favor partial, practice-oriented curricular integration with ethical safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GenAI is profoundly reshaping higher education, especially in the humanities and social sciences (HSS), where written and interpretive learning outcomes align closely with GenAI's capabilities. However, systematic empirical evidence on GenAI's educational impacts on HSS students remains scarce, creating a gap this study aims to fill.

Method: A large-scale survey of HSS students in China, guided by relevant learning theories, examining four dimensions: patterns of GenAI use, effects on learning processes and academic performance, challenges associated with GenAI use, and preferred approaches to curricular integration.

Result: Over half of students perceived enhanced learning motivation, independent thinking, and creativity, though a substantial minority reported little change or decline. A larger majority reported academic performance gains, potentially inflated by limitations in conventional assessment. Variations emerged by GenAI experience duration, discipline, and modestly by gender. Most valued ethics, but only slightly over half were satisfied with privacy protection. Accuracy limitations and overreliance were the top concerns. Students favored partial/optional curricular integration with practice-oriented training and recognized GenAI's importance for future careers.

Conclusion: Grounded in student perspectives, the study provides evidence-based recommendations for the responsible, pedagogically meaningful integration of GenAI into HSS curricula, emphasizing ethical safeguards, accuracy awareness, and practice-oriented training while avoiding overreliance.

Abstract: Generative artificial intelligence(GenAI) is reshaping learning in higher education, with particularly pronounced implications for the humanities and social sciences(HSS), where learning outcomes are commonly expressed through written and interpretive forms that align closely with GenAI's capabilities. Yet, systematic evidence on the educational impacts of GenAI on HSS students remains limited. Addressing this gap, this study draws on a large-scale survey of HSS students in China to examine its role in academic development. Guided by relevant learning theories, this study focuses on four dimensions: patterns of use, effects on learning processes and academic performance, challenges associated with GenAI use, and preferred approaches to curricular integration. We found that more than half perceived enhanced learning motivation, independent thinking and creativity, although a substantial minority reported little change or even decline. Comparatively, a notably larger majority reported academic performance gains, although these gains may partly reflect limitations in conventional assessment practices. The study identifies variations in perceived learning and performance improvements among students with differing durations of GenAI experience, along with observable disciplinary differences and modest gender differences. While an overwhelming majority valued the importance of ethical considerations, only slightly more than half were satisfied with privacy protection. Limited accuracy and overreliance emerged as the most pressing concerns reported by students. Students favored partial or optional curricular integration supported by practice-oriented training, and widely recognized GenAI's significance for their future professional development. Grounded in student perspectives, this study offers evidence-based recommendations for the responsible and pedagogically meaningful integration of GenAI

</details>


### [81] [Human-Centered Design: The Disclosure of Generative Artificial Intelligence for Emerging Professionals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24136)
*Sydney Lee*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper examines how a Human-Centered Design course (ITIS8300) integrated generative AI into student workflows through structured milestones, team roles (including an AI advocate), and mandatory disclosure reports. The approach demonstrated that safe, justified, and transparent AI use can enhance productivity and co-creation while mitigating risks of deskilling and over-reliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI offers efficiency gains in HCD workflows, but its integration raises concerns about ethical bias, deskilling of foundational skills, reduced deep work, and decreased professional autonomy — particularly for emerging professionals who lack established HCD foundations. Current self-report disclosure methods are insufficient, as they provide little guidance on appropriate use and may incentivize non-disclosure.

Method: A semester-long project in the ITIS8300 HCD course was structured with iterative milestones, defined team roles (including a generative AI advocate), and clear boundaries for human feedback integration. Students produced a high-level disclosure report documenting design processes, methodology, findings, and rationale for AI usage. The course emphasized freedom in execution while mandating transparent AI use and justification within HCI workflows.

Result: The course structure resulted in a high-level disclosure report that detailed design processes, methodology, findings, and rationale for AI usage. The approach demonstrated that when AI usage is safe, justified, and transparent, it can advance the field through AI-augmented workflows and support co-creation with increased productivity.

Conclusion: Structured integration of generative AI with mandatory transparency, clear boundaries, and human-feedback requirements mirrors a minimally regulated industry environment. This pedagogical approach shows that AI can serve as an effective tool — rather than a crutch — when emerging professionals are grounded in HCD principles, enabling AI-augmented workflows that advance the field while preserving humanistic values.

Abstract: As the Human centered design continues to grow, generative AI has the potential to streamline the research process by iterating tasks within established workflows to increase efficiency. However, integrating AI raises concerns surrounding ethical bias, complexity, and the lack of prioritization of humanistic values. Emerging professionals represent a cohort with the opportunity to learn Human Centered Design principles, yet without this foundation AI becomes more of a crutch than a tool, leading to reduced experience with deep work, decreased autonomy, and deskilling of key foundations. Disclosures are a common method to self report AI usage, but they provide little clarification on appropriate implementation and may encourage omission to avoid consequences. This paper reflects on experiences in the Human Centered Design course ITIS8300, which emphasized optimizing user experience, enhancing innovation and collaboration, and improving efficiency through iterative user feedback. A semester long project, structured through milestones and team roles including a generative AI advocate, resulted in a high level disclosure report detailing design processes, methodology, findings, and rationale for AI usage. The course offered freedom in execution while setting clear boundaries for incorporating human feedback, reinforcing justification for HCI workflows and encouraging transparent AI use. This approach mirrors an industry with minimal regulation, demonstrating that when AI usage is safe, justified, and transparent, it can significantly advance the field through AI augmented workflows and support co creation an increase productivity.

</details>


### [82] [A Dynamic Coupling Theory of Expertise Through Thinking Flow and Workflow Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24197)
*Annie Yuan*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper introduces Workflow Cognition, a framework that redefines expertise not as static knowledge or skill accumulation, but as a dynamic cognitive phenomenon emerging from the recursive coupling of Thinking Flow and Workflow Evolution. It proposes a new ontological definition of expertise and lays groundwork for computational modeling in AI+Expert systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing perspectives on expertise (tacit knowledge, deliberate practice, skill acquisition, expert performance) tend to describe conditions or outcomes rather than the underlying cognitive architecture. The authors identify a gap: no framework explains how expertise continuously emerges and evolves through cognition-in-practice.

Method: The paper develops a theoretical framework called Workflow Cognition, defined by the recursive coupling of two constructs: Thinking Flow (perception, interpretation, judgment, decision-making, reflection) and Workflow Evolution (continuous adaptation of actions, task structures, and operational strategies in situated practice). Illustrative comparisons across craft, creative production, education, and leadership demonstrate the framework's applicability, and a Dynamic Coupling Model of Expertise is introduced.

Result: The framework yields a new ontological definition: expertise is an emergent manifestation of Workflow Cognition operating across longitudinal professional experience. Knowledge, skills, decisions, aesthetic preferences, and behaviors are reframed as observable expressions of expertise, not expertise itself. The paper establishes foundations for future constructs: Longitudinal Tacit Cognition, Longitudinal Aesthetic Cognition, and Expertise Workflow Grammar.

Conclusion: Workflow Cognition provides a cognitive ontology of expertise that captures its dynamic, emergent nature. This theoretical contribution supports future computational representations of human expertise within AI+Expert systems, bridging cognitive theory and machine intelligence.

Abstract: Expertise has long been explained through tacit knowledge, deliberate practice, skill acquisition, and expert performance. While these perspectives have advanced understanding of expertise, they often describe its conditions or outcomes rather than the cognitive architecture through which expertise continuously emerges and evolves. This paper proposes Workflow Cognition as a theoretical framework for explaining expertise as a dynamic cognitive phenomenon. Workflow Cognition is defined as the cognitive architecture emerging from the recursive coupling of Thinking Flow and Workflow Evolution. Thinking Flow refers to ongoing processes of perception, interpretation, judgement, decision-making, and reflection; Workflow Evolution refers to the continuous adaptation of actions, task structures, and operational strategies within situated practice. Through their coupling, expertise is not treated as a static accumulation of knowledge or skill, but as an evolving process generated through cognition-in-practice.
  Building on this framework, the paper advances a new ontological definition of expertise: expertise is an emergent manifestation of Workflow Cognition operating across longitudinal professional experience. Knowledge, skills, decisions, aesthetic preferences, and behavioural patterns are therefore interpreted as observable expressions of expertise rather than expertise itself. Drawing on illustrative comparisons across craft, creative production, education, and leadership, the paper introduces a Dynamic Coupling Model of Expertise and establishes a foundation for future work on Longitudinal Tacit Cognition, Longitudinal Aesthetic Cognition, and Expertise Workflow Grammar. The framework contributes a cognitive ontology of expertise and supports future computational representations of human expertise within AI+Expert systems.

</details>


### [83] [Average Rankings Mask Per-Subject Optimality: A Friedman-Nemenyi Benchmark of EEG Motor-Imagery BCI Decoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24394)
*Xavier Vasques,Paul Barbaste,Olivier Oullier*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A large-scale benchmark of 1,056 motor-imagery EEG decoding pipelines across three public datasets shows no single pipeline dominates; the best choice is participant-dependent, with personalized selection yielding ~7 accuracy points over any fixed universal decoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field of EEG-based motor-imagery BCI is plagued by claims that one decoding pipeline (often a spatial or Riemannian method) is broadly superior. This paper tests the weakest version of that claim—that a single pipeline is preferable across participants—under the most favorable conditions (within-session decoding) using rigorous statistical methods.

Method: The authors used the MOABB framework to systematically evaluate 1,056 decoding configurations (feature extractors × scalers × classifiers), yielding >340,000 subject-level model fits. Three public left-vs-right motor-imagery datasets were used: PhysionetMI (109 participants), Cho2017 (52), and Zhou2016 (4), with two frequency bands (8–15 Hz and 8–30 Hz). All models were fit and tested within a single session per participant. Statistical comparisons employed Friedman omnibus tests, Nemenyi critical-difference analysis, and Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with effect sizes.

Result: Covariance tangent-space projection (cov-tgsp) and Common Spatial Patterns (CSP) are the strongest feature extraction families, but their ranking is dataset-dependent. On PhysionetMI (the largest, most heterogeneous cohort), they are statistically indistinguishable (Nemenyi p=0.27; Kendall's W=0.11). The single best pipeline is optimal for only 35% of PhysionetMI participants. Nonlinear descriptors perform best for roughly one-third of participants. Matching pipeline to participant adds approximately seven accuracy points over the best fixed choice. Classifier and scaler choices are secondary to feature representation, and the ranking is not an artifact of dimensionality.

Conclusion: Even under the easiest decoding regime, no single pipeline dominates. This establishes a quantitative lower bound on the personalization problem and makes a strong case for participant-aware model selection rather than pursuing a universal decoder for motor-imagery BCIs.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) is the dominant non-invasive modality for brain-computer interfaces (BCIs), yet reliable decoding of motor imagery is hampered by inter- and intra-individual variability. A recurring claim is that one decoding pipeline, most often a spatial or Riemannian method, is broadly preferable. We test the weakest version of that claim under the most favourable conditions. Using the Mother of All BCI Benchmarks (MOABB) framework, we evaluated 1,056 decoding configurations (feature extractor x scaler x classifier), >340,000 subject-level model fits, across three public left-versus-right motor-imagery datasets (PhysionetMI, 109 participants; Cho2017, 52; Zhou2016, 4) and two frequency bands (8-15 Hz, 8-30 Hz). Every model is fit and tested within a single session of a single participant, the easiest regime, giving every pipeline its best chance. We apply the statistics standard for multi-classifier comparison: Friedman omnibus tests, Nemenyi critical-difference analysis and Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with effect sizes. Covariance tangent-space projection (cov-tgsp) and Common Spatial Patterns (CSP) are the strongest families, but their ordering is dataset-dependent and, on the largest and most heterogeneous cohort (PhysionetMI), statistically indistinguishable (Nemenyi p = 0.27; Kendall's W = 0.11). At the individual level the single best pipeline is optimal for only 35% of PhysionetMI participants, and nonlinear descriptors are best for roughly one third; matching pipeline to participant adds about seven accuracy points over the best fixed choice. The ranking is not an artefact of dimensionality, and classifier and scaler choices are secondary to the feature representation. Even in the easiest regime, no single pipeline dominates: a lower bound on the personalization problem and a quantitative case for participant-aware model selection rather than a universal decoder.

</details>


### [84] [Optimizing Visual Analytics Workflows: From Theory to Practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24454)
*Philip Beaucamp,Alfie Abdul-Rahman,Rita Borgo,Wolfgang Jentner,Saiful Khan,Yiwen Xing,David Ebert,Min Chen*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper investigates the practical application of an information-theoretic methodology for analyzing trade-offs in visual analytics workflows, using action research with case studies across domains to assess feasibility, strengths, and obstacles, and proposes a roadmap for broader deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core challenge is bridging the gap between the theoretical principle of visual analytics—where human and machine processes complement each other—and its practical implementation. While an ontology and information-theoretic methodology exist for reasoning about trade-offs and optimizing VA workflows, there is a need to transform this methodology from theory into actionable practice.

Method: The authors employed the action research method, conducting case studies across different application domains. VA researchers with diverse backgrounds and experiences tested several hypotheses about applying the methodology in practice and proposed ways forward. The paper presents a collective analysis of these experiences.

Result: The study identified the strengths and feasibility of the theory-based methodology, as well as obstacles hindering its broad deployment in practice. Key challenges were documented through the collective analysis of the case study findings.

Conclusion: While the information-theoretic methodology for VA workflow optimization shows promise, significant obstacles to practical deployment exist. The authors outline a roadmap to address these challenges and facilitate broader adoption in real-world visual analytics practice.

Abstract: The principle of visual analytics (VA) is to provide integrated workflows where human-centric processes (e.g., visualization and interaction) and machine-centric processes (e.g., statistics and algorithms) complement each other. To implement this principle in practice, it is necessary to reason about the trade-offs among different processes and make optimal use of them in a workflow. Building on an existing ontology of the methodology for analyzing such trade-offs information-theoretically and for optimizing VA workflows systematically, we investigate ways to transform this methodology from theory to practice. In particular, we adopted the action research method. Through case studies in different application domains, VA researchers with different background knowledge and experiences offered their answers to several hypotheses about using the methodology in practice and proposed ways forward. In this paper, we present our collective analysis, the strengths and feasibility of this theory-based methodology, as well as the obstacles to its broad deployment in practice. To address these challenges, we outline a roadmap to remove such obstacles.

</details>


### [85] [Visualizing "We the People": Bridging the Perception Gap through Pluralistic Data Storytelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24635)
*Lisa Schirch,Beth Goldberg*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Traditional binary data visualizations oversimplify group conflicts and fuel polarization. This paper argues that AI-enabled, distribution-focused, interactive visualizations can highlight nuance and intergroup consensus, using the 2025 "We the People" deliberation as a case study to demonstrate a scalable intervention for strengthening democratic culture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional visual data storytelling uses binary, conflict-heavy graphics that oversimplify intra-group disagreements, erase ambiguity and shared values, and inadvertently foster "us versus them" thinking, thereby increasing political polarization.

Method: The paper examines deliberative technologies that map high-dimensional opinion spaces and visually highlight areas of both consensus and dissensus. As a case study, it analyzes the "We the People" deliberation (Jigsaw & Napolitan Institute, September 2025), in which over 2,400 Americans across all 435 congressional districts participated in an AI-supported, asynchronous dialogue on freedom and equality. AI synthesized long-form text inputs into interactive "opinion landscapes."

Result: The interactive opinion landscapes provided an alternative, pluralistic format for data storytelling that humanized diverse viewpoints and revealed hidden areas of substantial broad consensus that binary visualizations would have obscured.

Conclusion: Shifting from divisive, contrast-heavy visual frameworks to distribution-focused, interactive models is a highly scalable, low-cost intervention capable of bridging perceptual gaps and cultivating a more resilient, collaborative democratic culture.

Abstract: Traditional visual data storytelling relies on binary graphics that depict two simplified groups in conflict. This can increase political polarization by oversimplifying intra-group disagreements and erasing ambiguity and shared ideas or values. This can inadvertently foster "us versus them" thinking. Intentional, pluralistic design choices for AI-enabled digital platforms can produce visualizations that emphasize nuance, opinion distribution, and intergroup commonalities. To demonstrate this potential, we examine deliberative technologies that map high-dimensional opinion spaces and highlight areas of both consensus and dissensus. The paper highlights the We the People deliberation conducted by Jigsaw and the Napolitan Institute in September 2025, which engaged over 2,400 Americans across all 435 congressional districts in an AI-supported, asynchronous dialogue regarding freedom and equality. By utilizing AI to synthesize long-form, text-based participant inputs into interactive "opinion landscapes," the initiative provided an alternative format for pluralistic data storytelling that humanized diverse viewpoints and revealed hidden areas of substantial broad consensus. The paper concludes that shifting from divisive, contrast-heavy visual frameworks to distribution-focused, interactive models represents a highly scalable, low-cost intervention capable of bridging perceptual gaps and cultivating a more resilient, collaborative democratic culture.

</details>


### [86] [It's Complicated: On the Design and Evaluation of AI-Powered AAC Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24854)
*Blade Frisch,Will Wade,Dylan Gaines,Michelle Kinsella,Betts Peters,Tamara Broderick,Keith Vertanen*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper examines the challenges of evaluating AI-powered augmentative and alternative communication (AAC) systems, arguing that current metrics fail to capture the intersectional, multifaceted needs of AAC users. The authors explore six AAC problem spaces, propose AI applications for each, and recommend more robust, intersectionality-aware evaluation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation metrics for AI-enhanced AAC systems are insufficient because they cannot capture the complex, intersectional, and nuanced desires of AAC users, who are multidimensional beings with diverse identities and needs.

Method: The authors conduct a conceptual analysis exploring six distinct AAC problem spaces, examining how AI might be applied within each, and developing recommendations for intersectionality-informed evaluation methods. They also identify cross-cutting issues spanning these problem spaces.

Result: The paper identifies six distinct AAC problem spaces where AI can be applied, highlights the limitations of existing evaluation approaches in addressing intersectional user needs, and proposes robust evaluation frameworks that better account for users' multifaceted identities and preferences.

Conclusion: Evaluating AI-powered AAC requires moving beyond simplistic metrics toward intersectional evaluation methods that honor the complexity of users' lives. The proposed approaches offer a path toward more meaningful, user-centered assessment of AAC technologies.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) can enhance what people who use augmentative and alternative communication (AAC) are able to do with their systems. However, evaluating AI-powered AAC interfaces can be difficult. People are intersectional beings and current evaluation metrics can struggle to capture the multifaceted and nuanced desires people may have for their AAC. We explore the complicated nature of six AAC problem spaces, explore how AI might be used in these spaces, and suggest more robust methods of evaluation that take the intersectional nuances of people into account. We also discuss broader issues that arise across these problem spaces and how they could be addressed using our proposed evaluation methods.

</details>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [87] [EvidenceLens: A Claim-Evidence Matrix for Auditing Financial Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23724)
*Fengchen Gu,Xiaotian Ren,Zhengyong Jiang,Zhilu Zhang,Ángel F. García-Fernández,Angelos Stefanidis,Mian Zhou,Huakang Li,Jionglong Su*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: EvidenceLens is a visual analytics tool that helps financial analysts verify LLM-generated answers by decomposing them into atomic claims and aligning each claim against source evidence (text, tables, charts) through a multimodal claim-evidence matrix that exposes coverage gaps, contradictions, and unsupported assertions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM outputs in financial question answering are difficult to verify because fluent answers can seamlessly blend grounded statements with weak synthesis and unsupported claims across narrative text, tables, and charts. Conventional chat interfaces flatten these distinctions, creating unacceptable risk in high-stakes financial workflows.

Method: The system (1) decomposes LLM answers into atomic claims, (2) runs a lightweight multimodal alignment pipeline that maps each claim against source passages, table cells, and chart regions, (3) summarizes support composition, confidence, and gaps, and (4) renders results in a claim-evidence matrix visualization. A deterministic review-priority ranking maps backend signals into an auditable visual structure, and a JSON-based artifact schema supports reproducibility.

Result: Through representative report-auditing scenarios, EvidenceLens enables analysts to distinguish grounded claims from overconfident synthesis, making coverage, contradiction, and modality imbalance immediately visible—information that conventional chat interfaces obscure.

Conclusion: Treating financial QA as a claim-evidence alignment problem and surfacing alignment results through a multimodal matrix visualization provides an auditable, transparent alternative to opaque LLM chat outputs, supporting verifiability in high-stakes financial analysis.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to answer questions over annual reports, earnings decks, and analyst notes, yet their outputs remain difficult to verify in high-stakes financial workflows. A fluent answer can blend directly grounded statements, weak synthesis, and unsupported claims across narrative text, tables, and charts. We present EvidenceLens, a visual analytics prototype that treats financial question answering as a claim-evidence alignment problem. The system decomposes an answer into atomic claims, summarizes support composition and confidence, support gaps, and coordinates claim-level inspection with source passages, table cells, and chart regions. Its core visual representation is a multimodal claim-evidence matrix that makes coverage, contradiction, and modality imbalance immediately visible. To support reproducibility, we also specify a JSON-based artifact schema, a lightweight multimodal alignment pipeline, and a deterministic review-priority ranking that maps backend signals into an auditable visual structure. Through representative report-auditing scenarios, we show how EvidenceLens helps analysts distinguish grounded claims from overconfident synthesis that conventional chat interfaces flatten.

</details>


### [88] [INSPIRE: Intent-aware Neural Sponsored Product Retrieval for E-commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23889)
*Shasvat Desai,Hong Yao,Utkarsh Porwal,Kuang-chih Lee*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: INSPIRE is an intent-aware retrieval framework for sponsored grocery search that uses LLM-generated structured intent attributes (brand, flavor, dietary constraints, etc.) to augment dense bi-encoder representations, improving query-product matching in the high-stakes Walmart ecommerce context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grocery queries are short, ambiguous, and underspecified, yet food/beverage products often target specific intents (dietary preferences, sizes). At Walmart's massive scale, mismatches between user intent and retrieved sponsored products cause significant losses in engagement and monetization.

Method: A three-stage framework: (1) A teacher LLM generates structured multi-dimensional intent annotations from product titles/descriptions. (2) A lightweight student LLM is fine-tuned via LoRA-based supervised learning to predict intent attributes. (3) Predicted intents are incorporated into query and product representations within a bi-encoder dense retrieval model, enabling intent-augmented matching.

Result: Not explicitly stated in the abstract.

Conclusion: Not explicitly stated in the abstract.

Abstract: Walmart holds the largest share of the U.S. ecommerce grocery market, where food and beverage categories generate some of the highest search traffic and, consequently, drive a substantial portion of sponsored search revenue. At this scale, even small mismatches between user intent and retrieved products can lead to losses in both user engagement and monetization. Yet, understanding user intent in grocery search is inherently challenging. Queries are typically short, ambiguous, and highly diverse, often underspecifying critical preferences. From the advertisers perspective, many products are explicitly designed to target specific intents such as dietary preferences or size variants and must be surfaced at the right moment to be effective. Thus, we propose INSPIRE (Intent aware Neural Sponsored Product Retrieval for Ecommerce), an intent aware retrieval framework for sponsored search that leverages structured intent signals to better align user queries with relevant food and beverage products. INSPIRE represents intent as a set of structured, multi dimensional attributes derived from both user queries and product content, capturing explicit signals (e.g., brand, flavor) as well as implicit preferences (e.g., dietary constraints, cuisine types) that are often not directly expressed in queries. We develop a weakly supervised intent learning pipeline, where a large language model serves as a teacher to generate structured intent annotations from product titles and descriptions. We then distill these annotations by using them to finetune a lightweight student LLM model through LoRA based supervised finetuning that predicts intent attributes. We then introduce an intent augmented dense retrieval framework, where predicted intents are incorporated into query and product representations within a biencoder, enabling more precise matching between queries and sponsored products.

</details>


### [89] [Scaling Dense Retrieval with LLM-Annotated Training Data: Structured Mining and Progressive Curriculum for E-Commerce Sponsored Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23911)
*Md Omar Faruk Rokon,Shasvat Desai,Jhalak Nilesh Acharya,Isha Shah,Kumar Priyam,Brahanyaa Somasundaram,Vamsee Tangirala,Minuteresa Thomas,Vivek Arora,Vijay Manchi,Hong Yao,Kuang-chih Lee*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A scalable pipeline for generating high-quality dense retrieval training data without clicks or manual annotation, using multi-system disagreement mining, LLM-based graded annotation, and progressive curriculum training. Deployed at Walmart, it achieves significant gains in NDCG, reduces embarrassing retrievals, and improves business metrics in A/B testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Click-based training for e-commerce sponsored search suffers from position bias and tail-query sparsity, while manual annotation at scale (hundreds of millions of query-item pairs) is economically infeasible. The authors leverage the insight that heterogeneous retrieval systems disagree on most items, turning this disagreement into a structured training signal.

Method: A three-component end-to-end pipeline: (1) multi-channel retrieval mining from three production systems with rank metadata, (2) graded-relevance annotation via a calibrated three-model cascade achieving 89.1% agreement with human annotators, and (3) three-stage progressive curriculum training across five difficulty levels using 240M+ examples, applied to a two-tower BERT dense retrieval model.

Result: Offline: +5.1% NDCG@10 over click-trained baseline (largest gains on tail queries), embarrassing retrievals drop from 8.7% to 3.5%. Online A/B test (two weeks, tens of millions of ad requests per arm): +2.80% ad spend, +1.4% CTR, +2.8% eCPM, +2.9% click conversion rate.

Conclusion: The work provides a practical, scalable blueprint for replacing click-based training with structured LLM-annotated supervision in production retrieval systems, validated through both offline and online metrics at Walmart's sponsored search.

Abstract: How can we generate high-quality training data for dense retrieval models at production scale, without relying on click signals or manual annotation? This question is critical for e-commerce sponsored search, where click-based training suffers from position bias and tail-query sparsity, and manual labeling at the scale of hundreds of millions of query-item pairs is economically infeasible. Our work is driven by the following insight: heterogeneous retrieval systems disagree on most items they retrieve, and this disagreement creates a natural source of structured training signal -- easy positives where all systems agree, hard positives that only lexical systems find, and hard negatives that fool exactly one system. As our key novelty, we combine three ideas into an end-to-end pipeline: (a) multi-channel retrieval mining with rank metadata from three production systems, (b) graded-relevance annotation by a calibrated three-model cascade ) that reaches 89.1% agreement with trained human annotators, and (c) three-stage progressive curriculum training that organizes 240M+ training examples across five difficulty levels. We deploy the trained two-tower BERT model on Walmart's sponsored search and evaluate it against 30K queries labeled by trained third-party human annotators. First, we show that the system achieves +5.1% NDCG@10 over the click-trained production baseline, with the largest gain on tail queries . Second, we show that embarrassing retrievals (rating 0) drop from 8.7% to 3.5%. Third, a two-week online A/B test with tens of millions of ad requests per arm confirms +2.80% ad spend, +1.4% CTR, +2.8% eCPM, and +2.9% click conversion rate. Overall, our work provides a practical and scalable blueprint for replacing click-based training with structured LLM-annotated supervision in production retrieval systems.

</details>


### [90] [Unified Multi-Task Relevance Modeling for E-Commerce: Comparing Task Routing Architectures Across LLMs and Cross-Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23919)
*Md Omar Faruk Rokon,Jhalak Nilesh Acharya,Shasvat Desai,Hong Yao,Kuang-chih Lee*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A unified multi-task framework with task-specific private layers and majority-vote ensembling achieves state-of-the-art accuracy (89.96%) across six diverse e-commerce entity-pair relevance tasks. The paper reveals an architectural asymmetry: decoder-only LLMs rely heavily on text-prefix task routing, while cross-encoders are robust to its removal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current e-commerce relevance systems deploy separate models for different entity-pair relationship types (query-product matching, product similarity, etc.), preventing knowledge transfer and producing inconsistent relevance signals. The central challenge is building a single model that handles six heterogeneous tasks with varying data volumes, semantic requirements, and potentially conflicting learning signals.

Method: The authors propose a unified multi-task framework trained on six entity-pair tasks under a shared three-point relevance scale. They systematically compare three task routing architectures: (1) text prefix routing, (2) multi-head classification, and (3) multi-head with private transformer layers. These are evaluated across both LoRA-adapted decoder-only LLMs and fully fine-tuned cross-encoders. A majority-vote ensemble further exploits diversity from private-layer routing.

Result: (1) The MHP Ensemble (multi-head with private layers + voting) achieves 89.96% accuracy on 453K test examples, the best across all configurations. (2) Removing text prefixes without private layers severely degrades decoder-only LLMs, while cross-encoders remain robust — revealing an encoder-decoder asymmetry in task identity encoding. (3) Multi-task training yields up to 14% improvement on low-resource tasks over single-task baselines.

Conclusion: A shared multi-task framework with private-layer routing and ensembling can effectively unify diverse e-commerce relevance tasks, outperforming single-task models and enabling positive knowledge transfer, especially for low-resource tasks. The asymmetric dependence of decoder-only vs. encoder-based architectures on task routing mechanisms is a critical design consideration for multi-task NLP systems.

Abstract: How can we build a single relevance model that handles six different entity pair relationship types in e commerce from query product matching to product type similarity when each task has different data volumes, different semantic requirements, and potentially conflicting learning signals? This question is important because current industry practice relies on separate models for each task, preventing knowledge transfer and producing inconsistent relevance signals. Our work is driven by the following insight: encoder based and decoder only models encode task identity through different mechanisms, so the choice of task routing architecture how task identity is communicated to the shared model affects these two families in asymmetric ways. As our key novelty, we combine three ideas: (a) a unified multi task framework that jointly trains on six entity pair tasks under a shared three point relevance scale, (b) a systematic comparison of three task routing architectures (text prefix routing, multi head classification, and multihead with private transformer layers) across both LoRA adapted LLMs and fully finetuned cross encoders, and (c) a majority vote ensemble that exploits the diversity induced by private layer routing. First, we show that the MHP Ensemble (multi head with private layers) achieves 89.96% accuracy on 453K test examples the highest across all configurations . Second, we show that removing text prefixes without private layers causes severe degradation for decoder only LLMs while cross encoders remain robust , suggesting an encoder decoder asymmetry in task identity encoding. Third, we show that multi task training yields up to 14% improvement on low resource tasks over single task baselines.

</details>


### [91] [ChartWalker: Benchmarking the Cross-Chart RAG Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23997)
*Ning Tang,Chenghan Xie,Hanyang Yuan,Yi Li,Renhong Huang,Qian Kou,Xiaofeng Shi,Hua Zhou,Jiarong Xu*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ChartWalker introduces a framework for constructing challenging cross-chart RAG benchmarks using hierarchical knowledge graphs and structure-aware sampling to generate multi-hop QA pairs, releasing ChartWalker-Bench and ChartWalker-Agent to evaluate and improve multi-modal analytical systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for cross-chart RAG either focus on well-structured tables or generate questions via key-point extraction, leading to lexical overlap between queries and evidence, and logically inconsistent reasoning chains. There is a need for a principled, difficulty-controllable benchmark for genuine cross-chart reasoning.

Method: ChartWalker builds a hierarchical knowledge graph from charts that organizes entities and relations by granularity. A structure-aware sampling algorithm then synthesizes semantically coherent, multi-hop reasoning paths across charts, enabling explicit control over query difficulty and granularity for QA generation.

Result: ChartWalker-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark spanning diverse domains and query types, was released. Extensive evaluations across major RAG paradigms reveal significant performance gaps, demonstrating the benchmark's difficulty and diagnostic utility.

Conclusion: ChartWalker provides a robust framework for cross-chart RAG evaluation, exposing limitations in current systems and offering an agentic baseline (ChartWalker-Agent) to guide future multi-modal RAG system design.

Abstract: Cross-Chart Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is critical for complex multi-modal analytical tasks in scientific, business, and political domains. However, existing benchmarks either focus on tables, which are well-structured and textualized, or generate cross-chart questions by simply extracting key points, which often induces lexical overlap between queries and evidence and yields logically inconsistent reasoning chains. To address this, we introduce ChartWalker, a novel framework for constructing challenging cross-chart RAG tasks. ChartWalker features a hierarchical knowledge graph construction method tailored to charts, which organizes entities and relations by granularity to preserve analytical structure.
