We then propose a structure-aware sampling algorithm that synthesizes semantically coherent, multi-hop reasoning paths, enabling explicit control over query difficulty and granularity for QA generation. Built with this framework, we release ChartWalker-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark spanning diverse domains and cross-chart query types. Extensive evaluations across major RAG paradigms reveal significant performance gaps, underscoring the benchmark's difficulty and utility. Furthermore, we provide ChartWalker-Agent, an agentic baseline to facilitate analysis and inspire future system design.

</details>


### [92] [Dialogue to Discovery: Attribute-Aware Preference Elicitation for Conversational Product Search Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24194)
*Sarthak Harne,Natwar Modani,Debabrata Mahapatra,Shubham Agarwal*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: D2D is a preference elicitation framework for conversational product search that dynamically prioritizes informative attribute queries and strategically times recommendations, achieving 22-30% higher target accuracy, 6-16% less abandonment, and 27.5% shorter conversations vs. baselines, with user study validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational product search faces a fundamental trade-off: showing few items demands precise preference elicitation (prolonging conversations and risking abandonment), but rushing recommendations without understanding preferences yields poor matches and degraded UX. Existing systems lack principled mechanisms to balance query informativeness with recommendation timing.

Method: D2D is an attribute-oriented framework that exploits product attribute structure to dynamically steer conversations. It adaptively prioritizes the most informative attribute queries and strategically times product recommendations to minimize premature or off-target suggestions. Evaluation uses three curated Amazon Reviews datasets with simulated conversations under a multi-factor utilitarian patience framework, plus a complementary user study.

Result: D2D achieves a 22.2–29.9% improvement in target-finding accuracy, a 6.6–16.1% reduction in session abandonment, and 27.5% shorter average conversations compared to state-of-the-art baselines. The user study confirms significant gains in user satisfaction and perceived efficiency.

Conclusion: D2D demonstrates that dynamically exploiting product attribute structure for both query selection and recommendation timing yields substantial improvements in accuracy, efficiency, and user satisfaction in conversational product search.

Abstract: Conversational product search assistants offer a more expressive, natural, and interactive alternative to traditional keyword-based product search. With limited screen space, showing only a few items increases the need for precise preference elicitation, which can prolong conversations, leading to user frustration and session abandonment. Conversely, rushing to recommend items without a clear understanding of preferences risks poor matches and a degraded user experience. We present Dialogue to Discovery (D2D), an attribute-oriented preference elicitation framework that dynamically exploits the structure of product attributes to efficiently steer conversations toward the user's desired item. D2D adaptively prioritizes the most informative queries and strategically times product recommendations, reducing premature or off-target suggestions that harm engagement. To evaluate D2D, we curate three datasets from the Amazon Reviews corpus. In simulated conversations modelled using a multi-factor utilitarian patience framework, D2D achieves a 22.2-29.9% improvement in target-finding accuracy, 6.6-16.1% reduction in abandonment, and 27.5% shorter average conversations over the state-of-the-art baselines. A complementary user study further confirms significant gains in both user satisfaction and perceived efficiency.

</details>


### [93] [PETRA: Transforming Web Text for Petroleum-Engineering Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24346)
*Kirill Dubovikov,Omar El Mansouri,Hachem Madmoun,Yanda Li,Sandeep Kumar,Aya El Mir,Supriyo Ghosh,Writabrata Bhattacharya,Adrian Garcia-Garcia,Onkar Pandit,Sunil Kumar Sahu,Federico Castanedo,Larry Murray,Martin Takac,Salem Lahlou*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: PETRA is a large-scale dataset and pipeline (1.36M chunks, ~2B tokens) that converts noisy public web data into a curated petroleum-engineering corpus with synthetic supervision for adapting dense retrievers and rerankers. It boosts in-domain nDCG from 0.703 to 0.763 and improves a public Earth Science benchmark by 44% relative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Petroleum-engineering information retrieval suffers from a supervision gap: relevant textual evidence exists in public web data, but domain-specific relevance labels are scarce, limiting the performance of general-purpose retrievers and rerankers in this specialized domain.

Method: The authors construct PETRA through a multi-stage pipeline: (1) high-recall energy-domain web curation, (2) filtering via an energy-domain classifier achieving 98.4% test accuracy, (3) chunk-grounded query generation using LLMs, (4) LLM-written hard negatives, (5) retrieval-mined candidate lists scored by a teacher model. The resulting dataset feeds into dense retriever training (~859k embedding rows from ~224k anchors) and reranker training (~400k teacher-scored candidate rows). First-stage retrieval uses score fusion. A key finding is that retrieval-mined data only helps when repackaged as teacher-scored candidate lists matching the inference-time candidate distribution.

Result: PETRA improves first-stage in-domain nDCG from 0.703 to 0.763 via score fusion. Reranker adaptation yields a 44% relative improvement on a public Earth Science benchmark and a 23% gain on a six-task reasoning-intensive panel. Notably, high train-holdout accuracy on synthetic labels does not predict actual retrieval gains.

Conclusion: Domain adaptation via curated synthetic supervision (PETRA) substantially improves retrieval and reranking in petroleum engineering. The work highlights that synthetic label accuracy is a poor proxy for downstream retrieval performance and that aligning training candidate distributions with inference-time distributions is critical for reranker effectiveness.

Abstract: Petroleum-engineering search exposes a supervision gap for strong general retrievers: relevant evidence exists in public web text, but domain relevance labels are scarce. To address this gap, we propose PETRA, a large-scale Petroleum Engineering Text for Retrieval Adaptation dataset and pipeline that converts noisy public web data into a curated domain corpus and synthetic supervision for dense retrieval and reranking. PETRA contains 1.36M curated chunks, approximately 2B token equivalents, $\approx$859k, embedding training rows from $\approx$224k anchors, and roughly 400k teacher-scored reranker candidate rows. Its construction combines high-recall energy-domain curation, an energy-domain classifier with 98.4% test accuracy, chunk-grounded query generation, LLM-written hard negatives, and retrieval-mined candidate lists. PETRA improves first-stage in-domain Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain (nDCG) from 0.703 to 0.763 through score fusion. Reranker adaptation improves the public Earth Science benchmark by 44% relative and a six-task reasoning-intensive panel by 23%. Failed training recipes show that high train-holdout accuracy on synthetic labels does not predict retrieval gains; retrieval-mined data helps only after being repackaged as teacher-scored candidate lists sampled from the inference-time candidate distribution.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [94] [Beyond the Autoregressive Horizon: A Comprehensive Survey of Diffusion Models, World Modelling, and State Space Models for Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23690)
*Kishan Maharaj,Ashita Saxena,Srikanth Tamilselvam*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper argues that autoregressive (AR) models, despite their success in code generation, suffer from structural limitations in reasoning, planning, and execution grounding. It surveys three emerging non-AR paradigms—Diffusion Models, Code World Models, and State Space Models—and connects them to cognitive neuroscience to envision "System 2" code generation agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The heavy skew of existing code intelligence literature toward autoregressive models has created blind spots. AR models' next-token prediction paradigm inherently restricts global planning, long-range dependency maintenance, and grounding in program execution semantics. The authors aim to broaden the research landscape by highlighting alternative architectures that could overcome these logic and scaling bottlenecks.

Method: This is a position/survey paper. It systematically discusses three emerging paradigms: (1) Diffusion Models for code generation via holistic denoising, capturing long-range syntactic constraints; (2) Code World Models (CWMs) that simulate execution states to ground reasoning; and (3) State Space Models (SSMs) offering linear-time efficiency for massive contexts. The analysis is further enriched by drawing parallels with cognitive neuroscience, particularly the System 1/System 2 dual-process theory.

Result: The paper identifies key advantages of each paradigm: Diffusion Models excel at capturing global syntactic structure; CWMs enable execution-aware reasoning; SSMs provide efficiency for long-context code understanding. It synthesizes these into a roadmap for "System 2" code agents capable of deliberate, multi-step reasoning, contrasting with the fast, intuitive "System 1" style of current AR models.

Conclusion: Overcoming the limitations of next-token prediction requires embracing architectural diversity. Diffusion Models, CWMs, and SSMs each address distinct shortcomings of AR models. Integrating insights from cognitive neuroscience—specifically the System 2 framework—offers a promising path toward more deliberate, logically robust code generation agents. The field should move beyond its AR-centric focus.

Abstract: Autoregressive (AR) language models have driven significant progress in automated software engineering, enabling powerful code generation and assistance systems. However, the next-token prediction paradigm introduces structural limitations for code reasoning, including restricted global planning, challenges in maintaining long-range dependencies, and limited grounding in program execution semantics. Noting the heavy skewness of existing literature towards AR models, we discuss emerging paradigms that could potentially overcome the logic and scaling bottlenecks of next-token prediction by unlocking next-generation architectural capabilities for code intelligence. Specifically, we discuss the potential of Diffusion Models, which generate code via holistic denoising that captures long-range syntactic constraints often missed by AR models. We also discuss Code World Models (CWMs), which simulate execution states to support reasoning, and State Space Models (SSMs), which provide linear-time efficiency for massive contexts. By connecting these developments with findings from cognitive neuroscience, we outline directions for developing "System 2" code generation agents.

</details>


### [95] [From Heuristics to Transformers: A Comprehensive Survey of Type Inference from Stripped Binaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23692)
*Hua Zheng,Yuhang Guo,Kuanishbay Sadatdiynov,Cheng Wen,Muhammad Sadiq,Dugang Liu,Jawwad Ahmed Shamsi,Anam Qureshi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This survey chronicles the evolution of binary type inference techniques for stripped executables, tracing progression from rule-based heuristics and constraint-solving (e.g., BITY, BinSub) through context-aware neural models (e.g., EKLAVYA, CATI) to modern Transformer and GNN architectures (e.g., SeeType, TYGR), while identifying core challenges like optimization-induced semantics loss and proposing future neuro-symbolic directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovering high-level type information from stripped binaries is foundational to reverse engineering, vulnerability analysis, and decompilation, yet remains a persistent challenge due to the absence of symbol tables and debugging information. The paper is motivated by the need to systematically track how type inference methods have evolved and to identify unresolved bottlenecks.

Method: The paper conducts a literature survey that taxonomizes binary type inference approaches into three generations: (1) early rule-based heuristics and constraint-solving techniques employing "duck typing" principles (BITY, BinSub); (2) context-aware neural models (EKLAVYA, CATI); and (3) state-of-the-art Transformer-based and Graph Neural Network architectures (SeeType, TYGR). The survey analyzes each paradigm's methodology, assumptions, and limitations.

Result: The survey identifies a clear evolutionary trajectory from hand-crafted heuristics to learned representations, documents persistent core challenges—particularly optimization-induced semantics loss (where compiler optimizations obscure or eliminate type-revealing patterns) and structural type recovery (reconstructing composite types like structs and classes)—and highlights the emerging promise of neuro-symbolic inference as a synthesis of data-driven learning and formal logical reasoning.

Conclusion: Binary type inference has matured from simple duck typing to sophisticated neural architectures, yet fundamental gaps remain, especially in recovering structural types and handling heavily optimized code. The authors advocate for neuro-symbolic approaches that combine the pattern-recognition strengths of deep learning with the rigor of constraint-based reasoning as the most promising path forward.

Abstract: The recovery of high-level type information from stripped binaries-executables devoid of symbol tables and debugging information-is a cornerstone of software reverse engineering, vulnerability analysis, and decompilation. This survey tracks the evolution of binary type inference from early rule-based heuristics and static analysis to modern deep learning architectures. We analyze the shift from "duck typing" and constraint-solving techniques (e.g., BITY, BinSub) to context-aware neural models (e.g., EKLAVYA, CATI) and finally to state-of-the-art Transformer and Graph Neural Network (GNN) architectures (e.g., SeeType, TYGR). We identify core challenges, including optimization-induced semantics loss and structural type recovery, and propose future research directions in neuro-symbolic inference.

</details>


### [96] [Privacy Engineering: A Systematic Literature Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23696)
*Nemania Borovits,Damian Andrew Tamburri,Willem-Jan van den Heuvel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A systematic review of 90 studies (2018–2025) synthesizes GDPR-driven privacy engineering into thirteen dimensions forming two cores—PETs + Metrics + Verification, and Governance + Transparency + Organizational Measures—mediated by Modeling & Specification. Lifecycle mapping reveals concentration patterns across phases, domain-specific reweightings are identified, and under-researched areas (IRM, LM, DMPL) are flagged for future work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GDPR-imposed privacy obligations are increasingly shaping software engineering practice, yet the research landscape of privacy engineering remains fragmented. The authors aim to chart, synthesize, and structure this body of work to provide a coherent map for both practitioners and researchers.

Method: Systematic literature review with thematic synthesis of 90 studies published between 2018 and 2025. The synthesis identifies thirteen dimensions of privacy engineering, maps them onto the software development lifecycle (requirements, design, implementation, verification, operation, decommissioning), and examines how domain context reweights these dimensions.

Result: Thirteen dimensions cluster into two recurrent cores: (1) PETs with Privacy Metrics and Verification & Testing; (2) Governance & Accountability with Transparency & Communication and Organizational Measures. Modeling & Specification mediates between them. Lifecycle concentrations: requirements/design (MS, GA), implementation/verification (PETs, VT, PM, TC), operation/decommissioning (GA, OM, DSRM, IRM, LM). Domain analysis shows healthcare weights GA+VT+PETs; IoT/edge weights PETs+VT+PM; web measurement weights TC+VT; AI/ML weights PETs+PM. IRM, LM, and DMPL are under-addressed.

Conclusion: The synthesis delivers a practical, structured map of GDPR privacy engineering research and a replication-ready scaffold for ongoing assessment. Future work should prioritize Incident Response & Management, Lifelong Management, and Data Minimization & Purpose Limitation.

Abstract: Privacy obligations under GDPR increasingly shape software engineering. We synthesize 90 studies from 2018 to 2025 using a systematic review with thematic synthesis to chart privacy engineering. Thirteen dimensions form two recurrent cores: Privacy Enhancing Technologies (PETs) with Privacy Metrics (PM) and Verification and Testing (VT) and Governance and Accountability (GA) with Transparency and Communication (TC) and Organizational Measures (OM). Modeling and Specification (MS) mediates between the cores. Lifecycle mapping shows concentrations at requirements and design (MS, GA), at implementation and verification (PETs, VT, PM, TC) and at operation and decommissioning (GA, OM, Data Subject Rights Management (DSRM), Incident Response and Management (IRM), Lifelong Management (LM)). Handoffs link models to rules and tests, mechanisms to metrics and deployments such as enclaves and ledgers to governance records. Domains reweight but do not alter structure: healthcare weights GA with VT and PETs, IoT and edge weight PETs with VT and PM at device and edge, web measurement weights TC with VT, AI and ML weight PETs with PM. IRM, LM and Data Minimization and Purpose Limitation (DMPL) are less often primary foci, signaling priorities for future work. The results provide a practical map and a replication-ready scaffold for assessment and updates.

</details>


### [97] [From Task-Guided Conversational Graphs to Goal-Oriented Dialogue Runtimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23797)
*Mariano Garralda-Barrio*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: GODR introduces a design pattern for complex LLM conversations where multiple interdependent goals can be suspended, resumed, revised, or invalidated. It treats goals, lifecycle states, invalidation rules, and resumption contracts as first-class runtime objects, complementing—rather than replacing—existing graph/multi-agent orchestration frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph and multi-agent orchestration frameworks for LLM workflows lack robust mechanisms for conversational continuity when users juggle multiple interdependent objectives. Goal suspension, resumption, revision, and cross-goal invalidation cannot be reliably recovered from agent identity, chat history, or execution-graph position alone, leaving a gap at the high-complexity end of the design space.

Method: The paper proposes GODR, a framework-neutral design pattern that reifies goals, task frames, lifecycle state, invalidation rules, and resumption contracts as first-class runtime objects. It delegates bounded execution to existing graph runtimes, agents, tools, or APIs, and provides architecture-selection criteria. The work is conceptual and does not include empirical evaluation.

Result: No empirical results are reported. The paper frames evaluation as an agenda for future empirical validation and provides architecture-selection criteria for practitioners considering GODR adoption.

Conclusion: GODR fills a gap in LLM workflow orchestration for complex, interruptible, multi-domain conversations where goal continuity is critical. It is positioned as a complement to existing graph-based frameworks, not a replacement, and invites future empirical validation.

Abstract: Graph and multi-agent orchestration frameworks make production large language model (LLM) workflows practical, but they do not by themselves solve conversational continuity when users maintain several interdependent objectives. This conceptual systems paper focuses on the high-complexity end of that design space, where goals can be suspended, resumed, revised, and invalidated by actions in other goals. We introduce the Goal-Oriented Dialogue Runtime (GODR), a framework-neutral design pattern that treats goals, task frames, lifecycle state, invalidation rules, and resumption contracts as first-class runtime objects while delegating bounded execution to graph runtimes, agents, tools, or application programming interfaces (APIs). GODR is not proposed as a replacement for workflow graphs in simple guided processes; it is intended for complex, multi-domain, interruptible conversations where objective continuity cannot be recovered reliably from agent identity, chat history, or execution-graph position alone. The paper formalizes the problem, proposes runtime objects and architecture-selection criteria, and frames evaluation as an agenda for future empirical validation rather than as a measured performance claim.

</details>


### [98] [SemChunk-C: Semantic Segmentation for C Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23697)
*Boris Nazarov,Darya Frolova,Shaked Leibzirer,Pavel Kisilev*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces SemChunk-C, a family of lightweight LLM-based models (17M–150M parameters) for semantic code chunking of C-family languages, defining chunk categories and using LLMs to identify boundaries and assign functional attributes, outperforming larger models while improving downstream task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing chunking methods for C-family languages (fixed-size windows, heuristic splitting, syntax-based tools) fail to capture meaningful functional units due to complex syntax, macro expansion, and irregular structural patterns. This limits the effectiveness of retrieval and other downstream LLM-driven tasks, motivating a semantic approach to code chunking.

Method: The authors (1) define a set of code chunk categories; (2) train an LLM-based classifier to identify chunk boundaries and assign descriptive functional attributes (categories) to each chunk, leveraging contextual semantic understanding for flexible boundary adaptation; and (3) introduce SemChunk-C, a family of models built on the first four Ettin encoders with 17M, 32M, 68M, and 150M parameters, trained to identify cohesive code units such as data structures, interface blocks, and other components.

Result: SemChunk-C achieves high boundary accuracy and semantic coherence on various datasets, matching or outperforming chunkers based on much larger code-oriented LLMs. The approach also demonstrates robustness on real-world code with challenging constructs like nested definitions and macros, and shows improved performance on downstream tasks evaluated on curated benchmarks.

Conclusion: Lightweight, semantically-aware chunking models can effectively segment C-family code into meaningful functional units, surpassing larger models in chunking quality while being computationally efficient, and providing tangible benefits for downstream code-related tasks.

Abstract: Semantic segmentation of code written in a C-family language remains a challenging problem, due to the language's complex syntax, macro expansion, and irregular structural patterns. Existing chunking methods, such as fixed-sized windows, heuristic splitting, and syntax-based tools, often fail to capture meaningful functional units, limiting the efficacy of retrieval and other downstream LLM driven tasks.
  In this paper, we address the problem of chunking in C-related languages. First, we define a set of code chunk categories. Second, we train an LLM-based classifier to a) identify chunk boundaries, and b) assign each chunk a descriptive functional attribute (a category), which can be useful for downstream tasks. By leveraging the LLM's ability to capture semantic context within the code, we assume flexible chunk boundaries, allowing to adapt to the specific structure and context of each instance. Third, we introduce SemChunk-C, a family of lightweight language models for semantic chunking of C-related files (.c, .cpp, .h, .cs, etc.). These models are based on the first four Ettin encoders [1] with 17M, 32M, 68M, and 150M parameters. Despite their relatively small size, they are capable of identifying cohesive code units, such as data structures, interface blocks, and other components. Furthermore, we demonstrate the robustness of our approach on real-world code, including challenging constructs such as nested definitions and macros.
  We test our approach on various datasets, and show that it achieves high boundary accuracy and semantic coherence, matching or outperforming chunkers that are based on much larger code-oriented LLMs. We also validate the improved performance of the downstream tasks on a few curated benchmarks.

</details>


### [99] [A Unified Framework for Runtime Verification and Model-Based Diagnosis in LOLA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23720)
*Raik Hipler,Martin Leucker,Patrick Rodler*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: An integrated framework combining runtime verification and model-based diagnosis using the LOLA stream specification language, enabling continuous online fault detection and localization within a single formalism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional approaches to runtime verification and model-based diagnosis rely on separate toolchains and formalisms, creating integration gaps. The authors aim to unify these two tasks—fault detection and fault localization—into a single, continuous online process to reduce complexity and improve efficiency.

Method: The framework encodes system descriptions, component health states, and observations into the LOLA stream specification language. LOLA's stream-based semantics allow simultaneous reasoning over temporal behavior and health states. The approach handles both time-invariant (persistent) and transient faults, and supports nondeterministic observations naturally within the stream model.

Result: The integrated framework achieves continuous online fault detection and localization in a unified manner, eliminating the need for separate toolchains. It successfully handles multiple fault types (time-invariant, transient) and nondeterministic observations within the LOLA formalism.

Conclusion: Unifying runtime verification and model-based diagnosis within the LOLA stream specification language is feasible and advantageous, offering a streamlined, single-formalism approach to online fault monitoring and diagnosis.

Abstract: We present an integrated framework that unifies runtime verification and model-based diagnosis within the stream specification language LOLA. By encoding system descriptions, component health states, and observations into a single stream-based formalism, the approach enables continuous, online fault localization directly alongside fault detection, without requiring separate toolchains. The framework supports both time-invariant and transient faults, and naturally accommodates nondeterministic observations.

</details>


### [100] [JupOtter: Cell-Level Bug Detection in Jupyter Notebooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23877)
*Lukas Ottenhof,Thibaud Lutellier*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: JupOtter is a bug detection system for Jupyter Notebooks that uses cell-aware tokenization and cell-level prediction, outperforming static analyzers and LLMs on two of three benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Jupyter Notebooks are shifting from prototyping to complex program development, causing a surge of buggy notebooks on GitHub. Existing bug detection tools are not designed for the unique cell-based structure of notebooks, motivating a specialized approach.

Method: JupOtter introduces three key components: (1) a notebook-specific tokenizer that preserves cell boundaries and structure, (2) a fine-grained cell-level bug prediction model, and (3) OtterDataset, a new dataset of 21,000+ annotated notebooks with cell-level bug labels.

Result: JupOtter achieved superior cell-level bug detection F1 scores compared to static analyzers and large language models on two out of three evaluation datasets.

Conclusion: Notebook-specific design (cell-aware tokenization + cell-level prediction) yields meaningful gains over general-purpose tools for Jupyter Notebook bug detection.

Abstract: Jupyter Notebooks are an increasingly popular coding environment used across many domains, especially in Python-based data science and scientific computing. Originally used for prototyping and interactive exploration, notebooks are increasingly used to develop more complex programs, leading to a rapid rise in buggy notebooks on platforms like GitHub. To address this trend, we present JupOtter, a bug detection system designed specifically for Jupyter Notebooks. JupOtter features three novel contributions: (1) a notebook-specific tokenization strategy that preserves cell structure, (2) a cell-level bug prediction technique, and (3) a new labeled dataset, OtterDataset, containing over 21,000 notebooks annotated for fine-grained cell-level bug detection. JupOtter achieves cell-level bug detection F1 scores that surpass static analyzers and large language models in two out of three evaluation datasets.

</details>


### [101] [Automated Summarization of Software Documents: An LLM-based Multi-Agent Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24689)
*Duc S. H. Nguyen,Minh T. Nguyen,Phuong T. Nguyen,Juri Di Rocco,Davide Di Ruscio*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Metagente is an LLM-based multi-agent system using a Teacher-Student architecture to generate concise, accurate summaries of software documentation, outperforming baselines on real-world datasets for requirements analysis and technical documentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs and MAS are transforming software engineering, but there remains a need for effective summarization of software documentation to streamline SE workflows. Existing approaches lack the precision and relevance needed for requirements analysis and technical documentation.

Method: The paper proposes Metagente, an LLM-based MAS employing a Teacher-Student architecture where multiple LLM agents collaborate — a Teacher agent guides and refines the outputs of Student agents to enhance summary relevance and precision.

Result: Empirical evaluation on real-world datasets shows Metagente outperforms considered baselines, particularly improving summarization quality for requirements analysis and technical documentation tasks.

Conclusion: Metagente demonstrates the transformative potential of LLM-based MAS for software engineering summarization, while highlighting challenges and future research directions for seamless integration of these technologies into SE practices.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) and LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) are revolutionizing software engineering (SE) by advancing automation, decision-making, and knowledge processing. Their recent application to SE tasks has already shown promising results. In this paper, we focus on summarization as a key application area. We present Metagente, an LLM-based MAS designed to generate concise and accurate summaries of software documentation. Metagente employs a Teacher-Student architecture where multiple LLM agents collaborate to enhance relevance and precision of produced summaries. An empirical evaluation on real-world datasets demonstrates Metagente's effectiveness in streamlining workflows, outperforming the considered baselines. The evaluation provides evidence that Metagente improves summarization for requirements analysis and technical documentation. Our findings underscore the transformative potential of these technologies in SE, while identifying challenges and future research directions for their seamless integration.

</details>


### [102] [Bit-Precise Conformance Testing of Simulink Model Checkers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24719)
*Daisuke Ishii,Takashi Tomita,Toshiaki Aoki,Hideaki Takai*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper formalizes the bit-precise semantics of basic Simulink block types and proposes a conformance testing methodology to evaluate Simulink model checkers (SLDV and SmtMC) against the Simulink simulator. Experiments on 10 block types reveal SmtMC achieves 100% conformance, while SLDV shows only 80–96% pass rates due to errors, corner cases, and timeouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The absence of a complete, bit-precise formal semantics for Simulink undermines the reliability of existing verification tools (SLDV, SmtMC). These tools may analyze models differently from the actual Simulink simulator, raising doubts about their correctness. A systematic assessment of checker quality is needed.

Method: 1) Formalization of basic Simulink block types, including feature sets and bit-precise behavioral definitions. 2) A conformance testing method operating at the block-type level, measuring two relations: (i) model checker vs. Simulink simulation results, and (ii) SmtMC vs. SLDV results, using pass-rate metrics from test suites. 3) Empirical evaluation across 10 block types.

Result: SmtMC passed all test cases (100%). SLDV achieved only 94–96% conformance with Simulink simulation and 80–90% conformance with SmtMC. Failures were attributed to implementation errors, mishandled corner cases, and timeouts in SLDV.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that formalizing bit-precise block semantics and systematic conformance testing can expose significant reliability gaps in Simulink verification tools. SmtMC proves more faithful to Simulink's numerical behavior than SLDV, and the identified failure causes provide actionable insights for tool improvement.

Abstract: MATLAB/Simulink provides a practical modeling language and a simulation engine for the development of cyber-physical systems. To ensure the quality of the developed models, there are formal verification tools available, such as Simulink Design Verifier (SLDV) and third-party SMT-based model checkers (SmtMC). However, due to the absence of a semantics of Simulink that covers every element of models and the details of its numerical behavior, the reliability of the model checkers themselves is often doubtful, potentially analyzing models differently from the simulator. This work aims to verify the quality of the Simulink model checkers by addressing the following items. 1) Formalization of the basic block types of Simulink. It involves defining block type feature sets and the bit-precise behavior of the blocks. 2) A method for testing bit-precise conformance relations among the tools for each block type. The pass rate of our test suites measures (i) conformance of model checking results with simulation results by Simulink and (ii) conformance between the results of SmtMC and SLDV. 3) Experiment to perform tests on 10 block types. We confirmed that SmtMC efficiently passed all test cases, while SLDV achieved pass rates of only 94-96% and 80-90% for conformance (i) and (ii), respectively. We analyzed the causes of failed tests, such as errors, corner cases, and timeouts.

</details>


### [103] [Two-Level vs. Multi-Level Modelling: An Empirical Study of Cascading Maintenance Burden](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24721)
*Yuhong Fu,Weixing Zhang,Bowen Jiang,Haowei Cheng,Georg Grossmann,Karamjit Kaur,Matt Selway,Markus Stumptner*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a pre-registered, mutation-based empirical comparison testing whether multi-level modelling (MLM) reduces co-evolution burden compared to two-level modelling (2LM). By constructing semantically equivalent MLM counterparts from published 2LM co-evolution scenarios and applying identical mutations, the study measures post-change inconsistencies and modification footprint through automated consistency checking and hypothesis tests, providing the first controlled empirical framework for assessing paradigm-level structural effects on cascading maintenance burden.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In MDE, 2LM fragments domain knowledge across metamodel and model levels, making co-evolution a persistent source of inconsistencies and effort when core definitions change. While MLM is claimed to reduce this burden through structural unification, no controlled, paired comparison against 2LM has been conducted to test this claim empirically.

Method: A pre-registered, mutation-based empirical comparison: (1) curate a corpus of published 2LM co-evolution scenarios; (2) construct semantically equivalent MLM counterparts; (3) apply identical evolution mutations to both paradigms; (4) measure outcomes via automated consistency checking; (5) evaluate using pre-registered hypothesis tests. Positive controls and a blinded mapping protocol are employed to guard against bias.

Result: The paper describes the experimental design and benchmarking protocol rather than reporting completed experimental results. It operationalises co-evolution burden as two automatically measurable outcome variables and delivers a reusable framework for replication.

Conclusion: This design establishes the first empirical framework for determining whether paradigm-level structural choices (MLM vs. 2LM) affect cascading maintenance burden, operationalising co-evolution burden through automatically measurable variables and providing a reusable benchmarking protocol for future replication and extension.

Abstract: When a core definition changes, every dependent artefact must be updated, a cascading problem central to software maintenance. In Model-Driven Engineering (MDE), the dominant two-level modelling (2LM) paradigm fragments domain knowledge across metamodel and model artefacts that must be kept mutually consistent, making co-evolution a persistent source of inconsistencies and effort. Multi-level modelling (MLM) unifies these artefacts and is claimed to reduce co-evolution burden, but this has not been tested in a controlled, paired comparison against 2LM. We hypothesise that MLM's structural unification yields fewer post-change inconsistencies and a smaller modification footprint than 2LM for semantically equivalent evolution scenarios. To test this, we present a pre-registered, mutation-based empirical comparison of co-evolution behaviour in both paradigms. From a curated corpus of published 2LM co-evolution scenarios, we construct semantically equivalent MLM counterparts, apply identical evolution mutations to both, and measure outcomes through automated consistency checking and pre-registered hypothesis tests. Positive controls and a blinded mapping protocol guard against bias. This design provides the first empirical framework for assessing whether paradigm-level structural choices affect cascading maintenance burden, operationalising co-evolution burden as two automatically measurable outcome variables and delivering a reusable benchmarking protocol for replication and extension.

</details>


### [104] [AutoSpec: Safety Rule Evolution for LLM Agents via Inductive Logic Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24245)
*Pingchuan Ma,Zhaoyu Wang,Zimo Ji,Yuguang Zhou,Zhantong Xue,Zongjie Li,Shuai Wang,Xiaoqin Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: AutoSpec is a framework that automatically refines expert-written safety rules for LLM agents using inductive logic programming (ILP) and counterexample-guided inductive synthesis (CEGIS). By iteratively mining false positives/negatives from annotated traces and using ILP to identify discriminative predicates, it produces interpretable, high-quality rules that achieve F1 scores of 0.98 and 0.93 across two domains while reducing false positives by up to 94%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents that autonomously use tools pose safety risks (destructive commands, data leaks, constraint violations). Existing approaches suffer a critical tradeoff: hand-crafted safety rules are interpretable but brittle—conservative rules cause high false positives, while permissive rules miss unsafe behaviors (high false negatives). Neural classifiers, though accurate, lack the interpretability needed for safety-critical deployments. A method is needed that produces rules that are both precise and interpretable.

Method: AutoSpec uses counterexample-guided inductive synthesis (CEGIS) with inductive logic programming (ILP). Starting from expert-designed safety rules and a stream of user-annotated (safe/unsafe) execution traces, it iteratively: (1) evaluates current rules to find false-positive and false-negative counterexamples; (2) applies ILP to mine predicates that discriminate between these counterexamples (frequent in false negatives but rare in false positives, or vice versa); (3) generates candidate rule edits using these predicates; and (4) verifies candidates to select the best revision. The ILP-based predicate mining dramatically prunes the exponential search space of possible rule edits. Iteration continues until convergence.

Result: Evaluated on 291 execution traces across code execution and embodied agent domains, AutoSpec achieves rule F1 scores of 0.98 and 0.93 respectively, reduces false positives by up to 94% while maintaining high recall, and converges within 4–5 iterations. The ILP-guided approach achieves up to 4.8× higher F1 than heuristic CEGIS. The learned rules remain human-readable, auditable, and generalize to unseen scenarios.

Conclusion: AutoSpec demonstrates that ILP-guided CEGIS can automatically evolve brittle expert safety rules into precise, interpretable, and generalizable rules for LLM agent safety, effectively resolving the precision–recall–interpretability tradeoff that plagues both hand-crafted rules and neural classifiers.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly automate complex tasks by integrating language models with external tools and environments. However, their autonomy poses significant safety risks: agents may execute destructive commands, leak sensitive data, or violate domain constraints. Existing safety approaches face a fundamental tradeoff: hand-crafted rules are interpretable but brittle, with overly conservative rules blocking safe operations (high false positives) while permissive rules miss unsafe behaviors (high false negatives). Neural classifiers lack the interpretability required for safety-critical deployments.
  We present AutoSpec, a framework that automatically evolves deployed expert-designed safety rules from user safe/unsafe annotations through counterexample-guided inductive synthesis (CEGIS) guided by inductive logic programming (ILP). Starting from the expert rules and a stream of annotated traces, AutoSpec iteratively evaluates rules, mines false-positive and false-negative counterexamples, uses ILP to learn which predicates discriminate them, generates candidate rule edits, and verifies candidates to select the best revision. The key insight is that ILP efficiently identifies predicates that appear frequently in false negatives but rarely in false positives (or vice versa), dramatically pruning the exponential search space of rule edits. This continues until convergence, producing interpretable rules that balance precision and recall.
  We evaluate AutoSpec on 291 execution traces spanning code execution and embodied agent domains. AutoSpec raises rule F1 to 0.98 and 0.93 across the two domains, achieving up to 94% false positive reduction while maintaining high recall, and converges within 4-5 iterations. The ILP-guided approach achieves up to 4.8x higher F1 than heuristic CEGIS. The learned rules are human-readable, auditable, and generalize to unseen scenarios.

</details>


### [105] [MANGO: Automated Multi-Agent Test Oracle Generation for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24815)
*Pablo Valle,Shaukat Ali,Aitor Arrieta,Lionel Briand*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: MANGO is a multi-agent framework that automatically generates fine-grained test oracles from natural-language task descriptions for VLA-enabled robots, enabling detailed failure localization beyond traditional end-state symbolic oracles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test oracles for VLA robotic systems are manually constructed, costly, task/environment-specific, and only provide coarse end-state pass/fail assessments without insight into intermediate behavior or fault localization.

Method: MANGO employs three collaborative agents (Generator, Assessor, Judge) to: (1) produce a reusable library of atomic tasks, (2) generate simulator-grounded oracle definitions per atomic task, and (3) decompose complex instructions into ordered atomic action sequences with corresponding oracles, refined iteratively through structured feedback.

Result: Evaluated on LIBERO_10 and RoboCasa Humanoid Tabletop benchmarks, MANGO-generated oracles detected a comparable number of failures as symbolic oracles while accurately localizing faults and providing richer diagnostic information. Ablation studies validated component contributions and the effect of initial task set selection.

Conclusion: Automatic fine-grained test oracle generation for VLA-enabled robot testing is feasible and effective, offering scalable, reusable, and diagnostically richer alternatives to manually constructed symbolic oracles.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are emerging robotic control systems that integrate perception, language understanding, and action generation in a unified architecture. Existing testing approaches for VLA-enabled robots rely on manually constructed symbolic test oracles that determine task success from final environment states. These oracles are costly to construct, require domain expertise, and are often tightly coupled to specific tasks and environments, limiting scalability and reuse. Furthermore, they provide only end-state assessments of task outcomes, offering limited insight into intermediate behavior and fault localization. To address these limitations, we introduce MANGO, a multi-agent framework that automatically generates fine-grained oracles from natural-language descriptions of robotic tasks. MANGO first generates a reusable library of atomic tasks, then generates simulator-grounded oracle definitions for each atomic task, and finally produces executable fine-grained oracles by decomposing complex instructions into ordered sequences of atomic actions and corresponding oracles. The framework uses collaborative Generator, Assessor, and Judge agents that iteratively refine generated artifacts through structured feedback. We evaluate MANGO on the LIBERO_10 and RoboCasa Humanoid Tabletop benchmarks. Results show that MANGO generates executable, fine-grained oracles that detect a similar number of failures as symbolic oracles while accurately localizing them and providing richer diagnostic information. Through ablation studies, we further analyzed component contributions and the effect of initial task set, while preserving oracle quality. Overall, the results show the feasibility and effectiveness of test oracle generation for VLA-enabled robots testing.

</details>


### [106] [EnerInfer: Energy-Aware On-Device LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23001)
*Bohua Zou,Nian Liu,Binqi Sun,Matteo Mascherin,Debayan Roy,Yutao Liu,Yu Peng,Ning Jia,Haibo Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: EnerInfer is an on-device LLM inference framework that exploits NPU/memory frequency slack to improve energy efficiency and thermal management without degrading quality of experience, achieving up to 65% energy savings on phones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device LLM inference faces critical energy and thermal bottlenecks. Existing systems blindly optimize for decoding speed, overlooking that modest frequency reductions can preserve QoE while significantly improving efficiency. However, realizing this in production is difficult because optimal settings vary by model, engine, platform, and runtime conditions; commercial devices lack component-level power sensing; and thermal behavior depends on complex request/response dynamics.

Method: EnerInfer uses three key techniques: (1) disaggregated, model-structure-aware prediction to estimate throughput and power for unseen LLMs across NPU/DDR frequency settings without per-model profiling; (2) ranking-driven online feedback to select QoE-satisfying efficient configurations under runtime interference; and (3) lightweight limited-horizon thermal prediction to dynamically switch between energy-optimized and thermally constrained inference modes.

Result: On real-world LLMs, EnerInfer improved energy efficiency by up to 65% on phones, 12% on a laptop, and 24% on a development board, all without violating quality of experience constraints.

Conclusion: EnerInfer demonstrates that intelligently managing NPU/memory frequencies with model-structure-aware prediction and thermal feedback can unlock substantial energy savings for on-device LLM inference while maintaining user-perceived performance.

Abstract: On-device LLM inference is increasingly attractive for privacy-preserving, reliable, and cost-effective deployment, yet its energy and thermal costs remain a critical bottleneck. Existing systems primarily optimize for decoding speed, implicitly assuming that faster execution is always preferable. We show instead that on-device LLM inference often has exploitable configuration slack: modestly lowering NPU and memory frequencies preserves quality of experience (QoE) while substantially improving energy efficiency and reducing heat.
  Realizing this opportunity in production is challenging. The most energy-efficient NPU/DDR setting varies with the model, inference engine, platform, and runtime conditions, with no stable ranking across configurations. Commercial devices further lack component-level power sensing, and shell temperature evolves with request arrivals, response lengths, and thermal history. To address these challenges, we propose EnerInfer, the first on-device LLM inference framework that jointly manages energy efficiency, throughput, and thermal comfort for LLM workloads. EnerInfer replaces per-model profiling and sensor-heavy control with disaggregated, model-structure-aware prediction and ranking-driven online feedback. It predicts throughput and power for unseen LLMs across NPU/DDR frequency settings, selects QoE-satisfying efficient configurations under runtime interference, and uses lightweight limited-horizon thermal prediction to dynamically switch between energy-optimized and thermally constrained inference. Evaluations on real-world LLMs show that EnerInfer improves energy efficiency by up to 65%, 12%, and 24% on phones, a laptop, and a development board, respectively, without QoE violation.

</details>


### [107] [Toward Self-Evolution-Ready Workflow Harnesses: A Reversible Migration Path and Convertibility Taxonomy for Expert LLM Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24598)
*Yimo Lin,Zhen Zhang,Yibin Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Presents a Strangler-Fig migration approach that refactors static "LLM + script" workflows into composable, typed, auditable stages, using a three-tier convertibility taxonomy (A/B/C) as a routing stage to diagnose workflow readiness and enable adaptive execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing "LLM + script" workflows are expert-validated and valuable but remain static — they encode domain knowledge without adapting based on feedback. Current agent research focuses on greenfield agents and synthetic benchmarks, ignoring the problem of migrating active legacy workflows to more dynamic, feedback-responsive systems.

Method: The authors propose a reversible Strangler-Fig migration pattern that incrementally refactors legacy workflows into composable, typed, and auditable stages. The core mechanism is a three-tier convertibility taxonomy (categories A, B, and C), implemented as a routing stage within a system harness. This routing stage diagnoses a workflow's current readiness for conversion and routes execution accordingly, enabling incremental, safe migration.

Result: The abstract does not provide specific quantitative or empirical results. The contribution is the framework itself: a structured migration path and taxonomy for converting static LLM workflows into adaptive, feedback-driven systems.

Conclusion: The proposed Strangler-Fig migration path with the A/B/C convertibility taxonomy bridges the gap between static legacy LLM workflows and adaptive agent-based systems, offering a reversible, incremental, and auditable migration strategy.

Abstract: While expert-validated "LLM + script" workflows deliver significant value, they remain static: they encode hard-won domain knowledge yet fail to adapt execution based on feedback. Existing agent research predominantly targets greenfield agents and synthetic benchmarks, leaving the migration of active legacy workflows unresolved. To bridge this gap, we present a reversible, Strangler-Fig migration path that refactors legacy workflows into composable, typed, and auditable stages. Central to this framework is a three-tier convertibility taxonomy (A/B/C), implemented as a routing stage within the system harness, which diagnoses a workflow's readiness and routes it accordingly.

</details>


### [108] [Agon: An Autonomous Large-Scale Omnidisciplinary Research System Built on Prompt Economy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24177)
*Youran Sun,Xingyu Ren,Chugang Yi,Jiaxuan Guo,Kejia Zhang,Jianda Du,Haizhao Yang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Agon is a research orchestrator that automates the research production pipeline using LLMs, built on six design principles. Tested across 444 iterations, it demonstrates scalability but reveals a taxonomy of failures, distinguishing machine-fixable issues from those requiring human judgment, advancing a "machine scales, human steers" paradigm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are making research production scalable, but the new bottleneck is judging research claims rather than producing artifacts. There is a need for a system that can automate what can be validated internally while appropriately deferring to human scientists for judgments that exceed machine capability.

Method: The authors designed Agon based on six principles: (1) Prompt Economy — iterative refinement through prompt loops; (2) Future-Facing — forward-looking rather than retrospective; (3) Minimal Prompts — using concise, efficient prompts; (4) OmniDisciplinary — applicable across research domains; (5) Massive Parallelism — running many iterations concurrently; (6) Zero-Code — no human-written experimental code. The system was deployed across domains for 444 iterations using only small starting topics, with no human-written experimental code, and failures were taxonomized along severity, fixability, visibility, and capability locus.

Result: Agon successfully ran 444 Prompt Economy loop iterations across multiple domains starting from only small topics and zero human code, demonstrating scalability. The deployments also exposed new failure classes, which were organized into a taxonomy with four axes: severity, fixability, visibility, and capability locus. This taxonomy cleanly separates failures that the loops can detect and fix autonomously from those requiring human intervention.

Conclusion: Agon demonstrates that automated research orchestration is viable and is pushing the field toward a new paradigm of "machine scales, human steers," where machines handle scalable production and internal validation while humans focus on the irreducible judgments that remain beyond machine capability.

Abstract: Large language models are making research production scalable, shifting the bottleneck from producing artifacts to judging claims. We present \textsc{Agon}, a research orchestrator that validates what can be checked inside the workflow and leaves the remaining judgments to human scientists. \textsc{Agon} is built on six design principles: Prompt Economy, Future-Facing, Minimal Prompts, OmniDisciplinary, Massive Parallelism, and Zero-Code. We ran \textsc{Agon} across domains for 444 iterations of Prompt Economy loops, using only small starting topics and no human-written experimental code. These deployments demonstrate scalability while exposing new classes of failure. We organize these failures into a taxonomy along severity, fixability, visibility, and capability locus. The taxonomy separates failures the loops can see and fix from those that require human judgment. Together, these results show that \textsc{Agon} is pushing research toward a new paradigm: machine scales, human steers.

</details>


### [109] [Detecting AI Coding Agents in Open Source: A Validated Multi-Method Census of 180 Million Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24429)
*Arsham Khosravani,Audris Mockus*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces a multi-layered detection framework to identify generative AI coding agent traces across 180M+ Git repositories in World of Code. It reveals that single-signal detection methods (e.g., bot-account lookup) miss ~97% of agent activity, and that commit-attributed vs. PR-attributed agents represent nearly disjoint populations doing different kinds of work, demonstrating that no single detection channel is representative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI coding agents are increasingly present in open-source supply chains, but their prevalence is poorly understood due to diverse and often invisible traces. Existing adoption studies rely heavily on single signals (e.g., bot-account lookup), which the authors hypothesize dramatically undercounts agent activity and yields biased prevalence estimates. A comprehensive, multi-signal detection approach is needed to capture the true scope and behavioral patterns of AI agent adoption.

Method: The authors propose a multi-layered detection framework operating across the World of Code corpus (180M+ Git repositories) that integrates four detection modalities: (1) configuration-file scanning, (2) commit-message analysis, (3) author-identity matching, and (4) bot-signature lookup. Agent traces are classified into four behavioral types. All detection patterns are hand-validated with 495 labels, reporting per-cell precision and Wilson confidence intervals. The framework is applied across snapshots from December 2024 to April 2026, and results are compared against an independent pull-request census (AIDev) to assess population overlap and work-type differences.

Result: Multi-method detection identified 850,157 Claude Code commits in one snapshot, while bot-account lookup alone recovered only 28,154 (3.3%) — a 30x relative-recall gap. Commit-attributed agents generated over 320,000 commits per month, with Claude Code leading at 886,122 commits across 17,295 projects and dominating silent, configuration-file-only adoption (21,078 projects). The PR census (AIDev) missed 79% of commit-detected Claude Code adopters and essentially all Codex adopters. PR-deployed cloud agents (Codex, Cursor) surfaced as feature work, while commit-deployed in-editor agents (Claude Code, OpenHands, Aider) surfaced as maintenance work. The observed work profile follows deployment and detection mode rather than the tool itself.

Conclusion: Single-signal detection methods severely underestimate AI coding agent adoption by at least 30x. Commit-based and PR-based detection channels capture nearly disjoint agent populations performing qualitatively different work, meaning no single channel is representative. Reliable prevalence measurement requires multi-layered detection frameworks, and understanding the deployment-detection mode nexus is essential for characterizing how AI agents are actually used in open-source development.

Abstract: Generative AI coding agents are entering the open-source supply chain, yet their diverse and often invisible traces leave their prevalence poorly understood. We introduce a multi-layered detection framework that integrates configuration-file scanning, commit-message analysis, author-identity matching, and bot-signature lookup across World of Code (180M+ Git repositories), classifying agent traces into four behavioral types. No single method captures more than a fraction of activity: multi-method detection identifies 850,157 Claude Code commits in one snapshot, of which bot-account lookup_the signal most adoption studies rely on_recovers only 28,154 (3.3%), a 30x relative-recall gap, so single-signal prevalence estimates are biased low by at least this factor. Every detection pattern is hand-validated (495 labels) with per-cell precision and Wilson confidence intervals. Across snapshots from December 2024 to April 2026, commit-attributed agents generate over 320,000 commits per month; Claude Code leads (886,122 commits across 17,295 projects) and dominates silent, configuration-file-only adoption (21,078 projects). Compared against an independent pull-request census (AIDev), the two channels capture nearly disjoint agent populations_a PR census misses 79% of commit-detected Claude Code adopters and essentially all Codex adopters_and different kinds of work: PR-deployed cloud agents (Codex, Cursor) surface as feature work, while commit-deployed in-editor agents (Claude Code, OpenHands, Aider) surface as maintenance. The observed work profile follows deployment and detection mode rather than the tool itself, so no single channel is representative.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [110] [Systematic Exploration of 4-Expert Heterogeneous Mixture-of-Experts via Automated Pipeline Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23739)
*Yashkumar R Lukhi,Harsh Rameshbhai Moradiya,Radu Timofte,Dmitry Ignatov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An automated pipeline searched 4-expert MoE architectures from the LEMUR dataset, generating 4,463 candidates over 28 days on an RTX 4090. A critical coverage bias was discovered: alphabetical enumeration anchored the entire explored space to the AirNet family, exploring only 4.8% of theoretical combinations. ShuffleNet+MobileNetV3 ensembles achieved highest accuracy (mean 0.632), while FractalNet and MNASNet were identified as low-yield.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To replace manual design of heterogeneous Mixture-of-Experts architectures with an automated, large-scale, deterministic search pipeline, enabling systematic exploration of 4-expert combinations from the LEMUR neural network dataset ecosystem.

Method: A deterministic code-assembly generator systematically combines base architecture families from LEMUR into 4-expert MoE ensembles, each equipped with convolutional gating, temperature scaling, mixup augmentation, and cosine-annealed learning rate scheduling. The pipeline ran for 28 days on an NVIDIA RTX 4090, generating candidates via itertools.combinations (alphabetical enumeration), with 1,021 of 4,463 candidates successfully evaluated across 197 batches.

Result: Only 4.8% of the theoretical 23,751 possible 4-family combinations were explored, with the entire search space anchored to AirNet due to alphabetical enumeration bias. Within this constrained scope, ShuffleNet and MobileNetV3 consistently yielded the highest-accuracy ensembles (mean accuracy up to 0.632). FractalNet and MNASNet were identified as low-yield families. A stratified random sampling fix is proposed to correct the coverage bias.

Conclusion: The automated MoE search pipeline is viable but requires a stratified random sampling strategy to eliminate the AirNet-anchored coverage bias. ShuffleNet and MobileNetV3 are recommended as core components for future heterogeneous MoE4 ensembles, while FractalNet and MNASNet should be deprioritized. All artefacts are open-sourced in the NNGPT project.

Abstract: We present an automated large-scale search pipeline for heterogeneous 4-Expert Mixture-of-Experts (MoE4) architectures within the LEMUR neural network dataset ecosystem. Building on a hand-crafted heterogeneous MoE reference model, we replace manual design with a deterministic code-assembly generator that systematically combines base architecture families drawn from the LEMUR database into MoE4 ensembles, each governed by a convolutional gating network with temperature scaling, mixup augmentation, and cosine-annealed learning rate scheduling. Over a 28-day campaign on an NVIDIA RTX 4090, the pipeline generated 4,463 candidate models across 197 batches, of which 1,021 were evaluated successfully. A critical finding emerged from the campaign: due to alphabetical enumeration via itertools.combinations, the entire explored search space (4.8% of the theoretical 23,751 possible 4-family combinations) is anchored to a single family, AirNet. We characterise this coverage bias precisely, identify the root cause in the generator, and propose a stratified random sampling fix. Within the AirNet anchored scope, ShuffleNet and MobileNetV3 consistently co-produce the highest-accuracy ensembles (mean accuracy up to 0.632), while FractalNet and MNASNet are identified as low-yield families warranting exclusion in future campaigns. The pipeline, analysis artefacts, and corrected generator are released as part of the open-source NNGPT project at https://github.com/ABrain-One/nn-gpt

</details>


### [111] [Weight-Space Geometry of Offline Reasoning Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23740)
*Aleksandr Nikolich,Igor Kiselev,Vladimir Platonov,Karina Romanova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Comparing six offline RL and distillation methods (SFT, RFT, DFT, RIFT, Offline GRPO, DPO) on identical math data with attention-only LoRA, the authors find that SFT/RFT/RIFT yield nearly colinear weight updates and similar accuracy, while DFT, GRPO, and especially DPO produce increasingly divergent weight-space geometries — with DPO achieving the highest accuracy but residing in a near-orthogonal subspace with collapsed late-layer representational similarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline reinforcement-learning losses are widely used to distill reasoning abilities from large teachers into smaller student models, yet they are typically compared only on downstream accuracy. The authors aim to determine whether these methods are mechanistically distinct or converge to fundamentally similar weight updates, moving beyond accuracy benchmarks to weight-space and representational analyses.

Method: Six methods (SFT, RFT, DFT, RIFT, Offline GRPO, DPO) were trained on identical math rollouts from Qwen3-4B using attention-only LoRA. The resulting weight deltas were analyzed via: (1) cosine similarity between delta vectors, (2) principal-angle subspace analysis across 144 modules, (3) linear mode connectivity (loss-barrier interpolation), and (4) Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) for representational similarity. Accuracy was evaluated on GSM8K (n=1319) with pairwise McNemar tests, plus AIME26.

Result: Key findings: (i) SFT, RFT, and RIFT have nearly colinear weight deltas (cosine ≥0.97, top-1 principal angle ~7° median) and statistically indistinguishable GSM8K accuracy (87-88%, McNemar p≥0.15). (ii) DFT diverges more directionally than any reward-weighted method despite identical data. (iii) Offline GRPO adds a large orthogonal component (~67% globally, up to ~86% in late layers) while remaining in the SFT loss basin. (iv) DPO sits in a near-orthogonal subspace, shows a mode-connectivity barrier, and collapses late-layer CKA to ~0.46. DPO achieves highest accuracy on GSM8K (93.5%, p<10⁻⁹) and AIME26 (30.0% vs. 3.3-10.0%), but training used a 10× smaller learning rate — so the gap reflects both loss-function and optimizer choices.

Conclusion: Common offline RL distillation methods are mechanistically far from equivalent despite similar data. SFT, RFT, and RIFT collapse to near-identical solutions, while DFT, GRPO, and DPO yield increasingly distinct geometries. DPO's strong accuracy comes with dramatically different weight-space characteristics, but the confounding effect of learning rate differences leaves open whether these geometric properties are intrinsic to the loss function or an artifact of optimizer hyperparameters — a question left for future work.

Abstract: Offline reinforcement-learning losses (RFT, RIFT, DFT, Offline GRPO, DPO) are widely used to distill reasoning from large teachers into smaller students, and are typically compared on downstream accuracy alone. We ask whether they are mechanistically distinct or converge to a similar weight update. Training six methods (SFT, RFT, DFT, RIFT, Offline GRPO, DPO) on identical math rollouts from a single base model (Qwen3-4B) with attention-only LoRA, we analyze the resulting deltas via cosine similarity, principal-angle subspace analysis, linear mode connectivity, and CKA. We observe: (i) SFT, RFT, and RIFT have nearly colinear weight deltas (cosine >= 0.97, top-1 principal angle ~7 deg median over 144 modules) and comparable GSM8K accuracy (87-88%, n=1319; pairwise McNemar p >= 0.15); (ii) DFT diverges further in direction than any reward-weighted method despite using the same data; (iii) Offline GRPO adds a substantial component orthogonal to the SFT direction (~67% globally, up to ~86% in late layers) while staying in the SFT loss basin; (iv) DPO sits in a near-orthogonal subspace, shows a mode-connectivity barrier, and collapses late-layer CKA to ~0.46. DPO also reaches the highest accuracy in our protocol on both GSM8K (93.5%, McNemar p < 10^-9 vs. each other method) and AIME26 (30.0% vs. 3.3-10.0%); its training uses a 10x smaller learning rate than the others (the standard convention), so the update-norm and accuracy gaps reflect loss-function and optimizer choices jointly, and a learning-rate-matched DPO comparison is left for future work.

</details>


### [112] [A Survey on Federated Causal Discovery and Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23741)
*Xianjie Guo,Yuwei Wang,Guodu Xiang,Xiaoli Tang,Kui Yu,Han Yu,Qiang Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a systematic survey of federated causal reasoning, unifying federated causal discovery (FCD) and federated causal inference (FCI) as complementary stages of a single pipeline. It organizes FCD along three axes—methodological paradigm, federation topology, and structural scope—and categorizes FCI by target estimand and estimation strategy, while also examining practical dimensions like heterogeneity, missing data, and temporal dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal reasoning is essential for data-driven decision-making, but data are often distributed across institutions due to privacy regulations or communication constraints, preventing centralization. Federated learning enables collaborative analysis without raw data sharing, spurring the emerging fields of FCD and FCI. However, the interdisciplinary nature and lack of a comprehensive survey create entry barriers for researchers, motivating this systematic review.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic literature review structured around multi-dimensional taxonomies. For FCD, they propose a framework grounded in three core design decisions: how structures are learned (methodological paradigm), how data are partitioned (federation topology), and what structural knowledge each party obtains (structural scope). For FCI, they categorize methods by target estimand (average vs. individualized/conditional treatment effects) and estimation strategy (classical weighting to deep generative architectures). They also examine practical dimensions including temporal dynamics, data heterogeneity, missing data, and non-identical variable sets.

Result: The survey formalizes FCD and FCI as complementary stages of a unified federated causal reasoning pipeline, where FCD supplies the structural knowledge (causal graphs) required for valid effect estimation in FCI. It identifies shared concerns across both stages—privacy, communication efficiency, theoretical guarantees, and application domains—and highlights open challenges for future research.

Conclusion: Federated causal discovery and inference should be viewed as an integrated pipeline rather than isolated tasks. The paper concludes by identifying open challenges and calling for future work that bridges the gap between FCD and FCI, particularly around privacy guarantees, communication efficiency, and handling of real-world data complexities.

Abstract: Causal reasoning, which encompasses the discovery of causal structures and the inference of causal effects, is fundamental to data-driven decision making. In practice, data for reliable causal analysis are often distributed across institutions and cannot be centralized due to privacy regulations or communication constraints. Federated learning (FL) addresses this by enabling collaborative analysis without raw data sharing, giving rise to the rapidly growing field of federated causal discovery (FCD) and inference (FCI). However, the interdisciplinary nature of this field and the absence of a comprehensive survey present barriers to entry for researchers. This paper bridges that gap by providing a systematic review through multi-dimensional taxonomies. Grounded in the three core design decisions underlying any FCD solution, namely how structures are learned, how data are partitioned, and what structural knowledge each party obtains, we organize FCD along three axes: methodological paradigm, federation topology, and structural scope. We further examine key practical dimensions, including temporal dynamics, data heterogeneity, missing data, and non-identical variable sets. For FCI, we categorize methods by target estimand (average versus individualized/conditional treatment effects) and by estimation strategy, from classical weighting methods to modern deep generative architectures. Unlike prior works that treat FCD and FCI separately, we formalize their connection as complementary stages of a unified federated causal reasoning pipeline, where FCD supplies the structural knowledge required for valid effect estimation in FCI. Finally, we highlight their shared concerns regarding privacy, communication efficiency, theoretical guarantees, and application domains, and conclude by identifying open challenges for future research.

</details>


### [113] [Low-power analogue neural networks with trainable nonlinear connections for continuous control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23742)
*Ian T. Vidamour,Fernando Aguirre,Thomas J. Hayward,Matthew O. A. Ellis,Charles Swindells,Alexander McDonnell,Martin Trefzer,Finley Robins,Luca Manneschi,Susan Stepney,Tony Kenyon,Oliver J. Sutton,Jack C. Gartside,Ivan Y. Tyukin,Adnan Mehonic,Eleni Vasilaki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physical neural networks replace scalar weights with trainable nonlinear analog filters on connections. This Kolmogorov-Arnold-inspired approach achieves high parameter efficiency for smooth, continuous functions (e.g., robotics, control, MPPT) but not for classification tasks. Networks with ~35,000 connections transfer to low-power hardware (~30 μW projected CMOS), and the advantage stems from architectural nonlinearity placement, not device specifics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most physical neural network architectures constrain nonlinear device physics to act merely as scalar weights, underutilizing the rich dynamical behavior available in analog components. The authors aim to exploit trainable nonlinearities directly on physical connections to achieve more expressive computation with fewer parameters, inspired by the Kolmogorov-Arnold representation theorem.

Method: The authors implement Kolmogorov-Arnold-style networks physically by placing trainable nonlinear functions (realized as analog band-pass filters) on connections using field-programmable analog arrays (FPAAs). They evaluate these networks on smooth continuous tasks (robotic kinematics, continuous control, photovoltaic MPPT) versus classification tasks, compare parameter efficiency against multilayer perceptrons, physically deploy networks across ~35,000 connections, project a dedicated CMOS implementation, and validate generality via memristive simulations.

Result: The smoothness of the physical basis functions determines task suitability: networks excel at representing smooth, continuously valued targets with far fewer nodes/connections than MLPs, but show no parameter-efficiency advantage on classification-like decision boundaries. Trained networks transfer to hardware with quantified fidelity. A dedicated CMOS implementation is projected at ~30 μW. Memristive simulations reproduce the same behavior, confirming the advantage derives from placing trainable nonlinearity on connections rather than from any specific device technology.

Conclusion: Placing trainable nonlinear functions on physical connections (rather than using devices as scalar weights) yields significant parameter-efficiency gains for smooth continuous functions. This architectural principle is device-agnostic, suggesting a general design strategy for low-power analog machine learning hardware targeting regression, control, and related continuous-domain tasks.

Abstract: Physical neural networks promise low-power machine learning by computing directly with analogue device physics, but most architectures force nonlinear device responses to act as scalar weights. Inspired by Kolmogorov-Arnold networks, we place trainable nonlinear functions on the connections, making each physical connection a learnable computational element. Realising these functions as analogue band-pass filters on field-programmable analogue arrays, we find that the benefit is task-dependent and follows from the smoothness of the physical basis: the networks represent smooth, continuously valued targets, including robotic kinematics, continuous control, and photovoltaic maximum-power-point tracking, with far fewer nodes and connections than multilayer perceptrons, but offer no parameter-efficiency advantage on classification-like decision boundaries. Trained networks transfer to hardware across approximately 35,000 connections with quantified fidelity, and a dedicated CMOS implementation is projected to operate at approximately 30 microwatts. A memristive realisation reproduces the same behaviour in simulation, indicating that the advantage comes from placing trainable nonlinearity on connections, rather than from a particular device.

</details>


### [114] [Synergizing Physically Constrained MCMC and Chemical-Informed Gaussian Processes for Reaction Network Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23757)
*Runzhe Liu,Zihao Wang,Wenbo Yang,Shengyang Tao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PC-MCMC-CIGP integrates spike-and-slab MCMC topology sampling, thermodynamic/chemical constraints, and a chemical-informed GP residual model into a unified gray-box workflow for extracting interpretable kinetic equations from sparse, noisy time-series data. It also evaluates acquisition strategies, with PC-EI reducing low-yield suggestions and EI-style criteria yielding strongest final performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
