Motivation: Extracting interpretable governing equations from chemical time-series data is challenging because discrete reaction topology and continuous kinetic parameters are tightly coupled, and data are often sparse and noisy. Existing approaches lack integrated, physically constrained workflows with explicit uncertainty quantification for both model selection and experimental design.

Method: PC-MCMC-CIGP combines: (1) spike-and-slab prior MCMC for joint topology/parameter inference with hard conservation and thermodynamic plausibility screening; (2) a Chemical-Informed Gaussian Process (CIGP) residual model for parameter calibration; (3) uncertainty-aware acquisition functions (EI, GWU, PC-EI, uncertainty sampling, discrepancy hunting, random search) evaluated in a 10-seed study on styrene epoxidation. The gray-box workflow is fully reproducible.

Result: On the H2 + Br2 benchmark, the constrained sampler successfully distinguishes elementary radical pathways from deceptive phenomenological fits. On styrene epoxidation, the CIGP optimization loop improves final yield by 12.5% over the reported GP-BO baseline. The 10-seed acquisition study reveals that PC-EI substantially reduces low-yield BO suggestions, while EI-style criteria achieve the strongest final-yield performance.

Conclusion: Integrating physically constrained topology inference with chemical-aware GP surrogates and principled acquisition strategies yields both interpretable kinetic models and improved experimental optimization. The framework is reproducible and generalizable to other reaction systems, offering a robust approach for gray-box chemical kinetics modeling under data scarcity.

Abstract: Extracting interpretable governing equations from sparse, noisy chemical time-series data remains difficult because discrete reaction topology and continuous kinetic parameters are tightly coupled. We present PC-MCMC-CIGP, a reproducible gray-box workflow that combines spike-and-slab topology sampling, hard conservation and thermodynamic screening, and a Chemical-Informed Gaussian Process (CIGP) residual model for parameter calibration and experimental design. The methodological contribution is not a new MCMC or GP family in isolation; rather, it is the integration of these components into a physically constrained workflow with explicit uncertainty-aware acquisition choices. On the H2 + Br2 benchmark, the constrained sampler distinguishes elementary radical pathways from deceptive phenomenological fits in our experiments. On styrene epoxidation, the CIGP optimization loop improves final yield by 12.5% over the reported GP-BO baseline. A new 10-seed acquisition study shows that EI, GWU, PC-EI, uncertainty sampling, discrepancy hunting, and random search have different trade-offs: PC-EI substantially reduces low-yield BO suggestions, while EI-style criteria give the strongest final-yield performance.

</details>


### [115] [Exploring Dualistic Meta-Learning to Enhance Domain Generalization in Open Set Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23758)
*Xiran Wang,Jian Zhang,Lei Qi,Yang Gao,Yinghuan Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MEDIC introduces dualistic meta-learning with joint domain-class matching to address open set domain generalization. It tackles the label imbalance problem in one-vs-all classifiers by optimizing decision boundaries balanced across both domains and classes, outperforming prior methods on open set tasks while maintaining competitive closed set performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing domain generalization neglects label mismatch between source and target domains. Open set domain generalization addresses this but suffers from a critical imbalance problem: standard one-vs-all classifiers have few positive samples versus many negatives, skewing decision boundaries and causing over-rejection of OOD data from known classes in unseen domains.

Method: MEDIC employs a dualistic meta-learning strategy that performs implicit gradient matching across two task splits simultaneously: inter-domain splits (matching across different domains) and inter-class splits (matching across different classes). This dual optimization finds decision boundaries that are balanced for both domain shift and class separation, mitigating the positive-negative sample imbalance.

Result: MEDIC outperforms prior methods in open set domain generalization scenarios while maintaining competitive performance in closed set generalization settings.

Conclusion: The dualistic meta-learning approach with joint domain-class matching effectively resolves the boundary skew problem in open set domain generalization, offering a balanced solution that handles both unseen domains and unseen classes without sacrificing closed set performance.

Abstract: Domain generalization learns from multiple source domains to generalize to unseen target domains. However, it often neglects the realistic case of label mismatch between source and target. Open set domain generalization is then proposed to recognize unseen classes in unseen domains. A simple approach trains one-vs-all classifiers to separate each class and detect outliers as unknown. Yet, the imbalance between few positive samples and many negative samples skews the decision boundary towards the positive ones, leading the model to over-reject out-of-distribution data, even from known classes in unseen domains. In this paper, we propose a novel meta-learning stategy called dualistic MEta-learning with joint DomaIn-Class matching (MEDIC), which considers implicit gradient matching towards inter-domain and inter-class task splits simultaneously to find optimal boundaries balanced for both domains and classes. Experimental results show that MEDIC not only outperforms prior methods in open set scenarios, but also maintains competitive close set generalization ability.

</details>


### [116] [One Ruler: A Same-Hands Re-Evaluation of Bivariate Causal Direction on Tuebingen, with a Parameter-Free Compression Baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23767)
*Wietse Stienstra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper argues that headline accuracy comparisons on the Tübingen cause-effect pairs are misleading because each method is evaluated under different protocols. The authors conduct a uniform re-evaluation of all methods on the same 102 pairs with forced decisions and no tuning. They introduce a zero-parameter sorted-conditional compression baseline that achieves competitive performance, revealing that reported accuracies are inflated by test-set model selection and significance-gated abstention. Under the common ruler, all methods cluster in the low-to-mid 70s, and the simple compressor ties the strongest methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Published accuracy figures for causal discovery on the Tübingen cause-effect pairs are not comparable because each paper uses different pair subsets, weighting schemes, model-selection procedures, and decision rates. The authors aim to level the playing field by re-evaluating all methods under an identical, strict protocol to reveal true relative performance and expose inflation mechanisms.

Method: The authors re-evaluate multiple causal discovery methods on the identical set of 102 Tübingen cause-effect pairs under a strict protocol: no tuning allowed, and a forced decision on every pair. They introduce sorted-conditional compression as a minimal baseline — quantized, sorted, first-differenced data compressed with bz2, having zero fitted parameters. They compare methods using weighted accuracy and McNemar tests, and conduct a pre-registered falsification test. They also trace discrepancies in published figures (e.g., RECI's mis-copied cell, SLOPE's decided-subset reporting).

Result: Under the common ruler, the ranking differs sharply from the literature. The zero-parameter compression baseline achieves 74.7% weighted accuracy (p=3.7e-7). On the 100 pairs SLOPE was evaluated on, it scores 76.0%, only 1.2 points below SLOPE's forced-decision result of 77.2% (McNemar p=0.39, within noise). A faithful RECI re-run yields 70.7%, not the often-quoted 77.5%. SLOPE's published 82.4% is a decided-subset figure; scoring only on decided pairs reproduces 81.7%. All methods cluster in the low-to-mid 70s, and the compressor ties the strongest. Compression score magnitude strongly flags confounding (p=2.8e-68).

Conclusion: The paper concludes that headline accuracy comparisons in causal discovery are invalid due to inconsistent evaluation protocols. Test-set model selection and significance-gated abstention systematically inflate reported figures. A zero-parameter compression baseline matches state-of-the-art methods under fair comparison, challenging claims of progress. The authors call for standardized, forced-decision evaluation and release code, pre-registrations, and per-pair outputs for transparency.

Abstract: Headline accuracies on the Tuebingen cause-effect pairs are routinely compared across papers even though each is measured under its authors' own protocol -- different pair subsets, weightings, model-selection, and decision rates. We argue this is the wrong comparison and run the right one: a same-hands re-evaluation in which every method is run by us on the identical 102 pairs, with one strict rule -- no tuning and a decision forced on every pair. As a clean reference point we introduce a deliberately minimal baseline: sorted-conditional compression, which feeds quantized, sorted, first-differenced data to an off-the-shelf compressor (bz2) and has zero fitted parameters. Under the common ruler the ranking differs sharply from the literature. Our baseline reaches 74.7% weighted accuracy (p = 3.7e-7); on the same 100 pairs that SLOPE is evaluated on it scores 76.0%, a 1.2-point gap below the authors' own forced-decision SLOPE (77.2%) that is well inside noise (McNemar p = 0.39). A faithful re-run of RECI lands at 70.7% -- inside the original authors' reported error bar, not the 77.5% often quoted (which we trace to a mis-copied cell). SLOPE's published 82.4% is a decided-subset figure: scoring the authors' own stored output only on the pairs its significance test chose to answer reproduces 81.7%. Under the common ruler the methods cluster in the low-to-mid 70s and the zero-parameter compressor ties the strongest of them. We document the mechanisms that inflate published figures (test-set model selection, significance-gated abstention) and contribute two further results: compression score magnitude is a model-free confounding flag (p = 2.8e-68), and a pre-registered falsification test fails in an instructive way that bounds the method's theoretical interpretation. Code, pre-registrations, and per-pair outputs are released.

</details>


### [117] [EMAgnet: Parameter-Space EMA Regularization for Policy Gradient Self-Play in Large Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23995)
*Tristan Maidment,JB Lanier,Chase McDonald,Nathan Tsang,Eugene Vinitsky,Roy Fox,Albert Wang,Wesley N. Kerr*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EMAgnet replaces uniform policy regularization in PPO self-play with an exponential moving average of the policy parameters, creating an adaptive regularization target. It outperforms uniform-magnet regularization, especially in games with many strictly dominated strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform regularization in self-play PPO treats all actions equally, which is suboptimal in games with many non-viable or dominated actions. An adaptive regularization target that evolves with the agent's strategy could better focus learning on viable actions.

Method: EMAgnet uses an exponential moving average (EMA) of the last-iterate policy's network parameters as the regularization target, replacing the uniform distribution used in prior magnet-style regularization. The method is evaluated with both linear and power-law annealing schedules.

Result: EMAgnet achieves lower exploitability than uniform-magnet PPO self-play on most benchmarks, with consistent gains in games containing strictly dominated strategies. Benefits are demonstrated on both standard two-player zero-sum benchmarks and modified environments with exploration challenges.

Conclusion: An adaptive, EMA-based regularization target is a simple yet effective replacement for uniform regularization in self-play PPO, yielding more robust performance particularly when the action space contains many dominated actions.

Abstract: Recent work has established that regularized policy gradient methods such as PPO, when used in self-play, can match or exceed specialized game-theoretic algorithms for solving two-player zero-sum imperfect-information games. The uniform distribution has emerged as a strong policy regularization target for this purpose, but it regularizes equally toward all actions regardless of their viability. We introduce EMAgnet, which instead regularizes toward an exponential moving average (EMA) of the last-iterate policy's parameters, providing an adaptive regularization target that evolves with the agent's improving strategy. We evaluate EMAgnet on both standard two-player zero-sum benchmarks and modified benchmarks with exploration challenges and large numbers of strictly dominated strategies. Relative to PPO self-play with uniform-magnet regularization under both linear and power-law annealing schedules, EMAgnet achieves lower exploitability in the majority of tested environments, with consistent performance gains across games containing strictly dominated strategies.

</details>


### [118] [Deciphering Fingerprints of 3D Molecular Surfaces for Accurate Epitope Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23830)
*Fang Wu,Weihao Xuan,Jure Leskovec,Yejin Choi,Li Erran Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SurfBind is a surface-centric deep learning framework that predicts antibody epitopes directly from molecular surface representations, using a Transformer with patch-level modeling and cross-attention to achieve state-of-the-art performance on epitope identification benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing epitope prediction methods rely on sequence or backbone structural features and fail to effectively capture discontinuous, surface-driven epitopes that are critical to antibody-antigen recognition.

Method: SurfBind operates on 3D molecular surface representations using a Transformer-based architecture with three key components: (1) patch-level surface modeling that integrates geometric and physicochemical cues, (2) binder-aware cross-attention for interaction-aware learning, and (3) a hierarchical coarse-to-fine prediction paradigm.

Result: On the SAbDab and DB5.5 epitope identification benchmarks, SurfBind achieves state-of-the-art performance and demonstrates strong generalization to unseen antibodies and conformational states.

Conclusion: Interaction-aware molecular surface modeling is highly valuable for epitope prediction and for advancing understanding of the mechanisms underlying protein-protein interactions.

Abstract: Molecular surfaces encode the geometric and physicochemical patterns that determine antibody-antigen recognition, central to epitope prediction. However, existing methods rely on sequences or backbone structures and struggle to capture discontinuous, surface-driven epitopes. This study presents SurfBind, a surface-centric learning framework for epitope prediction that operates directly on molecular surface representations. SurfBind integrates geometric and physicochemical cues through a Transformer-based architecture with patch-level surface modeling, binder-aware cross-attention, and a hierarchical coarse-to-fine prediction paradigm. Experiments on challenging epitope identification benchmarks, including SAbDab and DB5.5, demonstrate that SurfBind achieves state-of-the-art performance and strong generalization across unseen antibodies and conformational states, highlighting the value of interaction-aware surface modeling for understanding the crucial mechanisms of protein-protein interactions.

</details>


### [119] [Reconstructing GRACE Terrestrial Water Storage with Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks: An Application to South America](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23833)
*Lukas Arzoumanidis,Lara Johannsen,Klara Middendorf,Annette Eicker,Youness Dehbi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A graph neural network (MTGNN) reconstructs monthly GRACE-like terrestrial water storage anomalies back to 1940 using only ERA5 meteorological forcing, achieving basin-scale correlation of 0.94. The model uses a hybrid adjacency matrix combining geodesic proximity and climatic teleconnections, matching or approaching state-of-the-art baselines with far fewer predictors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRACE/GRACE-FO satellite observations of terrestrial water storage (TWS) are invaluable for understanding the global water cycle, but the record begins only in 2002 — too short for climate-scale analyses. A reliable reconstruction extending further back in time is needed.

Method: The authors adapt a Multi-variate Time series Graph Neural Network (MTGNN), originally designed for urban traffic forecasting, to reconstruct TWS anomalies from 1940 onward. The model learns the relationship between daily ERA5 meteorological forcing (precipitation, evapotranspiration, runoff) and monthly GRACE TWSA. Spatial dependencies are encoded via a static hybrid adjacency matrix combining geodesic proximity with lagged climatic correlations, capturing both local coupling and large-scale teleconnections. Performance is benchmarked against GTWS-MLrec, RM-REC, and GRAiCE.

Result: The reconstruction achieves a grid-cell Pearson correlation of 0.69, basin-mean correlation of 0.94, and near-zero bias. It faithfully reproduces spatial fingerprints of the 2015/16 El Niño and 2020/21 La Niña. The graph-based model reaches basin-scale correlation within 0.025 of the best baseline while using roughly 1/2 to 1/10 of the predictors, though all models show weaknesses in arid regions.

Conclusion: The MTGNN architecture is a competitive, parsimonious approach for TWS reconstruction, leveraging interpretable spatial priors. The public release of the implementation supports reproducibility and further research.

Abstract: Terrestrial water storage (TWS) integrates snow, soil moisture, surface water, and groundwater and is a key indicator of how climate variability and human activity reshape the global water cycle. The GRACE and GRACE-FO satellite missions provide the only direct, globally consistent observations of TWS change, but their record only begins in 2002 which is too short for many climate-scale analyses. We present a deep learning application that reconstructs monthly GRACE-like TWS anomalies (TWSA) back to 1940 by learning the relationship between daily ERA5 meteorological forcing (precipitation, evapotranspiration, runoff) and monthly GRACE observations. In contrast to prior reconstruction approaches based on grid-cell-wise regression, CNNs, or LSTMs, we adapt a multi-variate time series graph neural network (MTGNN) architecture, which was originally developed for mobility and traffic forecasting on urban sensor networks to this satellite-geodesy task. Spatial dependencies are encoded in a static, interpretable hybrid adjacency matrix that combines geodesic proximity with lagged correlations of climatic time series, capturing both local hydrological coupling and large-scale teleconnections. The reconstruction achieves a grid-cell Pearson correlation of 0.69, a basin-mean correlation of 0.94, and a near-zero bias, and it reproduces the spatial fingerprints of the 2015/16 El Niño and 2020/21 La Niña events. A systematic comparison with established reconstruction approaches (GTWS-MLrec, RM-REC, GRAiCE) shows that the graph-based model is statistically competitive at basin scale, reaching a correlation within 0.025 of the best baseline while using only roughly half to a tenth of the predictors the other models require and revealing characteristic weaknesses in arid regions in all models. The complete implementation is publicly available at github.com/hcu-cml/MTGNN-TWS-Reconstruction-GRACE

</details>


### [120] [The Degeneracy Distillery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23838)
*T. Lucas Makinen,Deaglan J. Bartlett,Niall Jeffrey,Benjamin D. Wandelt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents the "degeneracy distillery," a method that automatically detects and symbolically resolves degenerate parameter combinations in physical models by estimating and flattening the Fisher information matrix. It discovers coordinate transformations that make parameter effects on data independent, reducing simulation budgets up to 10× for neural posterior estimation while providing physical insight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Degenerate parameters — those producing indistinguishable data — plague both machine learning label prediction and probabilistic inverse problems. Existing posterior-based methods only flatten information geometry at a single point, whereas a global approach is needed. The authors aim to automatically detect and symbolically resolve degeneracies from parameter-data pairs alone, without requiring observed data, to both improve sampling efficiency and yield physical understanding.

Method: The degeneracy distillery operates by (1) estimating the Fisher information matrix (FIM) from parameter-simulation pairs, and (2) flattening it globally through symbolic regression to discover coordinate transformations that diagonalize the FIM. This leverages the information geometry of the likelihood, characterizing degeneracies as intrinsic model properties. The resulting symbolic coordinates yield independent parameter effects on data, in contrast to single-point posterior flattening methods.

Result: The method discovers symbolic coordinate transformations that identify independent parameter combinations across synthetic and real-world problems. These coordinates flatten Fisher information globally in expectation. Downstream neural posterior estimation requires up to 10× fewer simulations at matched validation calibration compared to standard approaches.

Conclusion: The degeneracy distillery effectively automates the detection and symbolic resolution of parameter degeneracies, simultaneously improving simulation efficiency for posterior estimation and providing physical insight into the system under study.

Abstract: When two or more parameters or labels produce similar data, they are degenerate, or hard to distinguish. Degeneracies render both label prediction and inverse problems difficult, since both machine learning algorithms and probabilistic samplers rely on the distinguishability of data and its gradients with respect to parameters. However, identifying degeneracies in physical models or real-world datasets can be elucidating about the choice of model or the underlying process that produces the data. We present the degeneracy distillery, a method that (1) detects and (2) resolves degenerate parameter combinations (a) automatically and (b) symbolically, from parameter-data (or parameter-simulation) pairs alone, through estimation and flattening of the Fisher information matrix. By exploring the information geometry of the likelihood, we characterize degeneracies as an intrinsic property of the physical model, requiring no realised data observation. We demonstrate our approach on a range of synthetic and real-world problems, discovering symbolic coordinate transformations that identify the combinations of parameters of a model which yield independent effects on the data. The resulting coordinates flatten the Fisher information in expectation globally, in contrast to posterior-based methods that flatten only at a single point, and substantially reduce the simulation budget required for downstream neural posterior estimation. In test cases we require up to $10\times$ fewer simulations for posterior estimation at matched validation calibration whilst simultaneously gaining physical insight on the system.

</details>


### [121] [Machine Learning Modeling for Real-Time Melt Pool Monitoring in Laser Powder Bed Fusion Additive Manufacturing: A Hybrid Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23851)
*Inioluwa Emmanuel,Zhuo Yang,Ho Yeung,Xinyao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hybrid approach combining EfficientNetB0 feature embeddings with a Random Forest classifier achieves 94.58% accuracy and 1.15 ms per-image inference for real-time melt pool anomaly detection in LPBF, outperforming pure deep learning models in both accuracy and speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time monitoring of laser powder bed fusion (LPBF) additive manufacturing requires accurate and computationally efficient anomaly detection that can operate within the hardware constraints of open-architecture machines. Existing deep learning approaches face challenges with inference latency and limited in-situ training data.

Method: A binary classification framework using a balanced dataset of 1,200 melt pool images (Nickel superalloy 625, NIST AMMT platform). Three transfer learning architectures (ResNet50, EfficientNetB0, MobileNetV2) are benchmarked against two Random Forest approaches: a hybrid model using EfficientNetB0 feature embeddings and a baseline using raw pixel features. Data undergoes 80/20 train-test split, 90/10 validation split, standardized resizing, normalization, and label-preserving augmentation. Evaluation covers accuracy, precision, recall, F1, AUC, training time, inference latency, and CPU/GPU usage.

Result: The hybrid EfficientNetB0-plus-Random Forest achieved the best performance: F1 score of 0.9451, accuracy of 0.9458, AUC of 0.9904, and sub-millisecond inference (1.15 ms). Pure deep learning models showed higher inference times with lower accuracy.

Conclusion: Combining pre-trained convolutional features with classical ensemble methods provides a robust, computationally efficient solution for real-time melt pool anomaly detection in data-limited additive manufacturing settings, well-suited for factory-floor deployment.

Abstract: This work investigates the implementation of artificial intelligence and machine learning (AI/ML) for real-time monitoring in laser powder bed fusion (LPBF) additive manufacturing. We developed a binary image classification framework for distinguishing normal and abnormal melt pool images using a balanced dataset of 1,200 images collected from Nickel superalloy 625 on the NIST AMMT platform. The study evaluates accuracy and inference time based on control requirements and hardware limitations of open-architecture LPBF machines. We benchmark three transfer learning architectures (ResNet50, EfficientNetB0, and MobileNetV2) against two Random Forest approaches: one trained on EfficientNetB0 feature embeddings (hybrid) and one trained on raw pixel features (baseline). Images are stratified into 80/20 train-test splits, with a further 90/10 validation split on the training set, and undergo standardized resizing, normalization, and label-preserving data augmentation to emulate realistic process variability. Each model is evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, F1 score, and area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC), along with training time, inference latency, and CPU & GPU usage to capture deployability constraints relevant to factory-floor monitoring. The hybrid EfficientNetB0-plus-Random Forest approach achieves the best performance on the held-out test set, with an F1 score of 0.9451, accuracy of 0.9458, and AUC of 0.9904, while maintaining sub-millisecond per-image inference (1.15 ms). In contrast, purely deep learning models exhibit significantly higher inference times with lower accuracy. These results demonstrate that combining pre-trained convolutional features with classical ensemble methods provides a robust, computationally efficient route to real-time melt pool anomaly detection in data-limited additive manufacturing environments.

</details>


### [122] [Sesame: Structure-Aware Molecular Generation via Spatial Density-Map Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23856)
*Konstantin Yatsenko,Arvind Thiagarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sesame is a diffusion-based molecular generation model that uses a spatial pairformer to condition on both protein pockets and partial molecular scaffolds via continuous density maps, enabling de novo generation and fragment-based lead optimization within a unified framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative molecular models often treat de novo generation and scaffold-based lead optimization as separate problems. The authors argue that the ability to grow molecules from partial starting points (scaffolds/fragments) is central to lead optimization in medicinal chemistry and should be unified with de novo generation under a single conditioning mechanism.

Method: Sesame employs three key innovations: (1) a spatial pairformer module that conditions on partial molecular structure and protein pockets, both represented as continuous spatial density maps; (2) a diffusion framework that jointly denoises atom types, bond types, and 3D positions; and (3) a trajectory finetuning scheme that trains on the model's own sampling rollouts to iteratively improve generation quality. The model is trained on large-scale ligand-only and protein-ligand datasets.

Result: The abstract does not report specific quantitative results; it describes the architectural contributions and training methodology.

Conclusion: The unified conditioning mechanism via spatial density maps enables Sesame to handle both de novo generation and fragment-conditioned lead optimization, supporting the medicinal chemistry workflow where a chemist can prune a hit to a scaffold and have the model grow it productively.

Abstract: Generative molecular models for drug design are a promising direction with much active research. In the next phase of computational drug design, such models will need to understand small molecule structure and protein-ligand interactions, and they will need to possess the machinery to generate molecules \textit{de novo}. Incorporating each feature poses a critical challenge. Equally important, yet often treated as secondary, is the ability to grow a molecule from a partial starting point -- a scaffold or fragment supplied by a chemist -- which is the central operation of lead optimization. We present Sesame (Spatial Evoformer for a Structure-Aware Molecular Engine), a diffusion-based molecular generation model that leverages a novel spatial pairformer module to condition on partial molecular structure and the surrounding protein pocket, both expressed as continuous spatial density maps. This single conditioning mechanism supports both \textit{de novo} generation and fragment-conditioned lead optimization, letting a medicinal chemist prune a hit to a scaffold and have Sesame grow it in productive ways. In addition to this module, we also introduce a diffusion framework for joint denoising of atom types, bond types, and positions, along with a trajectory finetuning scheme that trains on the model's own sampling rollouts to improve generation quality. Sesame is trained on a large corpus of ligand-only and protein-ligand datasets.

</details>


### [123] [Are Safety Guarantees in Neural Networks Safe? How to Compute Trustworthy Robustness Certifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23858)
*Merkouris Papamichail,Konstantinos Varsos,Giorgos Flouris,João Marques-Silva*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses the problem of computing robustness certifications for neural networks. Since volume-optimal certifications are computationally intractable, the authors propose the "apothem measure" as an alternative optimization target and show it can be computed efficiently (linear number of oracle calls). They also introduce dual certifications and demonstrate at least 2× improvement in minimum edge length over prior work on MNIST and Fashion MNIST.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robustness certifications (axis-aligned hyper-rectangles around an input that guarantee no misclassification) are crucial for AI safety against adversarial examples. Existing approaches aim to maximize certification volume, but recent intractability results make volume-optimal computation infeasible. This motivates finding a tractable alternative metric that still yields practically useful certifications.

Method: The authors introduce the apothem measure — the minimum half-edge length of an axis-aligned hyper-rectangle — as an optimization target for robustness certification. They propose an oracle-based algorithm that computes apothem-optimal certifications using a linear number of calls to a NN verifier (relative to the input domain's diameter). They prove that no volume-optimal oracle-based algorithm exists (even ignoring oracle costs). Additionally, they introduce dual certifications to provide apothem-minimum upper bounds, and implement the ParallelepipedoNN system for evaluation.

Result: On MNIST and Fashion MNIST benchmarks, the proposed ParallelepipedoNN system achieves at least a two-fold improvement in minimum edge length compared to existing methods. The theoretical results show that apothem-optimal certifications are polynomial-time computable via oracle calls, while volume-optimal certifications remain provably out of reach even with unlimited oracle access.

Conclusion: The apothem measure offers a computationally tractable and practically effective alternative to volume maximization for robustness certification. The linear oracle-complexity algorithm and the dual certification framework advance both the theory and practice of neural network verification, with significant empirical gains demonstrated on standard benchmarks.

Abstract: A primary challenge in AI safety is the existence of adversarial examples -- slightly distorted inputs that cause a neural network (NN) to misclassify. To mitigate this problem, recent research focuses on the computation of robustness certifications, which, for a given input, determine the largest distortion the input may receive without breaking the network's prediction. Robustness certifications can be interpreted as an axis-aligned hyper-rectangle (multi-dimensional intervals). Most existing approaches focus on maximizing the certification's volume, but recent intractability results prohibit the computation of volume-optimal certifications in reasonable time. We introduce the apothem measure and show how to compute apothem-optimal certifications in a linear number of calls to a NN verifier (oracle) w.r.t. the input domain's diameter. Moreover, we prove that we cannot have a volume-optimal, oracle-based algorithm, even if we discard the oracle costs. Also, we introduce dual certifications -- an interval including all instances of a class -- thus providing apothem-minimum upper bounds to a robustness certification. Further, we present the ParallelepipedoNN system, which we evaluate on the standard MNIST and Fashion MNIST benchmarks. A preliminary comparison with existing work on the same datasets reveals at least two-fold improvement w.r.t. the minimum edge length.

</details>


### [124] [Exact Schur-Sylvester Dimensionality Reductions for Non-Smooth Stochastic Complexity and Manifold Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23867)
*Trenton Lau,Gary P. T. Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a reformulation of NML codelength computation for Lasso-like estimators that reduces per-step complexity from O(N³) to O(k³+N²k) via Schur complement and Sylvester's determinant identity, achieving >14,100× speedup while preserving exactness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exact NML codelength computation for regular non-smooth estimators (e.g., Lasso) has been computationally prohibitive due to cubic scaling bottlenecks: inverting an (N+k)×(N+k) KKT matrix for projection and computing the determinant of an (N−k)×(N−k) Gram matrix for volume integration in the PPMH sampler.

Method: The authors exploit the block Schur complement to reformulate the projection operator and apply Sylvester's determinant identity to the volume factor computation. Both operations are restructured to eliminate the O(N³) bottlenecks, reducing them to O(k³+N²k). The approach is generalized to Sparse SVMs, Elastic Net, and Group Lasso. A numerical stability analysis is provided, and efficiency is measured via Effective Sample Size (ESS) per second.

Result: Per-step complexity collapses from O(N³) to O(k³+N²k). Empirical benchmarks on high-dimensional datasets demonstrate a constant speedup exceeding 14,100× while maintaining double-precision numerical equivalence.

Conclusion: The proposed reformulation renders exact non-smooth NML estimation computationally tractable for large-scale statistical inference, removing the historical cubic-scaling barrier without any approximation loss.

Abstract: The exact computation of the Normalized Maximum Likelihood (NML) codelength for regular non-smooth estimators (e.g., Lasso) has been historically limited by the cubic scaling walls of manifold-constrained projection and volume integration. At each step of the geometric Propose-and-Project Metropolis--Hastings (PPMH) sampler, evaluating the projection operator requires inverting an $(N+k) \times (N+k)$ generalized KKT matrix, while calculating the volume factor requires the determinant of an $(N-k) \times (N-k)$ Gram matrix. This paper presents an exact, mathematically equivalent formulation that bypasses both bottlenecks by utilizing the block Schur complement and Sylvester's determinant identity. We prove that the computational complexity of both operations collapses from $\mathcal{O}(N^3)$ to $\mathcal{O}(k^3 + N^2 k)$ per step. We generalize this reduction to Sparse Support Vector Machines (SVMs), Elastic Net, and Group Lasso. Finally, we provide a rigorous numerical stability analysis and evaluate the sampler's efficiency using the Effective Sample Size (ESS) per second. Our empirical benchmarks on high-dimensional datasets confirm a constant speedup exceeding $14{,}100\times$ while maintaining double-precision numerical equivalence, rendering exact non-smooth NML estimation highly tractable for large-scale statistical inference.

</details>


### [125] [Federated Survival Analysis in Healthcare: A Multi-Model Evaluation on Cross-Institutional Heterogeneous Breast Cancer Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23871)
*Natalia Moreno-Blasco,Anusha Ihalapathirana,Pekka Siirtola,Miguel Fernandez-de-Retana*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic evaluation of federated survival analysis across three models (Cox PH, DeepSurv, RSF) and three FL optimization strategies (FedAvg, FedProx, FedAdam) on a multi-institutional breast cancer cohort, showing FL outperforms local training and can match or exceed centralized performance, with RSF being most robust and FedAvg/FedProx more stable than FedAdam.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Survival analysis requires large, diverse cohorts rarely available at single institutions, and privacy regulations prohibit data centralization. While federated learning offers a privacy-preserving alternative, its effectiveness for survival modeling under realistic heterogeneous conditions remains poorly understood.

Method: Systematic comparison of three survival models (Cox Proportional Hazards, DeepSurv, Random Survival Forest) under centralized, local, and federated training paradigms using a cross-institutional breast cancer dataset with naturally heterogeneous client distributions. Three federated optimization strategies (FedAvg, FedProx, FedAdam) are evaluated for gradient-based models. Models are assessed on discrimination, calibration, and robustness.

Result: FL consistently outperforms local training and approaches, occasionally exceeds, centralized performance. RSF achieves the best overall balance of discrimination, calibration, and robustness across heterogeneous clients. FedAvg and FedProx are stronger and more stable than FedAdam. Performance depends on the diversity of client distributions.

Conclusion: Federated survival analysis is a viable, privacy-preserving paradigm for healthcare. The authors provide practical, decision-oriented guidelines that map data, privacy, interpretability, and resource constraints to recommended model and training-paradigm choices for real-world clinical deployment.

Abstract: Survival analysis is central to clinical decision-making, yet reliable time-to-event models require large, diverse cohorts that are rarely available at a single institution, while privacy regulations restrict the centralization of patient data. Federated learning (FL) offers a privacy-preserving alternative by training shared models without exchanging raw data, but its effectiveness for survival modeling under realistic, heterogeneous conditions remains insufficiently understood. This paper presents a systematic, multi-model evaluation of federated survival analysis on a cross-institutional breast cancer cohort with naturally heterogeneous distributed clients. Three representative survival models, the Cox Proportional Hazards model, DeepSurv, and Random Survival Forest (RSF), are compared across centralized, local, and federated training, and three federated optimization strategies (FedAvg, FedProx, and FedAdam) are assessed for the gradient-based models. Results show that FL consistently outperforms local training and approaches, and occasionally exceeds, centralized performance, while RSF offers the best overall balance of discrimination, calibration, and robustness across heterogeneous clients. We further find that performance depends on the diversity of client distributions, and that FedAvg and FedProx are stronger and more stable than FedAdam. Based on these findings, we derive practical, decision-oriented guidelines mapping data, privacy, interpretability, and resource constraints to recommended model and training-paradigm choices for federated survival modeling in healthcare.

</details>


### [126] [MGI: Member vs Generated Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23872)
*Bihe Zhao,Michel Meintz,Juangui Xu,Franziska Boenisch,Adam Dziedzic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper formalizes "Member vs Generated Inference" (MGI), showing that existing membership inference and attribution methods fail to distinguish training data from model-generated samples. The authors propose Data Circuit Breaker (DCB), a three-stage method leveraging autoencoder and latent generator signals, which consistently outperforms prior approaches across multiple generative models and generalizes to derivative model settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As generative models produce increasingly realistic outputs and can memorize training data, a new challenge emerges: distinguishing whether a given sample is an authentic training member or was generated by the model. Existing membership inference methods systematically misclassify generated samples as training members, while attribution methods often misclassify training members as generated. Both fail because they rely on likelihood-related signals elevated for both training and generated samples. This motivates the formalization of MGI and the need for a method that can reliably separate the two.

Method: The authors propose Data Circuit Breaker (DCB), a three-stage method that exploits the modular architecture of modern generative models (autoencoder + latent generator). Stage 1 uses autoencoder reconstruction error — training samples reconstruct well, while generated samples (which pass through the autoencoder's encoder-decoder) exhibit distinct error patterns. Stage 2 examines latent-space characteristics, comparing the sample's latent representation against the generator's prior distribution. Stage 3 combines these complementary signals via a lightweight classifier. The method is evaluated on image autoregressive models and diffusion models, tested on near-duplicate memorized outputs, and extended to derivative model settings where new models are trained on generated data.

Result: DCB consistently outperforms both membership inference methods (which misclassify generated samples as members) and attribution methods (which misclassify members as generated) across multiple generative model architectures. It remains effective even when models reproduce near-duplicates of training samples and generalizes to challenging derivative model settings, where new models are trained on synthetic data from a source model.

Conclusion: The paper establishes MGI as a distinct and important inference problem, demonstrates that existing likelihood-based approaches are fundamentally ill-suited for it, and provides DCB as a robust solution that decomposes the generative pipeline to capture complementary signals. This has implications for data provenance, copyright verification, and auditing in an era of increasingly prevalent synthetic content.

Abstract: As generative models increasingly produce samples that are indistinguishable from human-created content, it becomes difficult to determine whether a given data point was part of a model's natural training set or was generated by the model itself, especially when models memorize and reproduce training data. We formalize this challenge as Member vs Generated Inference (MGI): given a sample and a target generative model, infer whether the sample is a true training member or a generated output of that model. Focusing on image generation, we show that existing membership inference methods systematically misclassify generated samples as training members, while attribution-based methods often misclassify true members as generated. This failure arises because both approaches rely on likelihood-related signals that are similarly elevated for training examples and for the model's own outputs. To address MGI, we propose Data Circuit Breaker (DCB), a three-stage method that combines complementary signals from a generative model's autoencoder and latent generator to distinguish training members from generated samples. Across multiple generative models, including image autoregressive and diffusion models, DCB consistently addresses the shortcomings of membership inference and attribution methods, remains effective even when models reproduce near-duplicates of training samples, and generalizes to challenging model derivative settings in which new models are trained on generated data.

</details>


### [127] [GRACE: Gated Refinement for Accurate Causal Edge Discovery in High-Dimensional Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23880)
*Mohammad Fesanghary,Abhinav Havaldar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRACE is a causal discovery method that refines a high-recall linear CI skeleton using gated $L_0$-regularized models, producing a clean bimodal separation for edge selection. It outperforms both constraint-based and score-based alternatives on synthetic benchmarks (up to $d=100$) and recovers 9/11 causal edges on a real river-flow dataset, while being up to 75× faster than nonlinear CI tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern time-series with many interacting variables make causal discovery difficult. Constraint-based methods using nonlinear conditional independence (CI) tests are statistically rigorous but computationally infeasible at scale. Score-based methods avoid CI testing but rely on arbitrary thresholds to binarize continuous edge scores, producing narrow, overlapping score distributions that make the binary decision brittle.

Method: GRACE uses a two-stage pipeline: (1) a fast linear CI skeleton (e.g., partial correlation) provides high-recall candidate edges; (2) a single gated predictive model with Hard Concrete gates and $L_0$ regularization prunes false positives. Each candidate edge has an independent gate whose value concentrates near 0 or 1 due to the $L_0$ penalty, yielding clean bimodal separation. The regularization strength is automatically adapted to problem dimensionality and skeleton density. A temporal bootstrap variant handles time-series with confounders, variable propagation lags, and distributional shifts.

Result: On synthetic benchmarks across diverse topologies (scale-free, Erdős-Rényi, small-world) up to $d=100$, GRACE substantially improves F1 over its base CI method while maintaining high precision, and outperforms attention-based and score-based alternatives. It matches or exceeds expensive nonlinear CI tests at ~75× speedup. On a real-world Elbe River flow dataset with rainfall confounders and distributional shifts, the temporal bootstrap variant recovers 9 of 11 true causal edges with only 1 false positive (F1=0.86, AUROC=0.99), reducing the skeleton's 106 false positives by 99%.

Conclusion: GRACE provides a principled, scalable approach to causal discovery that combines the statistical rigor of constraint-based methods with the efficiency of learned edge selection via $L_0$ regularization. The clean bimodal gate distribution eliminates the need for arbitrary binarization thresholds, and the method generalizes effectively to real-world non-stationary time-series settings.

Abstract: From climate teleconnections to gene regulation, modern time-series datasets encompass tens or hundreds of interacting variables, making causal discovery increasingly challenging. Constraint-based methods offer statistical rigor but their nonlinear CI tests are infeasible at scale, while score-based alternatives avoid CI testing but require arbitrary thresholds to binarize continuous edge scores. We propose GRACE ($\textbf{G}$ated $\textbf{R}$efinement for $\textbf{A}$ccurate $\textbf{C}$ausal $\textbf{E}$dge discovery), which refines constraint-based discovery using Hard Concrete gates with $L_0$ regularization: each candidate edge has an independent gate whose values concentrate near 0 or 1, yielding a clean bimodal separation that makes the binary decision robust, unlike the narrow, overlapping score distributions produced by $L_1$ and attention-based methods. A fast linear CI skeleton provides high-recall candidates; a single gated model then prunes false positives by learning which edges genuinely improve prediction, with automatic regularization adapted to problem dimensions and skeleton density. Systematic experiments on synthetic benchmarks, spanning diverse graph topologies (scale-free, Erdős-R'enyi, small-world) and dimensionalities up to $d=100$, show that GRACE substantially improves F1 over its base CI method while maintaining high precision, and outperforms attention-based and score-based alternatives. GRACE matches or exceeds expensive nonlinear CI tests at a fraction of the cost ($75\times$ faster). On a real-world river flow dataset, where rainfall confounders, variable propagation lags, and distributional shifts violate standard assumptions, a temporal bootstrap variant of GRACE recovers 9 of 11 causal edges along the Elbe River with only 1 false positive ($F_1 = 0.86$, AUROC${} = 0.99$), reducing the skeleton's 106 false positives by 99%.

</details>


### [128] [ARIA: Adaptive Region-Based Importance Allocation for Conditional Diffusion Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23898)
*Loay Mualem,Vinh Tong,Samir Darouich,Mathias Niepert*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ARIA is a framework for conditional diffusion model distillation that adaptively allocates training effort across coarse regions of the conditioning space by tracking teacher-student discrepancy online, improving performance especially in unseen and underrepresented regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge distillation in conditional diffusion models faces a bottleneck: paired image-condition data is limited, synthetic image generation is expensive, and condition pools (e.g., text prompts) are vast. While recent work addresses this by switching conditions during training, it raises the question of how to optimally allocate training effort across a large conditioning corpus.

Method: The proposed ARIA framework partitions the conditioning space into coarse regions and maintains online estimates of teacher-student discrepancy at the region level. Training updates are adaptively allocated to regions where misalignment persists, using a tracking mechanism grounded in bounded variance and drift assumptions, all while preserving the original distillation objective.

Result: ARIA improves over the RC (Region-Conditioned) baseline across most architectures and settings, with the most pronounced gains in unseen and underrepresented conditioning regimes. A theoretical analysis validates that the tracking mechanism follows the evolving discrepancy during training.

Conclusion: Adaptive allocation of training effort based on region-level teacher-student discrepancy is an effective and theoretically grounded strategy for scaling conditional diffusion distillation to large conditioning spaces.

Abstract: Distilling conditional diffusion models aims to transfer the behavior of a large teacher to a smaller student while preserving alignment across conditioning inputs. Unlike recognition tasks, knowledge distillation in conditional diffusion often struggles to transfer knowledge beyond the training distribution, since the predicted noise strongly depends on the conditioning signal. As a result, effective distillation requires exploring a large conditioning space. In practical settings, this creates a major bottleneck. Paired image-condition data may be limited, and generating synthetic images for every available condition is often computationally infeasible, while the pool of conditions, such as text prompts, can be extremely large. Recent work addresses this issue by switching conditions during training, exposing the student to a broader conditioning space without changing the distillation objective. Yet this raises a complementary question: once a large conditioning corpus is available, how should the training effort be allocated? In this work, we introduce ARIA, a framework that adaptively allocates training effort across coarse regions of the conditioning space. By maintaining online estimates of teacher-student discrepancy at the region level, ARIA focuses updates where misalignment persists while preserving the original distillation objective. Empirically, ARIA improves over RC across most architectures and settings, with the clearest gains observed in unseen and underrepresented regimes. We also provide a theoretical analysis showing that the proposed tracking mechanism follows the evolving discrepancy during training under bounded variance and drift assumptions.

</details>


### [129] [Cyclic Denoising Reveals Ultrastable Memories in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24000)
*Rishabh Sharma,Stefano Martiniani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cyclic denoising — repeated forward/reverse diffusion at controlled noise levels — serves as a novel extraction attack on image diffusion models, revealing memorized training data (stock photos, watermarks, web artifacts) by driving samples toward ultrastable attractors without requiring gradients, prompts, or training data knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a physics-inspired, gradient-free extraction attack that probes the learned distribution of diffusion models and audits memorization, addressing limitations of existing generate-and-filter methods that require prompted generation and post-hoc filtering.

Method: Cyclic denoising: iteratively applying forward diffusion (noising) followed by reverse diffusion (denoising) at controlled noise amplitudes, inspired by random organization in disordered solids. The method operates solely at the sampler level using fully unconditioned protocols across Stable Diffusion v1.4 (latent-space) and a pixel-space DDPM.

Result: The method reveals attractor basins with a broad stability spectrum, including ultrastable attractors that regenerate after near-total corruption and persist through thousands of cycles. Many correspond to memorized training images. A yielding-like transition is observed: low-amplitude cycling yields trivial fixed points/limit cycles; larger amplitudes induce rearrangements, basin hopping, and trapping in memorized attractor basins. Hierarchical partial absorption, prompt-stabilized basins, and cross-initial-condition universality are also observed.

Conclusion: Cyclic denoising is both a physics-inspired probe of generative model landscapes and a practical tool for memorization auditing, with significant implications for privacy, copyright compliance, and model fingerprinting.

Abstract: We introduce cyclic denoising -- repeated forward and reverse diffusion at controlled noise amplitudes -- as an extraction attack for image diffusion models. Inspired by random organization in disordered solids, cyclic denoising exposes regions of the learned distribution that are largely inaccessible to standard sampling. The dynamics drive samples toward attractors with a broad stability spectrum. The deepest attractors are ultrastable: they regenerate after near-total corruption and persist through thousands of noising-denoising cycles. Many of these attractors correspond to memorized training images, including stock photographs, brand watermarks, and web-crawl artifacts. The attack requires only sampler-level control, with no gradients, weight inspection, prompts, captions, or prior knowledge of the training data. Unlike generate-and-filter attacks, which rely on large-scale prompted generation and post-hoc similarity or membership-inference filtering, our main protocol is fully unconditioned. We demonstrate the phenomenon in Stable Diffusion v1.4 and in a pixel-space DDPM, showing consistent behavior across latent- and pixel-space diffusion models. Across noise amplitudes, we observe a yielding-like transition: low-amplitude cycling produces trivial absorbing fixed points or limit cycles, while larger amplitudes induce rearrangements, basin hopping, and long-lived trapping in structured memorized attractor basins. We also observe hierarchical partial absorption, prompt-stabilized basins, and cross-initial-condition universality of the recovered attractor set. Our results therefore show that cyclic denoising is both a physics-inspired probe of generative landscapes and a practical tool for memorization auditing, with implications for privacy, copyright compliance, and model fingerprinting.

</details>


### [130] [Closing the Loop: Formally Verified Law as a Reward Signal for Self-Improving Legal AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23913)
*Armin Heydari,Torben Leowald*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes an architecture combining LLM-driven autoformalization, a formal legal calculus (extending Catala), a verification kernel, and proof-trace-based explanations to create verifiable reward signals for legal AI training. Demonstrates on German procedural deadlines, US Commerce Clause analysis, and cross-jurisdictional sanction proportionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legal AI lacks verifiable training signals due to the open-textured nature of law, creating a reinforcement-learning loop gap. The authors aim to adapt the "LLM proposes, verifier disposes" paradigm from mathematical AI to law, providing either provable correctness (for computational law) or structural guarantees (for open-textured reasoning).

Method: A multi-component architecture: (1) LLM-driven autoformalization translates legal problems into a formal legal calculus extending Catala; (2) a verification kernel checks formal proofs; (3) explanation generation grounded in formal proof traces. The deterministic external verifier supplies verifiable outcomes, closing the RL loop gap by providing ground-truth reward signals for training.

Result: Demonstrated on three legally diverse tasks: procedural deadline calculations under German law, Commerce Clause analysis under U.S. constitutional law, and cross-jurisdictional sanction proportionality. For computational components, the architecture delivers provable correctness; for open-textured analysis, it guarantees structural completeness (all argument stages addressed, argumentation at correct stages, valid deductive links).

Conclusion: The architecture bridges formal verification and legal AI by providing verifiable reward signals, closing the RL training loop gap in law. It offers a structural advantage for legal AI training while respecting the distinction between mechanically computable legal rules and open-textured legal reasoning requiring structural rather than substantive guarantees.

Abstract: This article develops an architecture that creates a formally verifiable reward signal to train legal AI, adapting the LLM proposes, verifier disposes paradigm from mathematical AI to the distinctive demands of law. We present an architecture comprising LLM-driven autoformalization into a formal legal calculus extending Catala, a verification kernel, and explanation generation grounded in formal proof traces. For the computational components of law, the architecture provides provable correctness. For open-textured legal analysis, it provides structural guarantees: every required stage of the legal argument is addressed, argumentation is exercised at the correct stages and not omitted, and the deductive links between steps are valid. We demonstrate the architecture on procedural deadline calculations in German law, Commerce Clause analysis in U.S. constitutional law, and cross-jurisdictional sanction proportionality. We further show that the same architecture has a structural advantage for legal AI training: a deterministic external verifier supplies verifiable outcomes for legal problems and thereby closes the traditional reinforcement-learning loop gap in law.

</details>


### [131] [Catastrophic Compositional Generation: Why Vanilla Diffusion Models Fail to Extrapolate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23920)
*Duncan Soiffer,Chandler Squires,Yuan Guan,Jason Hartford,Pradeep Ravikumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper argues that compositional generation is fundamentally infeasible for standard conditional diffusion models at inference time. Through theory and experiments, it shows that score estimation error—not inference approximation error—is the primary bottleneck, especially when the target distribution is out-of-distribution relative to the training sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent methods like Feynman-Kac correction focus on reducing inference-time approximation error for compositional generation with diffusion models. However, the authors hypothesize that a more fundamental problem—score estimation error—limits performance, particularly in out-of-distribution compositional settings. The work aims to rigorously demonstrate this infeasibility and shift the research focus toward alternative approaches.

Method: The authors employ theory-guided generalization arguments to characterize the role of score estimation error versus inference approximation error. They design careful experiments on both synthetic and realistic datasets to empirically validate their conjecture that no inference-time technique can efficiently sample from compositionally-defined target distributions when they lie far from the source training distributions.

Result: Results show that score estimation error has a catastrophic effect on compositional generation performance when the target distribution is out-of-distribution relative to the sources. While Feynman-Kac correction and similar methods reduce inference-time approximation error, this benefit is dwarfed by the impact of inaccurate score estimates, confirming the infeasibility of vanilla conditional diffusion models for this task.

Conclusion: Compositional generation with vanilla conditional diffusion models is often infeasible at inference time. The dominant failure mode is score estimation error rather than inference approximation error, especially under distribution shift. The paper calls for fundamentally different approaches beyond inference-time correction techniques.

Abstract: The task of compositional generation involves using a conditional generative model, trained only on a subset of the possible conditions, to produce samples from compositionally-defined target distributions such as a geometric combination of the source distributions. In this work, we argue that this task is often infeasible for vanilla conditional diffusion models: we conjecture that no inference-time technique can efficiently produce samples from the target distribution in certain well-motivated settings. This idea is supported by theory-guided generalization arguments and carefully-designed experiments on both synthetic and realistic data. In particular, while recent methods such as Feynman-Kac correction reduce inference-time approximation error, our results show that score estimation error has a more catastrophic effect on performance when the target distribution is out-of-distribution with respect to the sources, highlighting the need for a different approach to this task.

</details>


### [132] [KLip-PPO: A per-sample KL perspective on PPO-Clip](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23932)
*Riccardo Colletti,Robin Holzinger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PPO's clipped surrogate objective is mathematically equivalent to a KL-penalized surrogate with a per-sample, closed-form coefficient that depends on the importance ratio and advantage. This identity holds at every minibatch step, and the two losses produce indistinguishable training curves on MuJoCo benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The literature treats PPO-Clip and PPO-KL as separate algorithms with distinct gradients, hyperparameters, and implementations. The authors aim to unify these formulations by exposing a structural equivalence that has been overlooked, revealing that the clipped surrogate implicitly encodes a per-sample KL penalty.

Method: The authors derive mathematically that the gradient of PPO's clipped surrogate equals the gradient of a KL-penalized surrogate where the KL coefficient is a closed-form function of the importance ratio and advantage, varying per sample. They validate this identity analytically at every minibatch step and across the entire inner loop, then empirically confirm indistinguishable training dynamics on five MuJoCo continuous-control tasks.

Result: The gradient equivalence holds exactly. On five MuJoCo benchmarks, the two formulations produce indistinguishable training curves. The analysis reveals that PPO-Clip's implicit per-sample penalty is a step function at the trust-region boundary, a fact obscured by the min operator in the standard formulation.

Conclusion: PPO-Clip and a per-sample adaptive KL-penalized objective are gradient-equivalent. This reformulation uncovers the step-function structure of the implicit penalty, suggesting that the shape of the per-sample coefficient is a natural design axis for generalizing PPO and developing new trust-region algorithms.

Abstract: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) is the standard policy-gradient algorithm for on-policy reinforcement learning. The literature presents it in two forms, a clipped surrogate that bounds the importance ratio between successive policies and a Kullback-Leibler penalty between them. These forms are treated as separate algorithms with their own gradients, their own hyperparameters, and their own reference implementations, and a sizeable body of empirical work compares them. We show that the gradient of the clipped surrogate is reproduced exactly by a Kullback-Leibler surrogate whose coefficient varies per sample, with closed-form dependence on the importance ratio and the advantage. The identity holds at every minibatch step and across the entire inner loop, and on five MuJoCo continuous-control benchmarks the two losses produce indistinguishable training curves. The reformulation exposes a structural feature of the clipped surrogate that the min notation hides. PPO-Clip's implicit per-sample penalty is a step function at the boundary of the trust region, and the shape of this coefficient is the natural design axis for generalising the algorithm. We sketch the resulting follow-up directions in the discussion.

</details>


### [133] [DREG: A Layer-Wise Jacobian Regularization as a General-Purpose Penalty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23942)
*Rowan Martnishn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A large-scale empirical study (960 experiments) finds that Derivative Regularization (DREG) achieves the highest overall accuracy among six regularizers, excels under GELU activations (relevant to modern transformers), and is most beneficial under data scarcity — all with a single fixed hyperparameter, supporting its use as a plug-and-play regularizer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the proliferation of regularization techniques for deep neural networks, there is limited systematic understanding of when, where, and why Derivative Regularization (DREG) — which penalizes large activation derivatives — is effective relative to other regularizers across diverse settings.

Method: A fully-crossed factorial sweep of 960 experiments spanning 4 activation functions, 6 regularizers (including DREG, Weight Decay, IGPen, and Spectral Normalization), 8 datasets (both vision and NLP), and 5 random seeds. DREG is applied with a single fixed hyperparameter (λ = 10⁻²·⁵) with no per-dataset tuning. Statistical significance assessed via Wilcoxon test.

Result: DREG achieves highest overall and clean-regime accuracy (significantly outperforming unregularized baseline, Weight Decay, and IGPen; p ≤ 0.031). It ranks second in noise robustness behind Spectral Normalization. DREG is globally best under GELU activation, particularly on messy vision and NLP benchmarks. Its advantage is most pronounced under data scarcity, consistent with serving as a geometric inductive bias compensating for limited data.

Conclusion: DREG functions as an effective plug-and-play regularizer, concentrating regularization pressure on layers with largest activation derivatives rather than uniformly constraining the network. Its strong performance under GELU and data-scarce regimes suggests direct applicability to modern transformer-based architectures and practical deployment scenarios.

Abstract: We present a large-scale empirical study isolating the contributions of the Derivative Regularization penalty (DREG). Across a fully-crossed factorial sweep of 960 experiments spanning 4 activations, 6 regularizers, 8 datasets, and 5 random seeds, we ask: when, where, and why does DREG work? Our results establish three principal findings. First, DREG achieves the highest overall and clean-regime accuracy among all regularizers evaluated (significantly so against the unregularized baseline, Weight Decay, and IGPen; Wilcoxon $p \leq 0.031$). It ranks second in noise robustness behind Spectral Normalization (SN) - the only two layer-wise regularizers in the study. Second, DREG is globally the best-performing regularizer under GELU, the default activation in modern transformer architectures, particularly on both messy vision and messy NLP benchmarks, suggesting direct applicability to frontier deep learning settings. Third, DREG's advantage over competing regularizers is most pronounced under data scarcity, consistent with its role as a geometric inductive bias that substitutes for the regularizing effect of data volume. Throughout, DREG is applied with a single fixed hyperparameter $λ= 10^{-2.5}$ and no per-dataset tuning, supporting its characterization as a plug-and-play regularizer for neural networks with nontrivial Jacobian structure. These findings are consistent with DREG's design: concentrating regularization pressure on layers where the activation derivative is largest, rather than constraining the network uniformly.

</details>


### [134] [Learning the Koopman Operator using Attention Free Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23957)
*Mohammed Nagdi,Evangelos-Marios Nikolados,Alexey Yermakov,Mars Gao,Nathan Kutz,Filippo Menolascina*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Koopman autoencoders enable linear latent-space prediction but suffer from long-horizon drift. This paper proposes two remedies: (1) an Attention-Free Transformer (AFT) memory block for temporal context aggregation, and (2) dynamic re-encoding via online change-point detection to project predictions back onto the manifold. The combined approach yields lower error accumulation, faster inference, and more robust long-horizon rollouts across three benchmark systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Koopman operator theory combined with autoencoders allows nonlinear dynamical systems to be linearly predicted in a latent space. However, long-horizon predictions often drift off the learned manifold, causing phase and amplitude errors—especially on systems with switching dynamics, continuous spectra, or strong transients. There is a need for lightweight, computationally efficient mechanisms to suppress this error divergence while preserving the fast inference benefits of Koopman-based predictors.

Method: Two complementary components are introduced: (1) An Attention-Free Transformer (AFT) latent memory block that aggregates a short window of past latents to produce a corrected latent before each Koopman update. AFT operates in linear time (O(T)) rather than quadratic attention, adding only ~30k parameters (3d² + T²). (2) Dynamic re-encoding using lightweight online change-point detection triggers (EWMA, CUSUM, and sequential two-sample tests) that detect when latents drift off the manifold and re-project predictions through the autoencoder decoder–encoder pipeline. The approach is evaluated on three benchmark systems: Duffing oscillator, Repressilator, and IRMA, with comparisons against Koopman autoencoders, multi-head attention, GRU, and Transformer autoencoders.

Result: Koopman+AFT with optional dynamic re-encoding consistently reduces error accumulation compared to baseline Koopman autoencoders and matched-capacity multi-head attention. It attains markedly lower long-horizon error (up to 1000 steps) than GRU and Transformer autoencoders—both from initial conditions and with 50-step context—while maintaining lower inference latency. Ablations over trigger policies are also reported.

Conclusion: The proposed Koopman+AFT predictor with dynamic re-encoding provides a fast, compact, and robust framework for long-horizon dynamical system prediction. By combining linear-time temporal context aggregation with online manifold correction, the approach effectively suppresses error divergence and stays on the learned manifold, outperforming both attention-based and recurrent alternatives in accuracy and speed.

Abstract: Learning Koopman operators with autoencoders enables linear prediction in a latent space, but long-horizon rollouts often drift off the learned manifold, leading to phase and amplitude errors on systems with switching, continuous spectra, or strong transients. We introduce two complementary components that make Koopman predictors more robust. First, we add an attention-free latent memory (AFT) block that aggregates a short window of past latents to produce a corrected latent before each Koopman update. Unlike multi-head attention, AFT operates in linear time and adds only $\approx$30k parameters ($3d^2 + T^2$, fewer than matched multi-head attention), yet captures the local temporal context needed to suppress error divergence. Second, we propose dynamic re-encoding: lightweight, online change-point triggers (EWMA, CUSUM, and sequential two-sample tests) that detect latent drift and project predictions back onto the autoencoder manifold. Across three benchmark systems -- Duffing oscillator, Repressilator, IRMA -- our model consistently reduces error accumulation compared to a Koopman autoencoder and matched-capacity multi-head attention. We also compare against GRU and Transformer autoencoders, evaluated both from initial conditions and with a 50-step context, and find that Koopman+AFT (with optional re-encoding) attains markedly lower long-horizon error while maintaining lower inference latency. We report improvements over horizons up to 1000 steps, together with ablations over trigger policies. The result is a fast, compact predictor that stays on the learned manifold over long horizons.

</details>


### [135] [Forget Without Compromise: Nexus Sampling for Streaming KV-Cache Eviction Under Fixed Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23961)
*Duc Duong,Hoang Anh Duy Le,Jianwen Xie,Anshumali Shrivastava,Zhaozhuo Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Nexus Sampling proposes a training-free KV cache eviction method that combines iterative "Nexus scoring" to surface bridge tokens with weighted reservoir sampling, replacing the standard direct-attention + deterministic top-K approach. It theoretically and empirically improves long-context retention, matching dense attention within 1% on LongBench at 80% eviction while using up to 10x less cache memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context and agentic LLM workloads exceed fixed KV cache budgets, forcing continuous token eviction. Existing methods rely on per-step direct-attention scoring followed by deterministic top-K selection, which irreversibly discards tokens that a single below-cutoff attention score cannot distinguish from noise. Subtly important "bridge tokens" — tokens that connect disparate pieces of context — are permanently lost under this paradigm.

Method: Nexus Sampling introduces two components: (1) Nexus scoring — an iterative walk over the direct-attention graph that propagates importance from directly attended tokens to "bridge tokens" that connect key context pieces, surfacing subtly important tokens that single-step attention misses; and (2) weighted reservoir sampling — a probabilistic retention mechanism that replaces deterministic top-K selection with inclusion-probability-based sampling, giving every token a chance of survival proportional to its Nexus score rather than an irreversible cutoff.

Result: At 80% KV cache eviction, Nexus Sampling matches dense attention performance within 1% on the LongBench benchmark. It outperforms deterministic top-K baselines on retrieval-heavy tasks and achieves up to 10x reduction in per-sequence cache memory usage.

Conclusion: Nexus Sampling provides a principled, training-free alternative to standard KV cache eviction. By replacing irreversible top-K selection with probabilistic weighted reservoir sampling driven by iteratively refined Nexus scores, the method better preserves subtly important tokens under aggressive cache budgets, enabling efficient long-context inference without sacrificing retrieval quality.

Abstract: Long-context and agentic LLM workloads push the KV cache past any fixed memory budget, forcing the inference stack to permanently evict tokens at every step of a continuous-inference stream. Existing methods all share the same template, a per-step direct-attention score followed by deterministic top-$K$ selection, which converts a single below-cutoff step into an irreversible verdict and permanently erases any subtly important token that direct attention cannot single out from noise.
