To address this challenge, we propose Nexus Sampling, a training-free eviction method that pairs Nexus scoring, an iterative walk over direct attention that surfaces bridge tokens, with weighted reservoir sampling, which retains tokens with inclusion probability in place of deterministic top-$K$. Theoretically, we show that Nexus Sampling dominates deterministic top-$K$ in long-run survival of subtly important tokens. Empirically, at 80% KV cache eviction, Nexus Sampling matches dense attention within 1% on LongBench while outperforming top-$K$ baselines on retrieval-heavy tasks, with up to 10x smaller per-sequence cache memory.

</details>


### [136] [3D Masked Autoencoders are Robust Learners of Volumetric and Multimodal Cellular Representations for Microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23964)
*Amirhossein Kardoost,Lion Gleiter,Tingying Peng,Carsten Marr*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 3D masked autoencoders consistently outperform 2D variants on volumetric single-cell microscopy tasks, with cross-modal alignment to protein language models and frequency-domain regularization yielding state-of-the-art protein localization and protein-protein interaction predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite cells being inherently 3D, self-supervised learning in fluorescence microscopy predominantly uses 2D projections. The authors aim to rigorously quantify whether native 3D modeling offers systematic advantages over 2D approaches for representation learning on volumetric data.

Method: The authors conduct a controlled comparison between 2D masked autoencoders (max-projection and slice-based) and 3D masked autoencoders on volumetric microscopy data. They incorporate cross-modal supervision by aligning visual representations with the pretrained protein language model ESM2, and introduce channel cross-attention and frequency-domain regularization to better exploit 3D spatial context.

Result: MAE-3D achieves ROC-AUC of 0.865 on protein-protein interaction, outperforming prior methods by up to +0.025. For protein localization, the best 3D model reaches AUC_micro of 0.952 and F1_micro of 0.742, absolute improvements of +0.003 and +0.010 over previous state-of-the-art. Cross-modal supervision with ESM2 benefits volumetric models more than 2D variants.

Conclusion: Native 3D modeling combined with multimodal protein language model alignment provides clear and consistent gains for representation learning in single-cell microscopy, establishing a new state of the art on key downstream tasks.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning in fluorescence microscopy often relies on 2D projections, despite the inherently three-dimensional nature of cells. We present a systematic comparison of 2D and 3D masked autoencoders (MAE-2D vs. MAE-3D) on volumetric microscopy data. Under matched architectures and training protocols, MAE-3D consistently outperforms 2D max-projection and slice-based variants on downstream single-cell tasks. We further align visual representations with a pretrained protein language model (ESM2) and show that cross-modal supervision yields larger gains for volumetric models. Channel cross-attention and frequency-domain regularization are critical for leveraging 3D spatial context. On a protein--protein interaction task, MAE-3D achieves a ROC--AUC of 0.865, outperforming prior methods by up to +0.025. For protein localization, our best 3D model attains state-of-the-art AUC$_{\text{micro}}$ (0.952) and F1$_{\text{micro}}$ (0.742), improving over previous approaches by +0.003 and +0.010 absolute, respectively. Overall, these results demonstrate the advantages of native 3D modeling and multimodal alignment for representation learning in single-cell microscopy.

</details>


### [137] [A Comparative Study of Bayesian Contextual Bandits for Real-Time Warehouse Sorter Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23977)
*Tina Dongxu Li,Mouhacine Benosman,Ken Meszaros,Trevor Dardik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper compares three ML frameworks (LR+GDO, XGB+BO, BCB) for real-time sorter diversion control in e-commerce warehouses. BCB achieves the highest reward uplift (2.03%) over the heuristic baseline, with superior characteristics including Bang-Bang optimality, online learning, and low inference latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sorter diversion systems in high-volume e-commerce warehouses rely on static weight configurations in cost functions, which cannot adapt to dynamic operational contexts (volume mode, congestion, equipment status, upstream/downstream dependencies). This motivates the need for real-time adaptive optimization.

Method: The authors conduct a comparative study of three hybrid ML frameworks: (1) Linear Regression with Gradient Descent Optimization, (2) XGBoost with Bayesian Optimization, and (3) Bayesian Contextual Bandits. A high-fidelity physics-aware emulator is used to overcome the cold-start problem and enable safe offline-to-online transition. Evaluation covers reward model predictive accuracy, contextual sensitivity, action distribution, and projected reward uplift.

Result: Tree-based reward models (XGB+BO) offer slightly better predictive power, but BCB achieves the highest overall performance with 2.03% reward uplift over the heuristic baseline. BCB also demonstrates a decisive time-optimal policy consistent with Bang-Bang control theory, continuous online learning capability, balanced exploration-exploitation, and significantly shorter inference latency.

Conclusion: The BCB framework shows strong potential for real-time control optimization in large-scale warehouse environments, warranting further investigation toward operational deployment.

Abstract: Efficient sorter diversion control of automated material handling systems (MHS) is critical for optimizing operational efficiency in large-scale warehouse environments. In this study, we use an inbound receiving sorter at a high-volume e-commerce warehouse as our primary use case, where the sorter diversion system relies on cost functions with static weight configurations that fail to adapt to highly dynamic system contexts, such as volume mode, congestion level, equipment physical status, and upstream/downstream dependencies. To address this real-time sorter diversion optimization challenge, we conducted a comparative study of three candidate hybrid machine learning frameworks: Linear Regression with Gradient Descent Optimization (LR+GDO), XGBoost with Bayesian Optimization (XGB+BO), and Bayesian Contextual Bandits (BCB). Model training and evaluation were enabled by leveraging a high-fidelity physics-aware emulator to overcome the cold-start problem and allow a safe transition from offline to online learning. We performed comprehensive evaluations including reward model predictive accuracy, contextual sensitivity, action distribution, and projected reward uplift. Our results demonstrate that while tree-based reward models offer slightly better predictive power, the BCB framework achieved overall higher performance with 2.03% reward uplift over the heuristic baseline. Furthermore, BCB exhibits several superior characteristics, such as its decisive time-optimal policy backed by Bang-Bang control theory, continuous online learning capability, strategic balance between exploration and exploitation, and significantly shorter inference latency. These results demonstrate the potential of the BCB framework for real-time control optimization in large-scale warehouse environments, motivating further investigation toward operational deployment.

</details>


### [138] [Offline Reinforcement Learning for Warehouse SLAM Throughput Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23978)
*Tina Dongxu Li,Mouhacine Benosman,Rajat Kumar,Kevin Tan,Ken Meszaros,Trevor Dardik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents an offline RL framework for optimizing SLAM throughput control in warehouses, using historical data to train policies that balance throughput maximization with downstream stability. CQL outperforms other offline RL algorithms, achieving 22.97% system health improvement and 3.18% reduction in throttling duration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SLAM throughput control in warehouse fulfillment environments directly affects system congestion and operational efficiency. Manual or heuristic-based throughput settings fail to adaptively balance the trade-off between maximizing throughput and maintaining downstream stability, motivating an RL-based approach that can learn optimal throttling behaviors from historical operational data without risky online exploration.

Method: The framework features: (1) a history-informed state representation capturing temporal dependencies; (2) action space abstraction for delayed-impact control; and (3) a reward function incorporating both upstream and downstream operational metrics. The architecture is algorithm-agnostic, instantiated with three offline RL algorithms (including CQL). Models are trained on de-identified historical logs from a large-scale warehouse. Policy evaluation employs a multi-method strategy: model-free approaches (immediate reward regression, long-horizon Fitted Q Evaluation) and model-based Deep Koopman dynamics evaluation.

Result: The CQL policy consistently outperforms alternatives across evaluation methods, achieving a 22.97% improvement in system health and a 3.18% reduction in average throttling duration compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Offline RL is a viable and effective approach for safe, scalable warehouse throughput control optimization, with CQL emerging as the strongest-performing algorithm in this domain.

Abstract: We present an offline reinforcement learning (RL) framework for optimizing SLAM throughput control in a warehouse fulfillment environment. SLAM (Scan/Label/Apply/Manifest) throughput directly influences system congestion and operational efficiency. Our RL-based control approach dynamically recommends SLAM throughput settings that adaptively balance throughput maximization with downstream stability through intelligent adjustment of throttling behavior. We include a history-informed state representation, action space abstraction for delayed-impact control, and a reward function that captures both upstream and downstream operational metrics. Our approach is algorithm-agnostic, enabling integration of multiple offline RL methods under a unified architecture. We instantiate our framework with three state-of-the-art offline RL algorithms, and trained the models offline using de-identified historical operational logs from a large-scale warehouse. Policy performance is evaluated using a comprehensive multi-method strategy. These include model-free approaches including immediate reward estimation via regression models and long-horizon Fitted Q Evaluation (FQE), as well as model-based Deep Koopman dynamics evaluation. Empirical results reveal that the CQL policy consistently outperforms alternatives, improving system health by 22.97% and reducing average throttling duration by 3.18%. These findings demonstrate the potential of offline RL for safe and scalable warehouse throughput control optimization.

</details>


### [139] [Learning to Trigger: Reinforcement Learning at the Large Hadron Collider](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23993)
*Zixin Ding,Shaghayegh Emam,Giovanna Salvi,Cecilia Tosciri,Abhijith Gandrakota,Jennifer Ngadiuba,Nhan Tran,Christian Herwig,David W. Miller,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a reinforcement learning approach (GFPO variants) for real-time tuning of trigger thresholds at the LHC, adapting to drifting conditions. The method improves in-tolerance time and signal efficiency over static baselines, and demonstrates the first RL-based trigger control on real CMS collision data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trigger menus at high-throughput facilities like the LHC are typically static and hand-tuned, becoming suboptimal as detector conditions, pileup, and background composition drift over time. There is a need for adaptive, online threshold tuning to maintain optimal signal efficiency while respecting bandwidth and rate constraints.

Method: The authors cast online trigger threshold tuning as a sequential decision-making problem. A reinforcement learning agent processes streaming summaries of recent rates and signal-sensitive features, then updates thresholds to maximize signal efficiency while tracking a target background rate within a tolerance band. They adapt Group-Filtered Policy Optimization (GFPO) to the streaming control setting and propose two variants, GFPO-F and GFPO-FR, which explicitly enforce background rate feasibility during training. Evaluation uses two representative triggers: an HT (total transverse energy) trigger and an anomaly-detection trigger based on reconstruction loss.

Result: On Monte Carlo streams, the agent improves the fraction of in-tolerance time intervals by 48% (HT) and 28% (AD), with up to 2% cumulative gain in signal efficiency on in-tolerance intervals. Transferring zero-shot to real CMS collision data (Run 283408), the agent achieves 56% (HT) and 28% (AD) in-tolerance improvement over baselines, with further signal-efficiency gains.

Conclusion: This work represents the first demonstration of RL-based trigger control on real LHC collision data, showing that learned adaptive threshold policies can significantly outperform static hand-tuned menus under realistic, drifting conditions, and can transfer from simulation to real data without fine-tuning.

Abstract: High-throughput scientific facilities such as the Large Hadron Collider depend on real-time event filtering (\textit{triggering}) under tight constraints on bandwidth, latency, and storage. In practice, trigger menus are largely static and hand-tuned and can become suboptimal as detector conditions, pileup, and background composition drift over time. We cast online threshold tuning as a sequential decision-making problem: a reinforcement learning agent ingests streaming summaries of recent rates and signal-sensitive features and updates trigger thresholds to maximize signal efficiency while tracking a target background rate within a tolerance band. We adapt Group-Filtered Policy Optimization (GFPO) to streaming control and introduce two variants (GFPO-F, GFPO-FR) that enforce background rate feasibility during training. On a benchmark that emulates realistic collider operation, we study two representative triggers: a total transverse energy ($H_{T}$) trigger sensitive to pileup variation, and an anomaly-detection (AD) trigger based on reconstruction loss for rare or non-standard signatures. On Monte Carlo streams, our agent increases the fraction of in-tolerance time intervals by 48\% ($H_T$) and 28\% (AD), with a cumulative gain of up to 2\% in signal efficiency on those in-tolerance intervals. Transferring from simulation to \emph{real} collision data (CMS Run 283408), the same agent, without fine-tuning, achieves a 56\% ($H_T$) and 28\% (AD) in-tolerance improvement over baselines, with further signal-efficiency gain on both triggers. To our knowledge, this is the \emph{first} demonstration of RL-based trigger control on real Large Hadron Collider collision data. Code is available at https://github.com/Zixind/GFPO\_LHC.

</details>


### [140] [Fast and Slow Variational Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24007)
*Subarnaduti Paul,Yohan Jung,Mohammad Emtiyaz Khan,Siddharth Swaroop,Thomas Möllenhoff,Martin Mundt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes CoVON (Continual IVON), an optimizer that incorporates fast-and-slow adaptation into the Variational Continual Learning (VCL) framework by merging past posteriors to slow knowledge drift, implemented seamlessly within the IVON optimizer (which has costs similar to Adam). CoVON consistently outperforms existing VCL optimizers and weight-regularization strategies across domain-incremental learning, continual pre-training, and LLM fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard optimizers like Adam lack built-in mechanisms for continual adaptation, specifically the fast-and-slow adaptation balance (stability vs. plasticity) rooted in neuroscience. Existing VCL-based approaches struggle to effectively incorporate slow adaptation to prevent catastrophic forgetting, and there is no consensus on how to integrate this principle into commonly used optimizers without significant overhead.

Method: The authors propose merging past posteriors (Gaussian approximations from previous tasks) to create a slowed-down, consolidated prior that reduces knowledge drift. This merged posterior serves as the prior in the VCL update, enabling fast-weight updates. The entire mechanism is implemented in the IVON (Improved Variational Online Newton) optimizer, which maintains per-parameter Gaussian posteriors and matches Adam's computational cost. The resulting CoVON optimizer performs Bayesian incremental learning with built-in slow adaptation via posterior merging, requiring no additional regularization terms.

Result: CoVON consistently improves over existing VCL-based optimizers and outperforms other weight-regularization strategies. These gains are demonstrated across three challenging continual learning settings: domain-incremental learning, continual pre-training, and fine-tuning of large language models.

Conclusion: Fast-and-slow adaptation can be effectively and efficiently integrated into modern optimizers through posterior merging within the VCL framework. CoVON provides a practical, near-zero-overhead solution for continual learning that bridges neuroscience-inspired principles with scalable deep learning optimization.

Abstract: Continual learning remains a major challenge for modern deep networks, partly because commonly used optimizers lack inherent mechanisms for continual adaptation. One such natural mechanism is fast and slow adaptation to balance stability and plasticity. This mechanism has deep roots in neuroscience and biology, but there is no consensus on how to best incorporate it in commonly used optimizers. Here, we show that this can be easily done via the VCL framework, where past posteriors are used as priors in the future. Our key idea is to incorporate slow adaptation via merging of past posteriors to slow down the drift in the knowledge as learning progresses. The merged posterior is then used as the prior in the VCL update to implement the fast-weight updates. These steps can be seamlessly implemented in the IVON optimizer, whose form and costs are nearly identical to that of Adam. We call this new optimizer the Continual IVON (CoVON) optimizer and show that it not only consistently improves over existing VCL optimizers, but also performs better than other weight-regularization strategies across domain-incremental learning, continual pre-training, and fine-tuning of large language models.

</details>


### [141] [You Don't Need to Run Every Eval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24020)
*Yuchen Zeng,Dimitris Papailiopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A public score matrix of 84 models × 133 benchmarks reveals the evaluation landscape is approximately rank-2, meaning a model's performance across all benchmarks is largely captured by just two latent factors. The authors develop BenchPress, a rank-2 matrix completion method, to accurately predict held-out scores and identify compact benchmark subsets that can recover full evaluation profiles within ~4 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern frontier model releases involve running 40+ benchmark evaluations, with many more conducted during training, design comparisons, and checkpoint selection. This is computationally expensive and potentially redundant. The authors investigate whether all these evaluations are necessary or whether the underlying structure of model performance across benchmarks permits dramatic reduction.

Method: The authors compile a public score matrix (84 models, 133 benchmarks, 2,604 filled cells, 23.3% density). They demonstrate approximate rank-2 structure via two confirmatory approaches: (1) hidden scores are best recovered using two factors, and (2) two factors explain >90% of score variation on shared benchmarks. They then design BenchPress, a logit-space rank-2 matrix completion method with a confidence layer that quantifies prediction trustworthiness. Using this, they search for minimal benchmark subsets (5-benchmark and cheaper 5-benchmark sets) that can recover full scorecards.

Result: BenchPress recovers held-out scores to within 4.6 points on average. A five-benchmark subset {GPQA-D, HLE, Codeforces, MMLU-Pro, ARC-AGI-1} recovers the full public scorecard to within 3.93 points. A cheaper alternative {GPQA-D, MMLU-Pro, Aider Polyglot, MATH-500, AIME 2026} predicts evaluations to within 4.55 points. The rank-2 structure holds robustly across the dataset.

Conclusion: The near-universal rank-2 structure of model benchmark scores means that running every evaluation is unnecessary; a small, strategically selected subset of benchmarks suffices to predict the rest with high accuracy. This has practical implications for reducing the cost and redundancy of model evaluation pipelines. The authors release the score matrix, BenchPress code, and an interactive prediction tool to facilitate adoption.

Abstract: A modern model release reports scores on 40+ benchmarks and the same evaluations were run many more times before it: to track training progress, compare design choices, and select the checkpoint for the release. But do we need to run every eval? We compile a public score matrix of 84 frontier models on 133 benchmarks (2,604 cells, 23.3% filled) and find it is approximately rank-2: a model's scores across all 133 benchmarks are largely determined by just two numbers. We confirm this in two ways: scores hidden from the matrix are best recovered using two factors, and two factors already explain over 90% of the variation among models on the benchmarks they share. Building on this, we design BenchPress: a logit-space rank-2 matrix completion method that recovers held-out scores to within 4.6 points, and a confidence layer that says when each prediction can be trusted. Using BenchPress, we find a subset of five benchmarks {GPQA-D, HLE, Codeforces, MMLU-Pro, ARC-AGI-1} that can recover the rest of a model's public scorecard to within 3.93 points. For a tighter inference budget, a cheaper set {GPQA-D, MMLU-Pro, Aider Polyglot, MATH-500, AIME 2026} can predict a model's evals to within 4.55. We release the score matrix, the BenchPress code, and an interactive tool that predicts any model's score on any benchmark.

</details>


### [142] [Information-Theoretic Classifier-Free Guidance with Adaptive Schedule Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24025)
*Haobo Chen,Xiangxiang Xu,Yuheng Bu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an information-theoretic framework for optimizing classifier-free guidance (CFG) schedules across the reverse diffusion trajectory, using a clean endpoint reference to specify desired consistency-coverage trade-offs and trajectory-level estimation formulas to avoid explicit density estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CFG improves condition consistency but reduces diversity — a consistency-coverage trade-off. It's unclear how to optimally allocate guidance strength across noise levels over the reverse trajectory, since CFG-induced distributions aren't simple fixed-time tilted distributions. Existing constant-guidance approaches are suboptimal.

Method: An information-theoretic framework that: (1) defines a clean endpoint reference specifying the desired trade-off, (2) formulates trajectory-level optimization to match the actual CFG-induced distribution to this reference, (3) derives sample-based formulas using score evaluations to estimate the objective without explicit density estimation, enabling practical schedule learning.

Result: On ImageNet-512 with EDM-XXL and COCO with SD-XL, learned schedules achieve competitive or improved consistency-coverage trade-offs over constant guidance baselines. The optimized schedules allocate guidance selectively across noise levels rather than uniformly.

Conclusion: Optimizing CFG schedules across the reverse trajectory via an information-theoretic objective yields better consistency-coverage trade-offs than constant guidance, with learned schedules revealing that guidance should be applied non-uniformly across noise levels.

Abstract: Diffusion models have achieved strong performance in image, text-to-image, and video generation, where conditional generation is often controlled by classifier-free guidance (CFG). CFG improves condition consistency by increasing a guidance weight, but stronger guidance typically reduces diversity and distributional coverage. It remains unclear how this consistency-coverage trade-off should be controlled across the reverse trajectory, since the distribution induced by CFG is not simply the fixed-time tilted distribution given by the guided score field. To address this issue, we propose an information-theoretic framework for CFG schedule optimization. Our approach uses a clean endpoint reference to specify the desired consistency-coverage trade-off, while optimizing the actual distribution induced by the guided sampler toward this reference. We derive trajectory-level formulas to estimate the objective from samples and score evaluations, avoiding explicit density estimation. On ImageNet-512 with EDM-XXL and COCO with SD-XL, the learned schedules achieve competitive or improved trade-offs over constant guidance and allocate guidance selectively across noise levels.

</details>


### [143] [RoPE-Aware Bit Allocation for KV-Cache Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24033)
*Fengfeng Liang,Yuechen Zhang,Jiaya Jia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Block-GTQ is a RoPE-aware key-cache quantization method that allocates bits block-wise based on frequency-block energy, significantly outperforming uniform quantizers on long-context retrieval, understanding, and reasoning tasks while enabling efficient packed-cache serving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KV-cache quantizers treat keys as flat vectors, ignoring that under RoPE a key's contribution to attention logits decomposes into position-dependent sums over 2D frequency blocks. High-energy RoPE blocks are more quantization-sensitive and deserve higher bit allocation.

Method: Block-GTQ is built on TurboQuant-MSE (TQ-MSE). For each layer and KV head, it computes a label-free energy score per RoPE block and greedily allocates integer bit widths by marginal gain, framing quantization as a block-wise bit-allocation optimization problem.

Result: Block-GTQ reduces per-layer MAE by 32-80% at 2-3 b/dim versus uniform TQ-MSE, winning all 367/367 layer comparisons. At K2V2 on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, NIAH average rises from 70.6→97.4 and LongBench-EN from 36.87→53.31. On AIME 2024/2025 with DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, K3V2 scores 51.7/37.5 vs fp16's 54.2/37.9. Packed K3V3 on Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct achieves 3.24× compression, 1.34× speedup at 128K, and peak memory reduction from 56.31→19.85 GB, remaining feasible at 256K/512K where fp16 OOMs.

Conclusion: Block-wise RoPE-aware bit allocation dramatically improves KV-cache quantization fidelity and downstream task performance, enabling near-fp16 quality at substantial compression ratios with practical serving benefits.

Abstract: Existing low-bit KV-cache quantizers often treat each cached key as a flat vector. Under RoPE, however, a key's contribution to a future attention logit decomposes into a position-dependent sum over two-dimensional frequency blocks. This makes key-cache quantization a block-wise bit-allocation problem: high-energy RoPE blocks are more sensitive to quantization error and should receive more bits. We introduce Block-GTQ, a RoPE-aware bit allocator for key-cache quantization built on TurboQuant-MSE(TQ-MSE). For each layer and KV head, Block-GTQ computes a label-free energy score for each RoPE block and greedily allocates integer bit widths by marginal gain. Under matched K/V bit budgets, Block-GTQ better preserves RoPE query-key logits on a ten-model diagnostic panel, cutting per-layer MAE by 32-80% at 2 and 3 b/dim K-only quantization and winning all 367/367 layer comparisons against uniform TQ-MSE. These fidelity gains translate to stronger downstream long-context retrieval, understanding, and reasoning. At K2V2 on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Block-GTQ raises the six-task NIAH average from 70.6 to 97.4, and the LongBench-EN average from 36.87 to 53.31. On AIME 2024/2025 with DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, without an fp16 recent-key buffer, Block-GTQ at K3V2 scores 51.7/37.5, close to fp16's 54.2/37.9, whereas uniform TQ-MSE collapses to 0.0/0.0. We further implement a packed-cache serving path. On a single H800 GPU with Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, packed K3V3 achieves 3.24x KV-cache compression with fp16-comparable quality, runs 1.34x faster than fp16 FlashAttention2 at 128K context, reduces peak memory from 56.31 GB to 19.85 GB, and remains feasible at 256K and 512K where fp16 OOMs. Code is available at https://github.com/JIA-Lab-research/blockgtq.

</details>


### [144] [Rapid FinFET Modelling Using an Autoencoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24046)
*Amit Sarkar Suman Sau,Swagata Mandal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning framework using an autoencoder compresses FinFET I-V curves into a low-dimensional latent space, with VDS explicitly incorporated as an input feature. The model reconstructs full I-V curves and extracts key device metrics (VTH, SS, gm) with high accuracy and minimal training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional compact models like BSIM-CMG require complex parameter extraction and may not fully capture device physics from measured data. There is a need for data-driven approaches that can efficiently model FinFET behavior, enable rapid device characterization, and support circuit-level simulation with minimal training data.

Method: A BSIM-CMG model was first calibrated to generate a dataset of ID-VG characteristics. An autoencoder was then trained on this data to compress full I-V curves into a low-dimensional latent space that encodes device physics. A key innovation is explicitly incorporating drain-to-source voltage (VDS) as an input feature to capture bias-dependent variation. The trained model reconstructs full I-V curves and directly extracts threshold voltage (VTH), subthreshold slope (SS), and peak transconductance (gm).

Result: The autoencoder-based framework successfully reconstructs full I-V curves and directly extracts critical device metrics (VTH, SS, gm) with high accuracy. The approach demonstrates that data-driven compact models built from actual characterization data can achieve strong performance with only minimal training data.

Conclusion: Data-driven compact models leveraging autoencoders offer a powerful and efficient alternative for FinFET device characterization, modeling, and circuit-level simulation. The explicit inclusion of bias parameters like VDS enhances model fidelity, and the approach requires minimal training data to achieve high accuracy.

Abstract: This work presents a machine learning framework that leverages an autoencoder (AE) for the efficient modeling of FinFET. We first calibrated a BSIM-CMG model to generate a dataset of current-voltage (ID-VG) characteristics. This data was used to train an autoencoder that compresses full I-V curves into a low-dimensional latent space, which intrinsically encodes key device physics. A key innovation is the explicit incorporation of parameter such as drain to source voltage (VDS) as an input feature, enhancing the model ability to capture bias dependent variation. The trained model successfully reconstructs full I-V curves and directly extracts critical device metrics including threshold voltage (VTH), subthreshold slope (SS), and peak transconductance (gm). This approach demonstrates that data driven compact models, built from actual characterization data, can achieve high accuracy with minimal training data, providing a powerful tool for rapid device characterization, modelling and circuit level simulation.

</details>


### [145] [RAVEN: A Regime-Aware Variable-context Expert Network for Financial Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24062)
*Cheng He,Zhenyu Guan,Xijie Liang,Defu Lian,Jiajia Li,Enhong Chen,Patrick P. C. Lee,Geng Hu,Zehao Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RAVEN is a Mixture-of-Experts framework that adaptively determines temporal context lengths for financial time series, using Cumulative Importance Thresholding to create nested windows routed to scale-specialized experts, plus a Global Compressed Representation branch and Correlation-Aware Weighting to align outputs. It achieves SOTA on stock index prediction, fund sales forecasting, and traffic benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial time series have non-stationary log-returns, extremely low SNR, and regime-dependent temporal dependencies. SOTA time series models use fixed context windows, which are mismatched to the time-varying optimal look-back horizon required by non-stationary price processes. This structural mismatch motivates an adaptive, data-dependent context mechanism.

Method: RAVEN consists of three core components: (1) A hierarchy of nested contiguous windows constructed by scoring patches in reverse chronological order and applying Cumulative Importance Thresholding (CIT) to derive prefix windows, each routed to a scale-specialized expert. (2) A Global Compressed Representation (GCR) branch operating in parallel over the full context to preserve global temporal coherence. (3) Correlation-Aware Weighting (CAW), which aligns variable-length expert outputs and penalizes pairwise cosine similarity before aggregation to mitigate redundancy from structured overlap among expert inputs.

Result: On cumulative log-return prediction, RAVEN improves Pearson correlation by 9.2% on HS300 and 20.2% on S&P500 versus baselines. On fund sales forecasting, MSE is reduced by 18.2%. On four PEMS traffic benchmarks, RAVEN achieves best results in 14 of 16 metrics.

Conclusion: Adaptive temporal context determination via a nested-window Mixture-of-Experts design effectively addresses the non-stationarity and regime-switching nature of financial time series, outperforming fixed-context SOTA models across diverse forecasting tasks.

Abstract: Financial time series forecasting presents structural challenges absent from standard benchmarks. Log-returns are non-stationary, exhibit exceptionally low signal-to-noise (SNR) ratios, and are governed by regime-dependent temporal dependencies. We identify a key limitation of state-of-the-art (SOTA) time series models in financial settings. A fixed context window is mismatched to the time-varying optimal look-back of non-stationary price processes. We propose the Regime-Aware Variable-context Expert Network (RAVEN), a Mixture-of-Experts framework designed to adaptively determine the temporal context for each input sample. Instead of relying on a fixed look-back horizon, RAVEN constructs a hierarchy of nested contiguous windows whose lengths are determined by the data itself. Specifically, RAVEN scores patches by learned importance in reverse chronological order and applies the Cumulative Importance Thresholding (CIT) mechanism to derive nested prefix windows, each routed to a scale-specialized expert. A Global Compressed Representation (GCR) branch runs in parallel over the full context, preserving global temporal coherence that local experts cannot guarantee. Because the nested routing induces structured overlap among expert inputs, we introduce a Correlation-Aware Weighting (CAW) to align variable-length expert outputs and penalize pairwise cosine similarity prior to aggregation. Experiments on cumulative log-return prediction (HS300, S&P500) and fund sales forecasting demonstrate that RAVEN achieves SOTA performances, improves Pearson correlation by 9.2% on HS300 and 20.2% on S&P500, and reduces MSE by 18.2% on fund sales forecasting, while achieving the best results in 14 of 16 metrics on four PEMS traffic benchmarks.

</details>


### [146] [Blockwise Policy-Drift Gating for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24084)
*Liwen Zheng,Haiyun Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes blockwise policy-drift gating, a lightweight mechanism that controls for distributional drift between old (behavior) and current student policies when reusing rollouts in on-policy distillation. By aggregating log-probability shifts over fixed token blocks and using them as reweighting gates for position losses, the method improves math reasoning solve rates without modifying teacher targets, supports, or the rollout policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampled-token on-policy distillation (OPD) is known to be fragile on long-horizon reasoning tasks. While local teacher-support matching (LSM) helps, rollout reuse introduces a mismatch between the behavior policy that generated trajectories and the current student being trained. The authors identify that this old-current policy drift is a practical and controllable signal that existing methods do not explicitly address.

Method: Blockwise policy-drift gating computes log-probability differences between the behavior (old) student and the current student along sampled token paths. These token-level shifts are aggregated over fixed-size blocks (e.g., 64 tokens), detached from the computation graph, mean-normalized, and used as multiplicative gates to reweight OPD position losses. The method is student-only: it does not alter teacher targets, teacher top-K supports, or the rollout policy.

Result: On a six-variant Qwen3 math reasoning benchmark (AIME24, AIME25, MATH500, AMC23) with a 200-step training budget, fixed 64-token block gating improves sampled-token OPD mean pass@8 from 0.4978 to 0.5160. Under Teacher-TopK/LSM, Block64 achieves the best four-benchmark mean pass@8 among all trained student variants.

Conclusion: Local old-current policy drift serves as an effective control signal for reused OPD rollouts. Block-level gating is a simple, default-worthy technique for improving solve-rate robustness in long-horizon reasoning tasks.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student policy using teacher signals computed on trajectories sampled by the student itself. Recent work shows that sampled-token OPD can be fragile on long-horizon reasoning tasks and that local teacher-support matching is a simple and effective repair. This paper introduces blockwise policy-drift gating, a lightweight student-only old-current drift controller for OPD under rollout reuse. The method computes log-probability shifts between the behavior student and the current student on the sampled token path, aggregates these shifts over fixed blocks or spans, and uses the resulting detached, mean-normalized gates to reweight OPD position losses. It does not change teacher targets, teacher top-K supports, or the rollout policy. In a six-variant Qwen3 math reasoning benchmark with a uniform 200-step training budget for all trained variants, we use pass@8 as the primary problem-level solve-rate metric. Fixed 64-token block gating improves sampled-token OPD mean pass@8 from 0.4978 to 0.5160 across AIME24, AIME25, MATH500, and AMC23. On Teacher-TopK/LSM, Block64 gives the best four-benchmark mean pass@8 among trained students. The results identify local old-current policy drift as a practical control signal for reused OPD rollouts and motivate block-level gating as a simple default for improving solve-rate robustness.

</details>


### [147] [NeuroSonic: Conditional Flow Matching for EEG-to-Speech Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24087)
*Wenhao Gao,Yifan Wang,Yijia Ma,Carl Yang,Wen Li,Chenyu You*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NeuroSonic proposes a deterministic conditional flow-matching framework for EEG-to-speech reconstruction that learns a probability-flow velocity field transporting noise-corrupted acoustic states toward clean speech under EEG conditioning, avoiding iterative stochastic sampling. Evaluated on CineBrain and EAV benchmarks, it achieves up to 26.3% improvement in perceptual quality over GAN-, diffusion-, and mean-flow baselines, with gains most pronounced in artifact-heavy segments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG-to-speech reconstruction suffers from a fundamental mismatch: EEG signals are weak, spatially diffuse, and highly variable, while speech exhibits strong harmonic and temporal coherence. This mismatch makes direct waveform regression unstable and causes stochastic multi-step generation (e.g., diffusion models) to be sensitive to artifact-dependent conditioning and subject variability.

Method: NeuroSonic uses a conditional flow-matching framework that learns a deterministic probability-flow ODE. EEG and audio are embedded into a shared token space and processed by a time-conditioned gated Transformer that parameterizes the transport ordinary differential equation. Rather than directly predicting waveforms or performing iterative denoising, the model transports a noise-corrupted acoustic state toward clean speech along a deterministic velocity field conditioned on EEG.

Result: On CineBrain and EAV benchmarks under cross-subject evaluation, NeuroSonic outperforms representative GAN-, diffusion-, and mean-flow baselines in distributional realism, spectral fidelity, and perceptual quality, achieving up to a 26.3% gain in overall perceptual quality. The performance gap is largest in artifact-heavy segments where conditioning variability is strongest.

Conclusion: Deterministic conditional transport via flow matching provides a stable and effective formulation for EEG-driven speech reconstruction, particularly addressing the instability challenges posed by the inherent mismatch between noisy EEG signals and structured speech trajectories.

Abstract: Reconstructing continuous speech from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) remains fundamentally challenging. EEG provides a weak, spatially diffuse, and highly variable measurement of distributed cortical activity, whereas speech is organized as a coherent acoustic trajectory with strong harmonic and temporal structure. The resulting mismatch makes waveform regression unstable and causes stochastic multi-step generation to be sensitive to artifact-dependent conditioning and subject variability. We introduce NeuroSonic, a conditional flow-matching framework for EEG-to-speech reconstruction. Instead of predicting waveforms directly or refining them through stochastic denoising, NeuroSonic learns a deterministic probability-flow velocity field that transports a noise-corrupted acoustic state toward clean speech under EEG conditioning. EEG and audio are embedded into a shared token space and processed by a time-conditioned gated Transformer that parameterizes the transport ordinary differential equation. This formulation models trajectory evolution explicitly while avoiding iterative stochastic sampling. We evaluate NeuroSonic on the CineBrain and EAV benchmarks under cross-subject evaluation. Across both datasets, the proposed method improves distributional realism, spectral fidelity, and perceptual quality over representative GAN-, diffusion-, and mean-flow baselines, with up to a 26.3\% gain in overall perceptual quality. The performance gap is most evident in artifact-heavy segments, where conditioning variability is strongest. These findings indicate that deterministic conditional transport provides a stable and effective formulation for EEG-driven speech reconstruction. Code is available at https://github.com/Y-Research-SBU/NeuroSonic/ .

</details>


### [148] [FedUP: One-Shot Federated Unlearning via Centroid-Guided Plug-in Filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24113)
*Feihong Nan,Zhengyi Zhong,Pan Wang,Weidong Bao,Xiongtao Zhang,Quan Wen,Ji Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedUP is a one-shot federated unlearning framework that uses lightweight pluggable filters trained at the server with DP-protected class centroids, reducing unlearning latency from minutes to seconds while preserving non-target knowledge and enabling reversibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current federated unlearning methods suffer from a dilemma: they either lose non-target knowledge during unlearning or incur high request latency due to multi-round communication and retraining. There is a need for an efficient, precise, and reversible unlearning mechanism compliant with regulations like the "right to be forgotten."

Method: FedUP freezes the original model parameters and trains lightweight pluggable filters solely at the server side — no client communication needed. These filters function as a "knowledge funnel" that screens out target data. Training uses differentially private (DP)-protected class centroid samples aggregated from clients. The one-shot, pluggable design enables unlearning in seconds and allows seamless reversal by simply removing the filters.

Result: Extensive experiments on diverse image and text tasks show FedUP effectively reduces non-target knowledge loss and achieves superior unlearning precision and efficiency across various scenarios compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: FedUP resolves the core dilemma in federated unlearning by decoupling unlearning from the original model via pluggable filters, achieving fast (seconds), precise, DP-protected, and reversible unlearning suitable for real-world deployment. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Federated unlearning (FU) is critical for complying with legal mandates like the right to be forgotten in decentralized systems, yet current methods face a persistent dilemma between non-target knowledge loss and high request latency. To resolve these issues, we propose FedUP, a one-shot federated unlearning framework utilizing lightweight pluggable filters that act as a "knowledge funnel" to screen out target data while preserving original model performance. By freezing original model parameters and training filters at the server side using differentially private (DP)-protected class centroid samples, FedUP bypasses the need for multi-round client-server communication and complex retraining, reducing unlearning latency from minutes to mere seconds. Additionally, the framework's pluggable architecture ensures inherent reversibility, enabling the seamless restoration of forgotten knowledge by simply removing the filters. Extensive experiments on diverse image and text tasks demonstrate that FedUP effectively reduces non-target knowledge loss and achieves superior unlearning precision and efficiency across various scenarios. Code is available at: https://github.com/suows/FedUP-code.

</details>


### [149] [When Top-1 Fails: Calibrating LoRA Monitors for Masked Diffusion LMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24119)
*Lucky Verma,Pratik Yadav*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Top-1 argmax concentration fails as a collapse warning for DLM LoRA/PEFT fine-tuning (zero precision across 816 configurations). The authors identify pre-equilibrium saturation as the cause and propose max LoRA gradient norm as a superior early signal, achieving precision 0.68 and F1=0.79 on held-out LLaDA-family data for identifying poor final-loss configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete diffusion language models (DLMs) inherit inexpensive confidence-based diagnostics from denoising-time monitors, but these have never been validated in the PEFT (parameter-efficient fine-tuning) regime. The authors aim to test whether top-1 argmax concentration — a natural candidate from the denoising diffusion literature — serves as a reliable early-warning signal for training collapse during DLM LoRA fine-tuning.

Method: The study conducts a large-scale empirical test across 816 LoRA/PEFT configurations spanning three DLM families. Two signals are evaluated: (1) top-1 argmax concentration (a token-side confidence metric inherited from denoising-time diagnostics) and (2) max LoRA gradient norm (a parameter-side signal that samples gradient routing through the adapter). For the gradient-norm approach, a train-optimized threshold is calibrated on a pooled held-out split of the LLaDA family and evaluated via precision, recall, and F1 against a top-decile final-loss criterion. Autoregressive controls and cross-family threshold generalization tests are performed to bound the scope of the method.

Result: Top-1 argmax concentration fires a collapse warning for every single configuration yet actual collapse (at the 200-step horizon) occurs in 0/816 runs — yielding zero precision. The failure is attributed to pre-equilibrium saturation: top-1 concentration is already near-ceiling before optimization begins and rapidly becomes insensitive to final training outcomes. In contrast, max LoRA gradient norm, with a trained threshold, identifies top-decile worst-loss configurations with precision 0.68 and F1=0.79, outperforming the all-positive top-1 baseline even at the lower split-bootstrap confidence bound. Cross-family threshold failure and autoregressive controls indicate the method is specific to short-horizon DLM-LoRA inspection, not a universal collapse detector.

Conclusion: The authors recommend: (1) abandoning top-1 argmax concentration as a PEFT collapse alarm, (2) logging max LoRA gradient norm early in training, and (3) calibrating thresholds per DLM family before routing high-risk runs for manual inspection. The gradient-norm signal is effective but limited to the DLM-LoRA short-horizon setting.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion language model (DLM) fine-tuning inherits inexpensive diagnostics from denoising-time confidence monitors, but their PEFT-training meaning is untested. We test top-1 argmax concentration as a collapse warning. Across 816 LoRA/PEFT configurations from three DLM families, the warning fires for every configuration while logs record 0/816 actual collapses at the 200 step horizon, giving zero precision. The cause is pre-equilibrium saturation: top-1 concentration is already high before optimization and quickly becomes insensitive to final training stability. We then evaluate max LoRA gradient norm, a parameter-side signal that samples gradient routing rather than token concentration. On a pooled held-out LLaDA-family split, a train-optimized threshold identifies top-decile final-loss configurations with precision 0.68 and F1=0.79, above the all-positive top-1 baseline even at the lower split-bootstrap confidence bound. Autoregressive controls and cross-family threshold failures bound the result to short-horizon DLM-LoRA inspection rather than a universal collapse detector. Workflow: drop top-1 as a PEFT alarm, log max-gradient early in training, and calibrate thresholds per DLM family before routing runs for inspection.

</details>


### [150] [Holistic Data Scheduler for LLM Pre-training via Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24133)
*Chenhao Dang,Jing Ma,Mingjie Liao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HDS frames online data mixing for LLM pre-training as a reinforcement learning problem using Soft Actor-Critic, with a multi-objective reward integrating data quality, inter-domain loss dynamics, and weight norms. It achieves comparable perplexity 44% faster than the next best method and improves MMLU 0-shot by 7.2%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing online data mixing (ODM) methods rely on a single optimization perspective, failing to capture the multi-dimensional nature of dynamic data composition needed in large-scale LLM pre-training. This limits both training efficiency and downstream model capability.

Method: The authors propose the Holistic Data Scheduler (HDS), which casts data scheduling as a continuous-control RL problem solved via Soft Actor-Critic (SAC). The key innovation is a holistic reward function with three components: (1) a data-driven reward measuring quality, (2) a loss-driven reward capturing cross-domain influence, and (3) a model-driven reward based on weight norms. Systematic ablation experiments across model sizes determine the optimal configuration.

Result: On The Pile benchmark, HDS matches the final validation perplexity of the next best method using 44% fewer training iterations. It also yields a 7.2% improvement on MMLU 0-shot and consistent gains on other benchmarks.

Conclusion: HDS demonstrates that a holistic, multi-objective RL-based approach to online data mixing significantly improves both training efficiency and final model quality, outperforming single-perspective ODM methods.

Abstract: The composition of training data, governed by the diversity of sources and their mixing strategy, is a cornerstone of Large Language Model (LLM) pre-training. Online Data Mixing (ODM), the technique of adaptively adjusting data mixtures during training, has emerged as a promising direction to improve efficiency. However, existing methods are constrained by their reliance on a singular optimization perspective, which fundamentally overlooks the need for complex LLM pre-training to consider the dynamic data composition from multiple dimensions. To overcome this limitation, we introduce the Holistic Data Scheduler (HDS), a novel online data mixing framework. HDS formulates the data scheduling challenge as a reinforcement learning problem in a continuous control space and leverages the Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) algorithm for its stability and sample efficiency in exploring the high-dimensional policy space. At the core of HDS lies a novel multi-objective, holistic reward function that integrates three critical perspectives: a data-driven reward for quality, a loss-driven reward capturing inter-domain influence, and a model-driven reward based on weight norms. To validate our design and determine its optimal configuration, we conducted systematic experiments on LLMs of various sizes. On The Pile benchmark, HDS reaches the final validation perplexity of the next best method with 44% fewer training iterations. Furthermore, it achieves a 7.2% improvement on the MMLU 0-shot task along with consistent gains on other benchmarks, showcasing its ability to enhance both training efficiency and final model capability.

</details>


### [151] [A Time-Reparameterized Cumulative Intensity Extrapolation Sampler for Discrete Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24140)
*Feiyang Fu,Hehe Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes TR-CIE, a novel sampler for discrete flow matching that improves generation quality under limited function evaluations by combining time reparameterization with cumulative intensity extrapolation, achieving better results than standard τ-leaping at one NFE per step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing discrete flow matching (DFM) relies on τ-leaping discretization for sampling, but efficient sampling under limited NFE remains underexplored. Standard discretizations suffer from stiffness near the terminal stage and suboptimal approximation of cumulative intensities on non-uniform grids.

Method: TR-CIE comprises: (1) a schedule-based time reparameterization that rescales the time grid via a change of variables to absorb schedule-dependent growth and mitigate terminal-stage stiffness; (2) a cumulative-intensity extrapolation updating rule that reuses cached model outputs from the prior step to improve stepwise cumulative intensity approximation on the resulting non-uniform grid. The sampler uses one NFE per step, matching standard τ-leaping cost.

Result: Experiments on synthetic tasks, text generation, and text-to-image benchmarks show improved sampling quality under limited NFE compared to standard τ-leaping and other baselines.

Conclusion: TR-CIER provides an efficient, theoretically grounded sampler for DFM that enhances sample quality under restricted function evaluations without additional model cost, validated across diverse generation tasks.

Abstract: Discrete flow matching (DFM) provides a principled framework for generative modeling on discrete state spaces via continuous-time Markov chain dynamics. In practice, sampling for DFM commonly employs discretizations such as $τ$-leaping, yet efficient sampling methods under a limited number of function evaluations (NFE) remain less studied. To address this gap, we propose the Time-Reparameterized Cumulative Intensity Extrapolation (TR-CIE) sampler, which aims to improve sampling quality when function evaluations are restricted. TR-CIE consists of two components. First, a schedule-based time reparameterization rescales the time grid according to the noise schedule. Under standard factorized DFM rate parameterizations, this transformation of variables absorbs the schedule-dependent growth term and mitigates stiffness near the terminal sampling stage. Second, we introduce a cumulative-intensity extrapolation updating rule. By reusing cached model outputs from the previous step as a history term, this improves the approximation of stepwise cumulative intensities on the resulting non-uniform time grid. We provide a theoretical analysis that bounds the local approximation error of cumulative intensities and establishes convergence results. The resulting sampler requires one NFE per step and introduces no additional model evaluations compared to the standard $τ$-leaping sampler. Extensive experiments on synthetic tasks, text generation, and text-to-image benchmarks demonstrate that our method improves sampling quality under limited NFE.

</details>


### [152] [AsyncOPD: How Stale Can On-Policy Distillation Be?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24143)
*Wonjun Kang,Kevin Galim,Seunghyuk Oh,Minjun Kang,Sanghyun Park,Donghoon Kim,Minjae Lee,Minseo Kim,Rishabh Tiwari,Yuchen Zeng,Hyung Il Koo,Kangwook Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First systematic study of staleness in asynchronous on-policy distillation (OPD) for LLMs, showing KL direction matters (forward KL is robust, reverse KL is vulnerable), proposing multi-sample Monte Carlo estimators to handle finite teacher-score caches, and delivering AsyncOPD — an open-source pipeline achieving 1.6×–3.8× throughput gains with comparable accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation faces an on-policy systems bottleneck where rollout generation dominates training time for reasoning workloads. Asynchronous training can alleviate this by decoupling rollout generation from learner updates, but introduces stale-policy data. While stale data effects have been studied in asynchronous RL, they remain underexplored in OPD, especially in practical settings with finite teacher-score caches necessitated by storage/transfer costs of full-vocabulary teacher logits.

Method: The authors systematically study staleness in asynchronous OPD by: (1) analyzing how KL direction affects robustness to stale rollouts (teacher-weighted forward KL vs. student-weighted reverse KL); (2) testing whether asynchronous RL stabilization methods (e.g., importance sampling corrections) mitigate OPD staleness; (3) analyzing the bias-variance tradeoff from finite teacher-score caches for sparse/sampled reverse-KL estimators and proposing multi-sample Monte Carlo (MC) to preserve correctability while reducing one-sample variance; (4) building and open-sourcing AsyncOPD, an end-to-end asynchronous OPD pipeline incorporating these estimator choices.

Result: Key findings: (1) Forward KL is robust to stale rollouts; reverse KL is vulnerable. (2) RL stabilization methods do not improve over simply recomputing the reverse-KL signal under the current student at learner time. (3) Multi-sample MC estimators effectively navigate the bias-variance tradeoff from finite teacher-score caches. (4) AsyncOPD achieves 1.6× to 3.8× training throughput improvement over strict synchronous training while maintaining comparable accuracy.

Conclusion: KL direction fundamentally shapes the stale-data problem in asynchronous OPD. For the vulnerable reverse-KL case, OPD-specific solutions (recomputing KL at learner time, multi-sample MC) outperform techniques borrowed from asynchronous RL. AsyncOPD demonstrates that asynchronous OPD is practically viable, offering substantial throughput gains without accuracy degradation.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own rollouts guided by teacher feedback and is becoming increasingly important for large language model (LLM) post-training. Like reinforcement learning (RL), however, OPD faces an on-policy systems bottleneck, as rollouts can dominate training time for reasoning workloads. Asynchronous training pipelines can alleviate this bottleneck by decoupling rollout generation from learner updates, but doing so introduces stale-policy data. While prior work has studied stale data in asynchronous RL, its effects in OPD remain underexplored. We present the first systematic study of staleness in asynchronous OPD, focusing on a practical setting where teacher feedback is implemented through local KL losses and full-vocabulary teacher logits are too expensive to store or transfer, necessitating finite teacher-score caches. We first show that KL direction changes the stale-data problem: teacher-weighted forward KL is more robust to stale rollouts, whereas student-weighted reverse KL is vulnerable. Second, for this vulnerable reverse-KL case, we study whether methods designed to stabilize asynchronous RL can mitigate OPD staleness. In our experiments, they do not improve over a simpler OPD-specific surrogate: recomputing the reverse-KL signal under the current student at learner time. Third, we analyze how finite teacher-score caches create a bias-variance tradeoff for sparse and sampled reverse-KL OPD estimators. This motivates multi-sample Monte Carlo (MC), which preserves MC correctability while reducing one-sample variance. Finally, we present and open-source AsyncOPD, a fully asynchronous OPD training pipeline built from these estimator choices. Experiments show that AsyncOPD improves training throughput by $1.6\times$ to $3.8\times$ over strict synchronous training while reaching comparable accuracy.

</details>


### [153] [Lightweight Transformer Models for On-Device Fault Detection: A Benchmark Study on Resource-Constrained Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24173)
*Disha Patel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks traditional ML methods against lightweight transformer architectures for on-device binary fault detection across three public datasets. It finds that lightweight transformers can match traditional ML on well-separated data but at massive cost in size/latency; quantization and an adaptive two-stage pipeline mitigate these costs. On severely imbalanced datasets, all methods struggle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device fault detection enables real-time diagnostics without cloud dependency, but deploying ML on resource-constrained hardware requires balancing accuracy, latency, and model size. The authors seek to determine whether lightweight transformer architectures can viably replace traditional ML methods in this regime.

Method: Benchmark of traditional ML (Random Forest, XGBoost, SVM, Logistic Regression) vs. lightweight transformers (DistilBERT, TinyBERT-6L, TinyBERT-4L, MobileBERT) on three public datasets: NASA C-MAPSS, SECOM, and UCI AI4I 2020. Metrics: F1-score, AUC, model size, CPU inference latency. Also evaluates INT8 dynamic quantization and a two-stage adaptive inference pipeline (quantized triage model routing 97.9% of predictions, large expert handling 2.1%).

Result: On C-MAPSS, lightweight transformers match traditional ML at 87.8% F1 but with ~100x model size and ~9000x latency. TinyBERT-4L is the most deployment-friendly transformer (55 MB, 18 ms CPU). INT8 quantization reduces size by 25% while preserving 86.9% F1. The adaptive pipeline achieves 87.6% F1 at 19.5 ms average latency. On severely imbalanced datasets (SECOM, UCI-PM), all methods struggle significantly.

Conclusion: Lightweight transformers can approach traditional ML accuracy on clean, well-separated fault detection data but remain impractical for pure on-device deployment due to size/latency overhead. Quantization and adaptive routing help narrow the gap, but extreme class imbalance remains a fundamental challenge for all current approaches.

Abstract: On-device fault detection enables real-time diagnostics without cloud dependency, but deploying machine learning models on resource-constrained hardware demands careful tradeoffs between accuracy, latency, and model size. We present a benchmark comparing traditional ML methods (Random Forest, XGBoost, SVM, Logistic Regression) against lightweight transformer architectures (DistilBERT, TinyBERT-6L, TinyBERT-4L, MobileBERT) for binary fault detection across three public datasets: NASA C-MAPSS turbofan degradation, SECOM semiconductor manufacturing, and UCI AI4I 2020 predictive maintenance. We evaluate classification performance (F1-score, AUC), model size, and CPU inference latency, and further assess INT8 dynamic quantization and a two-stage adaptive inference pipeline. Our results reveal that on well-separated sensor data (C-MAPSS), lightweight transformers match traditional ML at 87.8% F1 but at 100x the model size and 9000x the latency. TinyBERT-4L emerges as the most deployment-friendly transformer at 55 MB and 18 ms CPU latency. INT8 quantization reduces size by 25% while preserving 86.9% F1. Our adaptive pipeline, routing 97.9% of predictions through a quantized triage model and only 2.1% to a larger expert, achieves 87.6% F1 at 19.5 ms average latency. On severely imbalanced datasets (SECOM, UCI-PM), both traditional and transformer methods struggle significantly, highlighting fundamental limitations of current approaches for extreme class imbalance in fault detection. All code is publicly available.

</details>


### [154] [Project Ariadne: Prompt-Conditioned Route Generation for Synthesis Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24184)
*Anton Morgunov,Victor S. Batista*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Ariadne is a decoder-only transformer that generates full retrosynthetic routes from a single prompt containing the target molecule, optional constraints (depth, required starting materials), and the route itself. It eliminates the need for separate models for different planning specifications and achieves strong performance with dramatically faster training/inference than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrosynthetic planners either (1) use classical iterative search with single-step models requiring specialized algorithms for constrained variants, or (2) use direct route generation but train separate models for each planning specification. The authors aim to unify these under a single prompt-conditioned model that flexibly handles constraints without architectural changes.

Method: Ariadne is a decoder-only sequence model that represents the entire planning problem—target SMILES, optional constraint fields (route depth, required starting materials), and the retrosynthetic route—as a single prompt-completion sequence. A single 24-layer checkpoint is trained to generate routes conditioned on whatever constraints appear in the prompt, enabling flexible zero-shot compliance with depth and leaf-material specifications without separate models or search algorithms.

Result: On RetroCast/PaRoutes mkt-cnv-160, adding depth prompts raises Solv-0 by 13.7 points; adding required-leaf prompts raises it by 31.2 points. Ariadne surpasses the DESP bidirectional search planner on required-leaf Top-10 and Solv-0 using only 24 GPU-minutes versus 6.8 GPU-hours. On standard reconstruction, it matches DMS Explorer XL at roughly half the inference time. On route-holdout benchmarks, Ariadne shows its clearest gains, though AiZynthFinder MCTS remains stronger on several Solv-0 comparisons.

Conclusion: Prompt-conditioned sequence generation is a viable and flexible paradigm for retrosynthetic planning, extending direct generation beyond specialist single-purpose models. However, the lack of Tier-1–3 route checkers remains the critical bottleneck before such models become practically useful to experimental chemists.

Abstract: Retrosynthetic planning seeks to connect a target molecule to commercially available starting materials through a multistep route. Classical planners construct such routes by iteratively applying single-step reaction models within a search procedure; constrained variants often require specialized algorithms or architectural changes. Direct route generation reframes retrosynthesis as sequence generation, but existing direct-generation methods still train separate models for different planning specifications. We introduce Ariadne, a decoder-only route generator that represents the target, optional constraints, and route in one prompt-completion sequence. On the RetroCast/PaRoutes mkt-cnv-160 benchmark family, one 24-layer checkpoint follows route-depth and required-starting-material prompts: adding the corresponding prompt fields raises Solv-0 by 13.7 points for depth constraints and 31.2 points for required-leaf constraints. Ariadne also improves over DESP, a bidirectional search planner, on required-leaf Top-10 and Solv-0 in 24 GPU-minutes versus 6.8 GPU-hours. On standard reconstruction, Ariadne is comparable to DMS Explorer XL at about half the reported inference time. Across additional target-only benchmarks, Ariadne's clearest gains are on route-holdout reconstruction, whereas AiZynthFinder MCTS remains stronger on several Solv-0 comparisons. These results extend sequence generation from specialist retrosynthesis models to prompt-conditioned structural route generation. We release the codebase and training scripts to support further work, but do not introduce Tier-1--3 route checkers; those remain the main bottleneck before models of this kind can become useful to experimental chemists.

</details>


### [155] [Managing Task Execution for Unknown Workloads in Batteryless IoT: A Hardware-Agnostic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24340)
*Samer Nasser,Henrique Duarte Moura,Ritesh Kumar Singh,Maarten Weyn,Jeroen Famaey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes two hardware-agnostic dynamic scheduling strategies for batteryless IoT devices — a model-free Reinforcement Learning (RL) agent and an on-the-fly Approximated Prediction (AP) method — and compares them against an adaptive task rate approach (AsTAR) and optimized static thresholds. Evaluated using a simulation framework with real solar data and LoRa workloads, the analysis reveals distinct trade-offs: AP achieves near-oracle throughput, RL enables tunable survival-execution balance, and AsTAR excels at execution pacing across long energy gaps. Simpler static policies remain effective for devices with larger energy buffers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Batteryless, energy-harvesting IoT systems face highly volatile stored energy. As edge applications grow more complex, traditional energy-aware schedulers fail because they depend on static execution thresholds or pre-measured, hardware-specific task profiles that cannot handle unpredictable workloads. There is a need for dynamic, hardware-agnostic scheduling strategies that treat applications as black boxes and require no prior energy information.

Method: The authors propose two novel dynamic scheduling strategies: (1) a model-free Reinforcement Learning (RL) agent, and (2) an on-the-fly Approximated Prediction (AP) method. Both treat the application as a black box requiring no prior energy profiling. These are benchmarked against an adaptive task rate approach (AsTAR) and optimized static thresholds. Evaluation uses a custom-built, physically accurate simulation framework driven by real-world solar data and dynamic LoRa transmission profiles.

Result: The AP approach delivers lightweight, near-oracle task throughput. The RL agent provides a tunable balance between device survival and task execution. AsTAR excels at pacing execution across long energy gaps. For severely constrained systems with small capacitors, these advanced strategies offer critical resilience. However, devices with larger energy buffers can efficiently rely on simpler, less computationally expensive static policies without significant performance loss.

Conclusion: No single scheduling strategy dominates universally. The choice depends on hardware constraints and application requirements: AP for lightweight near-optimal throughput, RL for tunable survival-execution trade-offs, AsTAR for handling extended energy gaps, and static thresholds for well-buffered systems. The findings guide scheduler selection across the batteryless IoT design space.

Abstract: In recent years, the Internet of Things (IoT) paradigm has been shifting toward batteryless, energy-harvesting architectures. Sustaining reliable operation in these systems requires intelligent management of highly volatile stored energy. As edge applications grow in complexity, traditional energy-aware schedulers struggle with unpredictable workloads due to their reliance on static execution thresholds or pre-measured, hardware-specific task profiles. To overcome this, we propose two novel, hardware-agnostic dynamic scheduling strategies treating applications as a "black box," requiring no prior energy information: a model-free Reinforcement Learning (RL) agent and an on-the-fly Approximated Prediction (AP) method. We evaluate these methods against an adaptive task rate approach (AsTAR) and optimized static thresholds using a custom-built, physically accurate simulation framework driven by real-world solar data and dynamic LoRa transmission profiles. Rather than claiming universal superiority, our analysis exposes the distinct operational trade-offs of each method: the AP approach delivers lightweight, near-oracle task throughput; the RL agent provides tunable survival-execution balancing; and AsTAR excels at execution pacing across long energy gaps. Finally, we demonstrate that while these advanced strategies provide critical resilience for severely constrained systems with small capacitors, devices with larger energy buffers can efficiently rely on simpler, less computationally expensive static policies.

</details>


### [156] [Parallel Manifold Steering: Efficient Adaptation of Large Associative Memories via Residual Energy Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24396)
*Kanishk Awadhiya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: H-Res proposes hierarchical residual steering to adapt frozen Transformers by learning state-dependent vector fields on the activation manifold, preserving attention entropy and facilitating neural collapse while outperforming LoRA and VPT on associative retrieval tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting frozen Transformer DAMs faces a plasticity-stability dilemma: weight modification (e.g., LoRA) risks catastrophic interference, while prompt-based methods (e.g., VPT) clog the retrieval buffer with static tokens, degrading associative capacity.

Method: H-Res formulates adaptation as a control problem on the activation manifold using Neural ODEs. It learns a hierarchical, state-dependent vector field that steers token trajectories into task-specific basins of attraction without altering global equilibrium or expanding sequence length.

Result: Manifold Steering outperforms global weight modification by 26% on associative retrieval tasks and eliminates the computational overhead of prompt-based methods, scaling effectively to structured domains (VTAB).

Conclusion: H-Res resolves the plasticity-stability dilemma by modulating the effective energy landscape externally, preserving the foundation model's associative memory properties while enabling efficient task adaptation.

Abstract: Large Transformer models function as Dense Associative Memories (DAMs), retrieving knowledge via high-dimensional attractor dynamics driven by the self-attention mechanism \citep{ramsauer2020hopfield, wu2024attention}. However, adapting these frozen memory systems to new tasks presents a fundamental ``Plasticity-Stability'' dilemma. Current methods either risk catastrophic interference by modifying synaptic weights directly (e.g., LoRA) \citep{hu2021lora} or degrade associative capacity by clogging the retrieval buffer with static prompt tokens (e.g., VPT) \citep{jia2022vpt}. In this work, we propose \textbf{H-Res} (Hierarchical Residual Steering), a mechanism that modulates the effective energy landscape of the Transformer without altering its global equilibrium or expanding its sequence length. By formulating adaptation as a control problem on the activation manifold \citep{chen2018neuralode}, H-Res learns a state-dependent vector field that steers token trajectories into task-specific basins of attraction. We formally prove that H-Res preserves the attention entropy of the foundation model and facilitates Neural Collapse \citep{papyan2020prevalence}. Empirically, Manifold Steering outperforms global weight modification by 26\% on associative retrieval tasks and eliminates the computational overhead of prompt-based methods, scaling effectively to structured domains \citep{zha2023vtab}.

</details>


### [157] [Natural Identifiers for Privacy and Data Audits in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24408)
*Lorenzo Rossi,Bartłomiej Marek,Franziska Boenisch,Adam Dziedzic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Natural Identifiers (NIDs) — structured random strings like cryptographic hashes and shortened URLs that naturally occur in LLM training data — enabling post-hoc differential privacy auditing without retraining and dataset inference without requiring a private held-out non-member dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for auditing LLM privacy are impractical post-hoc: differential privacy auditing requires inserting canary data during training (costly retraining), and dataset inference requires a private IID held-out non-member dataset that is often unavailable. There is a need for scalable, post-hoc audit techniques that work on already-trained models.

Method: The authors propose Natural Identifiers (NIDs), structured random strings (e.g., cryptographic hashes, shortened URLs) that naturally appear in common LLM training corpora. Because NIDs follow predictable formats, unlimited additional same-distribution random strings can be synthetically generated. These synthetic NIDs serve dual purposes: (1) as alternative canaries for post-hoc differential privacy auditing, and (2) as same-distribution held-out data for dataset inference, eliminating the need for a private non-member dataset.

Result: Evaluation demonstrates that NIDs successfully enable post-hoc differential privacy auditing without any model retraining and allow dataset inference for any suspect dataset containing NIDs, without requiring access to a private non-member held-out dataset.

Conclusion: NIDs offer a practical, scalable solution for post-hoc privacy auditing of LLMs, overcoming key limitations of prior approaches by leveraging naturally occurring structured random strings already present in training data.

Abstract: Assessing the privacy of large language models (LLMs) presents significant challenges.
