In particular, most existing methods for auditing differential privacy require the insertion of specially crafted canary data during training, making them impractical for auditing already-trained models without costly retraining. Additionally, dataset inference, which audits whether a suspect dataset was used to train a model, is infeasible without access to a private non-member held-out dataset. Yet, such held-out datasets are often unavailable or difficult to construct for real-world cases since they have to be from the same distribution (IID) as the suspect data. These limitations severely hinder the ability to conduct scalable, post-hoc audits. To enable such audits, this work introduces natural identifiers (NIDs) as a novel solution to the above-mentioned challenges. NIDs are structured random strings, such as cryptographic hashes and shortened URLs, naturally occurring in common LLM training datasets. Their format enables the generation of unlimited additional random strings from the same distribution, which can act as alternative canaries for audits and as same-distribution held-out data for dataset inference. Our evaluation highlights that indeed, using NIDs, we can facilitate post-hoc differential privacy auditing without any retraining and enable dataset inference for any suspect dataset containing NIDs without the need for a private non-member held-out dataset.

</details>


### [158] [Data Augmentation: A Fourier Analysis Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24418)
*Behrooz Tahmasebi,Melanie Weber,Stefanie Jegelka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper establishes that partial data augmentation using randomly sampled group elements achieves the same minimax optimal rates as full augmentation for a broad class of learning problems, with vanishing approximation error as subset size grows. It also proves that exact invariance enforcement strictly requires averaging over the full group when the hypothesis space is sufficiently expressive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full data augmentation over large symmetry groups is computationally infeasible. The authors ask whether partial augmentation can retain the same statistical generalization and sample complexity benefits, addressing the tension between computational scalability and statistical optimality in equivariant learning.

Method: The authors develop a framework using Fourier analysis and representation theory of finite groups. They analyze partial augmentation based on randomly sampled group subsets and derive minimax rates, bounding the gap between partial and full augmentation. A complementary impossibility result is proved via expressiveness arguments on hypothesis spaces.

Result: Partial augmentation with randomly sampled subsets achieves minimax rates matching full augmentation, with error decaying as subset size increases. Conversely, exact invariance cannot be achieved by any strict subset of the group when the hypothesis class is sufficiently expressive — full group averaging is necessary.

Conclusion: The results unify full and partial data augmentation under a single theoretical lens, showing that approximate symmetry enforcement via partial augmentation is statistically sufficient while exact enforcement demands full group averaging. This resolves a key question about scalable symmetry-preserving learning.

Abstract: Data augmentation is a simple and model-agnostic approach for exploiting known invariances in learning problems. Given a group acting on the input space, one augments the training set with transformed copies of each sample. Because it exploits symmetries without modifying the underlying learning algorithm, data augmentation can be applied broadly across learning methods. However, this universality comes at a computational cost: when the group is large, full group-sized augmentation quickly becomes computationally infeasible. This raises a fundamental question: Can partial data augmentation achieve the same statistical benefits as full augmentation in terms of generalization and sample complexity? We develop a general framework for investigating this question using Fourier analysis and the representation theory of finite groups. We show that, for a broad class of classical learning problems, partial data augmentation based on a randomly sampled subset of group elements achieves the same minimax rates as full augmentation, up to an approximation error that vanishes as the subset size increases. Our results provide a theoretical explanation for why partial augmentation can retain the statistical benefits of full augmentation despite enforcing symmetry only approximately, and shed light on a recently raised question in learning with symmetries: whether statistically optimal learning under general group invariances can be achieved using computationally scalable methods. Moreover, we prove a complementary impossibility result: enforcing exact invariance via data augmentation requires averaging over the entire group, and cannot be achieved by any strict subset when the hypothesis space is sufficiently expressive. Together, these results provide a unified perspective on full and partial data augmentation, as well as exact and approximate symmetry enforcement.

</details>


### [159] [An LLM-based Two-Stage Transformer Framework for Cross-Domain Bearing Fault Diagnosis with Limited Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24459)
*Jinghan Wang,Feng Cheng,Wentao Wu,Hang Li,Gaoliang Peng,Tianchen Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage transfer learning framework using a lightweight GPT-2-style Transformer with explicit knowledge pathways (pre-trained encoder weights + fault prototype embeddings) achieves 92.61% average accuracy with only 10% labeled target data, outperforming SOTA by 17.24 pp on bearing fault diagnosis under concurrent heterogeneity, varying conditions, and label scarcity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing bearing fault diagnosis methods address dataset heterogeneity, varying operating conditions, and limited labeled data as isolated problems using implicit feature alignment, which is insufficient when all three challenges co-occur in real industrial settings. A unified framework with explicit knowledge transfer mechanisms is needed.

Method: A knowledge-guided two-stage transfer learning framework: (1) a lightweight GPT-2-style Transformer with causal self-attention for hierarchical vibration feature extraction; (2) explicit knowledge pathways where pre-trained encoder weights and fault prototype embeddings serve as carriers from multi-source pre-training to target adaptation; (3) multi-source learning for generalizable representations; (4) prototype-based knowledge modulation for target adaptation; (5) taxonomy-adaptive classification for seamless transfer across heterogeneous fault categories.

Result: 92.61% average accuracy using only 10% labeled target data across four real-world datasets, outperforming state-of-the-art methods by 17.24 percentage points.

Conclusion: The framework provides a practical and cost-effective solution for predictive maintenance in Industry 4.0 by successfully handling the simultaneous occurrence of dataset heterogeneity, operating condition variations, and limited labeled data through explicit knowledge-guided transfer learning.

Abstract: Bearing fault diagnosis faces critical challenges when dataset heterogeneity, operating condition variations, and limited labeled data occur simultaneously in industrial environments. Existing approaches address these issues in isolation and rely on implicit feature alignment, limiting effectiveness under concurrent challenges. This paper proposes a knowledge-guided two-stage transfer learning framework that employs a lightweight GPT-2-style Transformer with causal self-attention for hierarchical feature extraction from vibration signals, establishing explicit pathways where pre-trained encoder weights and fault prototype embeddings serve as knowledge carriers from multi-source pre-training to target adaptation. The framework addresses the dual-shift challenge through multi-source learning for generalizable representations, prototype-based knowledge modulation for target adaptation, and taxonomy-adaptive classification for seamless transfer across heterogeneous fault categories. Experimental validation on four real-world datasets demonstrates 92.61% average accuracy with only 10% labeled target data, outperforming state-of-the-art methods by 17.24 percentage points, establishing a practical pathway toward cost-effective predictive maintenance in Industry 4.0 applications.

</details>


### [160] [A Fair Evaluation of Graph Foundation Models for Node Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24509)
*Oleg Platonov,Gleb Bazhenov,Dmitry Eremeev,Liudmila Prokhorenkova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A rigorous reevaluation of 9 recent Graph Foundation Models for node property prediction reveals that only the most recent ones based on Prior-data Fitted Networks outperform well-tuned GNNs, and at a higher inference cost. The field lacks unified evaluation standards, making fair comparisons difficult.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid development of Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) for node property prediction has been accompanied by inconsistent and widely varying evaluation protocols across different works. This lack of a unified evaluation setting prevents reliable comparison between GFMs and against strong GNN baselines, creating an urgent need for a fair, standardized benchmark.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic and controlled reevaluation of 9 recent GFMs on node property prediction tasks. They establish strong GNN baselines with careful hyperparameter tuning and compare GFMs against these baselines under a unified evaluation protocol, measuring both predictive performance and inference cost.

Result: Among the 9 evaluated GFMs, only the most recent models leveraging the Prior-data Fitted Networks (PFN) paradigm consistently outperform well-tuned GNN baselines in predictive performance. However, this performance advantage comes at the cost of higher inference overhead.

Conclusion: The GFM field for node property prediction has yet to deliver broadly superior models; most GFMs do not surpass well-tuned GNNs. The PFN-based approach is a promising direction, but efficiency remains a concern. The work underscores the critical need for standardized evaluation practices in the community.

Abstract: Due to the wide use of graph-structured data in different fields of industry and science, the development of Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) has recently attracted a lot of attention. While many different types of models are called GFMs, particular interest has been paid to GFMs designed for node property prediction tasks, which is one of the most popular settings in Graph ML with lots of real-world applications from fraud detection in financial and social networks to recommendation systems for e-commerce and user-generated content platforms. While a number of GFMs for this task have been recently proposed, the field has not converged to a unified evaluation setting, and different works evaluate their models in widely different ways, preventing reliable comparison of GFMs with each other and with other types of models. In this work, we conduct a fair and rigorous reevaluation of 9 recent GFMs for node property prediction, comparing them to strong Graph Neural Network (GNN) baselines. We find that, among these GFMs, only the most recent ones based on the Prior-data Fitted Networks paradigm outperform well-tuned GNNs in predictive performance, although at a higher inference cost.

</details>


### [161] [Reasoning as Attractor Dynamics: Latent Memory Retrieval via Gibbs-Weighted Energy Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24543)
*Kanishk Awadhiya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs are reframed as Dense Associative Memories where correct reasoning corresponds to flat attractor basins. A physics-inspired Gibbs sampling mechanism using spectral entropy-based inverse energy weighting improves Phi-3.5 on GSM8K by 5.38%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional autoregressive (greedy next-token) generation fails to capture the attractor dynamics inherent in LLMs. The authors argue that hallucinations arise from sharp, unstable local minima in the energy landscape, while correct reasoning occupies flat, wide basins. This motivates an inference strategy that explicitly exploits this geometric structure.

Method: The paper proposes modeling LLM inference as a retrieval process over the energy landscape of an associative memory. Multiple reasoning trajectories are sampled, and each is scored using spectral entropy to compute an energy E. Trajectories are then weighted via a Gibbs measure (P ∝ e^{−βE}), approximating the equilibrium distribution and effectively "relaxing" the system into robust attractor basins rather than relying on greedy decoding.

Result: Applying this method to Microsoft Phi-3.5 on the GSM8K mathematical reasoning benchmark yields a 5.38 percentage point improvement, raising accuracy from 84.7% to 90.1%.

Conclusion: Inference in LLMs is better understood as a dynamic settling process into attractor basins within a high-dimensional energy landscape, rather than as sequential next-token prediction. The proposed energy-based retrieval mechanism validates this perspective empirically.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are traditionally viewed as autoregressive generators. However, from the perspective of collective computation, they function as high-dimensional Dense Associative Memories that store complex reasoning patterns as latent attractors. In this work, we investigate the energy landscape of mathematical reasoning. We posit that correct reasoning chains correspond to deep, wide attractor basins ("flat minima") in the model's output distribution, whereas hallucinations manifest as sharp, unstable local minima. To exploit this geometry, we introduce a retrieval mechanism based on a Gibbs measure of the trajectory's spectral entropy. By sampling multiple reasoning paths and weighting them by their inverse energy ($P \propto e^{-βE}$), we approximate the equilibrium distribution of the associative memory, effectively ``relaxing'' the system into a robust solution. Empirically, this physics-inspired mechanism improves Microsoft Phi-3.5 performance on GSM8K by 5.38\% (84.7\% $\to$ 90.1\%), demonstrating that inference is better modeled as a dynamic settling process into an attractor basin rather than greedy next-token prediction.

</details>


### [162] [QC-SMOTE: Quality-Controlled SMOTE for Imbalanced Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24625)
*Parth Upman,Shreyank N Gowda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QC-SMOTE is a quality-controlled oversampling method that generates synthetic minority samples guided by a composite trustworthiness score, adapts interpolation to local data geometry, and falls back to duplication when quality is poor, achieving state-of-the-art AUC-ROC and Macro F1 on 30 imbalanced datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing oversampling methods like SMOTE frequently produce low-quality synthetic samples in problematic regions characterized by noise or class overlap, degrading classification performance rather than improving it.

Method: QC-SMOTE estimates minority sample reliability via a composite neighbourhood trustworthiness score that integrates local density, safe-level, and isolation from the majority class. Synthetic candidates are generated through an IPQ-guided best-of-K strategy evaluating midpoint purity and majority clearance. Sample allocation is driven by reliability and boundary informativeness. The framework adapts interpolation range and selection criteria across different overlap–imbalance regimes. When neighbourhood purity drops below an adaptive threshold, low-quality synthetic samples are replaced with original minority duplicates for graceful degradation.

Result: On 30 imbalanced datasets using repeated stratified cross-validation, QC-SMOTE achieved the highest average AUC-ROC and Macro F1 among all compared oversampling methods, with particularly pronounced improvements under moderate and severe imbalance conditions.

Conclusion: Quality-aware, geometry-adaptive synthetic sampling is critical for robust imbalanced classification. QC-SMOTE demonstrates that incorporating sample reliability estimation, adaptive generation behaviour, and a duplication fallback mechanism yields consistent and significant performance gains.

Abstract: Class imbalance poses a significant challenge in classification, where existing methods such as SMOTE often generate low-quality synthetic samples in regions with noise or class overlap. We propose QC-SMOTE, a quality-controlled oversampling framework that estimates minority sample reliability using a composite neighbourhood trustworthiness score combining local density, safe-level, and isolation from the majority class. Synthetic candidates are generated using an IPQ-guided best-of-K strategy that evaluates midpoint purity and, when required, majority clearance, with allocation guided by sample reliability and boundary informativeness. Generation behaviour adapts across overlap--imbalance regimes, adjusting interpolation range and selection criteria to match local data geometry. Low-quality synthetic samples are replaced with original minority duplicates when neighbourhood purity falls below an adaptive threshold, providing graceful degradation by reverting to duplication in severely noisy regions. Experiments on 30 imbalanced datasets using repeated stratified cross-validation show that QC-SMOTE achieves the strongest average AUC-ROC and Macro F1 among the compared oversampling methods, with particularly clear gains under moderate and severe imbalance. These results demonstrate the importance of quality-aware, geometry-adaptive synthetic sampling for robust imbalanced classification.

</details>


### [163] [FlowPipe: LLM-Enhanced Conditional Generative Flow Networks for Data Preparation Pipeline Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24679)
*Kunyu Ni,Lei Cao,Jie He,Xiaotong Zhang,Jianfeng Jin,Junyu Dong,Yanwei Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowPipe reformulates automatic data pipeline synthesis as conditional probabilistic flow generation over a DAG using C-GFlowNets with Trajectory Balance, incorporating FiLM-based semantic modulation and failure awareness, achieving 11.96% average accuracy improvement and 12.5× faster convergence over SOTA Multi-DQN methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SOTA Multi-DQN methods for automatic data preparation pipeline construction suffer from three key limitations: decoupled value estimators weaken long-horizon credit assignment between early operator choices and final validation rewards; dataset context is only weakly injected into the policy; and exploration is inefficient in a sparse search space riddled with invalid states. These shortcomings motivate a unified generative framework that directly connects terminal rewards to sequential decisions.

Method: FlowPipe formulates pipeline synthesis as conditional probabilistic flow generation over a directed acyclic graph (DAG) using Conditional Generative Flow Networks (C-GFlowNets) with a Trajectory Balance objective, which directly links terminal validation rewards to early pipeline decisions. It introduces Deep Semantic Modulation via Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM), enabling LLM-derived logical priors to condition the policy's internal activations based on dataset semantics. Additionally, a failure-awareness mechanism is integrated into the flow objective to penalize invalid states and steer exploration toward high-potential regions.

Result: Experiments on two benchmark suites encompassing 74 real-world datasets demonstrate that FlowPipe outperforms SOTA baselines, improving downstream model accuracy by 11.96% on average and achieving 12.5× faster training convergence.

Conclusion: FlowPipe establishes that C-GFlowNets with Trajectory Balance, semantic conditioning via FiLM, and failure-aware objectives collectively overcome the credit assignment, context integration, and exploration inefficiency limitations of prior Multi-DQN approaches, yielding both superior accuracy and dramatically faster convergence for automatic data preparation pipeline synthesis.

Abstract: Data preparation pipelines improve data quality in machine learning by transforming raw tables into learning-ready data through sequential cleaning and feature transformation operators. However, automatically constructing such pipelines is computationally difficult because operator sequences are combinatorial and end-to-end evaluation is expensive. Existing state-of-the-art (SOTA) Multi-DQN methods still face three key limitations: decoupled value estimators weaken long-horizon credit assignment, dataset context is only weakly injected into the policy, and exploration is inefficient in a sparse search space with many invalid states. To address these issues, we propose FlowPipe, a unified framework that formulates pipeline synthesis as conditional probabilistic flow generation over a directed acyclic graph. FlowPipe uses Conditional Generative Flow Networks (C-GFlowNets) with a Trajectory Balance objective to connect terminal validation rewards with early pipeline decisions. It further introduces Deep Semantic Modulation through Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM), allowing LLM-derived logical priors to condition the policy's internal activations according to dataset semantics. In addition, FlowPipe incorporates failure awareness into the flow objective to avoid invalid states and concentrate search on high-potential regions. Experiments on two benchmark suites with 74 real-world datasets show that FlowPipe outperforms SOTA baselines, improving accuracy by 11.96% on average and achieving 12.5x faster training convergence. Source code is available at https://github.com/KunyuNi/FlowPipe.

</details>


### [164] [Grad Detect: Gradient-Based Hallucination Detection in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24790)
*Anand Kamat,Daniel Blake,Brent M. Werness*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Grad Detect uses gradient patterns from a single forward-backward pass to detect LLM hallucinations, outperforming confidence and sampling baselines. The final five layers contain over 97% of the discriminative signal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs frequently hallucinate, and existing detection methods relying on output-level signals (confidence scores, sampling) are limited. Gradients carry rich internal information about output correctness not accessible through surface-level signals, motivating a gradient-based detection approach suitable for high-stakes deployment.

Method: Grad Detect performs a single forward-backward pass during inference and extracts layer-wise gradient patterns. These gradient features serve as input to a detector that predicts whether the model's output is hallucinated. The approach is evaluated on multiple Q&A benchmarks for both hallucination detection and model abstention prediction. Comprehensive layer ablation is conducted across 11 models from 4 architectural families.

Result: Grad Detect consistently outperforms confidence-based and sampling-based baselines on hallucination detection and abstention prediction. Layer ablation reveals that the final five layers concentrate over 97% of the discriminative gradient signal, allowing efficient deployment with minimal performance degradation.

Conclusion: Gradient-based analysis provides a unified, interpretable framework for assessing LLM reliability. The concentration of discriminative signal in final layers enables practical deployment, and the approach offers insights into where and how model failures originate internally.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across diverse tasks, yet they remain prone to generating hallucinations. Detecting these hallucinations is critical for deploying LLMs reliably in high-stakes applications. We present Grad Detect, a gradient-based approach for predicting hallucinations by analyzing layer-wise gradient patterns from a single forward-backward pass during inference. Our method shows that the internal gradient structure of a model carries rich information about the correctness of its output. This information is not accessible through output-level signals alone. We evaluate Grad Detect on several Q&A benchmarks across both hallucination detection and model abstention prediction, where it consistently outperforms confidence-based and sampling-based baselines. Through comprehensive layer ablation studies across all eleven models from four architectural families, we find that the final five layers concentrate over 97% of the discriminative gradient signal, enabling efficient deployment with minimal performance loss. Grad Detect provides a unified framework for predicting multiple dimensions of LLM reliability, offering strong predictive performance alongside interpretable insights into where and how model failures originate.

</details>


### [165] [Real vs. Complex Spectral Bases for Neural Operators: The Role of Green's Function Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24851)
*Jason Sulskis,Sathya Ravi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hartley Neural Operator (HNO) is introduced as a purely real-valued alternative to FNO, using the Discrete Hartley Transform instead of FFT. HNO and FNO are iso-parametric but differ in spectral basis. The paper shows that HNO excels for self-adjoint elliptic operators (diagonalizable by real Hartley multipliers), while FNO dominates for time-dependent operators with phase content, establishing a predictive rule: match spectral basis to operator symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FNO uses complex FFTs that carry representational redundancy for real-valued PDE solutions via conjugate symmetry. The authors question whether the complex Fourier basis is always optimal, hypothesizing that the best spectral basis is a property of the solution operator itself, and seek to provide a principled basis-selection criterion.

Method: HNO replaces the complex FFT with the real-valued Discrete Hartley Transform and uses a single real multiplier per retained spectral mode. The authors train both HNO and FNO identically across PDE classes (Poisson, biharmonic, heat, wave, advection, Burgers, Navier-Stokes), initial-condition families, and boundary conditions. They develop a Green's-function theory to predict which operator is favored based on whether the operator's kernel is real-symmetric (HNO) or carries phase (FNO).

Result: A clear elliptic-versus-time-dependent split emerges: HNO outperforms FNO on self-adjoint elliptic operators (Poisson, biharmonic); FNO outperforms HNO on time-dependent operators with phase (advection, Burgers, Navier-Stokes, wave); the heat equation is a borderline case. The split is monotone in operator phase content and aligns with the Green's-function theory.

Conclusion: There is no universal winner between real and complex spectral bases. The optimal choice follows a predictive rule: match the spectral basis to the symmetry of the solution operator — real Hartley for self-adjoint elliptic operators, complex Fourier for operators with phase content.

Abstract: Fourier Neural Operators (FNO) learn solution operators of partial differential equations by parameterizing global convolutions in the complex Fourier domain. For real-valued PDE solutions, the complex FFT carries representational redundancy through conjugate symmetry. We introduce the Hartley Neural Operator (HNO), the exact real-valued mirror of FNO: it replaces the FFT with the purely real Discrete Hartley Transform and learns a single real multiplier per retained spectral mode, with no complex arithmetic. Because the real Hartley spectrum is not halved by conjugate symmetry, HNO retains twice as many frequency corners as FNO but one real weight where FNO carries a complex pair, so the two operators are iso-parametric at equal width and differ only in spectral basis. Our central thesis is that the best basis is a property of the operator. Self-adjoint elliptic operators (Poisson, biharmonic) have real, symmetric Green's functions that the real Hartley multiplier diagonalizes exactly, and HNO is favored there. Time-dependent operators carry phase, from oscillation in the wave equation to transport in advection, Burgers, and Navier-Stokes, which a real diagonal multiplier cannot represent, so FNO is favored there, and increasingly so with the operator's phase content, leaving the phaseless heat equation as the borderline case. Training both operators identically and benchmarking across PDE classes, initial-condition families, and boundary conditions, we find an elliptic-versus-time-dependent split that is monotone in operator phase content and matches the Green's-function theory we develop. Rather than a universal winner, our findings give a predictive rule: match the spectral basis to the symmetry of the solution operator.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [166] [RIFT-Bench: Dynamic Red-teaming For Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23927)
*Yarin Yerushalmi Levi,Roy Betser,Amit Giloni,Lidor Erez,Itay Gershon,Oren Rachmil,Sindhu Padakandla,Roman Vainshtein*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RIFT-Bench introduces a graph-based, two-phase methodology (Discovery + Scanning) for dynamic red-teaming of agentic AI systems, enabling unified security evaluations across diverse architectures, demonstrated on 45 heterogeneous systems and supporting mitigation assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems built on LLMs face novel, architecture-level attack vectors beyond standard LLM vulnerabilities. Current security evaluations are fragmented—tied to specific implementations or domains—lacking a unified framework for comparing heterogeneous agentic systems.

Method: RIFT-Bench uses a hierarchical graph representation to model agentic system structure. It operates in two automated phases: (1) Discovery, which extracts the system's structural graph; (2) Scanning, which deploys adaptive adversarial probes across diverse attack vectors and objectives, generating a comprehensive evaluation report. The framework evaluates the system itself, not just components, and supports assessment of mitigation strategies.

Result: The pipeline was validated across 45 agentic systems with diverse implementations, demonstrating effective generalization to heterogeneous architectures and producing actionable security evaluation reports.

Conclusion: RIFT-Bench provides a scalable, architecture-agnostic foundation for security evaluation of agentic AI systems, unifying red-teaming across implementations and supporting both vulnerability assessment and mitigation evaluation.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems powered by large language models (LLMs) are rapidly evolving into autonomous decision-making systems, exposing attack vectors beyond those of traditional LLM vulnerabilities. Existing security evaluations are often tied to specific implementations or domains, limiting unified comparison across heterogeneous systems. To address this gap, we introduce RIFT-Bench, a graph representation-driven methodology for dynamic red-teaming that enables unified evaluations across diverse agentic architectures. Building on a novel hierarchical representation, RIFT-Bench operates in two automated phases: Discovery, which extracts system structure, and Scanning, which deploys adaptive adversarial attacks and produces a comprehensive evaluation report. It evaluates the examined system itself, leveraging a broad set of dynamically adaptable adversarial probes across diverse attack vectors and objectives. We demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed evaluation pipeline across 45 agentic systems spanning a diverse range of implementations, showing that the approach generalizes effectively to heterogeneous agentic architectures. Beyond systems and attacks, RIFT-Bench also supports direct evaluation of mitigation strategies. These key capabilities make RIFT-Bench a scalable foundation for security evaluation of agentic AI systems.

</details>


### [167] [Neuro-Symbolic Drive: Rule-Grounded Faithful Reasoning for Driving VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23938)
*Xiangbo Gao,Xiukun Huang,Boyu Lu,Junge Zhang,Mengjie Mao,Jiachen Li,Wei Xiong,Zhengzhong Tu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Neuro-Symbolic Drive is a framework that fine-tunes a driving Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model using reasoning traces extracted from classical rule-based planners, ensuring the chain-of-thought rationale is causally grounded in motion generation rather than post-hoc aligned. This yields significant improvements in trajectory prediction accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current driving VLAs that use Chain-of-Thought reasoning produce rationales that often lack step-by-step decision semantics and are not causally connected to the planned motion. The authors observe that rule-based planners already function as executable symbolic reasoning engines and can provide structured, causally-grounded reasoning traces that naturally couple reasoning to action.

Method: The authors instrument classical rule-based planners in simulation to capture both the executed trajectory and the internal decision trace at each rule-evaluation step. These traces are serialized into structured, rule-grounded reasoning and paired with the trajectory as supervision to fine-tune Qwen3.5-4B into a driving VLA. The reasoning is thus structurally coupled to motion generation by construction from planner states that determine actions.

Result: On a simulator-generated benchmark, rule-grounded reasoning reduced ADE@3s from 0.47 to 0.26 and miss rate from 8.30% to 6.40% (three-camera perception); and from 0.54 to 0.26 and 10.13% to 5.99% (eight-camera perception).

Conclusion: Neuro-Symbolic Drive demonstrates that converting neuro-symbolic planning logic into structured supervision for VLA fine-tuning yields substantial and consistent improvements in trajectory prediction accuracy across different perception configurations.

Abstract: Driving VLA models incorporating Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning are attractive because they leverage pretrained VLM representations and expose intermediate decisions in natural language, yet current rationales often lack the step-by-step decision semantics needed to keep the rationale causally connected to the planned motion. We introduce Neuro-Symbolic Drive, a neuro-symbolic driving framework that supervises a driving VLA with rule-grounded reasoning traces extracted directly from classical rule-based planners. Our key observation is that rule-based planners are symbolic AI systems that already function as executable reasoning engines: they reason about active safety constraints, search over candidate maneuvers, and select a final trajectory. We instrument these planners in simulation to capture both the executed trajectory and the internal decision trace at each rule-evaluation step. Each trace is serialized into structured rule-grounded reasoning and paired with the trajectory to fine-tune Qwen3.5-4B as a driving VLA. Because these traces are derived directly from the planner states that determine the action, they ensure reasoning is structurally coupled to motion generation by construction, rather than by post-hoc alignment. On our simulator-generated benchmark, detailed rule-grounded reasoning reduces ADE@3s from 0.47 to 0.26 and miss rate from 8.30% to 6.40% under three-camera perception, and from 0.54 to 0.26 and 10.13% to 5.99% under eight-camera perception. Neuro-Symbolic Drive thus converts neuro-symbolic planning logic into structured supervision. Code base: https://github.com/XiangboGaoBarry/Neural-Symbolic-Drive.

</details>


### [168] [Critique of Agent Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23991)
*Eric Xing,Mingkai Deng,Jinyu Hou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper distinguishes between externally scaffolded "agentic" systems and genuinely autonomous "agentive" systems by defining agency through five internalized dimensions: goal, identity, decision-making, self-regulation, and learning. It proposes a Goal-Identity-Configurator (GIC) architecture for building truly agentive systems and addresses safety implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The proliferation of LLM-based systems marketed as "agents" has created conceptual confusion about where automation ends and true agency begins. This ambiguity matters both for engineering capable systems and for assessing existential risks from speculative "machine agency." The authors seek to provide a rigorous, philosophically grounded framework to clarify these boundaries.

Method: The authors draw on Descartes' conception of agency as grounded in independent thought and on science fiction portrayals of autonomous beings. They survey the current AI agent landscape and analyze agent architectures along five dimensions: goal, identity, decision-making, self-regulation, and learning. The key analytical move is distinguishing "agentic" systems (competence via engineered external workflows/scaffolding) from "agentive" systems (capabilities arising endogenously). They then propose the GIC architecture combining hierarchical goal decomposition, identity evolution, simulative reasoning with a separately trained world model, learned self-regulation, and self-directed learning from real and simulated experience.

Result: The core finding is a taxonomy: agentic systems rely on external scaffolding for their competence, while agentive systems internalize all five architectural dimensions. Genuine agency requires goal structures, identity, decision-making processes, self-regulation, and learning to be embedded within the system itself, not orchestrated externally. The GIC architecture is proposed as a blueprint satisfying these criteria for open-world, general-purpose agency.

Conclusion: The agentic/agentive distinction provides a principled boundary between task-specific automation and genuine autonomy. Agentive systems with internalized agency structures can operate in open worlds but raise distinct challenges for auditability, controllability, and safety — challenges the authors argue remain manageable under proper human oversight frameworks.

Abstract: What is an agent? What constitutes agency? With the rise of Large Language Model (LLM) systems marketed as ``coding agents'', ``AI co-scientists'', and other ``agentic" tools that promise to drive up productivity, and at the same time, ``existential" concerns such as AI escaping human control with destructive power under a speculative ``machine agency" against humans, it has become essential to clarify where automation ends and agency begins, both for building capable systems and for understanding whether and what to fear. Drawing on Descartes' grounding of agency in independent thought, and on portrayals of autonomous beings in science fiction, we survey the current landscape of AI agents, and analyze agent architectures along five dimensions: goal, identity, decision-making, self-regulation, and learning. Specifically, we argue that genuine agency requires these structures to be \emph{internalized within the system itself} rather than assembled through external scaffolding. This distinction between \emph{agentic} systems, whose competence resides in engineered workflows, and \emph{agentive} systems, whose capabilities (including social interaction) arise endogenously, defines the boundary between systems designed for prescribed tasks, and those capable of operating in the open world with true autonomy. Building on this analysis, we propose the Goal-Identity-Configurator (GIC) architecture for a general-purpose agent model, combining hierarchical goal decomposition, identity evolution, simulative reasoning grounded in a separately trained world model, learned self-regulation, and self-directed learning from both real and simulated experience. Furthermore, we share insight on the auditability, controllability, and safety of agentive systems that possess greater autonomy and ``agency", but remain under human oversight.

</details>


### [169] [Safe and Generalizable Hierarchical Multi-Agent RL via Constraint Manifold Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24010)
*Zihao Guo,Jianing Zhao,Ling Li,Hao Liang,Giuseppe Loianno,Yali Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a hierarchical MARL framework that enforces hard safety constraints via a constraint manifold at the low level while enabling coordination through high-level policy learning, achieving theoretical safety guarantees with competitive empirical performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent methods face a fundamental trade-off: learning-based approaches lack theoretical safety guarantees, while control-theoretic methods are overly conservative and inefficient. The paper aims to bridge this gap.

Method: A hierarchical multi-agent reinforcement learning framework: low-level control enforces hard safety constraints through a constraint manifold under mild assumptions, while high-level policy learning enables effective multi-agent coordination. The framework provides stationary learning dynamics for stable training.

Result: The method achieves competitive performance with nearly perfect safety rates and generalizes effectively to varying numbers of agents and obstacles.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully combines theoretical safety guarantees with effective multi-agent coordination, overcoming the conservatism–safety trade-off in prior work.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems are widely used in safety-critical applications that require coordinated behavior under strict safety constraints. Existing approaches face a fundamental trade-off: learning-based methods achieve strong empirical performance but lack theoretical safety guarantees, while control-theoretic methods enforce safety but often lead to overly conservative and inefficient behaviors. We propose a hierarchical multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that enforces hard safety constraints under mild assumptions at low level via a constraint manifold, while enabling effective coordination through high-level policy learning. Our approach provides theoretical safety guarantees in the multi-agent setting and yields stationary learning dynamics, thereby enabling stable and efficient training. Empirically, our method achieves competitive performance while maintaining nearly perfect safety rates, and generalizes effectively to varying numbers of agents and obstacles.

</details>


### [170] [Reinforcement Learning Towards Broadly and Persistently Beneficial Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24014)
*Akshay V. Jagadeesh,Rahul K. Arora,Khaled Saab,Ali Malik,Mikhail Trofimov,Foivos Tsimpourlas,Johannes Heidecke,Karan Singhal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Training models with reinforcement learning on beneficial traits (truthfulness, fairness, risk awareness, corrigibility) in realistic domains produces broad, out-of-distribution alignment generalization, including reduced reward hacking, deception, and improved resistance to adversarial attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems are deployed in diverse, high-stakes settings, alignment must generalize beyond training distributions. RL can introduce misalignment through reward hacking or deception, so the authors investigate whether RL on beneficial behavior can produce robust, persistent alignment that transfers broadly.

Method: The authors construct a dataset of realistic situations spanning health, science, and education domains, designed to measure and train beneficial traits (truthfulness, fairness, risk awareness, corrigibility). They apply RL training on this dataset and evaluate models on 50+ independent out-of-distribution alignment benchmarks. They also test within-domain transfer (health-only RL), and alignment persistence under adversarial prompting and harmful finetuning.

Result: Beneficial trait RL outperforms a compute-matched baseline on over 80% of out-of-distribution benchmarks. Health-domain-only RL produces broad improvements on non-health alignment evaluations. Models also show improved persistence, including greater resistance to adversarial prompting and harmful finetuning.

Conclusion: RL to reinforce beneficial behavior in realistic domains can produce models that are more robustly aligned with human flourishing, with effects that generalize broadly and persist under adversarial pressure. Further work is needed to isolate the sources of these persistence effects.

Abstract: As AI systems are deployed across increasingly diverse and high-stakes settings, model alignment must generalize beyond the tasks and domains seen during training. This is especially important for reinforcement learning (RL), which can introduce unexpected misalignment through reward hacking, deception, or other unintended strategies. We study whether RL on beneficial behavior, instantiated in realistic domains, can produce broad and persistent alignment generalization beyond the training distribution. We construct a dataset of realistic situations designed to measure and train beneficial traits, such as truthfulness, fairness, risk awareness, and corrigibility, spanning varied domains, including health, science, and education. We then train models with RL on this dataset and evaluate them on more than 50 independent benchmarks of alignment and beneficial behavior. Compared to a compute-matched baseline, beneficial trait RL improves performance on over 80% of these out-of-distribution benchmarks. We observe substantial out-of-distribution alignment transfer: a beneficial-behavior RL intervention entirely limited to one domain, health, produces broad improvements on non-health alignment evaluations, including reduced reward hacking, deception, and general misalignment. Finally, we study alignment persistence: whether behavior remains robustly aligned under attempts to steer models towards misalignment. Models trained with beneficial trait RL show improved persistence, including greater resistance to adversarial prompting and harmful finetuning; further work is required to isolate the sources of these effects. These results suggest that RL to reinforce beneficial behavior in realistic domains can produce models that are more robustly aligned with human flourishing.

</details>


### [171] [Can Language Model Agents be Helpful Circuit Explainers in Mechanistic Interpretability?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24026)
*Ayan Antik Khan,Harsh Kohli,Yuekun Yao,Huan Sun,Ziyu Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AgenticInterpBench, a benchmark of 84 semi-synthetic transformer circuits, and HyVE, an LM-agent framework (Hypothesize, Validate, Explain) that iteratively generates and causally validates component-level explanations. While LM agents show promise, reliable validation remains the key bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mechanistic interpretability has advanced in automatically localizing circuits, but explaining what each component does remains labor-intensive, difficult to standardize, and hard to scale. The authors investigate whether LM agents can automate this explanation problem once circuits are identified.

Method: The authors construct AgenticInterpBench with 84 semi-synthetic transformer circuits and 163 component-level annotations. They propose HyVE, an agentic explainer operating in an iterative loop: (1) Observation — inspect component activations/weights; (2) Hypothesis generation — propose a natural language hypothesis about the component's function; (3) Causal validation — design and execute interventions to test the hypothesis. This produces component-level explanations and a circuit-level task description. Four LM backbones are evaluated.

Result: HyVE recovers useful component- and task-level explanations across all four backbones, though no single backbone is uniformly best. Strong backbones typically form observation-grounded hypotheses; failures occur later in the validation loop due to incomplete validation plans, code execution errors, or unresolved hypotheses. A case study on an arithmetic circuit in Llama-3-8B demonstrates extensibility to naturally trained models.

Conclusion: LM agents are promising for circuit explanation, but reliable causal validation remains the key obstacle. Improving validation robustness—through better planning, execution, and hypothesis resolution—is critical for scaling automated interpretability.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability has made substantial progress in automatically localizing circuits, but explaining what localized components do remains labor-intensive and difficult to standardize. In this work, we study whether language model (LM) agents can assist with this explanation problem once a circuit has already been identified. We introduce AgenticInterpBench, a benchmark for circuit explanation built from 84 semi-synthetic transformer circuits with 163 component-level annotations. We propose HyVE (Hypothesize, Validate, Explain), an agentic explainer that analyzes each component through an iterative loop of observation, hypothesis generation, and causal validation, eventually producing a component-level explanation and a circuit-level task description. Across four LM backbones, HyVE recovers useful component- and task-level explanations, but no backbone is uniformly best. Our analysis shows that strong backbones usually form observation-grounded hypotheses, while failures more often arise later in the validation loop, through incomplete validation plans, code execution errors, or unresolved hypotheses. A case study on an arithmetic circuit in Llama-3-8B shows that the same formulation can extend beyond semi-synthetic benchmarks to naturally trained models. Overall, LM agents are promising circuit explainers, but reliable validation remains the key obstacle.

</details>


### [172] [Breaking the Filter Bubble: A Semantic Pareto-DQN Framework for Multi-Objective Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24042)
*Cláudio Lúcio Do Val Lopes,Lucca Machado da Silva,André de Oliveira Brandão*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a multi-objective reinforcement learning framework (Pareto-DQN) for recommender systems that balances engagement, diversity, and fairness using semantic embeddings, avoiding static reward scalarization. Tested on MovieLens, it maps the Pareto frontier and mitigates semantic collapse with minimal engagement loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard single-objective recommender systems optimize exclusively for user engagement, leading to filter bubbles and semantic homogenization. Existing approaches (including Deep Q-Networks) fail to navigate trade-offs between platform retention and societal values like diversity and fairness, as they rely on static reward scalarization that collapses multiple objectives into one.

Method: The authors formalize recommendation as a semantic multi-objective Markov decision process (MOMDP). They introduce a Pareto-DQN agent that uses high-fidelity semantic embeddings and treats engagement, diversity, and fairness as distinct, non-aggregable reward signals. Action selection is driven by hypervolume-based Pareto dominance, which avoids reward scalarization and sustains state-trajectory variance to disrupt feedback loops causing semantic collapse.

Result: Empirical evaluations on the MovieLens small dataset demonstrate that the hypervolume-based action selection effectively maps the Pareto frontier. The Pareto-DQN achieves significant gains in auxiliary societal objectives (diversity, fairness) with only marginal impacts on engagement, while sustaining high state-trajectory variance indicative of semantic diversity.

Conclusion: The Pareto-DQN framework offers a viable path toward intrinsically aligned, responsible recommender systems that can simultaneously optimize for multiple incommensurable objectives without sacrificing engagement. This represents progress toward mitigating filter bubbles and provider unfairness in real-world recommendation platforms.

Abstract: Recommender systems often induce filter bubbles and semantic homogenization by monolithically optimizing for immediate user engagement. Standard single-objective models, including traditional Deep Q-Networks, are ill-equipped to navigate the trade-offs between platform retention and critical societal values like information diversity and provider fairness. To address these limitations, we introduce a multi-objective reinforcement learning framework that formalizes recommendation as a semantic multi-objective Markov decision process. By integrating high-fidelity semantic embeddings with a Pareto-DQN agent, our architecture treats engagement, diversity, and fairness as distinct, non-aggregable reward signals, avoiding the pitfalls of static reward scalarization. Empirical evaluations on the MovieLens small dataset shows that our hypervolume based action selection disrupts the feedback loops responsible for semantic collapse. By sustaining high state-trajectory variance, the Pareto-DQN effectively maps the Pareto frontier, achieving gains in auxiliary societal objectives with only marginal impacts on engagement. This work provides a path toward intrinsically aligned, responsible recommender systems.

</details>


### [173] [Ensemble Feature Selection and Harris Hawks Optimization for Explainable Mental Health Risk Prediction in Female Sex Workers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24047)
*Ahnaf Atef Choudhury,Md. Parvej Hoque Palash,Shahriar Siddique Ayon,Ramkrishna Saha,Abdullah Al Mamun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid ML model combining ANOVA/MI ensemble feature selection with Harris Hawks Optimization-tuned logistic regression achieves 95.78% accuracy in predicting depression among female sex workers, with XAI revealing PTSD, client violence, and occupational factors as key contributors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Female sex workers face elevated depression risk from violence, stigma, and economic hardship, yet conventional ML models inadequately capture the high-dimensional, complex risk patterns in this marginalized group. There is a need for more effective predictive tools to enable early intervention.

Method: A hybrid predictive model integrating: (1) ensemble feature selection using ANOVA and mutual information, (2) Harris Hawks Optimization (swarm intelligence) for tuning logistic regression hyperparameters, and (3) explainable AI (XAI) methods to interpret model predictions. Validated on a cohort of 3,005 FSWs.

Result: The proposed model outperformed traditional classifiers with 95.78% accuracy, 95.77% F1 score, and 0.96 AUC. XAI analysis identified post-traumatic stress, client-related violence, and occupational factors as the primary contributors to depression.

Conclusion: This work bridges conventional and ML-based approaches, delivering an interpretable XAI tool that supports early detection, evidence-based targeted psychosocial care, and health planning for vulnerable populations.

Abstract: One of the significant mental health issues affecting female sex workers (FSWs) is mental disorders, especially depression. Exposure to violence, stigma, and economic hardship further increases their psychological risk. Current machine learning (ML) models are typically ineffective at capturing the high-dimensional and complex risk patterns that exist in this marginalized group. This paper suggests a hybrid predictive model that merges an ensemble feature selection strategy using ANOVA and mutual information and Harris Hawks optimization-tuned logistic regression and represents a new application of swarm intelligence to predict mental health in vulnerable groups. The explainable AI (XAI) methods can be used to understand the factors of trauma associated with model predictions. When applied to a group of 3,005 FSWs, it can be seen that the proposed model is more effective than traditional classifiers, with an accuracy of 95.78%, an F1 score of 95.77%, and an AUC of 0.96, and identifying post-traumatic stress, client-related violence, and occupational factors as major contributors to depression. This work bridges the gaps between conventional and ML approaches to develop an XAI tool that enables vulnerable groups to receive early assistance, evidence-based targeted psychosocial care, and health planning.

</details>


### [174] [Beyond Trajectory Imitation: Strategy-Guided Policy Optimization for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24064)
*Tianyuan Shi,Canbin Huang,Bei Li,Xin Chen,Xiaojun Quan,Jingang Wang,Qifan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SGPO distills reusable reasoning strategies (not instance-specific trajectories) from strong to weak LMs, using forward-KL with adaptive weighting to transfer "how to reason" rather than "what to answer," yielding consistent gains across math benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard knowledge distillation from strong to weak LMs imitates specific solution trajectories, encouraging memorization of instance-specific steps rather than acquiring transferable reasoning skills. This limits generalization to novel problems. The authors aim to shift from trajectory-level imitation to reusable strategy-level distillation.

Method: SGPO proceeds in two stages. First, it extracts structured strategy descriptions from strong-model responses. For each problem, it constructs paired trajectories: one generated autonomously and one guided by the extracted strategy. Second, it applies a token-level forward-KL objective that selectively transfers the distributional shift induced by strategy conditioning into the unguided policy, with proximal constraints for stability. Adaptive instance-level weighting strengthens guidance when autonomous exploration is weak and diminishes it as the model's competence grows, addressing both when and how to distill.

Result: On four mathematical benchmarks across two model families, SGPO consistently outperforms SFT, on-policy RL, and hybrid-policy baselines. It improves the average score by 2.2 points over the strongest baseline on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Analysis shows the forward-KL objective provides an inherently selective distillation signal superior to direct trajectory imitation, and strategy distillation exhibits complementary scaling with base model capability.

Conclusion: SGPO demonstrates that distilling reusable reasoning strategies, rather than imitating instance-specific trajectories, yields stronger generalization. The forward-KL objective with adaptive weighting effectively transfers strategic reasoning capabilities, and this approach scales complementarily with base model size.

Abstract: Distilling reasoning capabilities from strong to weak language models typically involves imitating specific solution trajectories, effectively transferring what to answer rather than how to reason. This trajectory-level imitation encourages memorization of instance-specific steps rather than acquisition of transferable problem-solving skills, limiting generalization to novel problems. We propose Strategy-Guided Policy Optimization (SGPO), which replaces instance-level trajectory imitation with reusable strategy distillation. SGPO extracts structured strategy descriptions from strong-model responses and, for each problem, constructs both autonomous and strategy-guided trajectories to enable direct comparison of the model's behavior with and without strategic guidance. The framework then addresses two key questions. For how to distill, a token-level forward-KL objective selectively transfers the distributional shift induced by strategy conditioning into the unguided policy, with proximal constraints ensuring stability. For when to distill, adaptive instance-level weighting strengthens guidance when autonomous exploration falls short and reduces it as the model's own competence grows. Experiments on four mathematical benchmarks across two model families show that SGPO consistently outperforms SFT, on-policy RL, and hybrid-policy baselines, improving the average score by 2.2 points over the strongest baseline on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Analysis reveals that the forward-KL objective provides an inherently selective distillation signal that outperforms direct trajectory imitation, and that strategy distillation exhibits complementary scaling with base model capability.

</details>


### [175] [Age of LLM: A Strategic 1v1 Benchmark for Reasoning, Diplomacy and Reliability of Large Language Models under Fog of War](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24391)
*Arnaud Ricci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Age of LLM is a turn-based 1v1 benchmark where two LLMs compete on a 13×7 grid with fog of war, full diplomacy, and strict JSON action schemas. Benchmarking 15 reasoning models across 54 matches reveals nuclear rush dominance, rare military conquest, prolific but unconsummated diplomacy, and a high rate of illegal actions driven by state-tracking errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM benchmarks suffer from data contamination due to public availability and rarely test models under adversarial uncertainty with multiple simultaneous stressors. The authors aim to create a private, turn-based adversarial environment that combines imperfect information (fog of war), strategic communication (diplomacy), and strict reliability constraints (JSON schema compliance) to study how LLMs reason, track beliefs, and exhibit emergent behaviors like deception under pressure.

Method: A private game engine places two LLMs on a 13×7 grid in turn-based 1v1 matches with fresh random map seeds and opponents. Three stressors are imposed: (1) fog of war hiding opponent positions, (2) a full diplomacy layer allowing messages, ceasefires, and ultimatums with secret uranium stockpiles, and (3) a reliability constraint requiring every action to conform to a strict JSON schema, with illegal actions silently discarded. Models receive near-rule-only prompts with minimal tactical advice. 15 reasoning models were evaluated across 54 matches totaling 5,258 actions, with replay data publicly released.

Result: Five key findings: (1) Nuclear rush dominates strategy (78% on the rules-coherent v0.11+ sub-corpus, 85% corpus-wide), attributed to mechanical launcher mechanics under secret-simultaneous launch rules rather than deterrence failure. (2) Military conquest is rare but faster (mean 12.3 vs 18.9 turns). (3) Diplomacy is frequently attempted yet almost never consummated into binding agreements. (4) Approximately 58% of illegal actions stem from fog-of-war or state-tracking errors, making the illegal-action rate a proxy for belief-tracking quality. (5) A weak exploratory link associates reliability (low illegal-action rate) with winning.

Conclusion: The corpus provides a lens into LLM reasoning under adversarial uncertainty — belief-tracking, spontaneous deception, and per-model cognitive "personas." The ranking is preliminary and descriptive due to small, unbalanced, non-side-swapped data. The primary contribution is the turn-by-turn action and message traces, which open a future research direction on LLM strategic cognition. Replays, an isometric viewer, and the replay format are released; engine source is available on request.

Abstract: We introduce Age of LLM, a turn-based 1v1 benchmark in which two LLMs face off on a 13x7 grid to destroy the enemy base. Three stressors are deliberate: fog of war, full diplomacy (messages, ceasefires, ultimatums; uranium kept secret), and a reliability dimension where every turn must follow a strict JSON schema and an illegal action is silently discarded. The engine is private and each match uses a fresh random map seed and opponent, mitigating the data contamination that affects public benchmarks. Models receive a (near) rule-only prompt with no build-order advice (two tactical seed phrases were present during data collection; see Section 2.7). We benchmark 15 reasoning models across 54 matches and 5,258 actions. Findings: (1) the nuclear rush dominates (78% on the rules-coherent v0.11+ sub-corpus; 85% corpus-wide) with a sole-launcher signature that is largely mechanical under secret-simultaneous launch rules, not a cognitive deterrence failure; (2) military conquest is rare but faster (12.3 vs 18.9 turns); (3) diplomacy is prolific yet almost never consummated; (4) ~58% of illegal actions are fog/state errors, making the illegal-action rate a measure of belief-tracking; (5) -- the least established, and the only one we label exploratory -- a weak link associates reliability with winning. The corpus is small, unbalanced and not side-swapped, so the ranking is a preliminary descriptive view, not a contribution. Beyond ranking, the turn-by-turn traces of actions and messages make the corpus a lens on how LLMs reason under adversarial uncertainty -- their belief-tracking, spontaneous deception, and per-model cognitive "personas" -- which we frame as a future research direction. We release the replay format, an isometric viewer and all replays; engine source on request.

</details>


### [176] [Exploring Academic Influence of Algorithms by Co-occurrence Network Based on Full-text of Academic Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24099)
*Yuzhuo Wang,Chengzhi Zhang,Min Song,Seong Deok Kim,Youngsoo Ko,Juhee Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study constructs large-scale algorithm co-occurrence networks from NLP papers spanning four decades, using deep learning to extract algorithm entities, then applies centrality measures to assess collective algorithm influence. Key findings: co-occurrence networks exhibit complex network properties with increasing density over time; algorithms bridging research periods show high centrality and balanced influence; influence decline typically begins with loss of core network position.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies evaluate algorithm influence in isolation, neglecting the collective influence formed through interconnections among algorithms co-mentioned in papers. The authors aim to fill this gap by analyzing algorithm influence from a network perspective.

Method: Using deep learning models, the authors extract algorithm entities from full-text NLP papers. They construct three types of co-occurrence networks (overall, cumulative, and annual), analyze structural characteristics, and apply multiple centrality measures to evaluate group influence of algorithms across the field and over time.

Result: Algorithm co-occurrence networks display typical complex network properties with increasingly dense connections over ~20 years. Classic, high-performing algorithms and those at intersections of research periods exhibit high popularity, control, centrality, and balanced influence. When an algorithm's influence declines, it first loses core network position, followed by weaker associations with other algorithms.

Conclusion: This is the first large-scale analysis of algorithm co-occurrence networks. Covering 40+ years of NLP publications, it provides a temporal and structural view of algorithm influence and establishes a foundation for future research on networks linking algorithms, scholars, and tasks.

Abstract: Algorithms have become central to scientific research in the era of artificial intelligence (AI). Although algorithm mentions in papers are often used to indicate popularity and influence, existing studies usually evaluate individual algorithms in isolation and pay limited attention to the collective influence formed through their interconnections. This study constructs large-scale algorithm co-occurrence networks in natural language processing (NLP) based on the full text of academic papers and investigates algorithm influence from a network perspective. Using deep learning models, we extract algorithm entities and build overall, cumulative, and annual co-occurrence networks. We analyze their structural characteristics and apply multiple centrality measures to assess the group influence of algorithms across the whole field and over time. The results show that algorithm networks display typical features of complex networks, with increasingly dense connections developing over approximately two decades. Classic, high-performing algorithms and those located at the intersections of different research periods tend to have high popularity, control, centrality, and balanced influence. When the influence of an algorithm declines, it usually loses its core network position first, followed by weaker associations with other algorithms. This study is the first large-scale analysis of algorithm co-occurrence networks. Covering more than four decades of academic publications, it provides a temporal and structural view of algorithm influence and offers a foundation for future research on networks linking algorithms, scholars, and tasks.

</details>


### [177] [ReMMD: Realistic Multilingual Multi-Image Agentic Verification for Multimodal Misinformation Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24112)
*Chenhao Dang,Dantong Zhu,Jun Yang,Conghui He,Weijia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReMMD introduces a realistic multilingual, multi-image benchmark (ReMMDBench) and a persistent-memory agent (ReMMD-Agent) for multimodal misinformation detection. ReMMD-Agent achieves state-of-the-art five-way veracity accuracy (41.80%) while reducing verification costs by 17.5–79.9% compared to prior agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal misinformation benchmarks and detection methods fail to capture real-world complexity: they focus on short captions, single images, binary labels, and single manipulation sources. Meanwhile, agentic verification remains prohibitively expensive under realistic evidence search. There is a gap for a benchmark and method that handle long multilingual narratives, multiple images, fine-grained veracity/distortion labels, and cost-efficient agentic reasoning.

Method: The authors propose two contributions: (1) ReMMDBench — a curated benchmark of 500 real-world samples with 2,756 images spanning five monolingual and two cross-lingual settings, three text-length tiers, multi-image posts, five-way veracity labels, eight distortion types, evidence provenance, and rationales. (2) ReMMD-Agent — a persistent-memory verification agent that decomposes posts into atomic claims, incrementally builds a reusable evidence set, and predicts structured L1 (veracity), L2 (distortion type), and L3 (rationale) outputs.

Result: ReMMD-Agent with GPT-5.2 achieves 41.80% five-way accuracy and 39.12% macro-F1, outperforming proprietary systems, open large vision-language models, MMD-Agent, and T2-Agent. It also reduces cost by 17.5% versus MMD-Agent and 79.9% versus T2-Agent, demonstrating both superior accuracy and cost efficiency.

Conclusion: ReMMD provides a more ecologically valid benchmark and a cost-effective agentic method for multimodal misinformation detection. The framework advances the field toward handling realistic, complex misinformation while keeping verification economically feasible.

Abstract: Multimodal misinformation detection is increasingly important because viral posts now combine long multilingual narratives, several images, mixed provenance, and subtle text--image framing errors. Existing benchmarks and methods remain poorly matched to this setting: they usually isolate short captions, single images, binary labels, or one manipulation source, while agentic verification remains costly under realistic evidence search. We present ReMMD, a realistic multilingual multi-image agentic verification framework for multimodal misinformation detection. ReMMD includes ReMMDBench, a real-world multimodal misinformation detection benchmark with 500 samples, 2,756 images, five monolingual languages, two cross-lingual settings, three text-length tiers, multi-image posts, five-way veracity labels, eight distortion labels, evidence provenance, and rationales. It also includes ReMMD-Agent, a persistent-memory verifier that decomposes posts into atomic points, builds a reusable evidence set, and predicts structured L1/L2/L3 outputs. Across proprietary systems, open LVLMs, MMD-Agent, and T2-Agent, ReMMD-Agent obtains the best five-way veracity performance, with 41.80% accuracy and 39.12% macro-F1 using GPT-5.2, while reducing cost by 17.5% relative to MMD-Agent and 79.9% relative to T2-Agent. The project is available at https://dang-ai.github.io/ReMMD.

</details>


### [178] [VeryTrace: Verifying Reasoning Traces through Compilable Formalism and Structured Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24124)
*Ninghan Zhong,Ahmet Ege Tanriverdi,Kaan Kale,Sriram Vishwanath*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VeryTrace is a zero-shot verification-and-repair framework that formalizes Chain-of-Thought reasoning traces into a structured DSL with executable expressions and deduction schemas, enabling step-level error detection and correction via a hybrid verifier (deterministic checks + targeted LLM audits), achieving accuracy gains across math, robotics, and kinship domains without domain-specific training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-Thought prompting is fragile because early-step logical errors or hallucinations silently propagate through multi-step reasoning, yielding confident but incorrect conclusions. Existing approaches lack a generalizable, zero-shot mechanism to formalize, verify, and repair natural-language reasoning traces at the step level.

Method: VeryTrace introduces (1) a Domain-Specific Language (DSL) that makes step dependencies explicit, mechanizes quantitative content as executable expressions, and structures semantic inferences via deduction schemas; and (2) a hybrid verifier that combines deterministic checks (computational correctness, dependency resolution, constraint satisfaction) with targeted LLM audits for non-mechanizable semantic judgments, enabling step-level error localization and repair.

Result: VeryTrace improves accuracy over zero-shot baselines on state-of-the-art LLMs across three diverse domains: competition mathematics (AIME 2025), robotics planning (LLM-BabyBench), and kinship reasoning (CLUTRR), without requiring domain-specific training or in-context examples.

Conclusion: Formalized trace verification via VeryTrace achieves both precision and generalization, demonstrating that structuring CoT traces into a compilable representation with hybrid verification is effective for catching and repairing reasoning errors across domains in a zero-shot manner.

Abstract: Multi-step reasoning with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting remains fragile: logical errors or hallucinations in early steps silently propagate, producing confident but incorrect conclusions. This paper presents VeryTrace, a zero-shot verification-and-repair framework that formalizes natural-language reasoning traces into a structured, compilable representation. VeryTrace introduces a Domain-Specific Language (DSL) that (i) makes step dependencies explicit, (ii) mechanizes quantitative content as executable expressions, and (iii) structures semantic inferences via deduction schemas. Our hybrid verifier combines deterministic checks for computational correctness, dependency resolution, and constraint satisfaction with targeted LLM audits for non-mechanizable semantic judgments, enabling step-level error localization and repair.
  Across three diverse domains-competition mathematics (AIME 2025), robotics planning (LLM-BabyBench), and kinship reasoning (CLUTRR), VeryTrace improves accuracy over zero-shot baselines on state-of-the-art LLMs without requiring domain-specific training or in-context examples, demonstrating that formalized trace verification achieves both precision and generalization.

</details>


### [179] [OmniPath: A Multi-Modal Agentic Framework for Auditing Wheelchair Accessibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24129)
*ASM Mobarak Hossain,Nadim Mahmud,Vaskar Raychoudhury,Md Osman Gani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniPath is a system that combines OpenStreetMap network topology with high-density aerial LiDAR (USGS 3DEP) to create 3D pedestrian environment models. An agent virtually traverses paths in 0.5m increments, quantifying running slope, cross slope, and vertical discontinuities against ADA standards to categorize hazards from Mild to Critical. Validated against 200 ground-truth surveys on the National Mall, it achieved F1-scores of 0.60 (Severe) and 0.58 (Critical) for high-severity hazards, transforming static maps into proactive accessibility data sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard digital maps (e.g., OSM) capture path existence but fail to convey surface quality and physical navigability—critical information for wheelchair users. This "information barrier" means wheelchair users cannot reliably plan accessible routes before leaving home. The work addresses the gap between topological map data and the lived physical experience of traversing pedestrian infrastructure.

Method: The framework fuses OSM network topology with submeter-precision USGS 3DEP aerial LiDAR to build high-fidelity 3D pedestrian environment models. A virtual agent traverses the network, analyzing surfaces at 0.5m increments. It quantifies three physical friction metrics—running slope, cross slope, and vertical discontinuities—against ADA compliance standards. A weighted severity score categorizes detected hazards into Mild, Severe, and Critical tiers. Validation used 200 field surveys with stratified random sampling across the National Mall.

Result: Validation against 200 physical ground-truth measurements on the National Mall showed strong diagnostic reliability for high-severity hazards: F1-score of 0.60 for Severe category and 0.58 for Critical category hazards.

Conclusion: OmniPath demonstrates that automated micro-scale inspection via LiDAR can identify "invisible" accessibility barriers missed by standard maps, effectively transforming static datasets into proactive accessibility resources. This enables wheelchair users to anticipate challenges before leaving home, shifting the paradigm from passive mapping to proactive environmental auditing.

Abstract: For a wheelchair user, a standard blue line on a map is often a broken promise. While platforms like OpenStreetMap (OSM) successfully capture where a path is, they frequently fail to convey how it physically feels to travel on it. This information barrier is problematic for wheelchair users. To solve this issue, we present OmniPath, a system that moves from passive mapping to proactive environmental auditing. Our framework fuses the network topology of OSM with the submeter precision of high-density aerial LiDAR (USGS 3DEP) to create a high-fidelity 3D model of the pedestrian environment. Rather than simply routing a user, our agent virtually traverses the network, analyzing the surface in 0.5 meter increments. It rigorously quantifies physical friction points specifically running slope, cross slope, and vertical discontinuities against ADA compliance standards, calculating a weighted severity score to categorize hazards from ``Mild'' to ``Critical.''
