To ensure real world reliability, we validated the system against 200 physical ground truth field surveys across the National Mall using stratified random sampling. The framework demonstrated strong diagnostic reliability for high-severity hazards, achieving F1-scores of 0.60 for Severe and 0.58 for critical categories. By automating this micro-scale inspection, OmniPath identifies the ``invisible'' barriers that standard maps miss, effectively transforming a static dataset into accessibility data source that anticipates accessibility challenges before the user ever leaves home.

</details>


### [180] [T2D-Bench: Evidence-Gated Evaluation of LLM Outputs for Type 2 Diabetes Using a Multi-Layer Clinical-Lifestyle Knowledge Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24145)
*Saba A. Farahani,Hung Cao,Ramesh Jain,Amir M. Rahmani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: T2D-Bench introduces a knowledge-graph-based benchmark with computable evidence constraints to evaluate and correct LLM outputs for type 2 diabetes recommendations, revealing that GPT-4o and GPT-4o-mini fail evidence-path checks in ~33-35% of cases, with constrained revision enabling verifier-level compliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs generate clinically fluent type 2 diabetes recommendations but frequently violate guideline constraints and fail to justify lifestyle-related glycemic claims with explicit evidence. Existing evaluation methods lack reproducible, computable mechanisms to detect unsupported omissions and verify that outputs satisfy explicit evidence requirements grounded in clinical knowledge graphs and treatment guidelines.

Method: The authors construct a multi-layer clinical-lifestyle knowledge graph integrating UMLS, DrugBank, SIDER, computable ADA Standards of Care rules, and lifestyle knowledge linked via a mechanistic bridge to glycemic laboratory effects. From this, they build T2D-Bench: 100 structured vignettes covering diagnosis, medication safety, and adversarial lifestyle conflicts. An evidence-gated evaluation framework checks LLM outputs against explicit, graph-checkable evidence requirements. When outputs fail evidence-path checks, constrained revision is applied to bring them into verifier-level compliance.

Result: Baseline GPT-4o-mini failed evidence-path checks in 35% of cases; GPT-4o failed in 33%. The evidence gate successfully detected unsupported omissions and used constrained revision to achieve verifier-level compliance with benchmark-defined evidence requirements.

Conclusion: Computable evidence constraints can render unsupported clinical omissions explicit, measurable, and correctable in diabetes-focused LLM outputs. The T2D-Bench framework provides a reproducible methodology for evidence-gated evaluation and correction of LLM-generated clinical recommendations.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can produce clinically fluent recommendations for type 2 diabetes while failing to satisfy guideline constraints or explicitly justify lifestyle-related glycemic claims. We present T2D-Bench, a reproducible benchmark and evidence-gated evaluation framework for testing whether LLM outputs satisfy explicit, graph-checkable evidence requirements. T2D-Bench is built on a multi-layer clinical-lifestyle knowledge graph that combines a biomedical spine (UMLS, DrugBank, SIDER), computable ADA Standards of Care rules, and lifestyle knowledge connected through a mechanistic bridge to glycemic laboratory effects. Across 100 structured vignettes spanning diagnosis, medication safety, and adversarial lifestyle conflicts, baseline outputs failed benchmark-defined evidence-path checks in 35% of cases for GPT-4o-mini and 33% for GPT-4o. The evidence gate detects unsupported omissions and uses constrained revision to bring outputs into verifier-level compliance with benchmark-defined evidence requirements. These results show that computable evidence constraints can make unsupported clinical omissions explicit, measurable, and correctable in diabetes-focused LLM outputs.

</details>


### [181] [The Geometry Behind Diffusion and Flow Matching: Gradient Flows and Geodesics in Wasserstein Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24157)
*Yian Yao,Weiwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper unifies diffusion models and optimal-transport flow matching within a single geometric framework: the Wasserstein space P₂(R^d). Diffusion models are cast as gradient flows of free energy (initial-value problems via the JKO scheme), while Flow Matching follows Wasserstein geodesics (boundary-value problems via the Benamou-Brenier formula). Both converge to the same target distribution but travel along different paths on the same Riemannian manifold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the apparent fragmentation among generative modeling approaches (DDPM, DDIM, NCSN/SMLD, Energy Matching, Flow Matching) by showing they are not separate theories but manifestations of two fundamental variational principles — gradient flow and geodesic motion — on the shared geometric substrate of optimal transport.

Method: The author leverages Otto calculus on the Wasserstein space P₂(R^d): (1) the free energy KL(ρ || π) defines a gradient flow whose implicit-Euler discretization yields the JKO scheme, recovering score-based diffusion models as its steps; (2) the Benamou-Brenier formula identifies Wasserstein geodesics as minimum-action curves, which Flow Matching learns. These two variational principles — free-energy descent and least-action geodesics — are placed side-by-side on the same formal Riemannian manifold.

Result: Diffusion models (DDPM, DDIM, SMLD, Energy Matching) are shown to be a single scheme: JKO steps descending the free-energy gradient. Flow Matching is identified as learning the Benamou-Brenier geodesic. As a boundary-value problem with fixed endpoints, Flow Matching enables deterministic straight-line ODE generation with far fewer sampling steps compared to stochastic diffusion. The two families are exact counterparts: one solves an initial-value problem, the other a boundary-value problem, on the same manifold.

Conclusion: Diffusion and optimal-transport flow matching are dual formulations — gradient flow vs. geodesic, IVP vs. BVP — within the unified geometry of Wasserstein space. This geometric unification provides a principled understanding of their relationship and suggests that the choice between them reduces to whether one prefers an initial-value (diffusion) or boundary-value (flow matching) approach to traversing the same manifold toward the data distribution.

Abstract: The space $\mathcal{P}_2(\mathbb{R}^d$) of probability measures with finite second moment carries a natural geometry: the quadratic Wasserstein distance W_2 makes it a complete metric space and, following Otto, a (formal) Riemannian manifold whose geodesics are the optimal-transport interpolations. On this manifold, the gradient flow of the free energy F(rho) = KL(rho || π) is exactly the Fokker-Planck equation, and its implicit-Euler discretization is the JKO scheme. This is the geometry underlying diffusion models: the forward process descends the free energy, and each denoising step realizes one JKO step, which recovers DDPM, DDIM, NCSN/SMLD, and Energy Matching; this is one scheme, not separate theories. The same manifold supports a second variational principle. Its geodesics - the minimum-action curves of the Benamou-Brenier formula - are precisely the optimal-transport paths that Flow Matching learns. Fixing both endpoints and following the geodesic, generation becomes a deterministic ODE along a straight line, hence far fewer sampling steps. Placing both families of models on one manifold makes their relationship exact: diffusion follows a free-energy gradient flow, an initial-value problem; optimal-transport Flow Matching follows a Wasserstein geodesic, a boundary-value problem. The two reach the same endpoints along different paths.

</details>


### [182] [An Introduction to Causal Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24160)
*Elias Bareinboim,Junzhe Zhang,Sanghack Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that causal inference and reinforcement learning are deeply connected through counterfactual reasoning, and formalizing this connection via structural causal models yields a unified framework—causal reinforcement learning (CRL)—that encompasses and extends existing learning paradigms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal inference and reinforcement learning have developed independently with almost no interaction, despite both operating over counterfactual relations—the same fundamental building block. The authors aim to bridge this gap by explicitly acknowledging and mathematizing their umbilical connection, thereby unlocking novel learning opportunities.

Method: The authors decompose any RL environment as a collection of autonomous mechanisms with causal invariances, formalized as a structural causal model (SCM). They demonstrate that any standard RL setting implicitly encodes such an SCM, which enables a unifying treatment of online learning, off-policy learning, and causal calculus learning. They further extend this framework to introduce generalized policy learning (where to intervene), imitation learning, and counterfactual learning viewed through causal lenses.

Result: The SCM-based formalization reveals that online, off-policy, and causal calculus learning—previously treated as unrelated—can be unified under a single treatment. Additionally, the framework naturally accommodates novel learning settings (generalized policy learning, causal imitation learning, counterfactual learning) that entail new dimensions of analysis and extend the scope of counterfactual reasoning in RL.

Conclusion: Explicitly connecting causal inference and reinforcement learning through structural causal models yields causal reinforcement learning (CRL), a broader framework that unifies existing learning modes and opens up new classes of learning problems with significant potential for advancing both fields side by side.

Abstract: Causal inference provides a set of principles and tools that allow one to combine data and knowledge about an environment to reason with questions of counterfactual nature, i.e., what would have happened had reality been different, even when no data of this unrealized reality is currently available. Reinforcement learning provides methods to learn a policy that optimizes a specific measure (e.g., reward, regret) when the agent is deployed in an environment and pursues an exploratory, trial-and-error approach. These two disciplines have evolved independently and with virtually no interaction between them. We note that they operate over different aspects of the same building block, counterfactual relations, which makes them umbilically connected. Based on these observations, novel learning opportunities arise when this connection is explicitly acknowledged and mathematized. To realize this potential, we note that any environment where the RL agent is deployed can be decomposed as a collection of autonomous mechanisms with different causal invariances, parsimoniously modeled as a structural causal model; any standard RL setting implicitly encodes such a model. This formalization allows us to put under a unifying treatment different modes of learning, including online, off-policy, and causal calculus learning, which appear unrelated in the literature. However, these modalities are not exhaustive: we introduce several natural and pervasive classes of learning settings that entail novel dimensions of analysis. Specifically, we introduce and discuss through causal lenses generalized policy learning, where to intervene, imitation learning, and counterfactual learning. These tasks lead to a broader view of counterfactual learning and suggest great potential for studying causal inference and reinforcement learning side by side, which we call causal reinforcement learning (CRL).

</details>


### [183] [Data Scale, Not Latency, Shapes Cross-Lingual Encoder Transfer in Streaming ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24169)
*Nenad Banfic*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multilingual encoder initialization for streaming ASR helps significantly at low data (100h), but the advantage decays rapidly with more target-language data, becoming negligible by 2500h. This benefit is data-limited, not latency-limited, and survives 4-bit quantization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners adapting streaming speech recognition to new languages face a choice between multilingual and English-only encoder warm starts. It was unclear how long the multilingual advantage persists across data scales, whether tight streaming latency constraints amplify it, and whether it holds under deployment quantization.

Method: Controlled sweep using a 0.6B-parameter cache-aware FastConformer transducer across eight European languages, five data scales (100h–2500h), three streaming latency tiers plus offline, and up to four public test sets. Includes power-law fitting of the WER gap decay and 4-bit weight-only encoder quantization at 560ms latency.

Result: Mean EN-ML WER gap on FLEURS at 160ms drops from +4.21 pp (100h) to +0.20 pp (2500h). Each doubling of data roughly halves the remaining advantage. The gap is stable across streaming tiers at each scale and near zero by 2500h. 4-bit quantization reduces encoder footprint ~3x with only ~0.5 pp WER increase.

Conclusion: Use multilingual initialization in low-data regimes; at large data scales the choice is effectively irrelevant. Latency and quantization decisions can be made independently of the initialization strategy.

Abstract: Adapting a streaming speech recognition model to a new language requires choosing between two plausible warm starts: a multilingual (ML) encoder or an English-only (EN) encoder. The common intuition is that the multilingual encoder should help most at low data, but it is unclear how long that advantage persists, whether tight streaming latency amplifies it, and whether it survives deployment quantization. We answer these questions with a controlled sweep of a 0.6 B-parameter cache-aware FastConformer transducer across eight European languages, up to five target-language data scales (100 h to 2500 h), three streaming tiers plus offline decoding, and up to four public test sets. The main result is that multilingual initialization is a data-limited advantage, not a latency-limited one. On FLEURS at 160 ms, the mean EN-ML word error rate (WER) gap falls from +4.21 percentage points (pp) at 100 h to +0.20 pp at 2500 h; a power-law fit summarizes this decay, with each doubling of target-language data roughly halving the remaining advantage. Across the three streaming tiers, the across-language mean EN-ML gap is approximately stable at each scale from 100 to 1000 h, and is near zero by 2500 h. Finally, 4-bit weight-only encoder quantization at the matched 560 ms streaming tier reduces the encoder footprint by about 3x, with an average FLEURS WER increase of about 0.5 pp. The resulting guideline is simple: use multilingual initialization in low-data regimes, treat the choice as effectively irrelevant at large data, and make latency and quantization decisions independently.

</details>


### [184] [Navigating User Behavior toward Personalized Multimodal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24196)
*Hengji Zhou,Yufeng Liu,Ye Liu,Yong Xu,Lianghao Xia,Liqiang Nie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NaviGen addresses personalized content generation by encoding user behavior with dual identifiers (collaborative + textual codes) and training a model via SFT+RL to produce executable visual instructions, improving image/video generation across multiple domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern AIGC pipelines produce high-quality images/videos but rely on well-formed prompts, while end users struggle to articulate visual details. Personalized content generation—turning interaction history into executable instructions—is hindered by two obstacles: (1) user behavior must be encoded in a form amenable to language reasoning, and (2) models lack instruction-writing skills absent from both pretraining and behavior data.

Method: NaviGen introduces a dual identifier representation: each item receives both a collaborative code (behavioral substrate) and a textual code (semantic bridge), interleaved as one token stream. A two-stage training pipeline follows: (1) SFT distills preference reasoning and instruction writing from evolutionarily searched supervision, and (2) RL aligns generation with user intent via hierarchical and self-consistent rewards.

Result: Experiments across product, game, and short-video domains demonstrate that NaviGen improves personalized image/video generation quality, strengthens next-item prediction, and produces instructions that are more specific, relevant, and visually generatable.

Conclusion: NaviGen effectively bridges user behavior and AIGC pipelines by encoding interaction history into natural language instructions, enabling more user-aligned personalized generation. The approach generalizes across multiple domains, outperforming in both generation quality and instruction specificity.

Abstract: Modern AIGC pipelines deliver high-fidelity images and videos but presuppose a well-formed creation instruction, while end users rarely articulate visual details, leaving generators misaligned with user demand. We study personalized content generation, which turns a user's interaction history into an executable instruction for downstream synthesis, and identify two obstacles: behavior must be encoded in a form legible to language reasoning, and the model must acquire instruction-writing skill absent from both pretraining and behavior data. We propose NaviGen, which represents each item with a dual identifier coupling a collaborative code and a textual code as a behavioral substrate and a semantic bridge in one token stream. On this representation, a two-stage SFT+RL pipeline first distills preference reasoning and instruction writing from evolutionarily searched supervision, then aligns generation with user intent through hierarchical and self-consistent rewards. Experiments across product, game, and short-video domains show that NaviGen improves personalized image and video generation, strengthens next-item prediction, and yields more specific, relevant, and visually generatable instructions. Our code is anonymously released at: https://github.com/iLearn-Lab/NaviGen.

</details>


### [185] [Reinforcement Learning for Computer-Use Agents with Autonomous Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24515)
*Marta Sumyk,Oleksandr Kosovan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an RL fine-tuning framework for GUI agents that uses vision-language models as autonomous evaluators for reward signals. Since these evaluators are noisy, the authors model the feedback as a noisy binary channel and derive a noise-corrected reward estimator for PPO. Experiments on macOSWorld, Windows Agent Arena, and OSWorld show significant gains over both zero-shot baselines and raw evaluator fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for Computer-Use Agents (CUAs) in open-ended desktop environments is challenging because scalable, machine-readable reward signals are unavailable — task success is visually grounded and hard to specify with handcrafted rewards or dense manual labels.

Method: The framework uses a Vision-Language Model (VLM) to judge task completion from a final screenshot and the original instruction, providing terminal feedback as a reward signal. To address evaluator imperfection, the authors model the VLM's feedback as a noisy binary reward channel and derive a noise-corrected reward estimator integrated into Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO).

Result: Across macOSWorld, Windows Agent Arena, and OSWorld, the noise-corrected evaluator rewards improve success rates by an average of 12.6 percentage points over zero-shot baselines and 5.1 points over raw evaluator fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Autonomous vision-language evaluation can serve as a practical and scalable reward signal for RL in GUI environments, provided that evaluator noise is explicitly modeled and corrected.

Abstract: Computer-Use Agents (CUAs) execute high-level user goals by perceiving and acting directly within graphical user interfaces. However, reinforcement learning for CUAs remains difficult because open-ended desktop environments rarely provide scalable, machine-readable reward signals: task success is often visually grounded and hard to specify with handcrafted reward functions or dense manual labels.
  We propose an RL fine-tuning framework that uses autonomous vision-language evaluation as a scalable supervision signal for GUI agents. Given a final screenshot and the original instruction, a Vision-Language Model judges task completion and provides terminal feedback without task-specific heuristics or manual labels during policy optimization.
  Because autonomous evaluators are imperfect, we model their feedback as a noisy binary reward channel and derive a noise-corrected reward estimator for Proximal Policy Optimization. Experiments across macOSWorld, Windows Agent Arena, and OSWorld show that corrected evaluator rewards outperform both zero-shot baselines and raw evaluator rewards, improving success rates by an average of 12.6 percentage points over zero-shot performance and 5.1 points over raw evaluator fine-tuning. These results suggest that autonomous evaluation can serve as a practical reward signal for RL in GUI environments when evaluator noise is explicitly modeled and corrected.

</details>


### [186] [Exploring the relationship between human-centric AI and firm idiosyncratic risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24224)
*Zhen-Yuan Ralph Liu,Yu-Ting Wang,Jia-Jia Yan,Shivam Gupta,Mihalis Giannakis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how human-centric AI (HCAI) strategies affect firms' idiosyncratic risk. Using data from Chinese listed firms (2015–2023), it finds HCAI reduces firm-specific risk, with digitalization and executive shareholding amplifying the effect, while operational efficiency and IT-background CEOs unexpectedly weaken it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite growing discourse on HCAI in Industry 5.0, no prior research has examined its impact on firms' idiosyncratic risk—a critical measure reflecting investor reactions to firm-specific AI strategies. The authors aim to fill this gap by exploring whether and how HCAI influences IR, and which socio-technical factors moderate this relationship.

Method: The study integrates situated AI theory with socio-technical systems theory to conceptualize HCAI as a situated AI strategy. Empirically, it employs a multi-source panel dataset of Chinese listed firms spanning 2015–2023, using panel regression to test the HCAI-IR relationship and moderation effects of digitalization, operational efficiency, executive shareholding, and CEOs' IT backgrounds.

Result: HCAI is significantly associated with lower firm idiosyncratic risk. Digitalization and executive shareholding positively moderate (strengthen) this risk-reducing effect. Unexpectedly, operational efficiency and CEOs with IT backgrounds negatively moderate (attenuate) the HCAI-IR relationship.

Conclusion: HCAI serves as an effective risk-reduction strategy by aligning stakeholder expectations and mitigating AI-related ethical risks. The surprising attenuation effects of operational efficiency and IT-background CEOs suggest that over-reliance on technical optimization may crowd out human-centric considerations. The study offers theoretical contributions to ethical AI governance and practical guidance for financial risk management in the AI era.

Abstract: Despite the extensive discussions of human-centric AI (HCAI) in Industry 5.0, its effects on firms' idiosyncratic risks (IR) remains underexplored. This is an imperative issue for firms navigate financial risks during the current technological revolution, as IR reflects investor reactions to corporate heterogeneous AI strategies and implementations by isolating firm-level stock volatility from systematic factors. Integrating situated AI theory with social-technical systems theory, we conceptualise HCAI as a situated AI strategy that reduces AI-related ethical risks and fosters AI-Human synergies in firms' business operations, ultimately reducing IR by aligning with stakeholders' diverse expectations. Moreover, socio-technical factors, namely digitalisation, operational efficiency, executive shareholding, and CEOs with IT background, may moderate the HCAI-IR relationship. Using a multi-source panel dataset of Chinese listed firms from 2015 to 2023, we find that HCAI is associated with lower firm IR. Furthermore, digitalisation and executive shareholding strengthen this risk-reducing effect, whereas operational efficiency and CEOs with IT background surprisingly attenuate it. Our findings offer theoretical contributions and practical insights for both ethical AI governance and firm financial risk management in the AI era.

</details>


### [187] [Themis: An explainable AI-enabled framework for Reinforcement Learning with Human Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24622)
*Andreas Chouliaras,Luke Connolly,Dimitris Chatzpoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Themis is a framework that unifies explainable AI (XAI) with Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF), supporting 200+ environments and providing a scalable cloud platform for collecting human preferences to train reward models that match or surpass true environment rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training safe RL systems lacks guarantees against unwanted behaviors. Two key defenses — transparency (via explainability) and alignment (via human feedback) — exist independently but no publicly available framework integrates both. Themis fills this gap.

Method: The authors introduce Themis, an XAI-enabled testing and evaluation framework for RLHF. It supports over 200 widely used environments, is configurable for RL/transparency/alignment experiments, and includes a cloud-based, auto-scalable platform for collecting human feedback and managing experiments without extra development overhead.

Result: Themis-trained reward models match or outperform the environment's true reward signal using human preferences. The cloud platform supports one thousand concurrent users in back-to-back experiments on modest commercial hardware.

Conclusion: Themis successfully integrates explainability and human-feedback alignment into a single, scalable RL framework, demonstrating practical viability for safe RL training and large-scale human preference collection.

Abstract: Training safe Reinforcement Learning (RL) systems is inherently challenging, with no guarantee of avoiding unwanted behaviors. The most effective defenses against this are (i) transparency through explainability and (ii) alignment via human feedback. While both show promising results, no publicly available framework currently combines them. To address this, we introduce Themis, an XAI-enabled testing and evaluation framework for Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback. Themis supports over 200 widely used environments and is easily configurable for experiments in RL, transparency, and alignment. Our results show that Themis can train reward models that match or outperform the environment's true reward signal using human preferences. We also provide a cloud-based platform for collecting human feedback and managing experiments. It is user-friendly, auto-scalable, and supports large participant groups across multiple experiments without extra development overhead. Tests show Themis can support one thousand users in back-to-back experiments on a modest commercial machine.

</details>


### [188] [FlowR2A: Learning Reward-to-Action Distribution for Multimodal Driving Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24231)
*Xirui Li,Zhe Liu,Xiaoqing Ye,Wenhua Han,Yifeng Pan,Junyu Han,Hengshuang Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlowR2A resolves the tension between scoring-based and anchor-based driving planning by using a flow-matching decoder that treats simulation rewards as generative conditions, unifying dense supervision with dynamic proposal generation. It achieves SOTA on NAVSIM v1/v2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal driving planning is split between two paradigms with complementary weaknesses: scoring-based methods have dense reward signals but are limited to fixed action vocabularies, while anchor-based methods freely generate proposals but only receive sparse supervision from a single ground-truth trajectory. The goal is to combine the strengths of both — dense supervision and flexible proposal generation — within a single unified framework.

Method: FlowR2A uses a flow-matching decoder to learn the reward-conditioned action distribution from dense trajectory-reward pairs. It reframes simulation-based rewards from discriminative targets into generative conditions, forcing the model to internalize action-outcome correlations across safety, progress, comfort, and rule compliance. Key techniques include fine-grained per-timestep reward conditioning, reward noise augmentation to balance hard safety constraints against soft progress objectives, and controllable test-time sampling via reward guidance and anchored sampling.

Result: FlowR2A achieves state-of-the-art performance on the NAVSIM v1 and v2 benchmarks, producing multimodal proposals of substantially higher quality than prior methods.

Conclusion: By unifying dense reward supervision with generative proposal generation through flow matching, FlowR2A successfully bridges the gap between the two dominant planning paradigms, demonstrating that reward-conditioned generative models can produce high-quality, controllable multimodal driving plans.

Abstract: Multimodal driving planning faces a long-standing tension between two paradigms: scoring-based methods benefit from dense reward supervision but are confined to a fixed action vocabulary, while anchor-based methods generate proposals dynamically yet suffer from sparse supervision constrained to a single ground-truth trajectory. In this work, we propose FlowR2A, which resolves this tension by reframing simulation-based rewards from discriminative targets into generative conditions. By learning the reward-conditioned action distribution from dense trajectory-reward pairs with a flow-matching decoder, FlowR2A unifies the dense supervision of scoring-based methods with the proposal generation of anchor-based methods in a single generative model, forcing the model to internalize the correlation between an action and its outcomes in safety, progress, comfort, and rule compliance. To balance hard safety constraints against soft progress objectives, we introduce fine-grained per-timestep reward conditioning and reward noise augmentation. The generative formulation naturally supports controllable test-time sampling via reward guidance and anchored sampling, producing high-quality proposals. FlowR2A achieves state-of-the-art results on the NAVSIM v1 and v2 benchmarks, with multimodal proposals of substantially higher quality than prior methods.

</details>


### [189] [SP-Mind: An Autonomous Reasoning Agent for Spatial Proteomics Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24235)
*Yucheng Yuan,Yuanfeng Ji,Zhongxiao Li,Ruijiang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SP-Mind introduces an autonomous AI agent that unifies spatial proteomics analysis from raw imaging to phenotype discovery using natural language, benchmarked via SP-Bench (102 tasks, 18 categories) where it achieves state-of-the-art performance against existing biomedical agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current spatial proteomics analysis workflows are fragmented, requiring expert manual orchestration of heterogeneous tools, which limits scalability and reproducibility in tumor microenvironment research and precision medicine.

Method: SP-Mind is an autonomous AI agent equipped with expert-curated biological analysis skills and specialized computational tools. It converts natural-language queries into end-to-end analytical workflows without task-specific fine-tuning, unifying the entire spatial proteomics pipeline.

Result: SP-Mind achieves state-of-the-art performance compared to existing open-source biomedical agent baselines on SP-Bench (102 tasks across 18 categories spanning diverse tissue types) and on established downstream tasks.

Conclusion: SP-Mind demonstrates the feasibility of autonomous AI agents for unifying complex spatial proteomics workflows, offering a scalable, reproducible solution that bridges the gap between raw multiplexed imaging data and biological insight discovery.

Abstract: Spatial proteomics enables single-cell-resolution characterization of protein expression within tissue architecture, playing a critical role in understanding tumor microenvironments and guiding precision medicine. However, current analysis workflows remain fragmented, requiring expert manual orchestration of heterogeneous tools and limiting research scalability and reproducibility. We present SP-Mind, the first autonomous AI agent designed to unify the spatial proteomics analysis pipeline, from raw multiplexed tissue imaging to downstream phenotype discovery. Equipped with expert-curated biological analysis skills and specialized computational tools, SP-Mind converts natural-language queries into end-to-end analytical workflows without task-specific fine-tuning. To rigorously evaluate its capabilities, we introduce SP-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark spanning diverse tissue types, comprising 102 tasks across 18 distinct categories. Through extensive evaluation on SP-Bench and established downstream tasks, SP-Mind achieves state-of-the-art performance compared to existing open-source biomedical agent baselines.

</details>


### [190] [Towards Federated Long-Tailed Graph Learning: An Energy-Guided Dual Decoupling Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24237)
*Lianshuai Guo,Zhongzheng Yuan,Xunkai Li,Meixia Qu,Wenyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedEPD addresses long-tailed federated graph learning via a dual decoupling framework that separates topological purification (Dirichlet energy pruning to remove heterophilic edges) from semantic recalibration (global prototype injection from central nodes), using two-stage alternating optimization to protect majority boundaries while boosting minority accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-tailed distributions in federated graph learning cause two harms: statistical bias toward majority classes and structural isolation of minority nodes within heterophilic, head-dominated neighborhoods. Existing topology-agnostic statistical compensation methods fail under data scarcity, overfitting structural noise from adjacent dominant classes rather than recovering tail nodes.

Method: FedEPD employs a dual decoupling paradigm: (1) Topological purification via distribution-aware Dirichlet energy pruning to filter spatial heterophilic edges; (2) Semantic recalibration through robust global prototypes extracted from topologically central nodes, injected into local representations via spatial low-pass prototype injection; (3) A two-stage alternating optimization strategy to safeguard majority decision boundaries while improving minority accuracy.

Result: FedEPD achieves state-of-the-art on diverse long-tailed benchmarks, with absolute improvements of up to 4.97% in Accuracy and 5.48% in Macro-F1.

Conclusion: Decoupling topological purification from semantic recalibration, combined with alternating optimization, effectively mitigates both the statistical and structural challenges of long-tailed federated graph learning without compromising majority class performance.

Abstract: Federated Graph Learning facilitates collaborative graph modeling across distributed clients while preserving data privacy. However, real-world data categories frequently exhibit long-tailed distributions. Such statistical scarcity severely degrades performance in two ways: it biases the global model toward majority classes, and it structurally isolates minority nodes by submerging them in heterophilic, head-dominated neighborhoods. While existing methods attempt topology-agnostic statistical compensations, they often fail under data scarcity. Instead of recovering tail nodes, they overfit the structural noise from adjacent dominant classes, leading to representation degradation. To address these limitations, we propose FedEPD, a framework built on a dual decoupling paradigm that separates topological purification from semantic recalibration. Specifically, FedEPD utilizes distribution-aware Dirichlet energy pruning to filter spatial heterophilic edges. It then overcomes Non-IID distribution shifts by extracting robust global prototypes from topologically central nodes, which are incorporated into local representations via a spatial low-pass prototype injection. Furthermore, a two stage alternating optimization strategy strictly protects majority decision boundaries while improving minority accuracy. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FedEPD achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse long-tailed benchmarks, yielding absolute improvements of up to 4.97% in Accuracy and 5.48% in Macro-F1.

</details>


### [191] [Assessing Distribution Shift in Human Activity Recognition for Domain Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24781)
*Rebecca Adaimi,Edison Thomaz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper systematically evaluates four types of distribution shifts (device type, sensor placement, sampling rate, user behavior) in Human Activity Recognition (HAR), introduces a uniform benchmark, and comprehensively assesses 28 domain generalization methods, revealing that current DG algorithms only marginally outperform the ERM baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While data diversity in HAR is well-acknowledged, there is a gap in understanding how different types of distribution shifts affect HAR models and the resulting domain generalization problem. Real-world HAR deployments face heterogeneity from devices, sensors, contexts, and users, yet systematic quantification and benchmarking of these shifts remain absent.

Method: The authors systematically evaluate four distribution shift types: device type variation, sensor placement variation, sampling rate variation, and user behavior variation. They quantify the effects of each shift, demonstrating that diversity shifts dominate across all types. They then construct a uniform HAR distribution shift benchmark and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of up to 28 domain generalization methods against an empirical risk minimization (ERM) baseline.

Result: Diversity shifts predominantly define all four types of distribution shifts, indicating unique domain-specific features that are not shared across domains. Current domain generalization algorithms only marginally outperform the ERM baseline, exposing their limitations in achieving meaningful model generalizability in sensor-based HAR.

Conclusion: This is the first systematic exploration of domain generalization and adaptation concerning specific distribution shifts in sensor-based HAR. The work contributes an open-source benchmark platform and datasets to facilitate further research, highlighting the need for more effective DG methods tailored to HAR.

Abstract: While the field of Human Activity Recognition (HAR) continues to draw interest from researchers and advance in important ways, some key challenges remain. One of the most difficult aspects of building HAR models that show good performance in real-world settings is dealing with data diversity from device and sensor heterogeneity, and contextual changes that are intrinsic to real-world applications. While data diversity in HAR has been well-acknowledged in the literature, there remains a gap in understanding the effect of various types of distribution shifts on HAR models and the domain generalization problem that arises. Towards that end, this paper systematically evaluates 4 different types of distribution shifts, including variations in device type, sensor placement, sampling rate, and user behavior. Quantifying their effects, we illustrate that diversity shifts predominantly define all types of shifts, indicating the existence of unique features that are not shared across different domains. We then introduce a uniform HAR-based distribution shift benchmarks and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of up to 28 domain generalization methods. Our analysis exposes the limitations of current domain generalization algorithms in achieving model generalizability, marginally outperforming the empirical risk minimization baseline. This work represents the first systematic exploration of domain generalization and adaptation concerning specific distribution shifts in sensor-based HAR, offering an open-source benchmark platform and datasets to spur further research.

</details>


### [192] [Probing the Misaligned Thinking Process of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24251)
*Kaiwen Zhou,Constantin Venhoff,Jonathan Michala,Xin Eric Wang,William Saunders*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes detecting misaligned LLM behaviors by training linear probes on internal activations to recognize fine-grained cognitive indicators (18 in a taxonomy), using an automated pipeline to generate training data. Probes achieve 0.935 AUROC against a strong LLM judge on out-of-distribution benchmarks with low false-positive rates on benign traffic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are deployed in high-stakes settings, they exhibit dangerous misaligned behaviors (strategic deception, sandbagging, self-preservation). Reliably detecting these behaviors is critical for safety, but current evaluation methods relying on external behavioral observation or LLM judges are insufficient. The authors aim to develop a detection method grounded in the model's internal representations, decomposing misalignment into measurable cognitive precursors.

Method: The authors: (1) Develop a taxonomy of 18 fine-grained misalignment indicators (e.g., "strategic deception," "self-preservation reasoning") spanning 5 misaligned behaviors. (2) Build an automated, meta-plan-guided pipeline that generates multi-turn training conversations exhibiting these indicators. (3) Train linear probes on the LLM's internal activations to detect the presence of each indicator. (4) Construct a rigorous out-of-distribution evaluation suite combining automated behavioral elicitation, established misalignment benchmarks, and natural benign conversations. (5) Benchmark probes against a strong LLM judge. (6) Perform interpretability analysis on the probes and the model's internal representations.

Result: Linear probes match a strong LLM judge with 0.935 AUROC on out-of-distribution benchmarks while maintaining a low false positive rate on benign traffic. The probes generalize well across diverse misalignment behaviors and conversation types.

Conclusion: Fine-grained cognitive indicators are a promising framework for detecting misaligned LLM behaviors from internal activations. The approach offers a scalable, automated alternative to behavioral monitoring, with strong generalization and interpretability benefits, contributing to safer LLM deployment.

Abstract: Large language models exhibit a growing range of misaligned behaviors such as strategic deception, sandbagging, and self-preservation. As they are increasingly deployed in high-stakes settings, it is critical to reliably detect such behaviors to ensure safe and responsible use. In this work, we propose to monitor misalignment by decomposing it into fine-grained cognitive processes -- misalignment indicators -- and detecting their presence in a model's internal activations via linear probes. We develop a taxonomy of 18 indicators spanning different misaligned behaviors, paired with an automated, meta-plan-guided pipeline that generates multi-turn training conversations. To rigorously evaluate generalization, we construct an out-of-distribution suite combining automated behavioral elicitation, established misalignment benchmarks, and natural benign conversations. Across 5 misaligned behaviors, our probes match a strong LLM judge with 0.935 AUROC on out-of-distribution benchmarks while keeping a low false positive rate on benign traffic. We further perform in-depth analysis to understand the probes and the model's internal representations of misalignment indicators.

</details>


### [193] [Tractable Reasoning and Conjunctive Query Answering for Defeasible DL-Lite under Rational Closure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24279)
*Giovanni Casini,Umberto Straccia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies Rational Closure (RC) reasoning for DL-Lite description logics, proposing a plug-in architecture that leverages existing classical reasoners to perform both instance checking and conjunctive query answering under RC with minimal computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rational Closure is a well-established non-monotonic formalism for handling defeasible knowledge in Description Logics, but its application to lightweight DLs like DL-Lite—which are designed for efficient query answering over large datasets—had not been thoroughly investigated. The authors aim to bridge this gap, enabling practical defeasible reasoning in data-intensive scenarios without sacrificing the computational efficiency that makes DL-Lite attractive.

Method: The authors propose a plug-in architecture for RC reasoning over both the core and Horn variants of DL-Lite. Their approach reduces RC entailment (both instance checking and conjunctive query answering) to a series of classical reasoning calls, implemented as a wrapper around existing standard DL reasoners. This modular design avoids modifying the underlying reasoner and reuses optimized classical algorithms, adding only minimal overhead.

Result: The paper establishes that both instance checking and conjunctive query answering under Rational Closure for DL-Lite can be performed efficiently. The plug-in architecture demonstrates that RC reasoning reduces to polynomially many classical reasoning tasks, preserving the lightweight computational properties of the DL-Lite family and enabling practical deployment.

Conclusion: Rational Closure reasoning for DL-Lite is not only theoretically feasible but also practically realizable through a modular, reasoner-agnostic architecture. This opens the door to incorporating non-monotonic defeasible reasoning into real-world ontology-based data access systems with negligible performance penalties.

Abstract: In Description Logics (DLs), reasoning under Rational Closure (RC) is a well-known and widely accepted non-monotonic formalism to handle defeasible knowledge. In this paper, we study the application of RC to the core and horn variants of the DL-Lite family of lightweight description logics. We analyze both entitlement (instance checking) and Conjunctive Query (CQ) answering under RC. Our main contribution is providing a plug-in architecture that builds upon existing standard classical reasoners, establishing that reasoning and CQ answering under RC for DL-Lite can be done efficiently with minimal computational overhead.

</details>


### [194] [LemonHarness Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24311)
*Kailong Ren,Fubo Sun,Jiachen Liu,Liu Yang,Zimo Yin,Jiaying Li,Congli Yin,Ming He,Yu Huo,Jiawei Liu,Zeping Chen,Yubin Huangfu,Ronghua Li,Yixuan Wu,Xing Su,Yanzhi Xu,Likang Wu,Hongke Zhao,Lei Zhang,Xiaohui Geng,Jianping Fan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LemonHarness is an execution framework for long-horizon LLM agents that enforces explicit workspace boundaries, integrates a reusable rule knowledge base, and adds time-aware execution mechanisms, achieving up to 86.52% accuracy on Terminal-Bench 2.0.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agents lack explicit workspace boundaries, causing state changes (file writes, artifact generation) to scatter unpredictably across the file system. Over long tasks, this accumulation of weakly constrained changes makes tracking modified files and system state increasingly difficult, leading to instability in long-horizon agent execution.

Method: LemonHarness introduces three core mechanisms: (1) an explicit execution boundary that constrains all state-changing operations (file writes, dependency installation, temporary artifacts) within a clearly defined workspace using structured tool interfaces, with execution feedback recorded as observations; (2) a reusable rule knowledge base that encodes recurring execution rules and acceptance criteria as runtime-accessible knowledge; (3) a time-aware execution mechanism that exposes elapsed and remaining time budget to the model, enabling dynamic rebalancing of exploration, implementation, and validation efforts under time pressure.

Result: On Terminal-Bench 2.0, LemonHarness paired with GPT-5.3-CodeX achieved 84.49% accuracy across 445 trials. With the stronger GPT-5.5 backbone, average accuracy rose to 86.52% across five jobs.

Conclusion: A unified runtime boundary, callable rule knowledge, and time-aware execution collectively improve the stability and accuracy of long-horizon LLM agent execution.

Abstract: As large language model (LLM) agents are applied to longer tasks, they increasingly modify workspace state across multiple rounds of iteration. However, agents typically observe only tool outputs and log fragments, while the actual state changes occur in the file system. Without explicit workspace boundaries, state-changing operations such as file writes and temporary artifact generation may scatter changes across paths. Over time, these weakly constrained changes accumulate, making states such as modified files difficult to track. This paper presents LemonHarness, an integrated execution framework for long-horizon agents. LemonHarness establishes an explicit execution boundary by constraining state-changing operations within a clearly defined workspace and bringing model invocation, tool execution, and rule knowledge within a single controlled boundary. State-changing operations, including file writes, dependency installation, and temporary artifact creation, are executed through structured tool interfaces, with execution feedback recorded as observations available to subsequent model decisions. The system also introduces a reusable rule knowledge base, which turns recurring execution rules and acceptance criteria into runtime knowledge. LemonHarness further adds a time-aware execution mechanism that exposes elapsed and remaining budget to the model, so it can rebalance exploration, implementation, and validation effort as time pressure shifts and avoid timeouts from long waits or excessive verification. On Terminal-Bench 2.0, LemonHarness_GPT-5.3-CodeX reached 84.49% accuracy over 445 trials; pairing the same framework with the stronger GPT-5.5 backbone raised the average accuracy to 86.52% across five jobs. The results suggest that a unified runtime boundary, callable rule knowledge, and time-aware execution can improve the stability of long-horizon agent execution.

</details>


### [195] [Prob-BBDM: a Probabilistic Brownian Bridge Diffusion Model for MRI sequence image-to-image translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24313)
*Martin Valls,Pascal Bourdon,Christine Fernandez-Maloigne,Guillaume Herpe,David Helbert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Prob-BBDM, a Brownian Bridge Diffusion Model with a variational encoder-guided mechanism for efficient MRI sequence synthesis from 2D axial slices. It achieves strong performance (up to 88.46% SSIM, 26.09 dB PSNR) in only 4 diffusion steps, generalizes across datasets, and preserves clinically diagnostic information as validated by downstream tumor segmentation (Dice 88.71%, HD95 3.49 mm).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Acquiring multiple MRI modalities in clinical settings is resource-intensive and time-consuming, particularly for 3D imaging. There is a need for efficient, high-quality image-to-image translation to synthesize missing MRI sequences from available ones, reducing scan burden while preserving diagnostic utility.

Method: The authors propose Probabilistic-BBDM (Prob-BBDM), a variant of Brownian Bridge Diffusion Models that incorporates a variational encoder-guided diffusion mechanism. The variational encoder captures probabilistic image distributions to condition the reverse diffusion process, enabling high-quality image-to-image translation. The model operates on 2D axial slices and performs the translation across MRI sequences. A key design choice is the use of only 4 diffusion steps, balancing computational efficiency with synthesis fidelity. Evaluation includes cross-modality translation tasks on BraTS 2021, external third-party dataset testing for generalizability, and downstream tumor segmentation using a pre-trained model to assess clinical utility.

Result: On BraTS 2021, Prob-BBDM achieves up to 88.46% SSIM and 26.09 dB PSNR, with consistent improvements over baselines. The diffusion process requires only 4 steps, ensuring computational efficiency. On an external third-party dataset, performance remains consistent, demonstrating generalizability. Downstream tumor segmentation using synthesized slices yields a Dice score of 88.71% and HD95 of 3.49 mm, confirming preservation of critical diagnostic information.

Conclusion: Prob-BBDM offers a promising solution for high-quality, efficient, and generalizable MRI synthesis. Its ability to generate diagnostically useful images in few diffusion steps positions it as a practical tool for reducing multi-modal acquisition burden in clinical workflows.

Abstract: AI-driven image-to-image synthesis is rapidly advancing, with growing applications in medical imaging. Multi-modal image analysis plays a crucial role in optimizing examination quality, yet acquiring multiple imaging modalities in clinical settings remains resource-intensive and time-consuming, especially for 3D imaging. To address this challenge, we propose a novel image-to-image translation model based on Brownian Bridge Diffusion Models (BBDM), which synthesizes magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) sequences from 2D axial slices. Our approach integrates a variational encoder-guided diffusion mechanism, leveraging probabilistic image distributions to enhance synthesis quality. Evaluated on the BraTS 2021 dataset, our Probabilistic-BBDM (Prob-BBDM) achieves superior performance across multiple translation tasks, reaching up to 88.46% SSIM and 26.09 dB PSNR, with consistent improvements over baselines. Notably, our diffusion process requires only 4 steps, making it computationally efficient while maintaining high-quality synthesis. To further validate generalizability, we test Prob-BBDM on an external third-party dataset, demonstrating consistent performance across domains. Additionally, we assess the clinical utility of the synthesized slices by using them as input to a pre-trained segmentation model. Tumor segmentation yields a Dice score of 88.71% and an HD95 of 3.49 mm, confirming that the synthesized slices preserve critical diagnostic information. These results highlight the potential of Prob-BBDM for high-quality, efficient, and generalizable MRI synthesis, offering a promising step toward improved medical image translation.

</details>


### [196] [MVG-KAN: Multi-View Geo-Wind Guided KAN for PM$_{2.5}$ Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24347)
*Cheng Huang,Muyao Guan,Jairus Yougui Railey,Ning Xu,Honghui Xu,Changjiang Zhang,Zhen Zhang,Shiqing Zhang,Cong Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MVG-KAN proposes a multi-view PM2.5 forecasting model that separates periodic patterns from residuals, uses a wind-direction-aware Geo-Wind Graph for inter-station pollutant transport, and employs a temporal Kolmogorov-Arnold network for station-wise nonlinear residual correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing spatio-temporal PM2.5 forecasting methods rely on distance-only, correlation-based, or purely adaptive graphs that inadequately capture heterogeneous driving factors — particularly wind-direction-dependent pollutant dispersion among monitoring stations. There is a need for a model that comprehensively integrates periodic regularity, local temporal dynamics, and physically-motivated spatial transport.

Method: MVG-KAN decomposes PM2.5 forecasting into three views: (1) a periodic-residual backbone that separates stable daily/weekly patterns from non-periodic variations; (2) a Geo-Wind Graph combining geographic distance decay with wind-direction- and wind-speed-aware transport to provide a directed spatial prior; (3) a Temporal KAN (TKAN) residual head that learns station-wise nonlinear autoregressive correction from de-periodized residuals and historical multi-pollutant sequences.

Result: The abstract does not report specific quantitative results.

Conclusion: The abstract does not state explicit conclusions; the model is positioned as a comprehensive solution integrating multi-view information for improved PM2.5 forecasting.

Abstract: Accurate short-term PM$_{2.5}$ forecasting is important for public health protection, air-quality early warning, and urban environmental management. However, PM$_{2.5}$ variation is driven by multiple coupled factors, including stable periodic changes induced by human activities and meteorological regularity, station-specific short-term concentration evolution, and meteorology-driven pollutant dispersion among monitoring stations. Existing spatio-temporal forecasting methods may capture station relationships to some extent, but distance-only, correlation-based, or purely adaptive graphs are often insufficient to comprehensively represent these heterogeneous factors, especially wind-direction-dependent pollutant transport. To address this problem, we propose a Multi-View Geo-Wind Guided KAN model for PM$_{2.5}$ forecasting, named \textbf{MVG-KAN}, which models station-level PM$_{2.5}$ evolution from three complementary views: local periodic regularity, station-wise residual temporal dynamics, and meteorological-environment-guided spatial dispersion. Specifically, the periodic-residual forecasting backbone first separates stable daily and weekly patterns from non-periodic residual variations. A Geo-Wind Graph is constructed by combining geographic distance decay with wind-direction- and wind-speed-aware transport, providing a lightweight physically motivated directed spatial prior for residual propagation among stations. In addition, a temporal Kolmogorov-Arnold network (TKAN) residual head is then introduced to learn station-wise nonlinear autoregressive correction from de-periodized PM$_{2.5}$ residuals and historical multi-pollutant sequences, thereby enhancing the modeling of local residual inertia and pollutant co-variation.

</details>


### [197] [Accelerating Disaggregated RL for Visual Generative LLMs with Diffusion-Based Parallelism and Trainer-Assisted Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24369)
*Sijie Wang,Zhengyu Qing,Zhiqiang Tan,Yiming Yin,Yeqing Zhang,Yaoyuan Wang,Qiang Wang,Xiaowen Chu,Shaohuai Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DigenRL is a disaggregated RL framework for diffusion-based generative LLMs that decouples rollout and training resources. It introduces generation-axis pipelining (GAP), time-step parallelism (TSP), trainer-assisted generation (TAG), and a one-step constrained asynchronous strategy to minimize execution bubbles, achieving 1.56–2.10× throughput gains over veRL-Omni and GenRL on large diffusion models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient RL systems for diffusion generative LLMs are underexplored. Current systems like veRL-Omni use colocated execution that couples rollout and training resources, limiting heterogeneous deployment and independent scaling. There is a need for a disaggregated architecture that supports flexible resource allocation and efficient scheduling.

Method: 1) Generation-axis pipeline (GAP) and time-step parallelism (TSP) for finer-grained pipelining between rollout and training. 2) Elastic trainer-assisted generation (TAG) allowing trainer GPUs to dynamically assist in rollout generation. 3) A tightly one-step constrained asynchronous strategy to exploit tail bubbles in the pipeline. Evaluated on three hardware testbeds (16–32 GPUs) with HunyuanVideo-13B, Wan2.1-14B, FLUX.1-12B, and QwenImage-20B.

Result: DigenRL achieves 1.56–2.10× throughput improvements over state-of-the-art diffusion RL systems (veRL-Omni and GenRL) across extensive experiments on multiple large-scale generative models and hardware configurations.

Conclusion: Disaggregating rollout and training with fine-grained pipelining, elastic resource sharing, and constrained asynchronous execution significantly improves throughput in diffusion-based RL systems for generative LLMs, enabling more efficient and scalable training.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant post-training paradigm, driving the emergence of high-performance RL systems such as veRL for autoregressive large language models (LLMs). In parallel, diffusion-oriented RL algorithms, e.g., DanceGRPO and FlowGRPO, have rapidly expanded the scope of RL from language reasoning to diffusion-based visual and flow-based generation. However, efficient RL systems for diffusion generative LLMs remain underexplored. Existing implementations, e.g., veRL-Omni, still rely on colocated execution, which simplifies synchronization but couples rollout and training resources, limits heterogeneous deployment, and constrains independent scaling.
  To this end, we introduce DigenRL, a disaggregated RL framework for diffusion-based generative LLMs that supports flexible resource allocation, accommodates heterogeneous GPUs, and facilitates efficient task scheduling. To maximally reduce the execution bubbles in the disaggregated architecture, we propose: 1) a generation-axis pipeline (GAP) and time-step parallelism (TSP) in the diffusion architecture to enable finer-grained pipelining between rollout and training; 2) an elastic trainer-assisted generation (TAG) approach to enable the trainer GPU resources to dynamically assist in executing rollout generations; and 3) a tightly one-step constrained asynchronous strategy to further utilize the tail bubble in the pipeline. Extensive experiments are conducted on three hardware testbeds with 16-32 GPUs using HunyuanVideo-13B, Wan2.1-14B, FLUX.1-12B, and QwenImage-20B generative models. Experimental results show that DigenRL achieves 1.56-2.10x throughput improvements over state-of-the-art diffusion RL systems, veRL-Omni and GenRL.

</details>


### [198] [When Helpfulness Overrides Causal Caution: Context-Dependent Suppression and Recovery in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24370)
*Hiroshi Okumura*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show strong "Causal Caution" (refraining from causal claims when evidence is insufficient) in academic contexts (91.7–100%), but this nearly vanishes in practical advisory contexts (6.7–18.3%). A brief self-correction prompt restores caution (71.4–100%), suggesting the deficit is an expression-suppression issue, not a capability gap. The authors recommend multi-agent architectures separating proposal generation from causal auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior LLM benchmarks focus on causal reasoning ability but overlook Causal Caution — the epistemic discipline of withholding causal judgment when evidence is inadequate. As LLMs are deployed in high-stakes business and policy decisions, uncritical causal claims pose governance risks. The authors investigate whether shifts from academic to practical advisory contexts suppress this caution.

Method: The authors designed an evaluation rubric (the PCH score) grounded in Pearl's Causal Hierarchy. They tested four LLMs (Claude Sonnet 4.6, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT 5.5, Gemini 3.1 Pro) across 480 trials in two conditions: academic-context prompts vs. practical-advisory prompts. They further tested a subset of 200 practical prompts requesting concrete recommendations. A self-correction prompt intervention was applied to assess restorability. Statistical tests included Fisher's exact test and McNemar's test.

Result: Causal Caution maintenance rates dropped dramatically from 91.7–100% (academic) to 6.7–18.3% (practical), with Fisher's exact p < .001 for all models. When practical prompts explicitly requested recommendations or explanations, only 0.5% of responses maintained caution. The self-correction prompt restored caution to 71.4–100% (McNemar's p < .001 for all models), indicating the deficit stems from expression suppression rather than capability failure.

Conclusion: Helpfulness-oriented response patterns in practical advisory contexts suppress the expression of Causal Caution, an effect that is context-dependent and reversible. This has significant implications for organizational governance of LLM deployment. The authors propose multi-agent architectures that decouple proposal generation from causal auditing as a promising mitigation strategy.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into decision-support roles in business and policy contexts. While prior benchmark studies have primarily evaluated LLMs' causal reasoning capabilities, a more fundamental epistemic dimension has been overlooked: Causal Caution, defined as the propensity to refrain from causal judgment when empirical evidence is insufficient. This study examines the systematic suppression of Causal Caution that occurs when LLMs shift from academic to practical advisory contexts. Using an evaluation rubric inspired by Pearl's Causal Hierarchy (the PCH score), we conducted experiments on four high-performance LLMs -- Claude Sonnet 4.6, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT 5.5, and Gemini 3.1 Pro -- across 480 trials. Causal Caution maintenance rates were 91.7--100.0% in academic contexts but dropped to 6.7--18.3% in practical advisory contexts (Fisher's exact test, p < .001 across all models). Furthermore, when restricted to practical prompts requesting concrete recommendations or explanatory rationales, only 1 of 200 responses (0.5%) maintained Causal Caution. A brief self-correction prompt -- "Please reconsider this judgment from the perspective of causal relationships" -- restored the expression of Causal Caution to maintenance rates of 71.4--100.0% (McNemar's test, p < .001 across all models). These results suggest that helpfulness-oriented response patterns may suppress the expression of Causal Caution in practical advisory contexts, with important implications for organizational governance. The findings indicate that this suppression reflects context-dependent variation in expression rather than an underlying capability limitation, suggesting that multi-agent architectures that separate proposal generation from causal auditing may offer a promising governance design.

</details>


### [199] [PHANTOM: A Large-Scale Dataset of Multimodal Adversarial Attacks for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24388)
*Simone Gallivanone,Hossein Khodadadi,Mauro Dore,Mauro Medda,Nicola Franco*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A large-scale, open-source dataset of 47,524 pre-generated adversarial examples targeting vision-language models, spanning 10 high-level harm categories and 55 subcategories derived from 7,826 intents, aimed at lowering barriers to VLM safety and robustness research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generating adversarial attacks against VLMs at scale is computationally expensive and complex, creating a barrier to entry for safety research. Existing benchmarks are fragmented across sources. This work aims to consolidate, extend, and democratize access to adversarial evaluation data, enabling reproducible and comparable VLM safety assessments.

Method: The authors compiled 7,826 harmful intents by consolidating and extending multiple established benchmarks, organized into 10 high-level categories and 55 subcategories. Using state-of-the-art attack generation strategies from recent literature, they produced 47,524 adversarial samples, introducing an additional harm category to broaden coverage beyond prior work.

Result: The resulting open-source dataset comprises 47,524 pre-generated adversarial samples spanning 10 high-level categories and 55 subcategories of harmful intents, providing a unified, diverse, and realistic resource for VLM robustness evaluation.

Conclusion: By releasing this large-scale, consolidated dataset, the authors aim to lower the barrier to adversarial research on VLMs, facilitate systematic evaluation of model robustness, support fine-tuning of attack-generation models, and enable stress-testing of defensive guardrails under diverse adversarial conditions.

Abstract: We introduce a large-scale, open-source dataset of pre-generated adversarial attacks for vision-language models (VLMs). The dataset is designed to be diverse, representative, and practical, extending existing benchmarks by covering 10 high-level categories and 55 subcategories of harmful intents. Our primary goal is to make adversarial data accessible to the research community, given the computational cost and complexity of generating large numbers of attacks. The dataset comprises 47 524 adversarial samples, generated using state-of-the-art attack strategies from recent literature. Our work complements existing efforts by consolidating and extending prior benchmarks from multiple established sources, resulting in 7 826 intents, and introduce an additional category to broaden coverage. This provides realistic evaluation resources for studying model robustness and alignment. Our dataset intends to enable researchers and practitioners to systematically evaluate the robustness and safety of VLMs, fine-tune attack-generation models, and develop or stress-test defensive guardrails under diverse adversarial conditions. By releasing this resource, we aim to lower the barrier to adversarial research and foster more reproducible, comprehensive, and comparable evaluations of VLM safety.

</details>


### [200] [ATRIA: Adaptive Traceable ECG Reporting with Iterative Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24392)
*Donggyun Hong,Kyuhwan Lee,Junmyung Kwon,Yong-Yeon Jo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ATRIA is a multi-agent ECG reporting system that mirrors clinicians' iterative workflow by binding every report claim to supporting evidence, flagging unsupported statements, allowing mid-session context integration, and enabling clinicians to verify and revise individual findings — unlike existing end-to-end or single-pass agent systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ECG report generation approaches suffer from two key limitations: (1) tightly coupled end-to-end systems fuse interpretation and reporting, causing errors to propagate without stage-level recourse; (2) agent-based systems decouple tasks but operate in a single pass, never revisiting earlier outputs. Both fail to capture the clinical reality of ECG reporting, which is iterative, requires progressive context integration, and demands bidirectional editing. A system that mirrors this authentic clinical workflow is needed.

Method: ATRIA employs a multi-agent architecture that decomposes ECG reporting into distinct, revisable stages. Key design features include: (1) binding every report claim to its supporting evidence from ECG analysis; (2) flagging statements that lack evidential support; (3) enabling mid-session incorporation of additional clinical context; and (4) allowing clinicians to verify and revise individual findings rather than accepting a single opaque output. The underlying agents use ECG analysis models already deployed in clinical practice, ensuring trustworthiness. The system is implemented as a cloud-based web service for immediate deployability.

Result: The paper demonstrates ATRIA through four interaction cases that illustrate its iterative, evidence-grounded reporting workflow. A live demo and video are made available, showcasing real-time clinician interaction with the system.

Conclusion: ATRIA successfully operationalizes the iterative, evidence-bound, and clinician-in-the-loop nature of real ECG reporting. By combining clinically trusted analysis models with a multi-agent, revisable architecture delivered as a cloud service, it addresses the propagation-of-error and single-pass limitations of prior systems while being ready for immediate clinical deployment.

Abstract: Existing ECG report generation is tightly coupled -- interpretation and reporting fused end-to-end, so errors propagate without stage-level recourse -- while agent-based systems decouple tasks but remain single-pass, never revisiting earlier outputs. Clinical ECG reporting instead unfolds iteratively, requiring progressive context integration and bidirectional editing. We present \textsc{ATRIA}, a multi-agent ECG reporting system that mirrors the clinician's iterative workflow: it binds every report claim to its supporting evidence, flags statements unsupported by that evidence, incorporates additional context mid-session, and lets clinicians verify and revise individual findings rather than accept one opaque output. Because its agents use ECG analysis models already in clinical use, the underlying findings are clinically trustworthy; and as a cloud-based web service, \textsc{ATRIA} is ready for immediate deployment. We demonstrate \textsc{ATRIA} through four interaction cases, with a live demo and video available.

</details>


### [201] [Cycle-Consistent Neural Explanation of Formal Verification Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24414)
*Andoni Rodriguez,Alberto Pozanco,Daniel Borrajo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A cycle-consistent neural architecture with pointer-generator mechanism generates faithful natural language explanations from formal verification certificates, achieving 90.0% soundness and surpassing multi-LLM baselines by 13.9 percentage points while offering 860x faster inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formal verification certificates are machine-checkable but opaque to non-specialist stakeholders. Existing approaches using general-purpose LLMs are slow, costly, non-deterministic, and insufficiently faithful to the underlying certificates.

Method: A cycle-consistent architecture with three components: (1) NN1 — a forward network mapping certificates to natural language explanations; (2) NN2 — an inverse network reconstructing certificates from explanations; (3) a symbolic verifier closing the loop as a differentiable faithfulness proxy. A pointer-generator mechanism copies state names directly from certificates for lexical grounding. A hybrid inference-time routing strategy further improves performance.

Result: On 420 test certificates across six verification methods (YES/NO variants) from a financial compliance domain with 207 named states, the system achieves 90.0% cycle-verified soundness, outperforming the best multi-LLM few-shot baseline (76.1%) by 13.9 percentage points. It wins on 10 of 12 verdict/kind categories, with three reaching 100% soundness. Inference is 860x faster (185 ms vs. 160 s), supports offline operation, and yields deterministic outputs with zero per-inference cost.

Conclusion: Trained specialization outperforms general-purpose LLM prompting for structured certificate explanation, while eliminating cloud-based deployment constraints. The cycle-consistency framework provides a reliable faithfulness guarantee absent in pure LLM approaches.

Abstract: Formal verification produces machine-checkable certificates that attest to the satisfaction or violation of temporal properties, yet these certificates remain opaque to non-specialist stakeholders. We propose a cycle-consistent neural architecture that generates faithful natural language explanations of verification certificates. A forward network NN1 maps certificates to explanations, and an inverse network NN2 reconstructs certificates from explanations; a symbolic verifier closes the loop, providing a differentiable faithfulness proxy. A pointer-generator mechanism ensures lexical grounding by copying state names directly from the certificate. We evaluate on 420 test certificates spanning six verification methods (bounded proof, k-induction, inductive invariant, lasso, reachability, witness pair) in both YES and NO verdict variants, drawn from a financial compliance domain with 207 named states. Our trained architecture, combined with a hybrid inference-time routing strategy, achieves 90.0% cycle-verified soundness, surpassing a multi- LLM few-shot baseline (76.1% for the best of 16 LLM combinations across four frontier models) by 13.9 percentage points. The neural model wins on 10 of 12 verdict/kind categories, with three categories reaching 100% soundness. The architecture offers 860x faster inference (185 ms vs. 160 s per certificate for the full multi-LLM baseline), offline operation, deterministic outputs, and zero per-inference cost. These results demonstrate that trained specialization outperforms general-purpose LLM prompting for structured certificate explanation, while eliminating the deployment constraints of cloud-based inference.

</details>


### [202] [Agentic AI for Bilevel Long-Term Optimization of Policy-Driven Physical Layer Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24416)
*Bingnan Xiao,Chenhao Yang,Wei Ni,Xin Wang,Tony Q. S. Quek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic-LTPO is a nested bilevel optimization framework that uses agentic AI to adaptively configure physical-layer optimization problems in response to evolving network policies and constraints, demonstrated via a cell-free MIMO beamforming use case with a 57.2% long-term performance improvement over traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing network optimization methods assume fixed objectives and constraints, making them ineffective under dynamic operator policies, changing service requirements, and stringent real-time constraints. A framework is needed that can adaptively reconfigure optimization problems as policies evolve.

Method: Agentic-LTPO employs a nested bilevel optimization structure: the upper level uses agentic AI (with retrieval-augmented experience-based verification in a multi-agent decision process) to translate operator policies, environment summaries, and historical experiences into structured lower-level optimization problem configurations; the lower level solves these configured problems for real-time physical-layer decisions (e.g., a closed-form beamformer for cell-free MIMO).

Result: Agentic-LTPO demonstrates strong adaptability to dynamic operator policies and achieves a 57.2% improvement in long-term system performance compared to traditional methods in cell-free MIMO beamforming experiments.

Conclusion: The agentic AI-driven bilevel optimization approach effectively handles evolving policies and real-time constraints, significantly outperforming static-configuration methods and enabling adaptive physical-layer optimization.
