Abstract: Network operators' changing policies, service requirements, and stringent real-time constraints render existing methods designed with fixed objectives and constraints ineffective. This paper presents Agentic long-term performance optimization (Agentic-LTPO), a nested bilevel optimization framework that can be applied to adaptive physical layer problem configuration. The key idea is to employ agentic AI to generate upper-level configurations in a bilevel optimization structure, where evolving operator policies, environment summaries, and historical experiences are translated into structured lower-level optimization problem configurations. The lower level solves the problems with updated configurations for real-time physical-layer decisions. Considering cell-free MIMO beamforming as a use case, we embody Agentic-LTPO by designing a new multi-agent decision process with retrieval-augmented experience-based verification in the upper level, together with a closed-form beamformer in the lower level. Experiments demonstrate that Agentic-LTPO exhibits strong adaptability to dynamic operator policies and effectively enhances the system's long-term performance by 57.2% compared to traditional methods.

</details>


### [203] [Can Aggregate Invariants Accelerate Continuous Subgraph Matching? Limits, Laws, and a Dynamic Spectral Index](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24421)
*Minghao Chen,Jiale Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether Laplacian interlacing-based spectral pruning, effective for static subgraph matching, can accelerate continuous subgraph matching (CSM) over dynamic graphs. It finds that lazily maintained spectral bounds lose pruning power rapidly under updates, but selective exact recomputation on small-neighborhood vertices sustains effective pruning at low cost. Integrated into CSM systems, spectral tests remove up to 51% of candidates and skip up to 47% of update enumerations, with a methodology for evaluating CSM filters and a reusable dynamic local-spectra index released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spectral filtering via Laplacian interlacing has shown substantial pruning effectiveness for static subgraph matching. The authors seek to determine whether such aggregate structural tests can similarly accelerate continuous subgraph matching over dynamic graphs, where the graph undergoes frequent updates and matching must be maintained incrementally.

Method: The work proceeds in three parts: (1) a formal analysis of perturbation relaxation showing that lazily maintained spectral bounds lose virtually all pruning power within four touching updates, characterizing the tightest safe rule; (2) demonstrating that exact spectral maintenance is viable when applied selectively — pruning utility and recomputation cost are anti-correlated across vertices, with hubs never pruning, so recomputing spectra only for small-neighborhood vertices upon touch sustains exact local spectra at microsecond-per-update cost; (3) integration into a decoupled CSM benchmark with an identical-minus-spectra control, evaluated across two engines, four real graphs, two stream types, and 77 solved queries, plus a constructed radius-stratified workload.

Result: The spectral tests remove up to 51% of candidates or safely skip up to 47% of update enumerations. Enumeration intermediates remain unchanged beyond the gates' skipped first-level bindings (typically zero). On a constructed radius-stratified workload, the method detects the exception when one exists, achieving a 99.9% reduction in intermediates and a 748× speedup. Aggregate tests accelerate only what scales with candidate sets (construction, list scans), never adjacency-guided exploration.

Conclusion: Aggregate structural tests like spectral filtering can accelerate CSM when maintained selectively and exactly on small-neighborhood vertices, but their benefit is confined to candidate-set-scale operations. The authors distill an intermediate-invariance methodology for evaluating CSM filters and release a reusable dynamic local-spectra index.

Abstract: Spectral filtering recently delivered substantial pruning for \emph{static} subgraph matching: Laplacian interlacing rejects candidates whose neighborhoods cannot host the query. We study whether such aggregate structural tests can accelerate \emph{continuous} subgraph matching (CSM) over dynamic graphs, and answer in three parts. First, lazily maintained spectral bounds are infeasible exactly where spectral pruning has value: we characterize the tightest safe rule over a formalized perturbation relaxation and show that even it loses essentially all pruning power within four touching updates. Second, exact maintenance is affordable when selective: pruning utility and recomputation cost are anti-correlated across vertices -- hubs provably never prune -- so recomputing small-neighborhood spectra on touch sustains exact local spectra at microseconds per update, complete by construction. Third, integrated into a decoupled CSM benchmark against an identical-minus-spectra control, the tests remove up to $51\%$ of candidates or safely skip up to $47\%$ of update enumerations, yet enumeration intermediates remain unchanged -- beyond the gates' skipped first-level bindings, typically zero -- across two engines, four real graphs, two stream types, and $77$ solved queries; a constructed radius-stratified workload confirms the instrument detects the exception when one exists ($-99.9\%$ intermediates, $748\times$ faster). Aggregate tests accelerate what scales with candidate sets -- construction, list scans -- never adjacency-guided exploration. We distill an intermediate-invariance methodology for evaluating CSM filters and release a reusable dynamic local-spectra index.

</details>


### [204] [ReM-MoA: Reasoning Memory Sustains Mixture-of-Agents Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24437)
*Heng Ping,Arijit Bhattacharjee,Peiyu Zhang,Shixuan Li,Wei Yang,Ali Jannesari,Nesreen Ahmed,Paul Bogdan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReM-MoA introduces a memory-augmented Mixture-of-Agents framework with ranked cross-layer reasoning memory and diversified memory routing, enabling sustained inference-time scaling gains as depth increases, outperforming prior MoA variants across five reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MoA architectures suffer from degradation, early plateauing, or saturation when scaling depth, failing to sustain gains from additional layers of LLM agents. The authors identify the absence of structured cross-layer reasoning memory as the key missing mechanism.

Method: ReM-MoA comprises two core mechanisms: (1) a Ranked Reasoning Memory that persistently stores reasoning traces from all layers, ranked by a comparative Reviewer Agent; and (2) a Curated Diversified Memory Routing scheme that exposes agents to distinct combinations of successful and failed traces to preserve exploration diversity. An optional multi-domain Reviewer distillation pipeline using frontier-model supervision further improves ranking quality.

Result: ReM-MoA consistently outperforms prior MoA variants across both depth and width scaling on five benchmarks (math, formal logic, code, knowledge, commonsense), with its advantage widening as depth increases.

Conclusion: Structured cross-layer reasoning memory is a critical missing mechanism for scalable multi-agent inference, and ReM-MoA's memory-augmented approach successfully sustains performance scaling with depth where prior MoA methods fail.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) architectures improve inference-time scaling by organizing multiple LLM agents into layered reasoning pipelines. However, existing MoA variants fail to sustain gains as depth increases, exhibiting degradation, early plateauing, or saturation. We propose ReM-MoA, a memory-augmented MoA framework that sustains scaling through two mechanisms: (1) a Ranked Reasoning Memory that persistently stores and ranks reasoning traces from all layers using a comparative Reviewer Agent, and (2) a Curated Diversified Memory Routing scheme that exposes different agents to distinct combinations of successful and failed traces, preserving exploration diversity while propagating high-quality reasoning. We further introduce an optional multi-domain Reviewer distillation pipeline that improves ranking quality through frontier-model supervision. Across five reasoning benchmarks spanning math, formal logic, code, knowledge, and commonsense, ReM-MoA consistently outperforms prior MoA variants across both depth and width scaling, and its advantage widens with depth, establishing structured cross-layer reasoning memory as a key missing mechanism for scalable multi-agent inference.

</details>


### [205] [Bayesian control for coding agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24453)
*Theodore Papamarkou,Vladislav Smirnov,Viktor Mazanov,Artem Vazhentsev,Preslav Nakov,Timothy Baldwin,Artem Shelmanov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper frames LLM code generation orchestration as cost-sensitive sequential hypothesis testing, using a Bayesian controller that maintains a belief over candidate correctness and dynamically decides tool-use actions. It shows this approach is most valuable when verification is costly and critics are imperfect, and the belief state doubles as an interpretable correctness score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current coding agents use fixed-rule orchestrators to manage tool-use decisions (diagnostics, verifiers), ignoring uncertainty about candidate correctness. This leads to inefficient allocation of expensive verification resources and poor uncertainty quantification. There is a need for a principled, cost-aware decision-making framework.

Method: The authors formulate orchestration as cost-sensitive sequential hypothesis testing with a Bayesian controller. The controller maintains a probabilistic belief over candidate correctness (correct vs. incorrect), updated via Bayes' rule using signals from imperfect critics. It dynamically selects actions—gather more evidence, refine, verify, or stop—by optimizing an expected cost-to-go that trades off verification cost against the risk of accepting incorrect code or rejecting correct code.

Result: Evaluated across six generators (LLMs) and nine coding benchmarks, the Bayesian controller proves most valuable when (1) verification is expensive and (2) critics are informative but imperfect. The belief state also yields an interpretable correctness score that outperforms token-probability and raw tool-success baselines for uncertainty quantification.

Conclusion: Bayesian sequential decision-making provides a principled, cost-effective alternative to fixed-rule orchestration in coding agents, particularly under costly verification regimes. The dual use of the belief state for both control and uncertainty quantification is a significant practical benefit.

Abstract: Modern coding agents pair LLM generators with various tools, including cheap diagnostics and expensive verifiers. The tool-use decisions are typically governed by orchestrators that often use fixed rules and ignore uncertainty. We formulate orchestration as cost-sensitive sequential hypothesis testing: a Bayesian controller maintains a belief over candidate correctness and dynamically decides whether to gather more evidence, refine the candidate, verify it, or stop. Across six generators and nine coding benchmarks, Bayesian control proves to be most valuable when verification is costly and critics are informative but imperfect. Beyond control, the belief state yields an interpretable correctness score that outperforms token-probability and raw tool-success baselines for uncertainty quantification.

</details>


### [206] [CompressKV: Semantic-Retrieval-Guided KV-Cache Compression for Resource-Efficient Long-Context LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24467)
*Xiaolin Lin,Jingcun Wang,Olga Kondrateva,Yiyu Shi,Bing Li,Grace Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CompressKV is a KV-cache compression framework for GQA-based LLMs that identifies Semantic Retrieval Heads (SRHs) to select critical tokens for retention and allocates cache budgets across layers based on offline eviction error estimates, achieving over 97% of full-cache performance with only 3% KV cache on LongBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KV cache eviction methods apply heuristic token scoring uniformly across all attention heads in GQA-based LLMs, ignoring the functional heterogeneity of different heads. This leads to eviction of semantically critical tokens and degrades long-context inference performance.

Method: CompressKV operates in two stages: (1) It identifies Semantic Retrieval Heads (SRHs) — attention heads that capture initial/final tokens of prompts and semantically important mid-context evidence — and uses only these heads for token retention scoring. (2) It allocates cache budgets across transformer layers based on offline-computed layer-wise eviction error estimates, giving more cache to layers with higher eviction sensitivity.

Result: On LongBench, CompressKV preserves over 97% of full-cache performance using only 3% of the KV cache on QA tasks. On Needle-in-a-Haystack, it achieves 90% accuracy with just 0.7% KV storage, consistently outperforming existing eviction methods across all memory budgets.

Conclusion: CompressKV demonstrates a significantly improved resource–performance trade-off for long-context LLM inference by leveraging head-level functional specialization and layer-wise budget allocation, enabling sustainable deployment on resource-constrained hardware.

Abstract: Long-context large language model (LLM) inference is increasingly constrained by the memory footprint and decoding cost of key-value (KV) caches, limiting sustainable deployment on resource-constrained hardware. Existing KV cache eviction methods typically apply heuristic token scoring over all heads in GQA-based LLMs. These methods ignore the different functionalities of attention heads, leading to the eviction of critical tokens and thus degrading the performance of LLMs. To address this issue, we propose CompressKV, a resource-efficient KV-cache compression framework for GQA-based LLMs. Instead of aggregating attention scores from all heads, CompressKV identifies Semantic Retrieval Heads (SRHs) that capture both the initial and final tokens of a prompt and semantically important mid-context evidence, and uses them to select tokens whose KV pairs should be retained. Furthermore, CompressKV allocates cache budgets across layers according to offline estimates of layer-wise eviction error. Experiments on LongBench and Needle-in-a-Haystack show that CompressKV consistently outperforms existing KV-cache eviction methods across memory budgets. Notably, it preserves over 97\% of full-cache performance using only 3\% of the KV cache on LongBench question-answering tasks and achieves 90\% accuracy with just 0.7\% KV storage on Needle-in-a-Haystack. These results demonstrate an improved resource--performance trade-off for long-context LLM inference. Our code is publicly available at: https://github.com/TUDa-HWAI/CompressKV

</details>


### [207] [The Latent Bridge: A Continuous Slow-Fast Channel for Real-Time Game Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24470)
*Bojie Li,Noah Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Latent Bridge that couples a slow reasoning VLM with a fast reactive VLM for real-time game playing. Instead of passing text between models (Text Bridge), the Latent Bridge projects the reasoning model's residual embeddings directly into the reactive model's input space. On Atari games and MetaDrive, the Latent Bridge matches or outperforms the Text Bridge, with gains predictable by whether slow reasoning alone beats fast reaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time computer agents for games must reconcile a latency-quality tradeoff: reasoning VLMs deliberate effectively but are too slow (~1.5s/response) for high-frequency control loops (15 Hz), while reactive VLMs act fast but struggle with planning. The authors aim to combine both strengths by coupling frozen models through a learnable communication channel.

Method: Two frozen VLMs of matched scale are coupled: a 9B reactive model (MiniCPM-o 4.5) and an 8B reasoning model (Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking). Three configurations are compared: (1) Fast-Only baseline, (2) Text Bridge where the slow model writes a suffix the fast model reads, and (3) the proposed Latent Bridge, which projects the slow model's residuals into the fast model's input-embedding space in a LLaVA-style manner using a learned projector, avoiding any text round-trip. Only the action decoder is fine-tuned per communication channel on held-out seeds across 7 Atari games and MetaDrive.

Result: The Latent Bridge matches or exceeds the Text Bridge across all domains, with significant improvements on MsPacman (+57%) and RoadRunner (+28%). Combining both channels causes destructive interference (RoadRunner -96%). The benefit of bridging is highly predictable: gains over Fast-Only for Latent and Text bridges correlate at r=0.93, and bridging helps only when slow reasoning already beats fast reaction (T > F). On MetaDrive, where the Text Bridge adds no value, the Latent Bridge is demonstrably inert.

Conclusion: A learned continuous Latent Bridge is an effective and predictable drop-in replacement for text-based communication between fast and slow VLMs, enabling real-time agents to benefit from slow reasoning without incurring text-translation overhead. Only one bridging channel should be used, and the approach generalizes safely — remaining inert when it cannot help.

Abstract: A real-time agent for general computer use - with games as the most demanding case - must act within tens of milliseconds while still planning over seconds. These two regimes sit at opposite ends of the latency-quality tradeoff. A reasoning VLM (Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking) deliberates effectively but requires ~1.5 s per response - far too slow for a 15 Hz control loop. In contrast, a reactive VLM (MiniCPM-o 4.5) acts in milliseconds but underperforms on planning-heavy tasks. We couple two frozen models of matched scale (9B reactive, 8B reasoning), leaving the communication channel as the sole trainable component. The standard coupling is a Text Bridge (T): the slow model writes a suffix the fast model reads. We introduce a learned continuous Latent Bridge (L) that projects the slow model's residuals into the fast model's input-embedding space in a LLaVA-style manner, avoiding any text round-trip; both are compared against Fast-Only (F). On 7 Atari games and a driving domain (MetaDrive), tuning the action decoder per channel on held-out seeds, the Latent Bridge matches or beats the Text Bridge in every domain: it significantly improves two games (MsPacman +57%, RoadRunner +28%) and is a safe drop-in elsewhere. Combining both channels interferes destructively (RoadRunner -96%), so only one should be used. The benefit is highly predictable: the bridge helps if and only if slow reasoning already beats fast reaction (T > F) - the Latent and Text gains over Fast-Only move together at r=0.93. MetaDrive is the controlled negative, where the Latent Bridge is demonstrably inert because the Text Bridge adds no value. We release replay recordings and reproducible pipelines.

</details>


### [208] [On the Smallness of the Large Language Models Scaling Exponents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24504)
*Sauro Succi,Peter V. Coveney,Alex Hansen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that scaling exponents in current LLM applications signal an unsustainable energy resource trajectory. It refutes the claim that this unsustainability stems from a numerical bias (pedestal effect) from neglecting non-zero infinite-data loss, and draws an analogy between data smoothness/roughness and fluid turbulence phenomenology to comment on scaling exponents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To critically examine the energy sustainability of scaling laws in large language models and to evaluate whether proposed explanations (such as the pedestal effect) genuinely resolve the unsustainability concern, thereby contributing to the broader discourse on the resource implications of scaling AI systems.

Method: The paper employs theoretical and phenomenological analysis: (1) it examines scaling exponents from current LLM applications and their energy resource implications; (2) it analytically addresses the "pedestal effect" — the claim that neglecting a non-zero asymptotic loss value introduces a numerical bias that makes exponents appear smaller — and demonstrates that correcting for this bias does not eliminate the sustainability problem; and (3) it draws an analogy with phenomenological models of fluid turbulence to qualitatively discuss how the smoothness or roughness of the data manifold may influence scaling exponents.

Result: The analysis yields three main findings: (1) current LLM scaling exponents indeed point to an unsustainable energy resource regime; (2) the pedestal effect, even when properly accounted for, does not resolve the unsustainability — the scaling exponents remain in a regime requiring prohibitive energy; (3) the smoothness (or roughness) of the underlying data distribution may affect scaling behavior, analogous to how surface roughness affects energy dissipation scaling in turbulent flows.

Conclusion: The unsustainability of LLM scaling is robust to corrections for the pedestal effect. The data manifold's geometric properties (smoothness/roughness) may offer additional explanatory power for observed scaling behaviors, meriting further investigation. The work underscores the urgency of developing more resource-efficient approaches to language model scaling.

Abstract: We discuss reasons why the scaling exponents of current Large Language Models (LLMs) applications are indicating an unsustainable regime in terms of energy resources. We further show that attributing the smallness of such exponents to a numerical bias due to the neglect of a non-zero value of the loss function in the limit of infinite data (``pedestal effect") does not remove the unsustainability issue. Finally, the effects of the smoothness (roughness) of the data on the scaling exponents is commented upon based on an analogy with phenomenological models of fluid turbulence.

</details>


### [209] [A specialized reasoning large language model for accelerating rare disease diagnosis: a randomized AI physician assistance trial](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24510)
*Haichao Chen,Songchi Zhou,Zhengyun Zhao,Shikai Hu,Xianghong Jin,Hongwei Ji,Li He,Shuli Li,Yiming Qin,Xin Tan,Runfeng Shi,Yih Chung Tham,Jiaye Zhu,Ye Li,Ye Jin,Longhao Cao,Dawei Li,Honghan Wu,Hongqiu Gu,Guanqiao Li,Tudor Groza,Chunying Li,Dian Zeng,Weihong Yu,Gareth Baynam,Saumya Shekhar Jamuar,Min Shen,Shuyang Zhang,Bin Sheng,Sheng Yu,Tien Yin Wong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RaDaR is a compact (32B), open-source reasoning LLM for rare disease diagnosis, trained on public and synthetic cases. It outperforms larger models on benchmarks, accelerates diagnosis by ~1.87 months in retrospective analysis, and improves physician accuracy by 21.44% in a randomized trial. Synthetic phenotype-anchored narratives prove effective for long-tail rare disease training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare disease diagnosis faces a critical bottleneck: millions are affected worldwide, yet timely diagnosis is hampered by a shortage of specialized clinicians. Existing LLMs are limited by poor clinical deployability, insufficient clinical grounding, and data scarcity. There is a need for an open, reproducible, and clinically useful AI that can reason through rare disease cases despite limited training data.

Method: The authors developed RaDaR, a 32B-parameter reasoning LLM. Training data comprised 49,170 publicly available free-text clinical cases and 104,666 synthetic cases generated with reasoning-enhanced training. Evaluation spanned public benchmarks, four external validation centers, a retrospective cohort study (measuring diagnostic lead time), and a randomized physician-assistance trial comparing RaDaR-assisted diagnosis vs. internet search alone. Synthetic-data ablation studies tested the contribution of phenotype-anchored narratives across the long tail of rare diseases, examining scaling trends.

Result: RaDaR achieved top performance among open-source models (including DeepSeek-R1 671B) on public benchmarks and across four external centers. In the retrospective cohort, RaDaR flagged the final diagnosis before clinical suspicion in 61.06% of cases, yielding a potential lead time of 1.87 months (50.18% of the within-center interval). In the physician trial, RaDaR assistance boosted diagnostic accuracy by 21.44 percentage points over internet search. Synthetic-data ablations confirmed that phenotype-anchored narratives provide useful training signal, with monotonic performance improvement as synthetic data scaled.

Conclusion: RaDaR, paired with its development and validation framework, delivers a deployable rare-disease reasoning model and a reproducible methodology for building diagnostic AI under data scarcity. The work demonstrates that compact, reasoning-augmented LLMs trained with synthetic phenotype-anchored data can meaningfully accelerate and improve rare disease diagnosis in real clinical settings.

Abstract: Rare diseases affect millions of individuals worldwide, yet timely diagnosis remains a major public health challenge due to scarcity of specialized clinical expertise. While large language models (LLMs) show promise to support rare disease diagnosis, current models are constrained by insufficient clinical deployability, limited clinically grounded evidence, and scarcity of training data. Here we present RaDaR (Rare Disease navigatoR), an open-source, compact reasoning LLM (32B parameters) for rare disease diagnosis. RaDaR was trained with 49,170 publicly available free-text cases and 104,666 synthetic cases with reasoning-enhanced training. RaDaR showed the strongest performance among evaluated open-source models, including the 671B DeepSeek-R1, across public benchmarks and four external validation centers. In a retrospective cohort, RaDaR prioritized the final diagnosis before documented clinical suspicion in 61.06 percent of cases, corresponding to a potential lead time of 1.87 months and 50.18 percent of the within-center interval. In a randomized physician-assistance trial, RaDaR assistance improved physicians' rare-disease diagnostic accuracy by 21.44 percentage points compared with internet search alone. Synthetic-data ablations suggested that phenotype-anchored narratives provide useful training signal for long-tail rare diseases, with a monotonic scaling trend within the tested data range. Together, RaDaR and its development and validation framework provide a deployable rare-disease reasoning model and a reproducible development framework for diagnostic AI under data scarcity.

</details>


### [210] [Governed Shared Memory for Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24535)
*Yanki Margalit,Nurit Cohen-Inger,Erni Avram,Ran Taig,Oded Margalit*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper formalizes the fleet-memory problem in multi-agent LLM systems, identifies four failure modes, proposes four systems-level primitives, implements them in MemClaw, and evaluates via ArgusFleet — revealing that long-context retrieval alone is insufficient and live production testing exposes critical enforcement and pipeline-ordering failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM environments lack robust shared knowledge management mechanisms. Existing approaches relying solely on long-context retrieval fail to address fundamental coordination challenges in production settings, necessitating explicit governance primitives for fleet-wide memory.

Method: Formalizes four failure modes (unauthorized leakage, stale propagation, contradiction persistence, provenance collapse) and defines corresponding systems-level primitives: scoped retrieval, temporal supersession, provenance tracking, and policy-governed memory propagation. These are implemented in MemClaw, a production multi-tenant memory service, and evaluated through ArgusFleet, a reproducible harness testing four governance dimensions on live production traffic rather than synthetic baselines.

Result: Provenance tracking reconstructed 100% of depth-four derivation chains with correct writer identity at sub-second per-hop latency. Propagation showed high intra-fleet visibility with zero cross-fleet leakage; strong write mode achieved write-to-visible latency of a single search round-trip. Two critical production issues were discovered: asymmetric scope enforcement (sub-tenant scope bypassed on direct GET-by-id for agent-scoped credentials) and a pipeline ordering conflict where a synchronous near-duplicate gate prematurely rejects contradictory writes before the async contradiction detector evaluates them.

Conclusion: Long-context retrieval alone is insufficient for production multi-agent memory. Governed shared memory requires explicit systems-level abstractions (scoped retrieval, temporal supersession, provenance tracking, policy-governed propagation), and live production evaluation is essential to uncover enforcement and pipeline-ordering failures that design-only approaches miss.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM environments require robust mechanisms for shared knowledge management. This paper formalizes the fleet-memory problem and identifies four foundational failure modes: unauthorized leakage, stale propagation, contradiction persistence, and provenance collapse. To address these, we define explicit systems-level primitives: scoped retrieval, temporal supersession, provenance tracking, and policy-governed memory propagation. These primitives are implemented in MemClaw, a production multi-tenant memory service, and evaluated via ArgusFleet, a reproducible harness testing four governance dimensions. Rather than a baseline comparison, this study measures a live production service, emphasizing real-world architectural insights and negative results. Key Evaluation Results Provenance: Successfully reconstructed 100% of depth-four derivation chains with correct writer identity at sub-second per-hop latency. Propagation: Demonstrated high intra-fleet visibility with zero cross-fleet leakage. Under strong write mode, write-to-visible latency was optimized to a single search round-trip. Production Architectural Issues Discovered Asymmetric Scope Enforcement: Tenant isolation held, but sub-tenant scope was initially bypassed on direct GET-by-id requests for agent-scoped credentials (disclosed and remediated during the study). Pipeline Ordering Conflict: While contradiction supersession works for admitted writes, a synchronous near-duplicate gate can prematurely reject contradictory writes before the asynchronous contradiction detector can evaluate them. Conclusion: Long-context retrieval alone is insufficient for production multi-agent memory. Governed shared memory demands explicit systems-level abstractions, and live evaluation is vital to expose enforcement and pipeline-ordering failures missed by design-only treatments.

</details>


### [211] [GUI vs. CLI: Execution Bottlenecks in Screen-Only and Skill-Mediated Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24551)
*Xiao Zhou,Siyue Zhang,Yilun Zhao,Jinbiao Wei,Tingyu Song,Arman Cohan,Chen Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A matched benchmark of 440 desktop tasks reveals GUI agents outperform CLI agents (59.1% vs 48.2%), but adding verifier-guided skill augmentation raises CLI to 69.3%, indicating CLI deficits stem from incomplete skill coverage rather than model capability. The two modalities expose distinct bottlenecks: GUI struggles with reliable long-horizon grounded interaction, while CLI is limited by skill interface coverage and scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of computer-use agents confound interaction modality (GUI vs. CLI) with differences in tasks, initial states, verifiers, and permitted actions, making it impossible to meaningfully compare agent performance across modalities. The authors seek to isolate modality as the independent variable.

Method: The authors introduce a matched execution-layer benchmark comprising 440 desktop tasks spanning 18 applications and 12 workflow categories. Screen-only GUI agents and skill-mediated CLI agents are given identical goals, initial states, and final-state verifiers, and are restricted to modality-native actions (GUI: mouse/keyboard; CLI: skill invocation). They further evaluate verifier-guided skill augmentation for CLI agents.

Result: In the controlled setting, the strongest GUI agent achieves a 59.1% full pass rate, outperforming the strongest original-skill CLI agent at 48.2%. However, verifier-guided skill augmentation raises CLI success to 69.3%, demonstrating that the CLI performance gap is largely attributable to incomplete skill coverage rather than underlying model capability.

Conclusion: GUI and CLI modalities present fundamentally different execution bottlenecks: GUI agents are constrained by reliable grounded interaction over long-horizon workflows, while CLI agents are constrained by the coverage and scalability of their available skill interfaces. Improving either modality requires targeting these distinct failure modes.

Abstract: Computer-use agents can execute software tasks through either graphical interfaces or programmatic command interfaces, but existing evaluations confound interaction modality with differences in tasks, initial states, verifiers, and permitted actions. We introduce a matched execution-layer benchmark of 440 desktop tasks across 18 applications and 12 workflow categories, where screen-only GUI agents and skill-mediated CLI agents receive identical goals, states, and final-state verifiers while being restricted to modality-native actions. In this controlled setting, the strongest GUI agent reaches a 59.1% full pass rate, outperforming the strongest original-skill CLI agent at 48.2%; however, verifier-guided skill augmentation raises CLI success to 69.3%, showing that much of the CLI deficit comes from incomplete skill coverage rather than model capability alone. These results suggest that GUI and CLI expose different execution bottlenecks: GUI agents are limited by reliable grounded interaction over long-horizon workflows, whereas CLI agents are limited by the coverage and scalability of their skill interfaces.

</details>


### [212] [Quant Convergence: Bridging Classical Value Investing and Modern Factor Models for Systematic Equity Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24575)
*Augusto Eiji Yamazaki,Hugo Garrido-Lestache Belinchon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper tests whether Benjamin Graham's value investing principles can serve as a stabilizing "low-pass filter" for modern machine learning models in stock selection. Using 20 years of S&P 500 data, three feature sets (pure Graham, modern market factors, combined) were tested with XGBoost, AutoGluon, and Random Forest models under a strict buy-and-hold strategy. Results show that complex models don't always win: the pure Graham Random Forest achieved the highest return (232.13%) with superior risk-adjusted performance (1.38 Calmar Ratio), while the Combined Random Forest posted the lowest maximum drawdown (34.53%). Graham's "margin of safety" framework effectively curbs AI overfitting to short-term noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern machine learning models in finance increasingly memorize short-term market noise rather than identifying companies with enduring intrinsic value. The authors hypothesize that Benjamin Graham's classic value investing rules could mathematically function as a "low-pass filter" — attenuating high-frequency market noise while preserving the low-frequency signal of genuine value — thereby preventing overfitting and excessive risk-taking in AI-driven investment strategies.

Method: The authors constructed three feature sets: (1) pure Graham rules based on classic value investing criteria, (2) modern market factors, and (3) a combination of both. These were evaluated using three model architectures — XGBoost, AutoGluon (automated deep learning), and Random Forest — trained on 20 years of S&P 500 data. Performance was assessed under a strict buy-and-hold strategy over a four-year test period (March 2022 to March 2026), with metrics including total return, maximum drawdown, and Calmar Ratio.

Result: The pure Graham Random Forest delivered the best overall performance: 232.13% return with a 1.38 Calmar Ratio. AutoGluon, despite high returns (222.68%), suffered a 39.78% drawdown due to over-concentration in volatile tech stocks prior to a market crash. The Combined Random Forest—integrating momentum factors with Graham rules—returned 202.91% while achieving the lowest maximum drawdown (34.53%) among all tested models, demonstrating effective risk mitigation through hybrid feature engineering.

Conclusion: Graham's "margin of safety" principle is not obsolete; it remains a highly effective mechanism for constraining modern AI models from assuming excessive risk. Simpler models grounded in value-investing discipline can outperform or match more complex architectures while preserving capital during downturns. The research validates that Graham's framework functions as a practical regularization strategy against overfitting in financial machine learning.

Abstract: Modern finance relies heavily on complex machine learning models to find patterns in the stock market. However, as these AI models get more complicated, they often memorize short-term market noise instead of finding companies with real, lasting value. We designed this research to test if Benjamin Graham's classic value investing rules could act as a mathematical "low-pass filter" to keep these modern models in check. We built three different sets of features - pure Graham rules, modern market factors, and a mix of both - and tested them against highly complex models (XGBoost and AutoGluon) using 20 years of S&P 500 data. By applying a strict buy-and-hold strategy over a four-year test period (March 2022 to March 2026), the results showed that more complex algorithms do not always win. While the AutoGluon model captured high returns (222.68%), it suffered a substantial 39.78% drop because it bought volatile tech stocks right before the market crashed. On the other hand, the pure Graham Random Forest achieved the highest overall return (232.13%) with much less risk (1.38 Calmar Ratio). Furthermore, the Combined Random Forest successfully mixed momentum with Graham's rules, making a 202.91% return while keeping the lowest maximum drop (34.53%) of any model tested. Ultimately, this research proves that Graham's "margin of safety" isn't outdated; it is actually a highly effective way to prevent modern AI from taking on too much risk.

</details>


### [213] [LLMs Prompted for Legal Context Object More: Overrefusal from Small On-Premises LLMs in Criminal Legal Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24585)
*Anastasiia Kucherenko,François Brouchoud,Dimitri Percia David,Andrei Kucharavy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Small, on-device LLMs exhibit dramatically increased refusal rates (2–20×) when prompted with authority-style prefixes (e.g., "acting as an assistant of the national supreme court"), compared to a no-prefix baseline. A known role-play jailbreak prefix yields inconsistent effects across models. This instability under realistic institutional framings raises concerns about selective assistance bias in legal applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legal professionals are already using personal LLMs for tasks like translation and reformulation, but even these low-stakes uses can introduce bias if the model selectively refuses to assist on certain topics. The authors aim to anticipate such biases by investigating how "overrefusal" manifests in small, on-device-deployable LLMs when handling legal prompts.

Method: The authors tested several modern small LLMs suitable for on-device deployment, comparing refusal rates across three prompt conditions: (1) a no-prefix baseline, (2) authority-style prefixes (e.g., "you are acting as an assistant of the national supreme court" or "defense lawyer"), and (3) a known role-play jailbreak prefix. Refusal rates were measured to quantify the impact of contextual framing on model responsiveness.

Result: Authority-style prefixes systematically increased refusal rates by 2–20× over the no-prefix baseline. The role-play jailbreak prefix produced mixed outcomes: sharply increased refusals in some models, negligible shifts in others. This indicates high instability in small LLMs under institutional contextual framings that real users might naturally employ.

Conclusion: Small, on-premises-deployable LLMs are fragile under context shifts that institutional users would plausibly introduce. Further investigation is essential to mitigate the risk of selective-assistance bias, even for seemingly innocuous legal use cases such as translation and reformulation.

Abstract: While the validity of LLMs' use in the legal context remains subject to ethical and legal debate, legal professionals are already experimenting with personal LLMs, if only for translation and reformulation. However, even such a seemingly innocuous use can introduce biases through case processing speed if LLM assistants selectively refuse assistance on certain topics. To better anticipate such biases, we investigate several modern small LLMs that are most likely to be used as on-device assistants, to assess the impact of overrefusal on legal prompts.
  Surprisingly, we find that authority-style prefixes (``you are acting as an assistant of the national supreme court'', ``[...] defense lawyer'') systematically increase refusal rates by 2--20x over the no-prefix baseline, while a known role-play jailbreak prefix shows mixed effects, sharply increasing refusals in some models and barely shifting them in others. The finding suggests that small on-prem deployable LLMs are unstable under contextual framings that a real institutional user might naturally introduce, and further investigation is essential to minimize opportunities for bias.

</details>


### [214] [AdversaBench: Automated LLM Red-Teaming with Multi-Judge Confirmation and Cross-Model Transferability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24589)
*Khanak Khandelwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdversaBench is a red-teaming pipeline that mutates seed prompts using five structured operators, queries a target model, and confirms failures via a three-judge panel with meta-judge tiebreaker. Experiments across reasoning, instruction-following, and tool-use categories show every seed produced confirmed failures, with operator effectiveness varying sharply by category, binary failure rates masking difficulty differences, judge agreement paradoxes, and successful zero-shot transfer from Llama 3.1 8B to Llama 3.3 70B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling adversarial evaluation of LLMs requires both generating hard inputs and reliably confirming failures. Existing approaches lack a systematic, end-to-end pipeline that combines structured mutation operators with robust failure verification.

Method: AdversaBench uses five structured mutation operators to perturb seed prompts, queries a target model, and confirms failures through a three-judge panel with a meta-judge tiebreaker. Experiments span 45 seeds across reasoning, instruction-following, and tool-use categories.

Result: (1) Operator effectiveness varies by category: inject_distractor scores 0.00 on instruction-following but 0.80–0.83 on reasoning/tool-use. (2) Instruction-following seeds required 2.4 attacker iterations vs. 1.1 for other categories. (3) Pairwise judge agreement of 80–87% coexists with near-zero Cohen's kappa due to label skew. (4) Adversarial prompts transfer zero-shot from Llama 3.1 8B to Llama 3.3 70B, indicating exploitation of general behavioral patterns.

Conclusion: Every seed produced a confirmed failure, demonstrating the pipeline's effectiveness. The findings highlight that operator selection should be category-aware, survival curves better capture attack difficulty than binary failure rates, judge agreement metrics must account for label skew, and adversarial mutations exploit general rather than model-specific weaknesses.

Abstract: Scaling adversarial evaluation of large language models requires both a method for generating hard inputs and a reliable way to confirm that resulting failures are real. We present AdversaBench, an end-to-end red-teaming pipeline that mutates seed prompts with five structured operators, queries a target model, and confirms failures through a three-judge panel with a meta-judge tiebreaker. We report experiments on 45 seeds across three categories: reasoning, instruction-following, and tool use. Every seed produced a confirmed failure. Four findings stand out. First, operator effectiveness varies sharply by category: inject_distractor scores 0.00 mean reward on instruction-following seeds but 0.80-0.83 on reasoning and tool-use. Second, binary failure rate hides difficulty: instruction-following seeds required 2.4 attacker iterations on average versus 1.1 for other categories, a gap visible in survival curves. Third, pairwise judge agreement of 80-87% coexists with near-zero Cohen's kappa due to label skew; category-level disagreement rates are more informative. Fourth, adversarial prompts generated against Llama 3.1 8B transfer zero-shot to Llama 3.3 70B, suggesting the mutations exploit general behavioral patterns rather than model-specific weaknesses. Code, dataset, and analysis scripts are available at https://github.com/khanak0509/AdversaBench .

</details>


### [215] [ASALT: Adaptive State Alignment for Lateral Transfer in Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24601)
*Anurag Akula,Satheesh K. Perepu,Abhishek Sarkar,Kaushik Dey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASALT is a multi-agent transfer learning method that handles mismatched state-space dimensionalities between source and target domains using observation-level and state-level adapters to map into a shared embedding space, improving sample efficiency and returns while mitigating negative transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MARL transfer learning methods impose the restrictive constraint that observation and global state space dimensionalities must be identical across domains, limiting practical applicability when source and target environments have heterogeneous state representations.

Method: ASALT introduces two adapter modules — observation-level adapters and state-level adapters — that map target-domain observations and global states into a shared embedding space aligned with the source domain. This enables knowledge transfer across both actors and critics despite mismatched dimensionalities, supporting strategy transfer across heterogeneous domains.

Result: In standard cooperative MARL benchmarks under multiple configurations, ASALT outperforms baselines in sample efficiency and global return, though effectiveness correlates with the degree of source–target domain mismatch. ASALT also mitigates negative transfer, a common failure mode in cross-domain policy transfer.

Conclusion: ASALT demonstrates that adapter-based embedding alignment is a viable and effective approach for cross-domain MARL transfer with mismatched state spaces, reducing negative transfer and improving learning efficiency in cooperative settings.

Abstract: Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) addresses the problem of training multiple agents that pursue collaborative, competitive, or mixed objectives. Prior work has investigated transfer learning between source and target domains in MARL; however, the majority of existing approaches impose the constraint that the dimensionalities of the observation space and the global state space must be identical across domains. In this paper, we introduce a method that explicitly accommodates mismatched state-space dimensionalities between source and target domains. The proposed approach, ASALT, incorporates both observation-level and state-level adapters that map the target-domain observations and global states into a shared embedding space, thereby enabling more effective transfer of knowledge across both actors and critics. These adapters can generate embeddings that support efficient strategy transfer across heterogeneous domains. Experimental results on multiple configurations in standard benchmark environments demonstrate that ASALT surpasses existing baselines in terms of sample efficiency and global return in cooperative settings, but its effectiveness depends on the degree of mismatch between source and target domains. Furthermore, our findings indicate that ASALT mitigates negative transfer, which frequently constitutes a major obstacle when transferring policies between domains with differing observation and action spaces.

</details>


### [216] [Uncertainty-Aware Longitudinal Forecasting of Alzheimer's Disease Progression Using Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24604)
*Arya Hariharan,Shreyank N Gowda,Anala M R*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A probabilistic framework using a Temporal Fusion Transformer with CORAL ordinal output and Mixture Density Network generates 5-year Alzheimer's disease progression trajectories with decomposed aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty, outperforming baselines on MCI-to-dementia prediction with near-nominal 90% credible interval coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning approaches to AD progression reduce the problem to single-step classification with flat diagnostic categories, failing to capture how patients evolve over multiple visits or how forecast uncertainty accumulates across the prediction horizon.

Method: A Temporal Fusion Transformer encoder is adapted with (1) a CORAL ordinal output layer for respecting disease-stage ordering, (2) asymmetric loss weighting and converter oversampling for MCI-to-dementia sensitivity, (3) an autoregressive Mixture Density Network for 5-year probabilistic trajectory generation across four outcomes (diagnosis state, CDR-SB, MMSE orientation, hippocampal volume), and (4) decomposed uncertainty estimation using analytic mixture variance (aleatoric) and a five-member bootstrap ensemble (epistemic). External validation is performed on OASIS-3.

Result: The model outperforms linear, recurrent, and transformer baselines on next-visit diagnosis prediction, with strongest gains on MCI-vs-dementia discrimination. Generated trajectories achieve near-nominal 90% credible interval coverage with widening uncertainty over the horizon. Epistemic uncertainty is elevated for rare progression archetypes, MCI/dementia patients, and under external evaluation on OASIS-3 where it correlates with increased prediction error.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides clinically meaningful longitudinal AD progression forecasts with well-calibrated and interpretable uncertainty decomposition, where epistemic uncertainty reliably signals distribution shift under external validation, offering a more trustworthy tool for patient monitoring and clinical decision-making.

Abstract: Longitudinal modelling of Alzheimer's disease progression is clinically useful only if it can describe not just the most likely next diagnosis, but how a patient may evolve over time and how reliable that forecast is. Most deep learning approaches reduce this problem to single-step classification, treating cognitively normal, mild cognitive impairment, and dementia as flat categories while providing limited insight into how uncertainty accumulates across future visits. We propose a probabilistic framework that combines ordinal diagnosis prediction, multi-horizon trajectory generation, and decomposed uncertainty estimation. A Temporal Fusion Transformer encoder is adapted with a CORAL ordinal output layer, asymmetric loss weighting, and converter oversampling to respect disease-stage ordering and improve sensitivity to MCI-to-dementia transitions. Conditioned on the learned patient-context representation, an autoregressive Mixture Density Network generates five-year probabilistic trajectories for diagnosis state, CDR Sum of Boxes, MMSE orientation, and hippocampal volume. On ADNI, the model outperforms linear, recurrent, and transformer baselines for next-visit diagnosis prediction, with the strongest gains on MCI-versus-dementia discrimination. Generated trajectories achieve near-nominal 90% credible interval coverage, widening uncertainty across the forecast horizon, and biomarker dynamics consistent with expected Alzheimer's disease progression. We further separate aleatoric from epistemic uncertainty using analytic mixture variance and a five-member bootstrap ensemble, which provides the strongest encoder diversity and output-level epistemic signal. Epistemic uncertainty is higher for rare progression archetypes, MCI and dementia patients, and under external evaluation on OASIS-3, where it increases alongside prediction error.

</details>


### [217] [ScaleToT: Generalizing Structured LLM Reasoning for Billion-Scale Low-Activity User Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24605)
*Tianbao Ma,Chang Xi,Yichuan Zou,Chengen Li,Linxun Chen,Zilong Lu,Yanan Niu,Zhaojie Liu,Han Li,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ScaleToT uses a small LLM-processed subset to learn structured reasoning (entropy-guided Tree-of-Thought) for user-state inference from sparse profiles, then distills this into a lightweight encoder for billion-scale low-activity users, achieving a 6.738% LTV lift while reasoning over only 7.32% of the population.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate user modeling requires rich interaction histories, but billions of low-activity users lack such data. LLMs can infer latent states from static profiles, yet reasoning becomes unreliable with sparse profiles, and applying LLMs at billion-scale is prohibitively expensive.

Method: ScaleToT operates in three stages: (1) A small subset of users is processed by an LLM using an entropy-guided Tree-of-Thought (ToT) refinement procedure to construct typed user-state chains with bounded reasoning; (2) These teacher-curated chains train a student model on static profiles via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Outcome-Driven Segment-Aware Implicit Reward Policy Optimization (OSIPO); (3) The student's reasoning representations are transferred to a lightweight profile encoder, providing shared reasoning signals for the remaining billions of users without LLM inference.

Result: In a randomized online A/B test on lifetime value (LTV) prediction in a billion-scale advertising deployment, ScaleToT increased LT30 by 6.738%. Offline reasoning covered only 7.32% of the potential population, drastically reducing compute cost versus full-population reasoning.

Conclusion: ScaleToT demonstrates that structured LLM reasoning can be effectively distilled and transferred to a lightweight encoder, enabling scalable, reliable user-state inference for low-activity populations at a fraction of the computational cost of applying LLMs directly to all users.

Abstract: Accurate user modeling often depends on rich interaction histories, which are unavailable for billions of low-activity users. Large Language Models (LLMs) can infer latent user states from static profiles, but this reasoning becomes unreliable when profiles are sparse, and applying an LLM to billions of users is prohibitively expensive. We present ScaleToT, which learns structured reasoning from a small LLM-processed subset and extends it to the broader low-activity user population. To improve reasoning reliability, ScaleToT constructs typed user-state chains with a bounded entropy-guided Tree-of-Thought (ToT) refinement procedure. To make this structured reasoning usable from sparse profiles, the teacher-curated chains are used to train a student model on static profiles through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Outcome-Driven Segment-Aware Implicit Reward Policy Optimization (OSIPO). ScaleToT then transfers the student's reasoning representations to a lightweight profile encoder, providing shared reasoning signals for the remaining users without LLM inference. We evaluate ScaleToT on lifetime value (LTV) prediction in a billion-scale advertising deployment. A randomized online A/B test increased LT30 by 6.738\%, while offline reasoning covered only 7.32\% of the potential population, greatly reducing compute cost compared with full-population reasoning.

</details>


### [218] [AI Tokenomics: The Economics of Tokens, Computation, and Pricing in Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24616)
*Quanyan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "AI tokenomics," a framework analyzing how tokens—the fundamental unit of computation in foundation model services—are generated, consumed, priced, allocated, and optimized. It distinguishes token expenditure from economic value, highlighting that value depends on marginal productivity, workflow position, hidden reasoning, risk, and downstream propagation, while identifying open research directions in measurement, calibration, and market design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tokens have become the de facto accounting unit across modern AI services, linking computation, memory, energy, pricing, and economic value. However, no unified framework exists to systematically analyze the token economy—how tokens are produced, consumed, priced, and optimized across AI workflows. This gap motivates a structured study of tokenomics to bridge technical token-level costs with broader economic questions of resource allocation, productivity measurement, and market design.

Method: The paper develops a conceptual framework that connects multiple levels of analysis: (1) token-level technical costs (computation, memory, energy), (2) workflow-level production functions that map token usage to outputs, (3) enterprise resource allocation strategies, (4) measurement and instrumentation methods for tracking token flows, and (5) emerging market-design considerations. The framework distinguishes between token expenditure and economic value by incorporating factors such as marginal productivity, workflow position, hidden reasoning activity, risk, and downstream propagation effects.

Result: The framework reveals that token expenditure is not equivalent to economic value; value is mediated by marginal productivity within a workflow, the position of token usage, unobservable reasoning chains (hidden tokens), risk factors, and cascading downstream effects. The paper identifies key open research directions: measuring hidden tokens, empirical calibration of token-productivity relationships, dynamic token allocation optimization, and designing token-based markets.

Conclusion: AI tokenomics provides a structured lens for understanding the economics of foundation model services. The core insight is the decoupling of token cost from token value, with implications for pricing models, resource allocation, and market design. The paper calls for empirical work to calibrate the proposed framework and to develop instrumentation for hidden-token measurement, dynamic allocation mechanisms, and efficient token-based markets.

Abstract: Tokens have become the practical accounting unit for modern foundation model services, linking information processing, computation, memory use, energy expenditure, pricing, and economic value. This paper develops a framework for AI tokenomics: the study of how tokens are generated, consumed, priced, allocated, and optimized across AI systems. We connect token-level technical costs to workflow-level production functions, enterprise resource allocation, measurement and instrumentation methods, and emerging market-design questions. The framework shows that token expenditure and economic value are distinct: value depends on marginal productivity, workflow position, hidden reasoning activity, risk, and downstream propagation effects. The paper concludes by identifying open research directions in hidden-token measurement, empirical calibration, token productivity, dynamic allocation, and token-based markets.

</details>


### [219] [Abstractions of Queries in Ontology-Based Data Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24618)
*Michel Leclère,Marie-Laure Mugnier,Guillaume Pérution-Kihli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies query abstraction in ontology-based data access (OBDA) using existential rules. It extends unions of conjunctive queries (UCQs) with limited inequality and a special predicate for database constants, enabling expression of minimally complete abstractions without increasing complexity. The paper also characterizes maximally sound abstractions by connecting them to the notion of maximum recovery from data exchange.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In OBDA, queries over multiple data sources are mediated through an ontology. Query abstraction aims to translate these queries to the ontology layer, but perfect abstractions may not always exist. This motivates studying minimally complete and maximally sound approximations, requiring an expressive yet computationally tractable query language.

Method: The authors work within an existential rule framework under certain answer semantics. They extend UCQs with a limited form of inequality and a special predicate that marks database constants. They analyze the expressiveness and computational complexity of this extended language for capturing minimally complete and maximally sound abstractions, and establish a formal connection between maximally sound abstractions and maximum recovery from data exchange theory.

Result: The proposed extension to UCQs does not increase the complexity of the relevant problems, yet it is sufficiently expressive to capture minimally complete abstractions (and thus perfect abstractions when they exist). Additionally, maximally sound abstractions are characterized through a novel connection to maximum recovery from data exchange.

Conclusion: The extended UCQ language strikes an effective balance between expressiveness and complexity for query abstraction in OBDA. It enables the representation of minimally complete abstractions and, through the link to maximum recovery, provides a formal foundation for understanding maximally sound abstractions.

Abstract: In ontology-based data access (OBDA), multiple data sources are integrated via mappings to an ontology. We consider an OBDA setting based on existential rules and the certain answer semantics. We address the recent issue of query abstraction, which consists of abstracting data queries by translating them to the ontology layer. Since a perfect abstraction may not exist, the notions of minimally complete and maximally sound abstractions have been introduced.
  We study abstractions within an extension of UCQs with a limited form of inequality and a special predicate marking database constants. While this extension does not lead to an increased complexity of the problems of interest, it is able to express minimally complete abstractions, hence perfect abstractions when they exist. We also characterize maximally sound abstractions by making a new connection with the notion of maximum recovery stemming from data exchange.

</details>


### [220] [When CQs Go Wrong: Challenges in CQ Verification with OE-Assist](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24619)
*Anna Sofia Lippolis,Mohammad Javad Saeedizade,Robin Keskisärkkä,Aldo Gangemi,Eva Blomqvist,Andrea Giovanni Nuzzolese*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates the challenges in Competency Question (CQ) verification for ontology evaluation, analyzing data from 19 participants across 20 tasks with LLM assistance, and concludes that a refinement tool is necessary to reduce ambiguity and complexity in CQs before they enter later ontology engineering phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CQ-verification is an established ontology evaluation method but remains time-consuming and error-prone due to linguistic nuances, ambiguities, and the need for precise alignment with formal ontology constructs. The authors aim to systematically understand what makes a CQ challenging and identify solutions to improve user performance in the verification process.

Method: The authors conducted an experiment with 19 participants who performed CQ-verification across 20 tasks, using an LLM assistant to support the ontology evaluation process. The experimental data was analyzed to identify factors contributing to CQ difficulty and to assess the effectiveness of LLM support.

Result: The results demonstrate the necessity of a dedicated tool to refine CQs prior to publication, in order to avoid ambiguity and excessive complexity that negatively impact downstream ontology engineering activities.

Conclusion: CQ-verification can be significantly impaired by poorly formulated competency questions. Proactive refinement of CQs — ideally through a specialized tool — is recommended to mitigate ambiguity and complexity before CQs are used in formal ontology evaluation, thereby improving both efficiency and reliability of the ontology engineering process.

Abstract: Competency Questions (CQs) are the central component of CQ-verification, an established process in which an ontology is evaluated against a set of natural language questions to determine whether the intended purpose of the ontology has been properly modelled. However, CQ-verification is often time-consuming and error-prone, as it requires careful interpretation of linguistic nuances and precise alignment with formal ontology constructs. Ambiguities and complexity in CQs can further complicate this process, leading to inconsistent modelling decisions and verification outcomes. In this paper, we investigate what makes a CQ challenging and possible solutions to enhance the users' performance in the CQ-verification process. We experimented with the data of 19 participants who performed CQ-verification on 20 tasks using an LLM assistant to support ontology evaluation. The results show the necessity of a tool to refine CQs before publishing them to avoid ambiguity or excessive complexity in later phases of the ontology engineering process.

</details>


### [221] [SAFARI: Scaling Long Horizon Agentic Fault Attribution via Active Investigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24626)
*Chenyang Zhu,Jiayu Yao,Kushal Chawla,Youbing Yin,Nathan Wolfe,Pengshan Cai,Jingyu Wu,Spencer Hong,Sangwoo Cho,Shi-Xiong Zhang,Daben Liu,Sambit Sahu,Erin Babinsky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAFARI is a diagnostic framework that uses tool-augmented loops with short-term memory to attribute faults in long-horizon agent trajectories, decoupling accuracy from context window limits and achieving 20%+ improvements while maintaining precision even when faults lie 5x beyond the context window.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As autonomous agents execute increasingly complex multi-step, multi-agent tasks, their execution trajectories exceed even the largest LLM context windows. Current diagnostic methods that load full trajectories into context suffer from attention dilution and fail entirely when traces exceed context limits, creating a need for scalable fault attribution mechanisms.

Method: SAFARI replaces linear context loading with a tool-augmented diagnostic loop. It equips LLMs with a specialized toolbox for reading and searching trajectory segments, combined with a persistent Short-Term Memory (STM) for cross-turn reasoning. This architecture decouples diagnostic accuracy from architectural context limits by enabling targeted, iterative investigation of trajectory segments rather than full-context loading.

Result: SAFARI outperforms prior state-of-the-art by 20% on the Who&When dataset (within a 1M token budget) and by 19% on the TRAIL GAIA subset (within a 25K token budget). Crucially, it maintains 0.58 precision even when the target fault is located 5x beyond the model's native context window, where traditional evaluators fail completely.

Conclusion: SAFARI demonstrates that tool-augmented, iterative diagnostic loops with persistent memory are a viable and effective paradigm for fault attribution in long-horizon agent trajectories, overcoming fundamental context-window limitations of existing linear approaches.

Abstract: As autonomous agents tackle increasingly complex multi-step, multi-agent tasks, their execution trajectories have scaled beyond the constraints of even the largest context windows. Current methods for effectively diagnosing agent failures load the full trajectory into an LLM's context window, which suffers from attention dilution and fails when agentic traces inevitably exceed context limits. To address this, we introduce SAFARI (Scaling long-horizon Agentic Fault AttRibution via active Investigation), a framework that replaces linear context loading with a tool-augmented diagnostic loop. By equipping LLMs with a specialized toolbox to read and search trajectory segments alongside a persistent Short-Term Memory (STM) for cross-turn reasoning, SAFARI effectively decouples diagnostic accuracy from architectural context limits. Our experiments demonstrate that SAFARI outperforms state-of-the-art results by 20% on the Who&When dataset within a 1M token budget, and by 19% on TRAIL GAIA subset on a 25K token budget. Most significantly, SAFARI maintains a 0.58 precision even when the target fault resides 5x beyond the model's native context window, a scenario where traditional evaluators fail entirely.

</details>


### [222] [CineCap: Structured Reasoning with Spatio-Temporal Anchors for Cinematographic Video Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24636)
*Xinyu Mao,Yuhui Zeng,Xiaokun Liu,Wenyu Qin,Meng Wang,Xin Tao,Pengfei Wan,Xiaohan Xing,Max Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CineCap is a framework for cinematographic captioning that combines structured reasoning grounded in spatio-temporal visual anchors with reinforcement learning using comprehensiveness, accuracy, and gated coverage rewards. It also introduces CineCap Bench (472 annotated video-caption pairs) and achieves state-of-the-art performance against proprietary and open-source baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal LLMs underexplore cinematographic captioning—the task of generating open-form descriptions across multiple cinematographic dimensions (camera movement, shot size, depth of field, composition, shooting angle). Unlike QA-based evaluation, this requires inferring professional film-language concepts from subtle visual evidence and producing captions that are both comprehensive and accurate.

Method: CineCap has two components: (1) structured reasoning with spatio-temporal anchors that grounds cinematographic descriptions in explicit visual evidence and organizes them into compact atomic reasoning for supervised fine-tuning; (2) reinforcement learning with comprehensiveness, accuracy, and gated coverage rewards to balance descriptive completeness and factual correctness. The authors also construct CineCap Bench, a manually annotated benchmark of 472 video-caption pairs.

Result: Extensive experiments show CineCap consistently outperforms strong proprietary and open-source baselines, establishing a new state of the art for cinematographic captioning.

Conclusion: CineCap advances fine-grained video understanding and supports controllable movie-quality video generation. The code, model checkpoint, and benchmark are publicly released.

Abstract: Cinematographic captioning aims to describe how a video is filmed using professional film-language concepts such as camera movement, shot size, depth of field, composition, and shooting angle. This capability is important for fine-grained video understanding and controllable movie-quality video generation, yet remains underexplored in existing multimodal large language models. Unlike question-answering-based evaluation of cinematic understanding, cinematographic captioning requires a unified open-form description over multiple cinematographic dimensions. This task is challenging for two main reasons: the model must infer professional cinematographic concepts from subtle visual evidence, and it must generate captions that are both comprehensive and accurate. Accordingly, we propose CineCap, a framework that combines structured reasoning with spatio-temporal anchors and reinforcement learning with comprehensiveness, accuracy, and gated coverage rewards. The former grounds professional cinematographic descriptions in explicit visual evidence and organizes them into compact atomic reasoning for supervised fine-tuning, while the latter improves the balance between descriptive completeness and factual correctness. In addition, we construct CineCap Bench, a benchmark of 472 manually annotated video-caption pairs for systematic evaluation. Extensive experiments show that CineCap consistently outperforms strong proprietary and open-source baselines, establishing a new state of the art for cinematographic captioning. The code, model checkpoint, and benchmark are publicly available in https://github.com/Hectormxy/CineCap.git.

</details>


### [223] [LaGO: Latent Action Guidance for Online Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24669)
*Kuan-Yen Liu,Ren-Jyun Huang,Ti-Rong Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LaGO proposes using pretrained LLMs as latent action priors to softly guide online RL (PPO), rather than as direct controllers, improving success rates on CLEVR-Robot (15.1%→27.2%) and Meta-World (2.7%→15.2%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work treats LLMs as direct controllers for planning, requiring precise action generation, which is unreliable. The authors aim to leverage LLM knowledge more robustly by using it as a soft prior rather than an explicit planner.

Method: LaGO integrates a pretrained LLM into online RL by converting the LLM's outputs into a latent action prior that softly guides policy optimization (PPO), rather than directly producing executable actions. The framework is evaluated on discrete (CLEVR-Robot) and continuous (Meta-World) control benchmarks.

Result: LaGO consistently outperforms Vanilla PPO: average success rate rises from 15.1% to 27.2% on CLEVR-Robot and from 2.7% to 15.2% on Meta-World. Stronger LLMs yield more effective guidance.

Conclusion: Using LLMs as latent action priors for soft guidance is more effective and reliable than using them as direct controllers. LLM knowledge can meaningfully improve online planning and sequential decision-making in RL.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong potential for planning and sequential decision-making, but prior work often relies on using them as direct controllers, which requires precise action generation and can be unreliable in practice. This paper proposes Latent Action Guidance for Online Reinforcement Learning (LaGO), a framework that uses a pretrained LLM as a latent action prior to softly guide online policy optimization, rather than treating the LLM as an explicit planner or controller. Experiments on both a discrete-control benchmark, CLEVR-Robot, and a continuous-control benchmark, Meta-World, demonstrate that LaGO consistently improves both reward and success rate over Vanilla PPO. In particular, LaGO increases the average success rate from 15.1% to 27.2% on CLEVR-Robot and from 2.7% to 15.2% on Meta-World. Our analysis further shows that stronger pretrained LLMs provide more effective guidance, suggesting that LLM knowledge can improve planning and online decision-making.

</details>


### [224] [Cost-Optimal Decision Diagrams for Stochastic Boolean Function Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24672)
*Xia Zong,Tuomo Lehtonen,Jussi Rintanen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of finding a cost-optimal deterministic strategy to evaluate propositional formulas when acquiring variable truth values incurs different costs. The authors propose a branch-and-bound exact algorithm with heuristics, pruning, and caching, achieving the first practical exact solver for this general setting. They also prove the problem is #P-hard and in PSPACE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many real-world decision-making scenarios (e.g., medical diagnosis, fault detection), observing different attributes incurs heterogeneous costs. The existing literature lacked practical exact algorithms that minimize expected evaluation cost for general propositional formulas under arbitrary variable costs and probabilistic truth assignments. This gap motivates the need for an exact, scalable solution.

Method: The authors formulate the problem as constructing a deterministic evaluation strategy (decision tree) that minimizes expected cost. They develop a branch-and-bound algorithm incorporating: (1) variable-selection heuristics to guide search order, (2) pruning rules to eliminate suboptimal branches, and (3) caching mechanisms to avoid redundant computation. They also introduce a greedy beam-search variant for trading solution quality against runtime.

Result: Experimental evaluation on random instances shows the exact algorithm scales to non-trivial problem sizes. The beam-search variant demonstrates an efficiency-quality trade-off. The approach is also tested on a structured heart-disease diagnosis instance. On the theoretical side, the problem is proved #P-hard and contained within PSPACE.

Conclusion: The paper delivers the first practical exact algorithm for cost-sensitive propositional formula evaluation under variable costs, backed by both empirical validation and complexity-theoretic characterization (#P-hard, in PSPACE). The greedy beam-search extension provides a tunable approximate alternative for harder instances.

Abstract: In many decision-making scenarios, acquiring information incurs different costs. We consider the problem of constructing a deterministic evaluation strategy that minimizes the expected cost of evaluating a propositional formula under variable costs and a probability distribution over truth assignments. We present a branch-and-bound algorithm with variable-selection heuristics, pruning, and caching. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first practical exact algorithm for this level of generality. Experiments on random instances demonstrate scalability and quantify the efficiency-quality trade-off of a greedy beam-search variant. We additionally evaluate a structured heart-disease diagnosis instance. Finally, we prove that the problem is $\#P$-hard and contained in $\mathrm{PSPACE}$.

</details>


### [225] [Decentralised AI Training and Inference with BlockTrain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24722)
*Peter Toth*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Spheroid BlockTrain proposes a decentralized training protocol where a model is split into independently trainable blocks using local objectives derived from a global target, achieving near-parity with end-to-end Transformers on WikiText while enabling geographically distributed training and inference over TCP/WAN.


<details>
