  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier AI training is dominated by hyperscalers with access to dense, centrally controlled accelerator clusters, creating structural barriers for open/independent AI efforts that lack capital, privileged infrastructure, and data-center geography. The work aims to democratize large-model training by enabling decentralized, geographically distributed training.

Method: The model is partitioned into independently trainable blocks, each optimized on a local objective derived from the same global target. At inference, blocks are composed into a single model. A shared-worker variant averages same-block updates across workers into one assembled model. Transport experiments use HTTP/TCP for real serialized checkpoints, including public-IP multi-host runs. Inference uses one block-stack traversal per full output over direct TCP across public-network GPU hosts.

Result: On byte-level WikiText, BlockTrain achieves cross-entropy 1.359 (perplexity 3.89), within ~0.04 CE of an end-to-end Transformer. A six-worker shared run reaches CE 1.385. A public-IP three-host run improves CE from 5.580 to 1.811 while moving 15.22 GB. For inference, the protocol serves up to a 75.80B-parameter logical fp16 shape over TCP across three public-network GPU hosts, outperforming a plain-autoregressive TCP pipeline baseline by emitting a full sequence per WAN traversal.

Conclusion: Decentralized block-wise training with local objectives is viable, achieving near-equivalent performance to end-to-end Transformers while drastically reducing per-worker resource requirements and enabling training/inference over wide-area networks, thus lowering barriers for open and independent AI development.

Abstract: Frontier AI training is increasingly shaped by access to dense, centrally controlled accelerator clusters. This creates a structural advantage for hyperscalers and large centralized laboratories, and makes open or independent AI efforts depend on scarce capital, privileged infrastructure, and data-center geography. We present Spheroid BlockTrain, a decentralized training protocol in which a model is partitioned into independently trainable blocks, each optimized on a local objective derived from the same global target and composed at inference into one model. On byte-level WikiText, BlockTrain reaches cross entropy 1.359 (perplexity 3.89), within about 0.04 CE of a same-setup end-to-end Transformer reference, while each active worker trains only one block and avoids full-model optimizer state. A shared six-worker block training run reaches CE 1.385 by averaging same-block updates into one assembled model. HTTP/TCP transport experiments move real serialized checkpoints and updates, including a public-IP three-host run that improves CE from 5.580 to 1.811 while moving 15.22 GB. For inference, the current BlockTrain path uses one block-stack traversal per full output and serves over direct TCP across three public-network GPU hosts up to a 75.80B-parameter logical fp16 shape, outperforming a matched plain-autoregressive TCP pipeline baseline because it emits a full sequence per WAN pipeline traversal rather than one token per traversal.

</details>


### [226] [Scaling Laws for Task-Specific LLM Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24747)
*Lavinia Ghita,Dhruv Desai,Ioana Boier*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper derives empirical scaling laws for domain-specific LLM compression using quantitative finance as a testbed, showing that supervision format (particularly chain-of-thought) is the key driver of the tradeoff between in-domain performance and general knowledge retention under iterative structural pruning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly capable but their large scale creates deployment challenges where latency and cost constraints matter. There is a lack of principled, empirically-grounded frameworks for compressing LLMs in domain-specific settings while understanding the tradeoff between preserving in-domain task quality and general knowledge.

Method: The authors use quantitative finance as the application domain. They compare logit-based and LoRA-based distillation under iterative structural pruning, introduce a blended chain-of-thought supervision loss to stabilize KL-divergence distillation over reasoning traces, and release a dataset called FinHeadlineMix. They systematically vary dataset size, compression ratio, supervision format, and pruning schedule to derive scaling laws.

Result: In-domain task quality degrades predictably with compression, while general-knowledge benchmarks collapse earlier. Chain-of-thought supervision actively recovers general knowledge that pruning erases, making supervision format the key driver of the in-domain vs. general knowledge tradeoff.

Conclusion: Domain-specific compression can be guided by predictable scaling laws. The choice of supervision format — particularly chain-of-thought blending — is critical for balancing in-domain performance with general knowledge retention, and the released dataset and framework provide reusable guidance for practitioners.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve strong performance across a growing range of domains, yet their scale poses deployment challenges in applications where latency and cost constraints are critical. This paper derives empirical scaling laws for domain-specific LLM compression, quantifying how in-domain and general knowledge performance scale with dataset size, compression ratio, supervision format, and iterative pruning schedule. Using quantitative finance as our application domain, we compare logit-based and LoRA-based distillation under iterative structural pruning, introducing a blended chain-of-thought supervision loss that stabilizes KL-divergence distillation over reasoning traces. In-domain task quality degrades predictably under compression while general-knowledge benchmarks collapse well before the same point; supervision format is the key driver of this tradeoff, with chain-of-thought supervision actively recovering general knowledge that pruning erases. We release the headline dataset FinHeadlineMix, scaling law results, and practical recommendations to provide a reusable framework for domain-specific compression decisions.

</details>


### [227] [Can Scale Save Us From Plasticity Loss in Large Language Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24752)
*J. Fernando Hernandez-Garcia,Tomás Figliolia,Beren Millidge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates plasticity loss in modern GPT-style Transformer models (5M–314M parameters) under multilingual continual learning. The authors find that plasticity loss persists in these architectures, follows a predictable sublinear scaling law with model size, and occurs even under stationary training—not just abrupt task shifts—suggesting larger models only delay but do not prevent the inevitable loss of adaptability to new data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plasticity loss has been studied for decades but primarily in older, smaller architectures and rarely in natural-language domains. The authors aim to determine whether this fundamental challenge to continual learning remains relevant in modern transformer-based LLMs.

Method: The authors train GPT-style Transformer models (5M to 314M non-embedding parameters) on a multilingual continual learning problem and measure plasticity loss via deterioration on a held-out Vietnamese probing task. They analyze scaling behavior and also test for plasticity loss under stationary multilingual training (no abrupt task changes).

Result: Plasticity loss is observed across all model sizes. Its onset follows a predictable scaling law, growing sublinearly with model size—larger models delay but do not eliminate the effect. Plasticity loss also occurs under stationary training, challenging the view that it is exclusive to continual learning with abrupt task boundaries.

Conclusion: Even large Transformer LMs trained on natural language will eventually lose the ability to efficiently adapt to new data after sufficiently long training, in both continual and stationary settings. Merely scaling parameter count is insufficient to fully prevent plasticity loss.

Abstract: The loss of plasticity - the ability of a network to learn new information after having already learned older information - is a fundamental challenge in creating artificial neural networks capable of continual learning. Although this phenomenon has been known for decades, it has mostly been studied in older, relatively small architectures and rarely in natural-language domains. To determine whether loss of plasticity remains a problem in the modern transformer-based LLM paradigm, we study plasticity loss in GPT-style Transformer models trained on a multilingual continual learning problem. Consistent with prior work, we find evidence of plasticity loss across models ranging from 5M to 314M non-embedding parameters, as measured by deterioration on a held-out Vietnamese probing task. We further find that the onset of plasticity loss follows a predictable scaling law, growing sublinearly with model size. These results suggest that larger models may delay the measurable effects of plasticity loss, but that increasing parameter count alone is likely to be insufficient to completely prevent it. We also find evidence of plasticity loss under stationary multilingual training, challenging the view that the phenomenon is exclusive to continual learning with abrupt task changes. Overall, our results suggest that even large Transformer language models trained on natural-language will eventually lose the ability to efficiently adapt to new data after sufficiently long training, in both continual and stationary settings.

</details>


### [228] [BluTrain: A C++/CUDA Framework for AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24780)
*Adhitya Charan,Adwaid Suresh,Anuj Kumar,Aparna A,Dhanakumar K,Dharun M S,Dinesh G,Goutham Kumar Reddy K,Harshini V M,Jenifa D,Jona Delcy C A,Kathirvel S,Killi Uma Maheswara Rao,Kiruthik Kanna M,Kurra Vishnu Sai,Madhumithaa G K,Navin Kumar,Ram Charan Golla,Revathi T,Rishikkanth R,Sanjay Krishna M,Surendra Vendra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BluTrain is a from-scratch deep learning training framework written in C++/CUDA that provides native implementations of every layer (tensors, autograd, linear algebra, memory allocator, distributed execution, MLIR compiler). In benchmarks training a 124M GPT-2 model on 8× RTX 6000 Ada GPUs, it achieves 407K tokens/s (vs. PyTorch's 395K), up to 22% memory reduction, and slightly better validation loss, all while preserving numerical fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: At scale, deep learning performance is dominated by systems engineering rather than model architecture. Existing frameworks introduce overhead and obscure hardware-level control. The authors sought a framework that gives absolute control over hardware expression while abstracting systems complexity, eliminating repetitive orchestration logic, and making modelling seamless.

Method: BluTrain was architected from first principles in standard C++ and CUDA, with every layer implemented natively: (1) a typed tensor module with reverse-mode autograd, (2) a linear-algebra library, (3) a caching allocator, (4) a multi-mode distributed-execution module, and (5) an MLIR-based deep-learning compiler. Evaluation uses a 124M-parameter GPT-2 baseline trained in FP32 on an 8-GPU RTX 6000 Ada system, comparing throughput, memory footprint, numerical fidelity, and final validation loss against PyTorch.

Result: BluTrain sustained 407K tokens/s versus PyTorch's 395K tokens/s (throughput improvement), achieved up to 22% memory footprint reduction, strictly preserved numerical fidelity, and converged to a marginally lower final validation loss.

Conclusion: BluTrain demonstrates that a from-scratch, natively implemented framework can outperform industry-standard baselines in both throughput and memory efficiency without compromising numerical fidelity or model quality. Since every layer is open to native tuning, the framework's performance ceiling can be progressively raised through further optimization.

Abstract: Progress in deep learning is, at scale, more a matter of systems engineering than of modelling: the behaviour of a model in training (its throughput, its memory footprint, and the numerical fidelity of the result) is determined less by the architecture itself than by how that architecture is expressed on the hardware. To achieve absolute control over this hardware expression while abstracting away systems complexity to make modelling seamless and eliminating the need for repetitive orchestration logic, BluTrain was architected from first principles as a robust, lightweight, and architecture-general training framework in standard C++ and the core CUDA programming model. Every layer is implemented natively: a typed tensor module with reverse-mode autograd, a linear-algebra library, a caching allocator, a multi-mode distributed-execution module, and an MLIR-based deep-learning compiler. In formal evaluations training a 124M-parameter GPT-2 baseline in FP32 on an 8-GPU 6000 Ada system, BluTrain outperforms industry-standard baselines in both throughput (sustaining an average of 407K tokens/s versus PyTorch's 395K tokens/s) and memory efficiency (achieving up to a 22% footprint reduction), while strictly preserving numerical fidelity and converging to a marginally lower final validation loss. With every layer explicitly open to native tuning, the performance ceiling is the framework's own to raise.

</details>


### [229] [Solving Inverse Problems of Chaotic Systems with Bidirectional Conditional Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24824)
*Peiyan Hu,Jian Zhang,Jiashu Pan,Ruiqi Feng,Tao Zhang,Zhi-Ming Ma,Yuan-Sen Ting,Gongjie Li,Tailin Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Bidirectional Conditional Flow Matching (Bi-CFM) to solve inverse problems in chaotic dynamics—inferring initial conditions from final states. It also presents CBi-CFM, a conservation-constrained variant. The method achieves superior distribution-level accuracy and orders-of-magnitude speedup across Lorenz, Circuit, Lorenz 96, and planetary systems, and demonstrates scalability to real-world collisional million-body globular cluster observations over 10 Gyr timescales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inverse problems in chaotic dynamics—recovering initial conditions from final states—remain largely unsolved due to ill-posedness, non-uniqueness, instability, and potentially chaotic time-reverse dynamics. Existing methods struggle with exponential error accumulation and fail to capture the inherent stochasticity of chaotic evolution over long timescales.

Method: Bi-CFM learns bidirectional conditional flow-based mappings between distributions of initial and final states, capturing the stochastic nature of chaotic evolution and mitigating exponential error growth. For systems with conservation laws, CBi-CFM extends Bi-CFM by incorporating conservation constraints into the flow matching framework.

Result: Bi-CFM achieves improvements across five distribution-level metrics over baselines on Lorenz, Circuit, and Lorenz 96 systems, with over 100× speedup. In the three-body planet-planet scattering problem, CBi-CFM yields conservation errors comparable to ground truth. On real globular cluster observations (collisional million-body systems over 10 Gyr), the method demonstrates state-of-the-art accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed Bi-CFM and CBi-CFM framework establishes a scalable, accurate, and fast solution to inverse problems for long-timescale chaotic dynamics, bridging the gap from idealized low-dimensional systems to real-world astrophysical many-body systems.

Abstract: Modeling chaotic systems is crucial yet challenging. Inverse problems in chaotic dynamics, namely inferring initial conditions from final states, remain largely unsolved because of ill-posedness, non-uniqueness, instability, and potentially chaotic time-reverse dynamics. We address this open problem with Bidirectional Conditional Flow Matching (Bi-CFM), which learns bidirectional mappings between distributions of initial and final states to capture the stochasticity of chaotic evolution and mitigate exponential error accumulation over time. Furthermore, for systems with conservation laws, we extend it to Conservation-constrained Bi-CFM (CBi-CFM). Across the classic Lorenz, Circuit, and high-dimensional Lorenz 96 systems, Bi-CFM improves five distribution-level metrics over baselines while achieving a speedup of more than two orders of magnitude. In the three-body planet-planet scattering problem in planetary dynamics, CBi-CFM better respects conservation laws, with conservation errors comparable to those of the ground truth. Finally, on real observations of globular clusters, collisional million-body systems shaped by $\sim 10^{10}$ years (10 Gyr) of evolution, our method represents an advance in accuracy, establishing a scalable route to solving inverse problems of long-timescale real-world chaotic dynamics.

</details>


### [230] [Difference-Making without Making a Difference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24832)
*Sander Beckers*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that Andreas & Günther's seven definitions of actual causation, classified into three supposedly distinct types (factual difference-making, counterfactual difference-making, and regularity-based), are not genuinely distinct. The author demonstrates that the most recent factual difference-making definition instantiates all three types simultaneously, collapsing the taxonomy. Comparative analysis on key examples further shows this undermines all seven accounts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge Andreas & Günther's taxonomic framework of actual causation by showing that their three categories are not mutually exclusive — a factual difference-making account can simultaneously satisfy the criteria of counterfactual difference-making and regularity-based accounts — thereby questioning the conceptual clarity and distinctness of all seven proposed definitions.

Method: Conceptual analysis and formal demonstration: the author shows that the most recent (factual difference-making) definition satisfies the formal conditions of all three account types, proving the categories overlap. This is supplemented by comparative testing of the novel account against the six prior accounts on crucial counterexample cases to reveal internal inconsistencies and broader undermining effects.

Result: The factual difference-making definition instantiates all three account types — factual difference-making, counterfactual difference-making, and regularity-based — demonstrating the distinctions are empty. Comparative case analysis reveals this collapse undermines the coherence of all seven of Andreas & Günther's accounts.

Conclusion: The three-way taxonomy proposed by Andreas & Günther is a distinction without a difference; the collapse of categories undermines the entire series of seven definitions, calling into question the viability of their approach to defining actual causation.

Abstract: Over a series of seven papers, Andreas & Günther have introduced seven definitions of actual causation and have classified them as belonging to three different, competing, types of accounts: factual difference-making, counterfactual difference-making, and regularity-based. I show that their most recent - factual difference-making - definition instantiates all three types, thereby proving that these are distinctions without a difference. I further compare their novel account to the other six accounts on several crucial examples, revealing that this undermines all seven of their accounts.

</details>


### [231] [Accuracy and Satisfaction in Multi-Turn LLM Dialogues for NFR Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24834)
*Ali Pourghasemi Fatideh,Wilder Baldwin,Maria Dhakal,Collin McMillan,Sepideh Ghanavati*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study evaluating multi-turn LLM-based dialogue (GitHub Copilot) for assessing HIPAA Non-Functional Requirements found that while developers tend to agree with the LLM, accuracy against expert ground truth is low. Longer responses and more information-providing turns reduce user satisfaction, whereas proactive system interactions improve it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation benchmarks for software development focus solely on functional correctness, neglecting the assessment of Non-Functional Requirements (NFRs). NFRs are vague, context-dependent, and span multiple program components, requiring collaborative, multi-turn reasoning that single-turn accuracy metrics cannot capture. A gap exists in understanding how well LLM-based dialogue assistants support NFR assessment.

Method: The authors conducted a user study with 49 programmers who used GitHub Copilot to assess 148 HIPAA-derived NFRs against the iTrust codebase (a HIPAA-compliant system). Assessments were evaluated across three dimensions: requirement satisfaction level, reasoning, and code localization. User satisfaction was modeled against system response characteristics (length, information-providing turns, proactivity). Accuracy was measured against expert-annotated ground truth.

Result: Developers showed high agreement with LLM assessments, but accuracy compared to expert ground truth was low. Modeling of user satisfaction revealed that longer system responses and more information-providing turns negatively affected satisfaction, while proactive system interactions had a positive effect.

Conclusion: LLM-based dialogue assistants require significant improvement in NFR assessment accuracy. System design should prioritize concise responses, minimize purely informational turns, and incorporate proactive interaction patterns to enhance developer satisfaction during collaborative NFR reasoning tasks.

Abstract: LLM-based dialogue assistants have become mainstream tools for software developers, yet current evaluation benchmarks focus exclusively on functional correctness. This leaves a critical gap in assessing the quality and accuracy of these conversations when handling Non-Functional Requirements (NFRs), which are inherently vague, context-dependent, and involve many parts of a program. Evaluating how well these systems support collaborative reasoning about NFRs requires methods that go beyond single-turn accuracy to capture both the correctness of the system's outputs and the quality of the multi-turn interaction. In this paper, we investigate the accuracy and quality of multi-turn conversations between developers and an LLM-based agent in the domain of Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA) regulatory compliance. We hired 49 programmers to interact with GitHub Copilot to assess 148 HIPAA-derived NFRs against the iTrust codebase, a system designed to comply with HIPAA regulations, across three dimensions: requirement satisfaction level, reasoning, and code localization. We find that developers tend to agree with LLM assessments, but accuracy against expert ground truth is low. We model user satisfaction and find that longer system responses and more information-providing turns negatively affect user satisfaction, whereas proactive interactions positively affect it. Our findings provide insights for designing LLM-based dialogue systems that support NFR assessment.

</details>


### [232] [Grading the Grader: Lessons from Evaluating an Agentic Data Analysis System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24839)
*Tian Zheng,Kai-Tai Hsu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates the reliability of automated grading for agentic data-analysis systems, developing a three-layer human-AI cascade (regex strict, LLM lenient, human snippet) evaluated on 153 QRData tasks. Both graders achieve 100% precision; lenient recall reaches 97%. A keyword-anchored extraction pipeline boosts strict recall by 60pp, and an iterative nudge mechanism raises run success from 36% to 97%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic data analysis systems produce complex, multi-component outputs (code, numerical results, diagnostics), making evaluation challenging. There's a need to distinguish genuine agent errors from grading artifacts and to understand how reliably automated graders can assess such systems.

Method: The authors apply LAMBDA (a multi-agent data-analysis system) to 153 numerical QRData tasks from DSGym. They develop a three-layer grading cascade: (1) strict regex matching, (2) LLM-based lenient grading, and (3) snippet-based human inspection. They also implement a keyword-anchored extraction pipeline and an iterative nudge mechanism (with/without original-question re-injection) to improve grading quality.

Result: Both automated graders achieve 100% observed precision (0/70 false positives). The lenient grader attains 97% recall against human labels. Keyword-anchored extraction raises strict grader recall by 60 percentage points over a last-number heuristic. The iterative nudge mechanism improves run success from 36% to 97% and lenient-pass rates from 16% to 46%. Question re-injection shows no benefit, confirming the nudge functions as an answer template cue. Variable type emerges as the task metadata field most consistently associated with grading dynamics and outcome grades.

Conclusion: Automated graders can achieve high precision with negligible false positives for agentic data-analysis outputs. Combining strict regex and lenient LLM-based grading with targeted extraction and nudging strategies significantly improves recall and run success. The nudge operates as an answer template cue rather than requiring semantic re-contextualization, and task metadata (especially variable type) strongly influences grading dynamics.

Abstract: Agentic data analysis systems produce rich outputs, including code, numerical results, and verbal diagnostics. This makes them more challenging to evaluate than single-turn LLM responses. It is therefore necessary to distinguish genuine disagreement between an agent's output and a ground-truth answer from grading artifacts. We investigate how reliably automated graders assess such a system and what strategies improve grading quality by applying LAMBDA, a multi-agent data-analysis system, on 153 numerical QRData tasks from DSGym. We develop and evaluate a three-layer human-AI grading cascade: strict regex matching, LLM-based lenient grading, and snippet-based human inspection, which combines non-GenAI and GenAI strategies with different failure profiles. Both automated graders achieve 100% observed precision (0/70 false positives). The lenient grader's recall is 97% against human labels. A keyword-anchored extraction pipeline raises the strict grader's recall by 60 percentage points over a last-number heuristic; the lenient grader is architecturally parser-independent. An iterative nudge mechanism raises grading run success from 36% to 97% and lenient-pass rates from 16% to 46%; comparing nudging with and without original-question re-injection shows that re-injection offers no benefit, confirming the nudge as an answer template cue. We further observe in this case study that variable type is the task metadata field most consistently associated with grading pipeline dynamics and observed outcome grades.

</details>


### [233] [Matching Tasks to Objectives: Fine-Tuning and Prompt-Tuning Strategies for Encoder-Decoder Pre-trained Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24841)
*Ahmad Pouramini,Hesham Faili*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Match Task to Objective (MTO) framework, which leverages multiple pre-training objectives to boost performance of encoder-decoder language models on generation and QA tasks. By aligning task data with appropriate objectives during unsupervised adaptation and using objective-aligned templates during fine-tuning, the method achieves over 120% performance gains in few-shot settings and also improves prompt-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt-based learning has become dominant in NLP, yet the impact of diverse pre-training objectives on downstream task performance—especially for commonsense knowledge retrieval and completion—remains underexplored. The authors aim to systematically investigate how aligning pre-training objectives with task requirements can substantially improve model performance in both few-shot and full-dataset scenarios.

Method: The Match Task to Objective (MTO) framework is proposed. It includes: (1) automated methods to determine the most suitable pre-training objective for a given task; (2) unsupervised adaptation by preparing task-related data according to the identified objective; and (3) designing novel templates during fine-tuning that align with pre-training and adaptation objectives. The approach is further extended to prompt-tuning, offering guidance for soft prompt engineering and optimization. Multiple pre-training objectives are incorporated across both stages.

Result: The proposed strategies achieve over 120% performance gain compared to conventional methods in few-shot settings. They significantly outperform related works in few-shot settings and surpass the baseline even in full-dataset scenarios. Prompt-tuning performance is also significantly enhanced through the extended methodology.

Conclusion: Incorporating multiple objectives during pre-training and fine-tuning, guided by the MTO framework, yields substantial performance improvements. The approach provides precise guidance for selecting and optimizing models for specific tasks and holds significant value for customizing PLMs. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Prompt-based learning has emerged as a dominant paradigm in natural language processing. This study explores the impact of diverse pre-training objectives on the performance of encoder-decoder pre-trained language models across generation and question answering tasks, with a focus on commonsense knowledge retrieval and completion. We highlight the benefits of incorporating multiple objectives during both pre-training and fine-tuning stages. We introduce the Match Task to Objective (MTO) framework and methods for determining the appropriate objective for a given task. This framework offers automated methods to prepare task-related data for adaptation through unsupervised training, based on the identified objective. In the fine-tuning stage, we design novel templates that align with the objectives of the pre-training and adaptation stages. When aligned with task requirements, these strategies can achieve a performance gain of over 120\% compared to conventional methods in few-shot settings. They significantly outperform related works in few-shot settings and exceed the baseline even in full-dataset scenarios. Furthermore, we extend this approach to include prompt-tuning methodologies, providing guidance for more effective soft prompt engineering and optimization. Our strategies significantly enhance prompt-tuning performance as well. These insights hold substantial value, precisely guiding the selection and optimization of models customized for specific tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/puraminy/MTO/

</details>


### [234] [World Models in Pieces: Structural Certification for General Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24842)
*Yikai Lu,Yifei Wu,Xinyu Lu,Tongxin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In the big-world regime, no agent can be universally capable. The paper proves general agents are not universal, rendering worst-case analysis uninformative, then introduces "structural certification"—a transition-local framework providing entry-wise guarantees on an agent's internal world model via bounded goal-conditioned performance. A constructive algorithm filters transitions using deep compositional goals, yielding an O(1/n)+O(δ) error bound that is tight in the small-δ regime, enabling certifiable deployment of general agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard uniform (worst-case) guarantees fail in the big-world regime because agent ability is inevitably specialized—they cannot be universally capable. This means uniform guarantees cannot distinguish between critical bottleneck failures and irrelevant failures. The authors aim to replace uninformative worst-case analysis with a localized certification framework that pinpoints exactly which transitions in an agent's world model are reliable for long-horizon planning.

Method: The authors first formally prove that general agents are not universal, establishing the inadequacy of worst-case analysis. They then introduce "structural certification," a transition-local framework that maps bounded goal-conditioned performance guarantees to entry-wise error bounds on the agent's internal world model. Their main contribution is constructive: they provide algorithms that filter specific transitions using deep compositional goals (goals with compositional structure) and prove that a general agent evaluated on these goals possesses a structural world model with error bounded by O(1/n) + O(δ). They further prove this bound is tight in the small-δ regime, with the existence of such a regime explicitly guaranteed by the certification framework.

Result: The paper delivers three key results: (1) a formal proof that general agents are not universal, (2) a constructive structural certification framework yielding an O(1/n) + O(δ) error bound on the agent's world model for filtered transitions, and (3) a tightness result showing the bound cannot be improved in the small-δ regime. These collectively enable the identification of specific transitions where long-horizon planning is certifiably reliable.

Conclusion: Structural certification overcomes the limitations of uniform worst-case guarantees in the big-world regime by providing localized, entry-wise guarantees on an agent's world model. This allows certifiable deployment of general agents by explicitly identifying the transitions where long-horizon planning can be trusted, effectively distinguishing critical bottlenecks from irrelevant failures.

Abstract: In the big-world regime, agents cannot be universally capable and their ability is inevitably specialized across a world model in pieces. Consequently, standard uniform guarantees fail to distinguish between the understanding of critical bottlenecks and irrelevant failures. We first formalize this limitation by proving that general agents are not universal, rendering standard worst-case analysis uninformative. To overcome this, we introduce structural certification, a transition-local framework that maps bounded goal-conditioned performance to entry-wise guarantees on the agent's internal world model. Our main contribution is constructive. We provide algorithms that filter specific transitions using deep compositional goals and prove that a general agent on these goals has a structural world model with a $\mathcal{O}(1/n) + \mathcal{O}(δ)$ error bound. Conversely, this bound is tight in the small-$δ$ regime, whose existence is explicitly guaranteed by our certification. These results enable the certifiable deployment of general agents by localizing the specific transitions where long-horizon planning is reliable.

</details>


### [235] [OpenThoughts-Agent: Data Recipes for Agentic Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24855)
*Negin Raoof,Richard Zhuang,Marianna Nezhurina,Etash Guha,Atula Tejaswi,Ryan Marten,Charlie F. Ruan,Tyler Griggs,Alexander Glenn Shaw,Hritik Bansal,E. Kelly Buchanan,Artem Gazizov,Reinhard Heckel,Chinmay Hegde,Sankalp Jajee,Daanish Khazi,Emmanouil Koukoumidis,Xiangyi Li,Hange Liu,Shlok Natarajan,Harsh Raj,Nicholas Roberts,Ethan Shen,Nishad Singhi,Michael Siu,Ashima Suvarna,Hanwen Xing,Patrick Yubeaton,Robert Zhang,Leon Liangyu Chen,Xiaokun Chen,Steven Dillmann,Saadia Gabriel,Xunyi Jiang,Anurag Kashyap,Boxuan Li,Yein Park,Minh Pham,Sujay Sanghavi,Lin Shi,Ke Sun,Yixin Wang,Zhiwei Xu,Erica Zhang,Siyan Zhao,Wanjia Zhao,Jenia Jitsev,Alex Dimakis,Benjamin Feuer,Ludwig Schmidt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The OT-Agent project presents a fully open data curation pipeline for training broadly capable agentic language models. Through 100+ ablation experiments, the authors identify key factors for generalization across diverse agentic tasks. Fine-tuning Qwen3-32B on their 100K-example dataset achieves 44.8% average accuracy across seven benchmarks, outperforming the best existing open agentic model (Nemotron-Terminal-32B, 40.9%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current open efforts for agentic training data (SWE-Smith, SERA, Nemotron-Terminal) target single benchmarks, leaving a gap in understanding how to curate data that enables generalization across diverse agentic tasks. The authors aim to systematically investigate data curation for broadly capable agents.

Method: The authors develop a fully open data curation pipeline and conduct over 100 controlled ablation experiments to systematically study each stage. They then assemble a 100K-example training set from this pipeline and fine-tune Qwen3-32B, evaluating on seven diverse agentic benchmarks. Scaling properties are assessed via compute-controlled comparisons across varying training set sizes.

Result: The fine-tuned Qwen3-32B achieves 44.8% average accuracy across seven benchmarks, a 3.9 percentage point improvement over Nemotron-Terminal-32B (40.9%). The training data demonstrates strong scaling properties, outperforming alternative open datasets at every training set size. Key insights on task sources and diversity are derived from the ablation studies.

Conclusion: A carefully designed, diverse data curation pipeline is critical for training agentic models that generalize across tasks. The project releases all training sets, the pipeline, experimental data, and models openly to advance future research in agentic model training.

Abstract: Agentic language models dramatically expand the applications of AI yet little is publicly known about how to curate training data for broadly capable agents. Existing open efforts such as SWE-Smith, SERA, and Nemotron-Terminal typically target a single benchmark, leaving open the question of how to train models that generalize across diverse agentic tasks. The OpenThoughts-Agent (OT-Agent) project addresses this gap with a fully open data curation pipeline for training agentic models. We conduct more than 100 controlled ablation experiments to systematically investigate each stage of the pipeline, yielding insights on the importance of task sources and diversity. We then assemble a training set of 100K examples from our pipeline and fine-tune Qwen3-32B on this dataset, which yields an average accuracy of 44.8% across seven agentic benchmarks and a 3.9 percentage point improvement over the strongest existing open data agentic model (Nemotron-Terminal-32B, 40.9%). Moreover, our training data exhibits strong scaling properties, outperforming alternative open datasets at every training set size in compute-controlled comparisons. We publicly release our training sets, data pipeline, experimental data, and models at openthoughts.ai to support future open research on agentic model training.

</details>
